R(M)=\{{s\in M}:{1\in sM}\}
for the submonoid of all right units of
M
. (The submonoid
R(M)
is also Green’s
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-class of
1\in M
.)

Lemma 4.23
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is an action pair in a monoid
M
.

(i)

If
s\in S\cap R(M)
, then
s^{+}=1
.

(ii)

If
S\subseteq R(M)
, then
(U,S)
is strong.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.15
(ii)
, it suffices to prove the first part. For this, suppose
{s\in S\cap R(M)}
, so that
1=st
for some
t\in M
. Combining this with
(

+

1)
, we have
{1=st=s^{+}st=s^{+}}
.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

It of course follows from Lemma
4.23
(ii)
that
(U,S)
is strong if
S
is contained in the group of (two-sided) units of
M
.

The condition
S\subseteq R(M)
is certainly not necessary for
(U,S)
to be strong; see Example
4.30
(out of
many
other examples considered in the paper).

The final result of this section shows that
(A2)
is equivalent to an ostensibly stronger condition.

Lemma 4.25
.

If
(U,S)
is an action pair, then for any
u,v,w\in U^{1}
and
s,t\in S
, we have

us=vt\ \Rightarrow\ u\cdot{}^{s}w=v\cdot{}^{t}w.

Proof.

Combining
us=vt
with
(A1)
, we have

u\cdot{}^{s}w\cdot s=us\cdot w=vt\cdot w=v\cdot{}^{t}w\cdot t.

It then follows from
(A2)
that
u\cdot{}^{s}w\cdot s^{+}=v\cdot{}^{t}w\cdot t^{+}
, and Lemma
3.1
(iv)
completes the proof.
∎

Before we move on, we note that there are obviously right-handed versions of our action pairs. By symmetry/duality, results concerning
right
pairs can be directly obtained from their left-handed counterparts, so we focus only on the left. The left-handed theory is our chosen focus, as it applies directly to all of our motivating examples.

There is also a notion of
right
-uniqueness (for
left
action pairs), where
{us=vt\ \Rightarrow\ s=t}
, in contrast to the left-uniqueness property
(SA2)
. Right-uniqueness is satisfied, for example, by free left restriction monoids, which are the topic of Chapter
7
. However, right-uniqueness is equivalent to certain right congruences on
S
(introduced in Section
4.4
) all being trivial, so there is no need to develop a parallel theory in this case.

4.2
Examples

The following collection of examples and non-examples highlight some subtleties in the definitions. They also show that the semigroups
US
arising from (weak/strong) action pairs
(U,S)
include some well known classes. Additional examples will be explored in greater depth in the next section, and in Part
II
of the paper.

Several of the examples discussed below use the transformation monoids
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
,
\mathcal{T}_{X}
,
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
, and so on, as defined in Section
2.2
.
Some involve inverse semigroups, as defined in Section
2.1
. For the calculations in such examples, we make use of the previously-mentioned fact that idempotents of an inverse semigroup commute. It is also clear that every idempotent of an inverse semigroup is its own inverse. On a number of occasions we make use of the easily checked fact that if
U
and
S
are submonoids of a monoid
M
, and if
SU\subseteq US
, then
US=\langle U\cup S\rangle
.

We begin with a somewhat degenerate construction:

Example 4.26
.

Let
U
and
S
be arbitrary semigroups, and assume
U\cap S=\varnothing
. Let
0
and
1
be symbols not belonging to
U\cup S
, and let
M=U\cup S\cup\{0,1\}
. Then
M
is a monoid under the product that extends the original products in
U
and
S
, and where additionally

us=su=0,\ \ \ x0=0x=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptx1=1x=x\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$, $s\in S$ and $x\in M$.}

(So
M
is the monoid completion of the
0-direct union
of
U
and
S
.) For any action of
S
on
U
by morphisms,
(U,S)
is a weak action pair in
M
with respect to the extended action of
S
on
U^{1}
. We then of course have
US=SU=\{0\}
.

The next series of examples involve idempotents and one- or two-sided units.

Example 4.27
.

Let
M
be an inverse monoid, and let

E=E(M)=\{{e\in M}:{e=e^{2}}\}\and G=G(M)=\{{g\in M}:{gg^{-1}=g^{-1}g=1}\}

be the semilattice of idempotents, and group of units of
M
. It is easy to see that
{(E,G)=(E^{1},G^{1})}
is a strong action pair in
M
:

(SA1)

For
g\in G
and
e\in E(=E^{1})
, we have
ge=(geg^{-1})g
, with
geg^{-1}\in E
. This shows that
gE\subseteq Eg
. In fact, it is easy to see that
gE=Eg
. (The action of
G
on
E(=E^{1})
from
(A1)
or
(SA1)′
is given by
{}^{g}e=geg^{-1}
.)

(SA2)

Suppose
eg=fh
for some
e,f\in E
and
g,h\in G
. Then from
e=fhg^{-1}
we obtain
e=fe
. Similarly,
f=ef
, and since idempotents commute, it follows that
e=f
.

(Alternatively, one could verify conditions
(A1)
and
(A2)
, and then apply Lemma
4.23
(ii)
.)
The resulting subsemigroup
EG=GE\leq M
is the largest
factorisable inverse submonoid
of
M
. See
[
14
,
42
]
for more on factorisable inverse monoids, and
[
24
]
for presentations. By the symmetry afforded by the inversion map,
(E,G)
is also a strong
right
action pair.

If we write
E^{\flat}=E\setminus\{1\}
, then
(E^{\flat},G)
is also a strong action pair in
M
(cf. Lemma
4.18
(ii)
). The subsemigroup
E^{\flat}G=GE^{\flat}=EG\setminus G
is an
almost-factorisable inverse semigroup
in the sense of Lawson
[
76
]
; see also
[
88
]
. Note that this class of inverse semigroups has an abstract definition, and it is a highly nontrivial result that any almost-factorisable inverse semigroup has the form
EG\setminus G
for suitable
E
and
G
(and
M
).

Generalisations of Example
4.27
will be discussed in Section
4.3
.

Example 4.28
.

Following on from the previous example, let
M
be an arbitrary monoid,
E
the set of idempotents of
M
, and
G
the group of units. Since
E
need not be a submonoid, we also write
U=\langle E\rangle
for the submonoid generated by the idempotents. Since the conjugate of an idempotent by a unit is an idempotent, we have an action of
G
on
U
given by
{}^{g}u=gug^{-1}
, with respect to which
g\cdot u={}^{g}u\cdot g
. Thus,
(A1)
holds, meaning that
(U,G)
is a weak action pair. Of course it follows that
(SA1)
holds as well. We have
UG=GU=\langle E\cup G\rangle
by
[
38
, Lemma 32]
.

Since
{}^{g}1=1
for all
g\in G
, conditions
(A2)
and
(SA2)
are equivalent for the pair
(U,G)
, and we note that these need not hold in general (although we have just seen in Example
4.27
that they do hold when
M
is inverse). For a specific example of this, let
M=\mathcal{T}_{2}=\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\}
, where

\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\hskip 20.00003pt\beta=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\
2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right),\hskip 20.00003pt\gamma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\
1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\and\delta=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\
2&1\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Then
U=E=\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\}
and
G=\{\gamma,\delta\}
, yet
\alpha\gamma=\beta\delta
.

Example 4.29
.

Again let
M
be an arbitrary monoid, and let
G
,
E
and
U=\langle E\rangle
be as in the previous example. This time let

L=L(M)=\{{s\in M}:{1\in Ms}\}\and R=R(M)=\{{s\in M}:{1\in sM}\}

be the submonoids of left and right units.

Let
s\in L
, so that
1=s^{\prime}s
for some
s^{\prime}\in M
. For any idempotent
e\in E
, we have
se=(ses^{\prime})s
, and it is easy to check that
ses^{\prime}
is an idempotent. This quickly leads to
sU\subseteq Us
, so that
(SA1)
holds for the pair
(U,L)
. We have
UL=\langle E\cup L\rangle
by
[
34
, Lemma 2.5]
.

Condition
(SA2)
still does not hold for
(U,L)
in general. Indeed, this is again witnessed by
M=\mathcal{T}_{2}
from Example
4.28
, since when
M
is finite every left unit is a (two-sided) unit so that
L=G
is the group of units. Even though
(U,L)
is not left-unique in general, we still always have
U\cap L=\{1\}
; see
[
34
, Lemma 2.1]
, and cf. Lemma
4.21
.

Regarding condition
(A1)
for the pair
(U,L)
, for each
s\in L
we fix some
s^{\prime}\in M
such that
1=s^{\prime}s
. Then for
s\in L
and
u\in U(=U^{1})
we define
{}^{s}u=sus^{\prime}\in U
, and we have
su={}^{s}u\cdot s
. We also have
{}^{s}(uv)={}^{s}u\cdot{}^{s}v
for all
s\in L
and
u,v\in U
. However, for
(A1)
to hold, we also require that
{}^{st}u={}^{s}({}^{t}u)
for all
s,t\in L
and
u\in U
: i.e.,
(st)u(st)^{\prime}=s(tut^{\prime})s^{\prime}
for all such
s,t,u
. This would be the case if we could choose the elements
s^{\prime}
in such a way that
(st)^{\prime}=t^{\prime}s^{\prime}
for all
s,t\in L
, as is the case for example when
M
is inverse (and
s^{\prime}=s^{-1}
).

The above discussion concentrated on the pair
(U,L)
. We note that the pair
(U,R)
might not even satisfy
(SA1)
. For a specific example of this, let
M=\mathcal{PB}_{\mathbb{N}}
be the partial Brauer monoid over
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
. The definition of this monoid is somewhat involved, so we refer the reader to
[
34
]
.
Consider the elements

\alpha={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{

}}\in\alpha U,

but we claim that
\alpha\eta\not\in U\alpha
, which then of course implies that
\alpha U\not\subseteq U\alpha
. In fact,
\alpha\eta
does not even belong to
\mathcal{PB}_{\mathbb{N}}\cdot\alpha
, as every element of
\mathcal{PB}_{\mathbb{N}}\cdot\alpha
contains at least one lower ‘hook’ (inherited from
\alpha
), while
\alpha\eta
does not.

For more on monoids generated by idempotents and one-sided units, see
[
34
,
35
]
.

Example
4.27
involved a (strong) action pair
(U,S)
for which
U
and
S
were both submonoids of
M
; in such cases, the monoid
US
contains both
U
and
S
. The next example, which is one of our original sources of motivation, provides a contrast.

Example 4.30
.

Let
n
be a positive integer, and write
{\bf n}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. In addition to the monoids
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, and so on, let
\mathcal{E}_{n}=\{{\mathrm{id}_{A}}:{A\subseteq{\bf n}}\}
be the semilattice of partial identities, and write
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})=\mathcal{E}_{n}\setminus\{\mathrm{id}_{\bf n}\}
. Then the following are all strong action pairs in
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
:

(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{T}_{n}),\hskip 20.00003pt(\mathcal{E}_{n},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})),\hskip 20.00003pt(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n}),\mathcal{T}_{n}),\hskip 20.00003pt(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n}),\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})).

Indeed, conditions
(SA1)
and
(SA2)
follow from the laws

\alpha\cdot\mathrm{id}_{A}=\mathrm{id}_{A\alpha^{-1}}\cdot\alpha\and\mathrm{id}_{A}\cdot\alpha=\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{A}.

(4.31)

One can check (though it is not entirely obvious, and in any case follows from results of later chapters; cf. Remark
8.64
) that the subsemigroups
US
of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
corresponding to the above pairs
(U,S)
are:

•

\mathcal{E}_{n}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n}=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
,

•

\mathcal{E}_{n}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
, and

•

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{n}
.

It is also worth noting that
\mathcal{E}_{n}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
contains
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
but not
\mathcal{E}_{n}
; it does however contain
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})=\mathcal{E}_{n}\setminus\{\mathrm{id}_{\bf n}\}
, a crucial property that features heavily in Chapter
6
. Presentations for the semigroups
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
,
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{n}
can be found in
[
29
,
31
,
30
,
32
]
.
Also, while
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{n}
contains
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})
, it is disjoint from both
\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
.

We also observe that if
(U,S)=(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n}),\mathcal{T}_{n})
or
(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n}),\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
, then the condition
\alpha U^{1}\subseteq U^{1}\alpha
in
(SA1)
cannot be replaced by
\alpha U\subseteq U\alpha
(cf. Remark
4.6
). Indeed, if
\alpha\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
is any map with image
A\subsetneq{\bf n}
, then
\alpha\cdot\mathrm{id}_{A}=\alpha\not=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\alpha
for any
B\subsetneq{\bf n}
.
For the same reason, the action of
S
on
U^{1}=\mathcal{E}_{n}
does not restrict to an action of
S
on
U=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})
, as
{}^{\alpha}\mathrm{id}_{A}=\mathrm{id}_{\bf n}
when
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)=A\subsetneq{\bf n}
.

Similar considerations show that none of the above pairs satisfy the right-handed version
U^{1}\alpha\subseteq\alpha U^{1}
of
(SA1)
when
n\geq 2
. Indeed, let
\alpha\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
be an arbitrary constant map, and fix some
\varnothing\subsetneq A\subsetneq{\bf n}
. Then for any
B\subseteq{\bf n}
,
\alpha\cdot\mathrm{id}_{B}
is equal to either
\alpha
or else the empty map, and so is never equal to
\mathrm{id}_{A}\cdot\alpha
. This all shows that
\mathrm{id}_{A}\cdot\alpha\in U\alpha\setminus\alpha U^{1}
, and so
U^{1}\alpha\not\subseteq\alpha U^{1}
.

We also have strong action pairs
(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{G}_{n})
and
(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n}),\mathcal{G}_{n})
in
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
. These lead to the (almost-)factorisable inverse semigroups

\mathcal{E}_{n}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{n}=\mathcal{I}_{n}\and\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})\cdot\mathcal{G}_{n}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n}),

which are special cases of Example
4.27
.

Generalisations of Example
4.30
will be discussed in Chapters
8
and
9
.

Example 4.32
.

Extending the last example, consider again the full and partial transformation semigroups
\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
over an arbitrary set
X
. Consider also the power set
{{\mathcal{P}}(X)=\{{A}:{A\subseteq X}\}}
, as a semilattice under intersection. Note that
\mathcal{T}_{X}
acts on
{\mathcal{P}}(X)
via

{}^{\alpha}\!A=A\alpha^{-1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $A\in{\mathcal{P}}(X)$ and $\alpha\in\mathcal{T}_{X}$.}

Now let
S
be a subsemigroup of
\mathcal{T}_{X}
, let
F
be a subsemilattice of
{\mathcal{P}}(X)
such that
F^{1}=F\cup\{X\}
is closed under the action of
S
, and set
U=\{{\mathrm{id}_{A}}:{A\in F}\}
.
Then
(U,S)
is a strong action pair in
M=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, as again follows from (
4.31
). So we have the subsemigroup

US=\{{\mathrm{id}_{A}\cdot\alpha}:{\alpha\in S,\ A\in F}\}=\{{\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{A}}:{\alpha\in S,\ A\in F}\}\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X},

with composition given by

\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{A}\cdot\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=(\alpha\beta)\mathord{\restriction}_{A\cap B\alpha^{-1}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\alpha,\beta\in S$ and $A,B\in F$.}

When
S=\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
F={\mathcal{P}}(X)
, we of course obtain
US=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, but varying
S
and
F
gives rise to other interesting cases.

(i)

Consider the case in which
X=\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
. Define transformations
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{N}}
by

\gamma_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&2&3&\cdots\\
1&2&3&4&\cdots\end{smallmatrix}\right)\and\gamma_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&2&3&\cdots\\
0&2&3&4&\cdots\end{smallmatrix}\right),

and define the (infinite cyclic) subsemigroups
S_{1}=\langle\gamma_{1}\rangle
and
S_{2}=\langle\gamma_{2}\rangle
of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
F
be the set of all subsets of
\mathbb{N}
of the form
x+\mathbb{N}=\{x,x+1,x+2,\ldots\}
with
x\geq 1
. So
F
is a subsemilattice of
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{N})
, and
F\cup\{\mathbb{N}\}
is closed under the actions of both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
(
F
is closed under the action of
S_{2}
, but not of
S_{1}
). So
(U,S_{1})
and
(U,S_{2})
are both strong action pairs in
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{N}}
, and in fact we have
US_{1}=US_{2}
. This shows that the ‘
S
component’ of a (strong) action pair
(U,S)
is not uniquely determined by the semigroup
US
itself. Note also that
US_{1}=US_{2}
contains no total maps, and also no idempotents. So in general, a semigroup
US
can be disjoint from both
U
and
S
.
Clearly
US_{1}=US_{2}
contains no isomorphic copy of
U
(as
U
consists entirely of idempotents), but any element of
US_{1}=US_{2}
generates an infinite cyclic subsemigroup, which is isomorphic to both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.

(ii)

Now let
X={\mathbb{Z}}=\{0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots\}
, and define

\gamma_{3}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\cdots&0&1&2&3&\cdots\\
\cdots&1&2&3&4&\cdots\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{Z}}.

Let
S_{3}=\langle\gamma_{3}\rangle
, and let
F
be as in
(i)
, but now considered as a subsemilattice of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{Z}})
. Note that
F\cup\{{\mathbb{Z}}\}
is not closed under the action of
S_{3}
, and in fact
(U,S_{3})
is not a weak action pair at all, as even
(SA1)
fails. For example, with
\mathbb{P}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}\in F
and
\mathbb{N}={}^{\gamma_{3}}\mathbb{P}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}\not\in F\cup\{{\mathbb{Z}}\}
, we have
\gamma_{3}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{P}}\in\gamma_{3}U^{1}
; on the other hand,
\operatorname{dom}(\gamma_{3}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{P}})=\mathbb{N}
, yet every element
\delta
of
U^{1}\gamma_{3}
has
\operatorname{dom}(\delta)={\mathbb{Z}}
or else
\operatorname{dom}(\delta)\subseteq\mathbb{P}
. However, we do have

A\cap B\alpha^{-1}\in F\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $A,B\in F$ and $\alpha\in S_{3}$,}

and it follows from this and the rule
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{A}\cdot\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=(\alpha\beta)\mathord{\restriction}_{A\cap B\alpha^{-1}}
that
US_{3}
is still a semigroup. In fact, by identifying
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{N}}
as a subset of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{Z}}
(consisting of all partial maps with domain and range contained in
\mathbb{N}
), we have
{US_{1}=US_{2}=US_{3}}
, where
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are as in
(i)
above. This time, note that
S_{3}
consists of units of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(iii)

Again consider
X=\mathbb{N}
, and let
S=\langle\gamma_{1}\rangle
, with
\gamma_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{N}}
as in
(i)
. This time we let
F
consist of all finite subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, which is again a subsemilattice of
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{N})
closed under the action of
S
. The resulting strong action pair
(U,S)
leads to the subsemigroup
US
of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{N}}
, as usual. This time
US
contains a single idempotent, namely the empty map
\varnothing
(which is of course a zero of
US
), and we note that
US
is a
nilsemigroup
, in the sense that every element of
US
has a power equal to
\varnothing
. It follows that
US
does not contain any subsemigroup isomorphic to
U
or to
S
.

Among other things, the above examples show that if one is given a semigroup known to be of the form
US
for some (strong) action pair
(U,S)
, it is not necessarily obvious what
U
and/or
S
might be.

It is also worth noting that the
reflection monoids
of Everitt and Fountain
[
39
,
40
]
are also special cases of the construction in Example
4.32
, or indeed of Example
4.27
.

The next example features a
weak
action pair
(U,S)
in partial transformation monoids, and here we no longer assume that
U
consists of partial identities.

Example 4.33
.

Let
Y
and
Z
be disjoint non-empty sets, let
X=Y\cup Z
, and let
M=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
. Let
S\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{Y}
and
V\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{Z}
be arbitrary subsemigroups. We may also regard
S
as a subsemigroup of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
(as any partial transformation of
Y
is of course a partial transformation of
X
), and we additionally define
U=\{{\mathrm{id}_{Y}\cup\alpha}:{\alpha\in V}\}
. So
U
is a subsemigroup of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, and clearly
U\cong V
. It is easy to check that

\alpha\beta=\beta\alpha=\alpha\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\alpha\in S$ and $\beta\in U^{1}=U\cup\{\mathrm{id}_{X}\}$.}

(4.34)

It immediately follows that condition
(SA1)
holds for the pair
(U,S)
. However, condition
(SA2)
only holds if
|U|=1
, as
\alpha\beta=\alpha^{\prime}\beta
for all
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in U
and
\beta\in S
. By contrast, the right-handed version of
(SA2)
holds:
\alpha\beta=\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\ \Rightarrow\ \beta=\beta^{\prime}
for all
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in U
and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in S
.

It also follows from (
4.34
) that condition
(A1)
holds with respect to the constant action:
{}^{\alpha}\beta=\mathrm{id}_{X}
for all
\alpha\in S
and
\beta\in U^{1}
. Condition
(A2)
is equivalent to
(SA2)
, so still fails (unless
|U|=1
). So the weak action pair
(U,S)
is not an action pair. In this case we have
US=S(=SU)
.

In Example
4.27
we considered factorisable inverse monoids, which came from strong action pairs present in any inverse monoid. As a foreshadowing of the next section, we now consider another action pair present in any inverse semigroup, though these are generally not strong.

Example 4.35
.

Let
S
be an inverse semigroup with semilattice of idempotents
E=E(S)
. Define the map
S\to E^{1}:s\mapsto s^{+}=ss^{-1}
. It is easy to check that
(SA1)
holds for the pair
(E,S)
, and that the unary operation
s\mapsto s^{+}
satisfies
(

+

1)
–
(

+

4)
. For example, if
s,t\in S
, then

st^{+}=stt^{-1}=ss^{-1}stt^{-1}=stt^{-1}s^{-1}s=(st)(st)^{-1}s=(st)^{+}s,

giving
(

+

2)
. For
(

+

4)
, suppose
es=ft
for some
e,f\in E
and
s,t\in S
. Then certainly
(es)^{+}=(ft)^{+}
. But

(es)^{+}=(es)(es)^{-1}=ess^{-1}e^{-1}=ess^{-1}e=eess^{-1}=es^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt(ft)^{+}=ft^{+},

so that
es^{+}=ft^{+}
, as required. It follows from Proposition
4.7
that
(E,S)
is an action pair in the monoid
S^{1}
. By Lemma
4.15
(ii)
, this pair is strong if and only if
ss^{-1}=1
for all
s\in S
, which occurs precisely when
S
is a group.

To understand the pair
(E,S)
via the original Definition
4.2
, we need to understand the action of
S
on
E
. For this, let
s\in S
and
e\in E
. Then
se=ss^{-1}se=ses^{-1}s=ses^{-1}\cdot s
. It follows from the proof of Proposition
4.7
(see (
4.8
)) that
{}^{s}e=ses^{-1}\cdot s^{+}=ses^{-1}\cdot ss^{-1}=ses^{-1}
. This all shows that the action of
S
on
E
is given by conjugation, as in Example
3.3
(ii)
.

As usual, the pair
(E,S)
leads to the subsemigroup
ES
of
S
, and of course we have
ES=S
. Although this is not an interesting/new subsemigroup, it leads to a number of other interesting subsemigroups, as
(E,T)
is an action pair for any
T\leq S
, by Lemma
4.18
(i)
. We will explore this in more detail in the next section, where we extend our scope to left restriction monoids.

4.3
Left restriction monoids

As just noted, another family of natural examples comes from the class of left restriction monoids, as defined in Section
2.3
. For the duration of this section we fix some such monoid
M
with identity
1
. So
M
has a (basic) unary operation
s\mapsto s^{+}
satisfying

(L1)

x^{+}x=x
,

(L2)

x^{+}y^{+}=y^{+}x^{+}
,

(L3)

(x^{+}y)^{+}=x^{+}y^{+}
,

(L4)

xy^{+}=(xy)^{+}x
,

(L5)

x^{+}x^{+}=x^{+}
,

(L6)

(x^{+})^{+}=x^{+}
.

(Recall that the defining identities are
(L1)
–
(L4)
, while
(L5)
and
(L6)
are consequences.)
It follows quickly from
(L1)
that
1^{+}=1
. As before, in the calculations to follow we write
=_{1}
to indicate an application of
(L1)
, and similarly for
=_{2}
, and so on.

The following subsets of
M
will play an important role in all that follows:

\displaystyle E=E(M)

\displaystyle=\{{s\in M}:{s=s^{2}}\},

\displaystyle L=L(M)

\displaystyle=\{{s\in M}:{1\in Ms}\},

\displaystyle P=P(M)

\displaystyle=\{{s^{+}}:{s\in M}\},

\displaystyle R=R(M)

\displaystyle=\{{s\in M}:{1\in sM}\},

\displaystyle T=T(M)

\displaystyle=\{{s\in M}:{s^{+}=1}\},

\displaystyle G=G(M)

\displaystyle=L\cap R.

The elements of
P
are called
projections
. By
(L2)
,
(L3)
and
(L5)
,
P
is a (monoid) semilattice. By
(L6)
,
P
is fixed pointwise by the
+
operation, meaning that
u=u^{+}
for all
u\in P
. If
M
is inverse (with
s^{+}=ss^{-1}
), then
P=E
is precisely the semilattice of (all) idempotents of
M
. However, this need not be the case for an arbitrary left restriction semigroup, as for example with
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
(with
\alpha^{+}=\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}
and
P=\{{\mathrm{id}_{A}}:{A\subseteq X}\}
), although we always of course have
{P\subseteq E}
. In fact,
E
need not be a subsemigroup at all, let alone a semilattice; again consider
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
. The other subsets defined above
are
submonoids, however. The submonoids
L
and
R
consist of all left and right units of
M
, respectively, and
G
is the group of (two-sided) units. We have
L=R=G
if
M
is finite (see for example
[
34
, Lemma 2.3]
), but this need not hold for infinite
M
. We will discuss
T
shortly, but our first goal is to show that
(P,M)
is an action pair; see Proposition
4.37
. For this, and for later use, it is convenient to prove a simple fact, which we have already seen in Example
4.35
for the special case of inverse semigroups.

Lemma 4.36
.

If
S
is a left restriction semigroup with semilattice of projections
P=P(S)
, then
(us)^{+}=us^{+}
for any
u\in P^{1}
and
s\in S
.

Proof.

This is obvious for
u=1
; otherwise,
(us)^{+}=_{6}(u^{+}s)^{+}=_{3}u^{+}s^{+}=_{6}us^{+}
.
∎

Proposition 4.37
.

Let
M
be a left restriction monoid, and write
P=P(M)
. Then
(P,M)
is an action pair.

Proof.

We use Proposition
4.7
. For
(SA1)
, let
s\in M
. Then for any
u\in P
we have

su=_{6}su^{+}=_{4}(su)^{+}s,

(4.38)

which gives
sP\subseteq Ps
.

It remains to check that the map
M\to P:s\mapsto s^{+}
(i.e., the
+
operation itself) satisfies conditions
(

+

1)
–
(

+

4)
. Now, conditions
(

+

1)
and
(

+

2)
are simply
(L1)
and
(L4)
, respectively. For
(

+

3)
, let
s,t\in M
. Then

(st)^{+}=_{1}((s^{+}s)t)^{+}=(s^{+}(st))^{+}=_{3}s^{+}(st)^{+}=_{2}(st)^{+}s^{+}.

Finally, for
(

+

4)
, suppose
u,v\in P
and
s,t\in M
are such that
us=vt
. Then certainly
(us)^{+}=(vt)^{+}
, and it follows from Lemma
4.36
that
us^{+}=vt^{+}
.
∎

Remark 4.39
.

If
S
is a left restriction
semigroup
, then
(P,S)
is an action pair in the monoid
S^{1}
, where again we write
P=P(S)=\{{s^{+}}:{s\in S}\}
. Indeed, this follows from the same proof as above.

Alternatively, when
S
is a left restriction semigroup,
S^{1}
becomes a left restriction
monoid
in a unique way by putting
1^{+}=1
. Proposition
4.37
says that
(P^{1},S^{1})
is an action pair in
S^{1}
, and we then apply (both parts of) Lemma
4.18
.

Remark 4.40
.

Given any action pair
(U,S)
we have the associated semigroup
US
. In the case of the pair
(P,M)
, this subsemigroup is precisely
PM=M
itself. Thus, the pair
(P,M)
does not lead to an ‘interesting’ subsemigroup of
M
. Similarly, the results of Chapter
6
will be of no use in describing presentations for
M
by means of the pair
(P,M)
, since they assume we already know a presentation for
M
(and
P
). Nevertheless, knowing that
(P,M)
is an action pair is useful in describing many more such pairs below (using Lemma
4.18
). The pair
(P,M)
will also be extremely important in Chapter
5
.

The proof of Proposition
4.37
utilised the alternative formulation of action pairs from Proposition
4.7
, but it is convenient to understand the associated action of
M
on
P(=P^{1})
. To do so, fix some
u\in P
and
s\in M
. Then
su=(su)^{+}s
, by (
4.38
). By the proof of Proposition
4.7
(see (
4.8
)), we therefore have
{}^{s}u=(su)^{+}s^{+}=(su)^{+}
, where we used
(

+

3)
in the last step. To summarise, the action of
M
on
P
is given by

{}^{s}u=(su)^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $s\in M$ and $u\in P$.}

(4.41)

If
M
is inverse (with
s^{+}=ss^{-1}
), then this action has the simple form
{{}^{s}u=su(su)^{-1}=sus^{-1}}
; cf. Examples
3.3
(ii)
and
4.35
. It is worth noting that the action (
4.41
) goes back to work of Fountain on the narrower class of
adequate semigroups

[
44
]
; see also
[
53
]
where it is used in the broader class of
Ehresmann semigroups
.

Next we wish to prove a result concerning action pairs of the form
(Q,S)
, where
Q\leq P
and
S\leq M
. The set
T
introduced above will play an important role in this, specifically in determining which of these pairs are strong.

Lemma 4.42
.

Let
M
be a left restriction monoid, and write
T=T(M)
and
R=R(M)
. Then

(i)

T
is a submonoid of
M
,

(ii)

R\subseteq T
, with equality if
M
is inverse,

(iii)

M\setminus T=\{{s\in M}:{s^{+}\not=1}\}
is a right ideal (and hence a subsemigroup) of
M
.

Proof.

(i)
. Define the relation
\sigma=\big\{{(s,t)\in M\times M}:{s^{+}=t^{+}}\big\}
. Clearly
\sigma
is an equivalence (it is the kernel of the map
s\mapsto s^{+}
), and it follows quickly from Lemma
3.1
(ii)
that
\sigma
is a left congruence. Lemma
2.1
then tells us that
T=\{{s\in M}:{s\mathrel{\sigma}1}\}
is a submonoid.

(ii)
. Lemma
4.23
(i)
gives
R\subseteq T
. If
M
is inverse, and if
s\in T
, then
1=s^{+}=ss^{-1}
, so
s\in R
.

(iii)
. Let
s\in M\setminus T
and
t\in M
. So
s^{+}\not=1
, and we must show that
(st)^{+}\not=1
. But if
(st)^{+}=1
, then
s^{+}=s^{+}\cdot 1=s^{+}(st)^{+}=_{3}(s^{+}\cdot st)^{+}=_{1}(st)^{+}=1
, a contradiction.
∎

Remark 4.43
.

The equality
T=R
need not hold if
M
is not inverse. For example, in
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
we have

T(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})=\mathcal{T}_{X}\and R(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{T}_{X}}:{\alpha\text{ is injective}}\}.

The same example also shows that the right ideal
M\setminus T
(which is
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{X}
in this case) is not always a left ideal.

The next result follows immediately from Proposition
4.37
, and Lemmas
4.15
and
4.18
.

Proposition 4.44
.

Let
M
be a left restriction monoid, and write
P=P(M)
and
T=T(M)
. Then

(i)

(Q,S)
is an action pair for any
Q\leq P
and
S\leq M
such that
Q^{1}
is closed under the action of
S
given in (
4.41
),

(ii)

such a pair
(Q,S)
is strong if and only if
S\subseteq T
. ∎

Remark 4.45
.

In particular,
(P,S)
and
(P^{\flat},S)
are both action pairs for any
S\leq M
, where we write
P^{\flat}=P\setminus\{1\}\leq P
. Such pairs are strong if and only if
S\subseteq T
. Thus, the following action pairs are all strong:

(P,T),\hskip 20.00003pt(P,R),\hskip 20.00003pt(P,G),\hskip 20.00003pt(P^{\flat},T),\hskip 20.00003pt(P^{\flat},R)\and(P^{\flat},G).

As usual, these lead to the subsemigroups
PT
,
PR
and so on.
We first note that
PG(=GP)
is in fact a factorisable inverse monoid. Indeed, this follows from the easily checked facts that:

•

E(PG)=P
,

•

g^{-1}u={}^{g^{-1}}ug^{-1}\in PG
is an inverse of
ug
for any
u\in P
and
g\in G
.

The inverse semigroup
P^{\flat}G(=GP^{\flat})=PG\setminus G
is
almost-factorisable
.
The submonoid
PT
and the subsemigroup
P^{\flat}T
are
left-factorisable
and
almost-left-factorisable
in the terminology of
[
114
]
. Note that
PG=GP
has a left-right duality, where the corresponding right action is given by
{e^{g}=g^{-1}eg}
, making it a restriction monoid; similar comments apply to the semigroup
{P^{\flat}G=GP^{\flat}}
.

(As we will see in Chapter
6
, the very fact that
P^{\flat}=P\setminus\{1\}
is a subsemigroup of
P
is important in certain applications; see for example Assumption
6.33
and Remark
6.34
.)

The action pair
(P,L)
is strong precisely when
L\subseteq T
. When
M
is inverse, this is equivalent to
L\subseteq R
(cf. Lemma
4.42
(ii)
), and hence to
L=R=G
.

Finally, the action pair
(P,M)
itself is strong if and only if
M=T
, meaning that the left restriction structure of
M
is trivial (
s^{+}=1
for all
s\in M
). When
M
is inverse, this is equivalent to
M
being a group (
ss^{-1}=1
for all
s\in M
), as we observed in Example
4.35
. For the action pair
(P^{\flat},M)
, one can check that

P^{\flat}M=M\setminus T=\{{s\in M}:{s^{+}\not=1}\}.

We showed in Lemma
4.42
(iii)
that this subsemigroup is in fact a right ideal of
M
.

4.4
The first structure theorem

Having now discussed an extensive collection of examples, including the class of left restriction semigroups, we return to the general theory. The main goal of this section is to describe the structure of a semigroup
US
arising from an action pair
(U,S)
, showing how such a semigroup can be built from a semidirect product
U\rtimes S
and a special congruence. We do this in Theorem
4.53
below, but we begin with a simple result that holds more generally for a
weak
action pair
(U,S)
. For such a pair, the action from
(A1)
allows for the formation of the (external) semidirect product
U\rtimes S
, as in Definition
3.4
.

Proposition 4.46
.

If
(U,S)
is a weak action pair, then the map
\pi:U\rtimes S\to US
given by
(u,s)\pi=us
is a surmorphism with kernel

\theta=\big\{{((u,s),(v,t))}:{u,v\in U,\ s,t\in S,\ us=vt}\big\}.

Consequently,
\theta
is a congruence on
U\rtimes S
, and
US\cong(U\rtimes S)/\theta
.

Proof.

If
u,v\in U
and
s,t\in S
, then

(u,s)\pi\cdot(v,t)\pi=us\cdot vt=u\cdot{}^{s}v\cdot st=(u\cdot{}^{s}v,st)\pi=((u,s)\cdot(v,t))\pi,

so that
\pi
is a morphism. Surjectivity is clear, as is the description of the kernel. The final assertion follows from the First Isomorphism Theorem.
∎

Remark 4.47
.

Recall from Proposition
3.32
that

M=\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}

is a subsemigroup of
U\rtimes S
, and that the map

F:U\rtimes S\to M:(u,s)\mapsto(us^{+},s)

is a retraction. (Note that to apply Proposition
3.32
we need to keep Remark
4.4
in mind, which says that if
S
is a monoid then it acts monoidally on
U^{1}
.) Because of the identity
s=s^{+}s
(cf. Remark
4.4
), the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\rtimes S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces US}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\mathord{\restriction}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(4.48)

In particular,
\pi\mathord{\restriction}_{M}:M\to US
is still a surmorphism, so we also have

US\cong M/\vartheta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\vartheta=\ker(\pi\mathord{\restriction}_{M})=\theta\mathord{\restriction}_{M}.

Now consider an action pair
(U,S)
. Of course Proposition
4.46
holds for this pair, and we keep the meaning of the surmorphism
\pi:U\rtimes S\to US
, and the congruence
\theta=\ker(\pi)
. For any
u\in U^{1}
, we define the relation

\theta_{u}=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{us=ut}\big\}.

(4.49)

We also continue to write
s^{+}={}^{s}1
for
s\in S
.

Lemma 4.50
.

Let
(U,S)
be an action pair, and let
u,v\in U
and
s,t\in S
.

(i)

We have
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}(v,t)\ \Leftrightarrow\ us^{+}=vt^{+}
and
(s,t)\in\theta_{us^{+}}
.

(ii)

If
(U,S)
is strong, then
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}(v,t)\ \Leftrightarrow\ u=v
and
(s,t)\in\theta_{u}
.

Proof.

The second part follows from the first, given Lemma
4.15
(ii)
. For the first part, suppose
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}(v,t)
: i.e.,
us=vt
. Then
us^{+}=vt^{+}
follows from
(

+

4)
. Together with
(

+

1)
, it follows that
us^{+}s=us=vt=vt^{+}t=us^{+}t
, so
(s,t)\in\theta_{us^{+}}
. The converse is similar.
∎

Proposition
4.46
showed that, given an action pair
(U,S)
, the semigroup
US
can be viewed abstractly as a homomorphic image of a semidirect product
U\rtimes S
. Our next goal is to establish a converse of this fact; namely, we wish to describe
all
homomorphic images of semidirect products that correspond to action pairs in this way. To do so, we first establish some special properties of the above congruence
\theta
, and the associated relations
\theta_{u}
.
Here and elsewhere, if
\sigma
is a relation on a semigroup
T
, and if
x\in T
, we write
x\cdot\sigma=\big\{{(xs,xt)}:{(s,t)\in\sigma}\big\}
. The diagonal/equality relation on any set
X
is denoted
\Delta_{X}=\big\{{(x,x)}:{x\in X}\big\}
.

Lemma 4.51
.

For an action pair
(U,S)
, and with the above notation, the following all hold:

(i)

For all
u\in U
and
s\in S
, we have
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}(us^{+},s)
.

(ii)

For all
s,t\in S
, we have
(s^{+},s)\mathrel{\theta}(t^{+},t)\ \Rightarrow\ s=t
.

(iii)

For all
u,v\in U
and
s,t\in S
, we have
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}(v,t)\ \Rightarrow\ us^{+}=vt^{+}
.

(iv)

We have
\theta_{1}=\Delta_{S}
.

(v)

For all
u\in U
, the relation
\theta_{u}
is a right congruence on
S
.

(vi)

For all
u,w\in U
, we have
\theta_{u}\subseteq\theta_{wu}
.

(vii)

For all
u\in U
and
x\in S
, we have
x\cdot\theta_{u}\subseteq\theta_{{}^{x}u}
.

(viii)

For all
u,w\in U
and
(s,t)\in\theta_{u}
, we have
u\cdot{}^{s}w=u\cdot{}^{t}w
and
(s,t)\in\theta_{u\cdot{}^{s}w}
.

Proof.

These are easily checked, using the definition of
\theta
and
\theta_{u}
. We additionally use
(

+

1)
for
(i)
and
(ii)
, and Lemma
4.50
for
(iii)
. We just give the details for
(vii)
and
(viii)
.

(vii)
. We have

(s,t)\in\theta_{u}\ \Rightarrow\ us=ut\ \Rightarrow\ xus=xut\ \Rightarrow\ {}^{x}u\cdot xs={}^{x}u\cdot xt\ \Rightarrow\ (xs,xt)\in\theta_{{}^{x}u}.

(viii)
. We begin by noting that

(s,t)\in\theta_{u}\ \Rightarrow\ us=ut\ \Rightarrow\ u\cdot{}^{s}w=u\cdot{}^{t}w,

by Lemma
4.25
. Combining this with
(A1)
and
us=ut
, it also follows that

u\cdot{}^{s}w\cdot s=usw=utw=u\cdot{}^{t}w\cdot t=u\cdot{}^{s}w\cdot t,

so that
(s,t)\in\theta_{u\cdot{}^{s}w}
.
∎

We now use these properties of
\theta
(and the
\theta_{u}
) to construct a set of axioms to abstractly characterise the quotients of semidirect products giving rise to action pairs. To keep the ‘concrete’ pairs
(U,S)
separate from the abstract, we will use the notation
V
and
T
for the latter.

Definition 4.52
.

Suppose
V
and
T
are semigroups such that
T
has a left action on
V^{1}
(the monoid completion of
V
) by semigroup morphisms, written
(s,u)\mapsto{}^{s}u
, for
s\in T
and
u\in V^{1}
. For
s\in T
, we write
s^{+}={}^{s}1\in V^{1}
. Suppose
\sigma
is a congruence on the semidirect product
V\rtimes T
, and for each
u\in V
define the relation

\sigma_{u}=\big\{{(s,t)\in T\times T}:{(u,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(u,t)}\big\}.

If
V
is not a monoid (so
1\not\in V
), we additionally define
\sigma_{1}=\Delta_{T}
.
We say
\sigma
is
special
if the following all hold:

(S1)

For all
u\in V
and
s\in T
, we have
(u,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(us^{+},s)
.

(S2)

For all
s,t\in T
, we have
(s^{+},s)\mathrel{\sigma}(t^{+},t)\ \Rightarrow\ s=t
.

(S3)

For all
u,v\in V
and
s,t\in T
, we have
(u,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(v,t)\ \Rightarrow\ us^{+}=vt^{+}
.

(S4)

We have
\sigma_{1}=\Delta_{T}
.

(S5)

For all
u\in V
,
\sigma_{u}
is a right congruence on
T
.

(S6)

For all
u,w\in V
, we have
\sigma_{u}\subseteq\sigma_{wu}
.

(S7)

For all
u\in V
and
x\in T
, we have
x\cdot\sigma_{u}\subseteq\sigma_{{}^{x}u}
.

(S8)

For all
u,w\in V
and
(s,t)\in\sigma_{u}
, we have
u\cdot{}^{s}w=u\cdot{}^{t}w
and
(s,t)\in\sigma_{u\cdot{}^{s}w}
.

If
T
acts by monoid morphisms (i.e., if
s^{+}=1
for all
s\in T
), then several simplifications arise in
(S1)
–
(S8)
. For example,
(S1)
is trivially true, and
(S3)
says
(u,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(v,t)\ \Rightarrow\ u=v
.

Here is the main structural result of this chapter.

Theorem 4.53
.

Suppose
V
and
T
are semigroups such that
T
has a left action on
V^{1}
by semigroup morphisms. Suppose also that
\sigma
is a special congruence on the semidirect product
V\rtimes T
. Then there is an action pair
(U,S)
in some monoid
M
such that

U\cong V,\hskip 20.00003ptS\cong T\and US\cong(V\rtimes T)/\sigma.

If
T
acts on
V^{1}
by monoid morphisms, then
(U,S)
is strong.

Conversely, given any action pair
(U,S)
, we have
US\cong(U\rtimes S)/\theta
for some special congruence
\theta
on the semidirect product
U\rtimes S
.

Proof.

The last assertion follows from Proposition
4.46
and Lemma
4.51
. The remainder of the proof concerns the first two assertions, for which we fix
V
,
T
and
\sigma
with the specified properties.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
T
and
V^{1}
are disjoint. With a slight abuse of notation, we let
T^{1}=T\cup\{1\}
be the monoid obtained from
T
by adjoining
1
(the identity of
V^{1}
) as an identity,
whether or not
T
was already a monoid
. The reason for doing this is that
T
might be a monoid acting non-monoidally on
V
, and we will soon construct an action of
T^{1}
on
V^{1}
that is required to be monoidal. (But we stress that
V=V^{1}
if
V
is a monoid.)

We denote the action of
T
on
V^{1}
by
(s,u)\mapsto{}^{s}u
(
s\in T
,
u\in V^{1}
), and we extend this to a monoidal action of
T^{1}
on
V^{1}
in the usual way, by further defining
{}^{1}u=u
for all
u\in V^{1}
.
We then have the semidirect product
V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
, which contains
V\rtimes T
as a proper subsemigroup. (Note that
V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
is not necessarily a monoid, although
(1,1)
is always a left identity, as the action of
T^{1}
on
V^{1}
is monoidal; cf. Lemma
3.11
.) Throughout the proof, we write
s^{+}={}^{s}1
for
s\in T^{1}
; we will make frequent use of the properties of these elements listed in Lemma
3.1
, often without explicit reference. Since the action is monoidal, we have
1^{+}=1
.

The main effort in the proof goes into establishing the following:

Lemma 4.54
.

There is a congruence
\Sigma
on
V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
such that the following all hold:

(
\Sigma
1)

\sigma=\Sigma\mathord{\restriction}_{V\rtimes T}
,

(
\Sigma
2)

For all
u,v\in V^{1}
, we have
(u,1)\mathrel{\Sigma}(v,1)\ \Leftrightarrow\ u=v
.

(
\Sigma
3)

For all
u\in V^{1}
and
s\in T^{1}
, we have
(u,s)\mathrel{\Sigma}(us^{+},s)
.

(
\Sigma
4)

For all
u,v\in V^{1}
and
s,t\in T^{1}
, we have
(u,s)\mathrel{\Sigma}(v,t)\ \Rightarrow\ us^{+}=vt^{+}
.

Proof.

We define
\Sigma
by specifying its equivalence classes. Each
\sigma
-class in
V\rtimes T
will determine a unique
\Sigma
-class, so consider some such
\sigma
-class
K
. By
(S2)
,
K
contains at most one element of the form
(s^{+},s)
with
s\in T
.

(K1)

If
K
does contain such an element
(s^{+},s)
, then
K\cup\big\{(1,s)\big\}
is a
\Sigma
-class. (Note that this is just
K
itself if
s^{+}=1\in V
. If
s^{+}=1\not\in V
, then no
\sigma
-class contains
(s^{+},s)
.)

(K2)

Otherwise,
K
is a
\Sigma
-class.

All elements of
V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
not yet assigned to
\Sigma
-classes are now assigned to singleton
\Sigma
-classes. These elements are precisely:

•

(u,1)
, for each
u\in V^{1}
, and

•

(1,s)
, for each
s\in T
for which
s^{+}=1\not\in V
(if any). (Note that such an element is still of the form
(1,s)=(s^{+},s)
.)

It is clear from the construction that
\Sigma
is an equivalence on
V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
, and that
(

Σ

1)
and
(

Σ

2)
both hold. So it remains to show that
\Sigma
is compatible, and establish
(

Σ

3)
and
(

Σ

4)
. We begin with these two conditions.

(

Σ

3)
. This follows from
(S1)
when
u\in V
and
s\in T
, and is trivial when
s=1
. When
u=1
,
(

Σ

3)
says
(1,s)\mathrel{\Sigma}(s^{+},s)
; this is clear if
s^{+}=1
, and is true by definition of
\Sigma
otherwise; cf.
(K1)
.

(

Σ

4)
. Suppose
(u,s)\mathrel{\Sigma}(v,t)
, where
u,v\in V^{1}
and
s,t\in T^{1}
. We must show that
us^{+}=vt^{+}
. This is obvious if
(u,s)=(v,t)
, or follows from
(S3)
if
(u,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(v,t)
, so we assume otherwise. By construction (and by symmetry), it follows that
(u,s)=(1,s)
and
(v,t)
belongs to the
\sigma
-class of
(s^{+},s)
, with
s^{+}\in V
; cf.
(K1)
. But then
(v,t)\mathrel{\sigma}(s^{+},s)
, and it follows from
(S3)
and Lemma
3.1
(i)
that

vt^{+}=s^{+}s^{+}=s^{+}=1\cdot s^{+}=us^{+},

as required.

As noted above, it remains (for the proof of the lemma) to establish the compatibility of
\Sigma
. To do so, let
({\bf a},{\bf b})\in\Sigma
and
{\bf c}\in V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
. We must show that

{\bf c}\cdot({\bf a},{\bf b})=({\bf c}{\bf a},{\bf c}{\bf b})\in\Sigma\and({\bf a},{\bf b})\cdot{\bf c}=({\bf a}{\bf c},{\bf b}{\bf c})\in\Sigma.

(4.55)

Clearly (
4.55
) holds if
{\bf a}={\bf b}
, so we now assume that
{\bf a}\not={\bf b}
. Since
{\bf a}\mathrel{\Sigma}{\bf b}
, it follows that
{\bf a}
and
{\bf b}
belong to a
\Sigma
-class of type
(K1)
or
(K2)
.
By the form of these classes, we may write

{\bf a}=(u,s),\ \ \ {\bf b}=(v,t)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt{\bf c}=(w,x),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where $u,v,w\in V^{1}$, $s,t\in T$ and $x\in T^{1}$,}

and we have

{\bf c}\cdot({\bf a},{\bf b})=((w\cdot{}^{x}u,xs),(w\cdot{}^{x}v,xt))\and({\bf a},{\bf b})\cdot{\bf c}=((u\cdot{}^{s}w,sx),(v\cdot{}^{t}w,tx)).

(4.56)

Case 1.
Suppose first that
({\bf a},{\bf b})\in\sigma
; in particular, we have
u,v\in V
. If
{\bf c}\in V\rtimes T
, then (
4.55
) follows from the fact that
\sigma
is compatible and
\sigma\subseteq\Sigma
. So we are left to consider the cases in which
w=1
and/or
x=1
. We will soon consider these separately, but we first note that
(S3)
gives
us^{+}=vt^{+}
. Combining this with
(S1)
, we have

(us^{+},s)\mathrel{\sigma}(u,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(v,t)\mathrel{\sigma}(vt^{+},t)=(us^{+},t)\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ (s,t)\in\sigma_{us^{+}}.

(4.57)

Case 1.1.
Suppose
w=1
. Examining (
4.56
), we must show that

(a)

({}^{x}u,xs)\mathrel{\Sigma}({}^{x}v,xt)
, and

(b)

(us^{+},sx)\mathrel{\Sigma}(vt^{+},tx)
.

For
(b)
, we note that (
4.57
) and
(S5)
give
(sx,tx)\in\sigma_{us^{+}}
, and so

(us^{+},sx)\mathrel{\sigma}(us^{+},tx)=(vt^{+},tx).

For
(a)
, we note that (
4.57
) and
(S7)
give
(xs,xt)\in\sigma_{{}^{x}(us^{+})}
. There is now a subtle point concerning the possibility that
{}^{x}(us^{+})
might equal
1
.

•

If
{}^{x}(us^{+})=1
, then it follows from
(S4)
that
xs=xt
, and so
({}^{x}(us^{+}),xs)=({}^{x}(us^{+}),xt)
.

•

If
{}^{x}(us^{+})\not=1
, then
{}^{x}(us^{+})\in V
and
({}^{x}(us^{+}),xs)\mathrel{\sigma}({}^{x}(us^{+}),xt)
, by definition of
\sigma_{{}^{x}(us^{+})}
, since
(xs,xt)\in\sigma_{{}^{x}(us^{+})}
.

Either way, we have
({}^{x}(us^{+}),xs)\mathrel{\Sigma}({}^{x}(us^{+}),xt)
. Now,

{}^{x}(us^{+})={}^{x}u\cdot{}^{x}(s^{+})={}^{x}u(xs)^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt{}^{x}(us^{+})={}^{x}(vt^{+})={}^{x}v(xt)^{+}.

Combining all the above with
(

Σ

3)
, we conclude that

({}^{x}u,xs)\mathrel{\Sigma}({}^{x}u(xs)^{+},xs)=({}^{x}(us^{+}),xs)\mathrel{\Sigma}({}^{x}(us^{+}),xt)=({}^{x}v(xt)^{+},xt)\mathrel{\Sigma}({}^{x}v,xt),

completing the proof of
(a)
.

Case 1.2.
Now suppose
x=1
. Since we have already dealt with the
w=1
case, we will also assume here that
w\not=1
. Examining (
4.56
) again, this time we must show that

(c)

(wu,s)\mathrel{\Sigma}(wv,t)
, and

(d)

(u\cdot{}^{s}w,s)\mathrel{\Sigma}(v\cdot{}^{t}w,t)
.

For
(c)
, we note that (
4.57
) and
(S6)
give
(s,t)\in\sigma_{wus^{+}}
. Since
wus^{+}\in V
(as
u\in V
), it follows that
(wus^{+},s)\mathrel{\sigma}(wus^{+},t)
. Combining this with
(S1)
and
us^{+}=vt^{+}
, we have

(wu,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(wus^{+},s)\mathrel{\sigma}(wus^{+},t)=(wvt^{+},t)\mathrel{\sigma}(wv,t).

For
(d)
, and keeping
w\not=1
in mind, we note that (
4.57
) and
(S8)
give

\displaystyle us^{+}\cdot{}^{s}w=us^{+}\cdot{}^{t}w

\displaystyle\and(s,t)\in\sigma_{us^{+}\cdot{}^{s}w}.

By Lemma
3.1
(iv)
we have
us^{+}\cdot{}^{s}w=u\cdot{}^{s}w
and
us^{+}\cdot{}^{t}w=vt^{+}\cdot{}^{t}w=v\cdot{}^{t}w
. So in fact,

\displaystyle u\cdot{}^{s}w=v\cdot{}^{t}w

\displaystyle\and(s,t)\in\sigma_{u\cdot{}^{s}w},

which gives
(u\cdot{}^{s}w,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(u\cdot{}^{s}w,t)=(v\cdot{}^{t}w,t)
, completing the proof of
(d)
.

Case 2.
We now assume that
({\bf a},{\bf b})\notin\sigma
(but we still of course have
({\bf a},{\bf b})\in\Sigma
). By definition of
\Sigma
, and swapping
{\bf a}
and
{\bf b}
if necessary, we may assume that
{\bf a}=(1,s)
, and that
{\bf b}=(v,t)
belongs to the
\sigma
-class of
{\bf d}=(s^{+},s)
, with
s^{+}\in V
. Since
({\bf b},{\bf d})\in\sigma
, it follows from Case 2 that
{\bf c}\cdot({\bf b},{\bf d})\in\Sigma
and
({\bf b},{\bf d})\cdot{\bf c}\in\Sigma
. So by transitivity, we can complete the proof of (
4.55
), and hence of the lemma, by showing that

\displaystyle{\bf c}\cdot({\bf a},{\bf d})\in\Sigma

\displaystyle\and({\bf a},{\bf d})\cdot{\bf c}\in\Sigma.

For this, we first note that

\displaystyle{\bf c}\cdot({\bf a},{\bf d})=((wx^{+},xs),(w\cdot{}^{x}(s^{+}),xs)

\displaystyle\and({\bf a},{\bf d})\cdot{\bf c}=(({}^{s}w,sx),(s^{+}\cdot{}^{s}w,sx)).

By
(

Σ

3)
, we have

{\bf c}{\bf a}=(wx^{+},xs)\mathrel{\Sigma}(wx^{+}(xs)^{+},xs)=(w(xs)^{+},xs)=(w\cdot{}^{x}(s^{+}),xs)={\bf c}{\bf d}.

On the other hand, we have
{\bf a}{\bf c}={\bf d}{\bf c}
, as
{}^{s}w=s^{+}\cdot{}^{s}w
.
We have now completed the proof of Lemma
4.54
.
∎

Returning now to the proof of the theorem, we fix a congruence
\Sigma
on
V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
as in Lemma
4.54
, and we define
M=(V^{1}\rtimes T^{1})/\Sigma
. We denote the
\Sigma
-class of
(u,s)\in V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
by
[u,s]
, so that

M=\big\{{[u,s]}:{u\in V^{1},\ s\in T^{1}}\big\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with operation}\hskip 20.00003pt[u,s]\cdot[v,t]=[u\cdot{}^{s}v,st].

We already observed that
(1,1)
is a left identity for
V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}
, so of course
[1,1]
is a left identity for
M
. It follows from
(

Σ

3)
that

[u,s]\cdot[1,1]=[us^{+},s]=[u,s]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in V^{1}$ and $s\in T^{1}$,}

so that
[1,1]
is a right identity for
M
. Consequently,
M
is a monoid with identity
[1,1]
.

Now we define

U=\big\{{[u,1]}:{u\in V}\big\}\and S=\big\{{[s^{+},s]}:{s\in T}\big\}.

Clearly
U\leq M
, and it follows from
(

Σ

2)
that
U\cong V
. We have
S\leq M
because of the rule
[s^{+},s]\cdot[t^{+},t]=[(st)^{+},st]
, which itself follows from
s^{+}\cdot{}^{s}(t^{+})={}^{s}(t^{+})=(st)^{+}
. We have
S\cong T
because of
(S2)
.

Next we verify that
(U,S)
is an action pair in
M
by using Proposition
4.7
. Property
(SA1)
follows from

[s^{+},s]\cdot[u,1]=[{}^{s}u,s]=[{}^{s}u,1]\cdot[s^{+},s].

We define the map
S\to U^{1}:[s^{+},s]\mapsto[s^{+},s]^{+}=[s^{+},1]
. When checking
(

+

1)
–
(

+

3)
we make extensive use of Lemma
3.1
. For
(

+

4)
, we must show that

[u,1]\cdot[s^{+},s]=[v,1]\cdot[t^{+},t]\ \Rightarrow\ [u,1]\cdot[s^{+},s]^{+}=[v,1]\cdot[t^{+},t]^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in V^{1}$ and $s,t\in T$.}

Expanding the products, this amounts to showing that

[us^{+},s]=[vt^{+},t]\ \Rightarrow\ [us^{+},1]=[vt^{+},1]

for all such
u,v,s,t
. For this we use
(

Σ

3)
and
(

Σ

4)
:

\displaystyle[us^{+},s]=[vt^{+},t]\ \Rightarrow\ [u,s]=[v,t]\ \Rightarrow\ us^{+}=vt^{+}\ \Rightarrow\ [us^{+},1]=[vt^{+},1].

This completes the proof that
(U,S)
is an action pair in
M
.

If
T
acts on
V^{1}
by monoid morphisms, then
s^{+}={}^{s}1=1
for all
s\in T
, and it follows that
[s^{+},s]^{+}=[1,1]
for all
s
, so that
(U,S)
is strong by Lemma
4.15
(ii)
.

Next we note that

US=\big\{{[u,1][s^{+},s]}:{u\in V,\ s\in T}\big\}=\big\{{[u,s]}:{u\in V,\ s\in T}\big\},

where in the last equality we use the fact that
[u,1][s^{+},s]=[us^{+},s]=[u,s]
for
u\in V
and
s\in T
, which itself follows from
(

Σ

3)
. Finally, we define a map

\Psi:V\rtimes T\to US\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pt(u,s)\Psi=[u,s].

It is clear that
\Psi
is a surmorphism (it is the restriction to
V\rtimes T
of the natural projection
V^{1}\rtimes T^{1}\to(V^{1}\rtimes T^{1})/\Sigma=M
). If
u,v\in V
and
s,t\in T
, then by the definition of
\Psi
, and by
(

Σ

1)
, we also have

(u,s)\Psi=(v,t)\Psi\ \Leftrightarrow\ [u,s]=[v,t]\ \Leftrightarrow\ (u,s)\mathrel{\Sigma}(v,t)\ \Leftrightarrow\ (u,s)\mathrel{\sigma}(v,t),

so that
\ker(\Psi)=\sigma
. Thus,
US\cong(V\rtimes T)/\sigma
by the First Isomorphism Theorem.
∎

Remark 4.58
.

Recall from Remark
4.47
that the surmorphism
\pi:U\rtimes S\to US
factors through the subsemigroup

M=\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}\leq U\rtimes S.

It is possible to use Theorem
4.53
to classify the congruences
\sigma
on semigroups of the form
M
such that
M/\sigma
comes from an action pair. We have chosen not to do this, however, because semidirect products are arguably the more natural/direct construction. Nevertheless, the semigroup
M
will play a very important role in Chapter
5
.

4.5
Congruence conditions

We conclude Chapter
4
with a sequence of results concerning generating sets for the congruence
\theta
on
U\rtimes S
defined in Proposition
4.46
, where
(U,S)
is an action pair in a monoid
M
. For convenience, let us recall that

\theta=\big\{{((u,s),(v,t))}:{u,v\in U,\ s,t\in S,\ us=vt}\big\}.

The reason we are interested in describing such generating sets is that they feed into presentations for the semigroup
US\cong(U\rtimes S)/\theta
in Chapter
6
.

The first result is the most general, and applies to any action pair
(U,S)
. Roughly speaking, it shows that
\theta
is generated by pairs of two kinds:

•

pairs with fixed
U
-coordinate, coming from the right congruences
\theta_{u}
in (
4.49
), and

•

pairs with fixed
S
-coordinate, coming from the
s\mapsto s^{+}
map in Proposition
4.7
.

Lemma 4.59
.

If
(U,S)
is an action pair, then
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
, where

\Omega=\big\{{((u,s),(u,t))}:{u\in U,\ (s,t)\in\theta_{u}}\big\}\cup\big\{{((u,s),(us^{+},s))}:{u\in U,\ s\in S}\big\}.

Proof.

Write
\theta^{\prime}=\Omega^{\sharp}
. It is easy to check that
\Omega\subseteq\theta
, so
\theta^{\prime}\subseteq\theta
. It remains to show that
\theta\subseteq\theta^{\prime}
, so suppose
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}(v,t)
. By Lemma
4.50
(i)
, we have
us^{+}=vt^{+}
and
(s,t)\in\theta_{us^{+}}
. But then

(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(us^{+},s)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(us^{+},t)=(vt^{+},t)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(v,t).\qed

If the pair
(U,S)
is strong, then the previous result is essentially vacuous; cf. Lemma
4.50
(ii)
. The generating set
\Omega
from Lemma
4.59
can be reduced when
U
and
S
are both submonoids of the over-monoid
M
, as we now show. For the statement, recall from Remark
4.4
that
s=s^{+}s
, which tells us that
(1,s)\mathrel{\theta}(s^{+},s)
.

Lemma 4.60
.

If
(U,S)
is an action pair, and if
U
and
S
are both submonoids, then
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
, where

\Omega=\big\{{((u,s),(u,t))}:{u\in U,\ (s,t)\in\theta_{u}}\big\}\cup\big\{{((1,s),(s^{+},s))}:{s\in S}\big\}.

Proof.

Write
\theta^{\prime}=\Omega^{\sharp}
. It again suffices to show that
\theta\subseteq\theta^{\prime}
. Given Lemma
4.59
, it suffices to show that
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(us^{+},s)
for all
u\in U
and
s\in S
. For this, we have

(u,s)=(u,1)(1,s)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,1)(s^{+},s)=(us^{+},s).\qed

Further simplifications are available when the pair
(U,S)
is strong. We begin by discussing certain conditions that might be satisfied by such a pair.

Lemma 4.61
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a strong action pair, and consider the following conditions:

(i)

(\exists u\in U)\ (\forall s\in S)\ s=su
,

(ii)

(\forall s\in S)\ (\exists u\in U)\ s=su
,

(iii)

(\exists u\in U)\ (\forall s\in S)\ s=us
,

(iv)

(\forall s\in S)\ (\exists u\in U)\ s=us
,

(v)

(\forall s\in S)\ (\exists u\in U)\ (\exists t\in S)\ s=ut
,

(vi)

(\exists s,t\in S)\ (\exists u\in U)\ s=ut
,

(vii)

(\exists v\in U)\ (\forall u\in U)\ (\forall s\in S)\ us=usv
,

(viii)

(\forall u\in U)\ (\forall s\in S)\ (\exists v\in U)\ us=usv
,

(ix)

(\exists v\in U)\ (\forall u\in U)\ (\forall s\in S)\ u=u\cdot{}^{s}v
,

(x)

(\forall u\in U)\ (\forall s\in S)\ (\exists v\in U)\ u=u\cdot{}^{s}v
.

The following implications hold:

(iii)
(iv)
(v)
(vi)
(vii)
(viii)
(i)
(ii)
(ix)
(x)
\Rightarrow
\Rightarrow
\Rightarrow
\Rightarrow
\Rightarrow
\Rightarrow
\Rightarrow
\Downarrow
\Downarrow
\Updownarrow
\Updownarrow

Proof.

All implications other than
(vi)
\ \Rightarrow\
(vii)
,
(vii)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(ix)
and
(viii)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(x)
are obvious, and in fact hold for non-strong action pairs as well. We treat the other implications now.

(vi)
\ \Rightarrow\
(vii)
. If
(vi)
holds, then we have
x=vy
for some
x,y\in S
and
v\in U
. To show that
(vii)
holds (with respect to this
v
), let
u\in U
and
s\in S
be arbitrary. Then
u\cdot sx=usvy=u\cdot{}^{s}v\cdot sy
, so
(SA2)
gives
u=u\cdot{}^{s}v
. But then
us=u\cdot{}^{s}v\cdot s=usv
, as required.

(vii)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(ix)
and
(viii)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(x)
. Using
usv=u\cdot{}^{s}v\cdot s
and
(SA2)
, we have
{us=usv\ \Leftrightarrow\ u=u\cdot{}^{s}v}
.
∎

Remark 4.62
.

The conditions listed in Lemma
4.61
are quite natural. Indeed, items
(i)
–
(viii)
have the equivalent formulations:

(i)

Some element of
U
is a right identity for
S
.

(ii)

Every element of
S
has a right identity from
U
.

(iii)

Some element of
U
is a left identity for
S
.

(iv)

Every element of
S
has a left identity from
U
.

(v)

S\subseteq US
.

(vi)

S\cap US\not=\varnothing
.

(vii)

Some element of
U
is a right identity for
US
.

(viii)

Every element of
US
has a right identity from
U
.

While
(ix)
and
(x)
may not appear to be quite as natural, we have shown that they are equivalent (when
(U,S)
is strong) to
(vii)
and
(viii)
, and these alternative formulations will be useful in the next proof.

One important special case in which all of conditions
(i)
–
(x)
obviously hold is when
U
is a submonoid of the over-monoid
M
; note that we only have to check
(i)
and
(iii)
, which clearly hold in this case.

Remark 4.63
.

It is also worth noting that the conditions listed in Lemma
4.61
are not all equivalent.
For example, consider the partial transformation monoid
M=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
, where
n\geq 2
, and the subsemigroups
U=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})
,
S_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
S_{2}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
. As noted in Example
4.30
, the strong action pairs
(U,S_{1})
and
(U,S_{2})
give rise to the same subsemigroup:

US_{1}=US_{2}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}).

One can check that:

•

(U,S_{1})
satisfies only
(viii)
and
(x)
, while

•

(U,S_{2})
satisfies only
(ii)
,
(viii)
and
(x)
.

On the other hand, some of the conditions of Lemma
4.61
are equivalent under further assumptions. For example, if every element of
U
has a left identity (which happens for example if
U
is regular), then
(iv)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(v)
. Indeed, suppose
(v)
holds, and let
s\in S
be arbitrary. Then
(v)
gives
s=vt
for some
v\in U
and
t\in S
. But then if
u\in U
is a left identity for
v
, it follows that
s=vt=uvt=us
, showing that
(iv)
holds.

A similar argument shows that if
U
has a left identity, then
(v)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
, so it follows that
(iii)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(iv)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(v)
in this case.

Remark 4.64
.

Finally, we note that the implication
(vi)
\ \Rightarrow\
(vii)
need not hold if
(U,S)
is not strong. For example, consider the symmetric inverse monoid
S=\mathcal{I}_{X}
where
|X|\geq 2
. Then
(U,S)
is an action pair, where
U=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})=\{{\mathrm{id}_{A}}:{A\subsetneq X}\}
. Indeed, this is easy to check directly, and it also follows from Remark
4.45
, as
\mathcal{I}_{X}
is inverse, and hence left restriction, and
U=P^{\flat}
in the notation of that remark. Condition
(vi)
holds trivially for this pair, as we can take
s=t=u=\mathrm{id}_{A}
for any
A\subsetneq X
. However,
(vii)
does not hold since

US=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{X}}:{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)\not=X}\}

does not have a right identity, let alone a right identity from
U
.

For the rest of this section, we assume that for each
u\in U
, the right congruence
\theta_{u}
is generated (as a right congruence) by some set of pairs
\Omega_{u}\subseteq S\times S
. For any set
X
, and any set of pairs
\Sigma\subseteq X\times X
, we write

\Sigma^{-1}=\big\{{(y,x)}:{(x,y)\in\Sigma}\big\}.

Lemma 4.65
.

If
(U,S)
is a strong action pair, and if any of the conditions listed in Lemma
4.61
hold (e.g., if
U
is a submonoid), then
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
, where

\Omega=\big\{{((u,s),(u,t))}:{u\in U,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{u}}\big\}.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.61
we may assume that
(x)
holds. As usual, it suffices to show that
\theta\subseteq\theta^{\prime}
, where
\theta^{\prime}=\Omega^{\sharp}
. By Lemma
4.50
(ii)
, this amounts to showing that
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,t)
for all
u\in U
and
(s,t)\in\theta_{u}
, so fix some such
u,s,t
. Since
\theta_{u}
is generated (as a right congruence) by
\Omega_{u}
, there is a sequence

s=s_{1}\to s_{2}\to\cdots\to s_{k}=t,

where
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in S
, and such that for each
1\leq i<k
,

s_{i}=a_{i}c_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts_{i+1}=b_{i}c_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $(a_{i},b_{i})\in\Omega_{u}\cup\Omega_{u}^{-1}$ and $c_{i}\in S^{1}$.}

Since
s=s_{1}
and
t=s_{k}
, we can show that
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,t)
by showing that
(u,s_{i})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,s_{i+1})
for each
1\leq i<k
. This is clear if
c_{i}=1
, since then

((u,s_{i}),(u,s_{i+1}))=((u,a_{i}),(u,b_{i}))\in\Omega\cup\Omega^{-1}\subseteq\theta^{\prime}.

So now we assume that
c_{i}\not=1
. By
(x)
, there exists
v\in U
such that
u=u\cdot{}^{a_{i}}v
. First note that

u\cdot{}^{b_{i}}v\cdot b_{i}=u\cdot b_{i}\cdot v=u\cdot a_{i}\cdot v=u\cdot{}^{a_{i}}v\cdot a_{i}=u\cdot a_{i},

where we used
(a_{i},b_{i})\in\theta_{u}
(which implies
ua_{i}=ub_{i}
) in the second step;
(SA2)
then gives
u\cdot{}^{b_{i}}v=u
. It then follows that

(u,s_{i})=(u,a_{i})\cdot(v,c_{i})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,b_{i})\cdot(v,c_{i})=(u,s_{i+1}),

as required.
∎

Remark 4.66
.

The above proof shows that
\theta
is in fact generated by
\Omega
as a
right
congruence.

The previous result showed that, under certain conditions, a generating set for the congruence
\theta
can be constructed by incorporating generating sets for the right congruences
\theta_{u}
. We now aim to take this further, by additionally incorporating a generating set for the semigroup
U
. This of course necessitates stronger assumptions, and these include requiring that the
\theta_{u}
behave well with respect to joins. Before stating the result, we first establish some notation, which will be of use throughout.
For
u,v\in U
we write

\displaystyle v\preceq u\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 10.00002ptu=wv

for some
w\in U^{1}
.

(4.67)

(So
\preceq
is Green’s
\geq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{L}}}
-preorder on
U
.) It is easy to see that

\displaystyle v\preceq u\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\ \Rightarrow\ \hskip 10.00002pt\theta_{v}\subseteq\theta_{u}

for all
u,v\in U
.

(4.68)

It follows immediately that for any
V\subseteq U
,

\displaystyle\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop v\preceq u}\theta_{v}

\displaystyle\subseteq\theta_{u}

for all
u\in U
.

(4.69)

Here and elsewhere, the join of equivalence relations is taken in the lattice of all equivalence relations (over the same set). In particular, the join in (
4.69
) is the smallest equivalence relation on
S
containing all of the
\theta_{v}
(
v\in V
,
v\preceq u
); note that this join could involve infinitely many terms. If
U
is commutative, it also follows from (
4.68
) that

\displaystyle\theta_{u_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\theta_{u_{k}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\theta_{u_{1}\cdots u_{k}}

for all
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\in U
,

(4.70)

as
u_{i}\preceq u_{1}\cdots u_{k}
for each
i
, by commutativity.

The next result concerns the cases in which we have equality in (
4.69
) or (
4.70
).
The remaining results of this section assume
U
and
S
are submonoids of the over-monoid
M
; one could state them in greater generality, similarly to Lemma
4.65
, but the combinations of conditions become unweildy, and we prefer the cleaner statements below.

Lemma 4.71
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a strong action pair in a monoid
M
, and suppose additionally that
U
and
S
are submonoids of
M
.

(i)

If there exists a subset
V\subseteq U
such that

\theta_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop v\preceq u}\theta_{v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$,}

then
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
, where

\Omega=\big\{{((v,s),(v,t))}:{v\in V,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{v}}\big\}.

(ii)

If
U
is commutative, and if
\theta_{uv}=\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
for all
u,v\in U
, then for any monoid generating set
V
for
U
we have
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
, where

\Omega=\big\{{((v,s),(v,t))}:{v\in V,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{v}}\big\}.

Proof.

(i)
. Suppose
V\subseteq U
satisfies the stated assumption. As ever, it suffices to show that
\theta\subseteq\theta^{\prime}
, where
\theta^{\prime}=\Omega^{\sharp}
. By Lemma
4.50
(ii)
, this amounts to showing that
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,t)
for any
u\in U
and
(s,t)\in\theta_{u}
, so fix some such
u,s,t
. By the assumption on
\theta_{u}
, there is a sequence

s=s_{1}\to s_{2}\to\cdots\to s_{k}=t

such that each
(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in\bigcup_{v\in V_{u}}(\Omega_{v}\cup\Omega_{v}^{-1})
, where
V_{u}=\{{v\in V}:{v\preceq u}\}
. Since
{(u,s)=(u,s_{1})}
and
(u,t)=(u,s_{k})
, it suffices to show that
(u,s_{i})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,s_{i+1})
for each
1\leq i<k
, so fix some such
i
. Then
(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in\Omega_{v}\cup\Omega_{v}^{-1}
for some
v\in V_{u}
, and so
((v,s_{i}),(v,s_{i+1}))\in\Omega\cup\Omega^{-1}
. Since
v\preceq u
we have
u=wv
for some
w\in U(=U^{1})
, and then

(u,s_{i})=(w,1)\cdot(v,s_{i})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(w,1)\cdot(v,s_{i+1})=(u,s_{i+1}).

(ii)
. Suppose the assumptions of
(ii)
hold, and let
V
be an arbitrary generating set for
U
. It suffices to show that the assumption of
(i)
holds with respect to this
V
. By (
4.69
), this amounts to showing that

\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop v\preceq u}\theta_{v}\supseteq\theta_{u}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$,}

so fix some such
u
. Since
U=\langle V\rangle
, we have
u=v_{1}\cdots v_{k}
for some
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V
, and so

\theta_{u}=\theta_{v_{1}\cdots v_{k}}=\theta_{v_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\theta_{v_{k}}\subseteq\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop v\preceq u}\theta_{v},

since each
v_{i}\preceq u
by commutativity of
U
.
∎

The generating sets in Lemmas
4.65
and
4.71
can be further simplified in the (very) special case that
U
and
S
are submonoids of
M
, with
S
a
group
. To deal with this case, we introduce another piece of notation.

For an arbitrary weak action pair
(U,S)
, we define the sets

S_{u}=\{{s\in S}:{us=u}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for each $u\in U$.}

(4.72)

It is easy to see that each
S_{u}
is a (possibly empty) subsemigroup of
S
. When
S
is a submonoid of
M
, each
S_{u}
is a (non-empty) submonoid of
S
; if
S
is additionally a group, each
S_{u}
is a subgroup. The next result concerns this group case, when
(U,S)
is an action pair (not just a weak action pair). For the statement, we fix a
group
generating set
\Gamma_{u}
for
S_{u}
, for each
u\in U
; so each
S_{u}
is generated as a monoid by
\Gamma_{u}\cup\Gamma_{u}^{-1}
. Note that in this case
S
is contained in the group of units of
M
, so the action pair
(U,S)
is automatically strong by Lemma
4.23
(ii)
.

Lemma 4.73
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a (strong) action pair in a monoid
M
, and suppose additionally that
U
and
S
are submonoids of
M
, with
S
a group. Then
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
, where

\Omega=\big\{{((u,1),(u,s))}:{u\in U,\ s\in\Gamma_{u}}\big\}.

Proof.

Once again, it suffices to show that
(u,s)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,t)
for all
u\in U
and
(s,t)\in\theta_{u}
, where
\theta^{\prime}=\Omega^{\sharp}
. First note that
(s,t)\in\theta_{u}\ \Rightarrow\ us=ut\ \Rightarrow\ ust^{-1}=u
, so that
st^{-1}\in S_{u}
. It then follows that
st^{-1}=g_{1}\cdots g_{k}
for some
{g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}\in\Gamma_{u}\cup\Gamma_{u}^{-1}}
. For each
1\leq i\leq k+1
write
s_{i}=g_{i}\cdots g_{k}t
. Since
s_{1}=s
and
s_{k+1}=t
, it suffices to show that
(u,s_{i})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,s_{i+1})
for each
1\leq i\leq k
.

So fix some such
1\leq i\leq k
. If
g_{i}\in\Gamma_{u}
, then clearly
(u,1)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,g_{i})
. Otherwise,
{(u,1)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,g_{i}^{-1})}
, so post-multiplying by
(1,g_{i})
gives
(u,g_{i})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,1)
. Either way (and keeping
s_{i}=g_{i}s_{i+1}
in mind), it follows that

(u,s_{i})=(u,g_{i})\cdot(1,s_{i+1})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,1)\cdot(1,s_{i+1})=(u,s_{i+1}),

as required.
∎

As with Lemma
4.71
, the conclusion of Lemma
4.73
can be strengthened when the subgroups
S_{u}
behave well with respect to joins. (The join
\bigvee_{i}H_{i}
of a collection of subgroups is the smallest subgroup containing the union
\bigcup_{i}H_{i}
.)
The statement again uses the relation
\preceq
on
U
given in (
4.67
).

Lemma 4.74
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a (strong) action pair in a monoid
M
, and suppose additionally that
U
and
S
are submonoids of
M
, with
S
a group.

(i)

If there exists a subset
V\subseteq U
such that

S_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop v\preceq u}S_{v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$,}

then
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
, where

\Omega=\big\{{((v,1),(v,s))}:{v\in V,\ s\in\Gamma_{v}}\big\}.

(ii)

If
U
is commutative, and if
S_{uv}=S_{u}\vee S_{v}
for all
u,v\in U
, then for any monoid generating set
V
for
U
we have
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
, where

\Omega=\big\{{((v,1),(v,s))}:{v\in V,\ s\in\Gamma_{v}}\big\}.

Proof.

As in Lemma
4.71
, part
(ii)
follows from part
(i)
.

To prove
(i)
, it suffices by Lemma
4.73
to show that
(u,1)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,s)
for all
u\in U
and
s\in\Gamma_{u}
, where
\theta^{\prime}=\Omega^{\sharp}
, so fix some such
u,s
. Then
s\in S_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in V_{u}}S_{v}
, where again we write
V_{u}=\{{v\in V}:{v\preceq u}\}
, so
s=g_{1}\cdots g_{k}
for some
{g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}\in\bigcup_{v\in V_{u}}(\Gamma_{v}\cup\Gamma_{v}^{-1})}
. (Recall that
\Gamma_{v}
is a group generating set for
S_{v}
.) Writing
s_{i}=g_{i}\cdots g_{k}
for each
1\leq i\leq k+1
, we have
s_{1}=s
and
s_{k+1}=1
, so it suffices to show that
(u,s_{i})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(u,s_{i+1})
for each
1\leq i\leq k
. But for any such
i
, we have
g_{i}\in\Gamma_{v}\cup\Gamma_{v}^{-1}
for some
v\in V_{u}
, and
u=wv
for some
w\in U
. As in the proof of Lemma
4.73
, we have
(v,1)\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(v,g_{i})
, and (again remembering that
s_{i}=g_{i}s_{i+1}
), it follows that

(u,s_{i})=(w,1)\cdot(v,g_{i})\cdot(1,s_{i+1})\mathrel{\theta}^{\prime}(w,1)\cdot(v,1)\cdot(1,s_{i+1})=(u,s_{i+1}).\qed

Remark 4.75
.

As in (
4.68
), we have

\displaystyle v\preceq u\ \Rightarrow\ S_{v}

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{u}

for all
u,v\in U
.

It follows that

\displaystyle\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop v\preceq u}S_{v}

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{u}

for all
u\in U
,

and if
U
is commutative, that

\displaystyle S_{u_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee S_{u_{k}}

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{u_{1}\cdots u_{k}}

for all
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\in U
.

Thus, in order to apply Lemma
4.74
, we only need to check the forwards inclusion in the relevant join equation.

5
Proper action pairs

Proposition
4.46
showed that the semigroup
US
arising from a weak action pair
(U,S)
is a homomorphic image of a semidirect product
U\rtimes S
. Theorem
4.53
took this further in the case that
(U,S)
is an action pair (not just a weak action pair), by additionally classifying the congruences on semidirect products that lead to action pairs in this way.
The purpose of the current chapter is to prove a number of additional structure theorems concerning semigroups arising from (weak) action pairs. These results are inspired by important classical results on inverse semigroups, and wider classes such as left restriction semigroups, each of which involve the notion of a
proper
semigroup from the relevant class.

We discuss these classical results in Section
5.1
(see Theorems
5.11
and
5.12
), as well as some of the backstory behind their development. Section
5.2
shows that the mere existence of an action of
S
on
U^{1}
(for semigroups
U
and
S
) allows one to construct an action pair
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
in a suitable monoid
M
, with
\underline{U}\cong U
and
\overline{S}\cong S
, and with the respective actions of
S
and
\overline{S}
on
U^{1}
and
\underline{U}^{1}
matching up appropriately. Some aspects of this construction bear similarities with ideas in the proof of Theorem
4.53
. In Section
5.3
we introduce the key notion of a
proper
action pair, and show that a semigroup
US
arising from an arbitrary weak action pair
(U,S)
has a ‘proper cover’. More specifically, Theorem
5.39
shows that there exists a proper pair
(U^{\prime},S^{\prime})
with
U^{\prime}\cong U
and
S^{\prime}\cong S
, and a natural surmorphism
U^{\prime}S^{\prime}\to US
. As an application of this, we show how to deduce the classical Theorem
5.11
as a corollary.
Sections
5.4
–
5.7
contain four embedding theorems for proper
monoids
.

•

Theorem
5.60
(in Section
5.5
) is the most general of these, and states that a monoid
US
arising from a proper action pair
(U,S)
can be embedded in a semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
, where
\mathcal{U}
is a monoid containing
U
, and
\sigma
is a certain natural congruence on
S
. When
S
is inverse,
\sigma
is the least group congruence. The monoid
\mathcal{U}
constructed in the proof is defined by means of a presentation that incorporates the structure of the monoids
U
and
S/\sigma
, and the action of
S
on
{U(=U^{1})}
.

•

Theorem
5.47
(in Section
5.4
) is actually a special case of Theorem
5.60
, stated and proved under the additional assumption that the elements
s^{+}={}^{s}1
(
s\in S
) are central in
U
. This allows us to construct a very different monoid
\mathcal{U}
during the proof, and the resulting semidirect product is in fact an (unrestricted) wreath product. In the special case that
S
is left restriction, this
\mathcal{U}
turns out to be a semilattice (built in a natural way from the ideals of
U=P(S)
), and this allows us to deduce the classical Theorem
5.12
as a corollary.

•

Theorems
5.82
and
5.102
(in Sections
5.6
and
5.7
) are specialised versions of Theorem
5.60
. They show that when
U
is assumed to have certain additional structural properties (such as being a semilattice or a left-regular band), then
M=US
embeds in
\mathcal{U}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
, as above, where
\mathcal{U}
has the same structural property as
U
.

5.1
Background

In the early years of the development of semigroup theory, three directions of attack emerged to understand the structure and behaviour of an inverse semigroup
S
. These all had reference to the semilattice
E=E(S)=\{{x\in S}:{x=x^{2}}\}
of idempotents of
S
, and were subsequently developed and applied to much broader classes. Specifically these directions are:

•

the use of Munn’s
fundamental inverse semigroup

T_{E}
, built from order-isomorphisms of principal ideals of
E

[
93
]
;

•

the Ehresmann-Schein-Nambooripad approach, which uses the
trace groupoid
of
S
(which has identities
E
), together with the natural partial order on
S
, to build an inductive groupoid from which
S
can be recovered; a comprehensive account of the genesis of this material is contained in Lawson’s monograph
[
77
]
;

•

McAlister’s theory of
proper
(a.k.a.
E
-unitary
) inverse semigroups
[
86
,
87
]
.

Our current inspiration stems from this final direction, and we now describe it in a little more detail, beginning with the key definition(s). The following can be found, for example, on p55 of
[
17
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
S
be a semigroup, and
A\subseteq S
an arbitrary subset. We say
S
is
A
-unitary
if the following implications hold:

ax\in A\ \Rightarrow\ x\in A\and xa\in A\ \Rightarrow\ x\in A\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a\in A$ and $x\in S$.}

(5.2)

Remark 5.3
.

Consider an
inverse
semigroup
S
, with semilattice of idempotents
E=E(S)
. Then
S
is
E
-unitary when the implications in (
5.2
) hold with respect to
A=E
. It is easy to check (when
A=E
) that either of the implications in (
5.2
) follows from the other. Thus,
S
is
E
-unitary if and only if

\displaystyle ex\in E

\displaystyle\ \Rightarrow\ x\in E

for all
e\in E
and
x\in S
.

It is again easy to check that this condition is equivalent to

\displaystyle e=ex

\displaystyle\ \Rightarrow\ x\in E

for all
e\in E
and
x\in S
.

(5.4)

This all paves the way for the following:

Definition 5.5
.

An inverse semigroup
S
is
proper
if it is
E
-unitary, where
E=E(S)
: i.e., if (
5.3
) holds (cf. Remark
5.3
).

Remark 5.6
.

There are many other equivalent characterisations of proper (
E
-unitary) inverse semigroups; see for example
[
64
, Proposition 5.9.1]
. One is for
\mathscr{R}\cap\sigma
(or equivalently
\mathscr{L}\cap\sigma
) to be trivial, where
\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{L}
are two of Green’s relations, and where
\sigma
is the least group congruence. Thus, one can coordinatise the elements of a proper inverse semigroup by an idempotent and a group element. For broader classes, such as the left restriction semigroups we consider below, being
E
-unitary is a weaker condition than being proper; the definition of proper semigroups in these classes is given below.

In two landmark papers, McAlister showed that:

•

any inverse semigroup has a
proper cover

[
86
]
: i.e., a preimage under an idempotent-separating morphism that is proper, and

•

any proper inverse semigroup
S
is isomorphic to a so-called
P-semigroup

[
87
]
. The latter is built from an action of the group
G=S/\sigma
(where here
\sigma
is the least group congruence on
S
) on a semilattice containing
E
. Both the difficulty and the beauty of this approach is that the action of
G
on
E
is not in general closed. It is also worth noting that while we know from the outset that
S
acts on
E
(cf. Example
3.3
(ii)
), we also need
S/\sigma
to act on (a semilattice containing)
E
.

Subsequently, O’Carroll
[
98
]
showed that a proper inverse semigroup
S
embeds into a semidirect product
F\rtimes G
, where
F
is a semilattice containing
E
, and again
G=S/\sigma
. Immediately one sees a connection with (left) action pairs, since a proper inverse semigroup
S
is coordinatised by two components, one from a semilattice and another from a group. Of course,
S
might not be the internal product of these constituents.

There are many proofs of McAlister’s ‘P-theorem’, and of O’Carroll’s subsequent embedding theorem. In addition to the original strategies, we mention here the approaches of Munn
[
95
]
, Billhardt
[
8
]
and Steinberg
[
111
]
, as well as the alternative strategy of Petrich and Reilly
[
99
]
using only partial actions (which yields a somewhat differerent formulation, but which nevertheless deserves mention here). A splendid account may be found in
[
78
]
, where
[
99
]
is referred to as ‘the maverick alternative’.

The approach of McAlister and O’Carroll for inverse semigroups prompted analagous work for larger classes of semigroups; see for example
[
49
,
113
,
112
,
115
,
3
,
83
,
9
,
75
]
. The extensions are, roughly speaking, in two directions:

•

One is to keep the condition of
S
being regular, but weaken the condition that
E(S)
forms a semilattice: e.g., it could be a left-regular band (these satisfy the identities
x^{2}=x
and
xyx=xy
).

•

The other direction is to drop the condition that
S
is regular, and here the main body of work retains commutativity of (certain) idempotents.

In particular, a theory analogous to that for inverse semigroups has been developed for left restriction semigroups and the special (and in some sense generating) classes of left ample and weakly left ample semigroups; see for example
[
43
,
46
,
52
,
51
,
9
]
. The results of this chapter take this all much further, and apply to semigroups and monoids arising from (weak) action pairs.

Here is the appropriate extension to left restriction semigroups of the notion of a proper inverse semigroup
[
47
,
57
,
75
]
.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
S
be a left restriction semigroup with semilattice of projections
P=P(S)
. Define the congruence

\displaystyle\sigma=\sigma_{S}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{es=et\ (\exists e\in P)}\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{es=ft\ (\exists e,f\in P)}\big\}.

We say
S
is
proper
if

s=t\hskip 20.00003pt\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 20.00003pts^{+}=t^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts\mathrel{\sigma}t\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $s,t\in S$.}

(5.8)

Remark 5.9
.

In fact,
\sigma=\nabla_{P}^{\sharp}
is the least congruence on
S
that identifies all projections. As explained in
[
56
]
,
\sigma
is also a unary semigroup congruence, meaning that
{(s,t)\in\sigma\ \Rightarrow\ (s^{+},t^{+})\in\sigma}
. The latter also follows quickly from
(A2)
and the definition of
\sigma
, as
(P,S)
is an action pair; cf. Proposition
4.37
and Remark
4.39
.

When
S
is inverse (regarded as a left restriction semigroup with
s^{+}=ss^{-1}
),
\sigma
is the least group congruence on
S
. Definitions
5.5
and
5.7
are equivalent when
S
is inverse; see for example
[
77
]
or
[
64
, Chapter 5]
. It is worth noting that
s^{+}=t^{+}
is equivalent to
s\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}t
in an inverse semigroup, where
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
is one of Green’s relations.

If
S
