is left ample (a left restriction semigroup that additionally satisfies the quasi-identity
xz=yz\Rightarrow xz^{+}=yz^{+}
), then
\sigma
is the least right cancellative monoid congruence on
S

[
43
]
. If
S
is weakly left ample (a left restriction semigroup in which
P(S)=E(S)
), then
\sigma
is the least unipotent monoid congruence on
S

[
52
]
.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
S
be a left restriction semigroup. A
cover
of
S
is a left restriction semigroup
C
together with a projection-separating surmorphism
\psi:C\to S
. Such a
\psi
is called a
covering morphism
, and is required to be a unary semigroup morphism, in the sense that
(xy)\psi=(x\psi)(y\psi)
and
x^{+}\psi=(x\psi)^{+}
for all
x,y\in C
. It follows quickly that
\psi
restricts to an isomorphism
P(C)\to P(S)
. It is worth noting that when
C
and
S
are inverse semigroups (regarded as left restriction semigroups with
x^{+}=xx^{-1}
), any semigroup morphism
C\to S
automatically preserves the
-1
operation, and hence also the
+
operation.

Here are the formal statements of the results mentioned above:

Theorem 5.11

(cf.
[
86
,
47
,
9
]
)
.

Every left restriction semigroup (and in particular every inverse semigroup) has a proper cover.

Theorem 5.12

(cf.
[
98
,
75
,
9
]
)
.

Let
S
be a proper left restriction semigroup (in particular an inverse semigroup) with semilattice of projections
P=P(S)
. Then
S
embeds into a semidirect product
{Q\rtimes(S/\sigma)}
, where
Q
is a semilattice containing
P
.

As we have already stated, our main goal in the current chapter is to devise a suitable notion of a
proper
(weak) action pair, and formulate and prove corresponding extensions of Theorems
5.11
and
5.12
in this context; see Theorems
5.39
,
5.47
,
5.60
,
5.82
and
5.102
.
These more general results will also lead to new proofs of Theorems
5.11
and
5.12
.

5.2
From actions to action pairs

(Weak) action pairs are defined in terms of an action of a semigroup
S
on the monoid completion
U^{1}
of a semigroup
U
, where
U
and
S
are contained in a common over-monoid
M
. The purpose of the current section is to show that the mere existence of an action (by morphisms) leads to an action pair that mimicks the original action, in a technical sense made precise below. This construction will be harnessed in the next section in order to show that any semigroup arising from a (weak) action pair has a ‘proper cover’.

For convenience of reference, we will gather the standing assumptions for this section here, and also fix some notational conventions:

Assumption 5.13
.

(i)

We assume that
U
and
S
are semigroups, and that
S
has a left action on
U^{1}
via semigroup morphisms, denoted
(s,u)\mapsto{}^{s}u
.

For example, if
S
has an action on
U
itself, and if
U\not=U^{1}
, then this can be extended to an action on
U^{1}
by additionally defining
{}^{s}1=1
for all
s\in S
. In this case, the action of
S
on
U^{1}
is by monoid morphisms. More generally, however, we do not assume that the action in
(i)
is by monoid morphisms.

(ii)

If
S
happens to be a monoid, then we assume in addition that the action of
S
on
U^{1}
is monoidal: i.e., that the identity of
S
acts identically.

Here, as usual,
U^{1}
denotes
U
if
U
is a monoid or else
U^{1}=U\cup\{1\}
, where
1
is an adjoined identity.
We use the same convention for
S^{1}
, and we assume without loss of generality (and for notational convenience) that the identities of
U^{1}
and
S^{1}
are the same. But note that we do not assume that
U
and
S
are contained in a common semigroup; in particular, we are not assuming at this point that
(U,S)
is a (weak) action pair. However, we will show that
(U,S)
can indeed be identified with an action pair
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
in a suitable monoid
M
.

The requirement for
S
to act monoidally (if it is a monoid) in Assumption
5.13
(ii)
is necessary for the results of this section. In the next, we will apply these results in the case that
(U,S)
is a weak action pair, and we noted in Remark
4.4
that whenever
S
is a monoid, it acts monoidally on
U^{1}
in this case.

The action of
S
on
U^{1}
allows for the formation of the (external) semidirect product
U\rtimes S
, as in Definition
3.4
. As usual, and if necessary, we can extend the action of
S
on
U^{1}
to a monoidal action of
S^{1}
on
U^{1}
(it is already monoidal by assumption if
S=S^{1}
), and we then have the semidirect product
U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}
, which contains
U\rtimes S
as a subsemigroup. Although
U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}
might not be a monoid (cf. Lemma
3.11
), it follows from Proposition
3.32
(and monoidality of the action of
S^{1}
on
U^{1}
) that

M=\big\{{(u,s)\in U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}\leq U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}

is a monoid with identity
(1,1)
.
Here, as usual, we write
s^{+}={}^{s}1
for all
s\in S^{1}
, noting that
1^{+}=1
.

We begin by identifying two natural subsemigroups of
M
. To this end, we define

\underline{u}=(u,1)\and\overline{s}=(s^{+},s)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $u\in U$ and $s\in S$,}

and we set

\underline{U}=\{{\underline{u}}:{u\in U}\}\and\overline{S}=\{{\overline{s}}:{s\in S}\}.

It is clear that
\underline{U}\subseteq M
, while
\overline{S}\subseteq M
follows from Lemma
3.1
(i)
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Given Assumption
5.13
, and with the above notation, we have

(i)

\underline{U}\leq M
and
\underline{U}\cong U
,

(ii)

\overline{S}\leq M
and
\overline{S}\cong S
.

Proof.

The first part is clear. The second follows quickly from the fact that for all
s,t\in S
,

\overline{s}\cdot\overline{t}=(s^{+},s)\cdot(t^{+},t)=(s^{+}\cdot{}^{s}(t^{+}),st)=({}^{s}(t^{+}),st)=((st)^{+},st)=\overline{st},

(5.15)

where we used parts
(iv)
and
(ii)
of Lemma
3.1
.
∎

For convenience in what follows, we also write
\underline{1}=(1,1)
, even if
1\not\in U
. On a number of occasions during the next proof (and later in the paper) we make use of the fact that
{\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}=(us^{+},s)}
for all
u\in U
and
s\in S
; in particular,
(u,s)=\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}
if
u=us^{+}
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Given Assumption
5.13
, and with the above notation,

(i)

(\underline{U},\overline{S})
is an action pair in
M=\big\{{(u,s)\in U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}
,

(ii)

(\underline{U},\overline{S})
is strong if and only if the action of
S
on
U^{1}
is by monoid morphisms,

(iii)

\underline{U}\cdot\overline{S}=\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}
.

Proof.

(i)
.
By Lemma
5.14
,
\underline{U}
and
\overline{S}
are subsemigroups of
M
. Since
\underline{U}\cong U
and
\overline{S}\cong S
, we have an action of
\overline{S}
on
\underline{U}^{1}=\underline{U}\cup\big\{(1,1)\big\}
, given by

{}^{\overline{s}}\underline{u}=\underline{{}^{s}u}=({}^{s}u,1)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $u\in U^{1}$ and $s\in S$.}

Using Lemma
3.1
(iv)
, one can easily check that
\overline{s}\cdot\underline{u}=({}^{s}u,s)={}^{\overline{s}}\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}
for all
u\in U
and
s\in S
. This shows that
(A1)
holds.

For
(A2)
, we must show that

\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}\ \Rightarrow\ \underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}^{+}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U^{1}$ and $s,t\in S$.}

Noting that

\overline{s}^{+}={}^{\overline{s}}\underline{1}=\underline{{}^{s}1}=\underline{s^{+}}=(s^{+},1),

(5.17)

and similarly for
\overline{t}^{+}
, the required implication follows quickly from the fact that

\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}=(us^{+},s),\hskip 20.00003pt\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}=(vt^{+},t),\hskip 20.00003pt\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}^{+}=(us^{+},1)\and\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}^{+}=(vt^{+},1).

(ii)
.
By Lemma
4.15
(ii)
,
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
is strong if and only if
\overline{s}^{+}=\underline{1}
for all
s\in S
. But by (
5.17
),

\overline{s}^{+}=\underline{1}\ \Leftrightarrow\ (s^{+},1)=(1,1)\ \Leftrightarrow\ s^{+}=1\ \Leftrightarrow\ {}^{s}1=1.

The assertion quickly follows.

(iii)
.
First suppose
u\in U
and
s\in S
are such that
u=us^{+}
. Then

(u,s)=(us^{+},s)=\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}\in\underline{U}\cdot\overline{S}.

Conversely, suppose
{\bf a}\in\underline{U}\cdot\overline{S}
, so that
{\bf a}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{s}
for some
v\in U
and
s\in S
. Then with
u=vs^{+}\in U
, we have

{\bf a}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{s}=(vs^{+},s)=(u,s)\and us^{+}=vs^{+}s^{+}=vs^{+}=u,

using Lemma
3.1
(i)
.
∎

5.3
Proper action pairs and a covering theorem

We are now almost ready to introduce the notion of a
proper
action pair; see Definitions
5.21
and
5.25
. The main result of the current section is Theorem
5.39
, which shows that any semigroup arising from a (weak) action pair is a natural homomorphic image of a semigroup arising from a proper pair. The proof of the theorem utilises the
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
construction from the previous section. Throughout the current section we comment on the special case of left restriction semigroups, and we end by showing how the classical Theorem
5.11
follows from our Theorem
5.39
.

Consider a weak action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
. As ever, we denote the action of
S
on
U^{1}
by
(s,u)\mapsto{}^{s}u
. We define

S^{+}=\{{s^{+}}:{s\in S}\}=\{{{}^{s}1}:{s\in S}\},

noting that this subset of
U^{1}
need not be a subsemigroup. In any case, we also define

P=P(U,S)=\langle S^{+}\rangle,

which is the subsemigroup of
U^{1}
generated by
S^{+}
. Since
S^{+}
consists of idempotents (cf. Lemma
3.1
(i)
), it follows that
P
is an idempotent-generated semigroup, and hence is a submonoid of
U^{1}
if and only if
1\in S^{+}
; indeed, the latter claim follows from
[
35
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Lemma 5.18
.

The subsemigroup
P
of
U^{1}
is closed under the action of
S
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
(ii)
,
S^{+}
is closed under the action of
S
; so too therefore is
\langle S^{+}\rangle=P
.
∎

Remark 5.19
.

By Lemma
5.18
(and consulting Definition
4.2
), it follows immediately that
(P,S)
is a weak action pair as well. If
(U,S)
is a (strong) action pair, then so too is
(P,S)
, by Lemma
4.18
(ii)
; when
(U,S)
is strong, Lemma
4.15
(ii)
gives
P=\{1\}
.

Remark 5.20
.

When
S
is a left restriction semigroup,
S^{+}=\langle S^{+}\rangle
is precisely the semilattice of projections
P(S)
. This explains our choice of notation for
P=P(U,S)
. Recall from Proposition
4.37
(and Remark
4.39
) that
(P,S)
is an action pair in
S
.

In order to give the definition of a proper action pair, we need an appropriate congruence to play the role of
\sigma
from Definition
5.7
.

Definition 5.21
.

Let
(U,S)
be a weak action pair in a monoid
M
, and write
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
. Define the relation

\displaystyle\kappa=\kappa(U,S)

\displaystyle=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{ps=qt\ (\exists p,q\in P)}\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{ps=qt\ (\exists p,q\in P^{1})}\big\},

and let
\sigma=\sigma(U,S)=\kappa^{\sharp}
be the congruence on
S
generated by
\kappa
.

Lemma 5.22
.

The relation
\kappa
is reflexive, symmetric and compatible. Consequently,
\sigma
is the transitive closure of
\kappa
.

Proof.

We just prove the first statement, as the second immediately follows. Reflexivity, symmetry and right-compatibility of
\sigma
are clear. For left-compatibility, let
(s,t)\in\kappa
and
x\in S
. So
ps=qt
for some
p,q\in P
.
By
(A1)
we have
{}^{x}p\cdot xs=x\cdot ps=x\cdot qt={}^{x}q\cdot xt
. Since
{}^{x}p,{}^{x}q\in P
, by Lemma
5.18
, it follows that
(xs,xt)\in\kappa
.
∎

The next result concerns a special case in which we have
\sigma=\kappa
. For the statement, recall
[
22
]
that a semigroup
T
is
right-reversible
if any two left ideals (equivalently, any two principal left ideals) of
T
have non-empty intersection: i.e., if for all
x,y\in T
, we have
ax=by
for some
a,b\in T
. Examples of right-reversible semigroups include semilattices (or in fact arbitrary commutative semigroups), or more generally left-regular bands; the latter satisfy the identity
xyx=xy
(and
x=x^{2}
).

Lemma 5.23
.

If
P
is right-reversible, then
\sigma=\kappa
.

Proof.

It suffices by Lemma
5.22
to show that
\kappa
is transitive (in this case). So suppose
s\mathrel{\kappa}t\mathrel{\kappa}x
for some
s,t,x\in S
. Then
ps=qt
and
p^{\prime}t=q^{\prime}x
for some
p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}\in P
. By right-reversibility we have
aq=bp^{\prime}
for some
a,b\in P
, and then
ap\cdot s=aqt=bp^{\prime}t=bq^{\prime}\cdot x
, with
ap,bq^{\prime}\in P
, so that
s\mathrel{\kappa}x
, as required.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

As a special case, when
S
is a left restriction semigroup,
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle=P(S)
is a semilattice, so
\sigma=\kappa
coincides with the relation
\sigma_{S}
from Definition
5.7
.

We can now give the definition of proper pairs and semigroups.

Definition 5.25
.

We say a weak action pair
(U,S)
is
proper
if for all
u,v\in U^{1}
and
s,t\in S
,

us=vt\hskip 20.00003pt\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 20.00003ptus^{+}=vt^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts\mathrel{\sigma}t.

(5.26)

If
(U,S)
is proper, the semigroup
US
is then said to be
(U,S)
-proper
.

The following simple fact will be used without explicit reference from now on:

Lemma 5.27
.

Any proper weak action pair is an action pair.

Proof.

We need to show that
(A2)
holds: i.e., that
us=vt\ \Rightarrow\ us^{+}=vt^{+}
for all
u,v\in U^{1}
and
s,t\in S
. But this clearly follows from (
5.26
).
∎

Remark 5.28
.

Consider a
strong
action pair
(U,S)
. Since
s^{+}=1
for all
s\in S
, by Lemma
4.15
(ii)
, we have
P=S^{+}=\{1\}
, and it follows immediately that
\kappa=\Delta_{S}
(the equality relation on
S
), and hence that
\sigma=\Delta_{S}
. Definition
5.25
then says that
(U,S)
is proper precisely when

us=vt\hskip 20.00003pt\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 20.00003ptu=v\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts=t\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U^{1}$ and $s,t\in S$.}

This then implies that the surmorphism
\pi:U\rtimes S\to US:(u,s)\mapsto us
from Proposition
4.46
is injective, and hence an isomorphism.

Figure
1
gives a Venn diagram displaying the various classes of pairs studied in the paper.

SAP
PAP
AP
WAP

Figure 1:
Venn diagram indicating inclusions among the classes of strong action pairs (SAP), proper action pairs (PAP), action pairs (AP) and weak action pairs (WAP).

Now that we have defined proper action pairs, we begin by showing that these are indeed the ‘correct’ generalisation of proper left restriction semigroups.

Proposition 5.29
.

If
S
is a left restriction semigroup with semilattice of projections
P=P(S)
, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

S
is proper, as in Definition
5.7
,

(ii)

(P,S)
is a proper action pair, as in Definition
5.25
.

Proof.

We have already noted in Remark
5.20
that
P=S^{+}=\langle S^{+}\rangle
in this case, and in Remark
5.24
that the definitions of
\sigma
from Definitions
5.7
and
5.21
coincide.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. Suppose first that
S
is proper, and let
u,v\in P^{1}
and
s,t\in S
. We must show that

\displaystyle us=vt\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 20.00003ptus^{+}=vt^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts\mathrel{\sigma}t.

(5.30)

Since
S
is proper, and since
us,vt\in S
, it follows from (
5.8
) that

\displaystyle us=vt\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 20.00003pt(us)^{+}=(vt)^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptus\mathrel{\sigma}vt.

(5.31)

By Lemma
4.36
, we have
{us^{+}=vt^{+}\ \Leftrightarrow\ (us)^{+}=(vt)^{+}}
. It also follows quickly from the definition of
\sigma
(in Definition
5.7
) that
s\mathrel{\sigma}t\ \Leftrightarrow\ us\mathrel{\sigma}vt
, keeping in mind the fact that
P
is a semilattice. Equivalence of (
5.30
) and (
5.31
) is now clear.

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
. Conversely, suppose
(P,S)
is proper, and let
s,t\in S
. We must show that

s=t\hskip 20.00003pt\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 20.00003pts^{+}=t^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts\mathrel{\sigma}t.

As the forwards implication is clear, suppose
s^{+}=t^{+}
and
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
. Then with
u=v=s^{+}=t^{+}\in P
, we have
us^{+}=s^{+}=t^{+}=vt^{+}
. Since
(P,S)
is proper, and since also
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
, it follows from (
5.26
) that
us=vt
: i.e.,
s=t
.
∎

The next result shows that when the action pair
(U,S)
is proper, and when
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
satisfies a certain kind of semigroup identity, the congruence
\sigma
can be described equationally, rather than by asserting the existence of suitable elements of
P
.

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
(U,S)
be a proper action pair, and suppose
P
satisfies a semigroup identity of the form
f(x,y)x=g(x,y)y
, where
f(x,y),g(x,y)\in\{x,y\}^{*}
. Then

\sigma=\kappa=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{f(s^{+},t^{+})s=g(s^{+},t^{+})t}\big\}.

Proof.

The identity implies that
P
is right-reversible, so Lemma
5.23
gives
\sigma=\kappa
. For the rest of the proof we write

\sigma^{\prime}=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{f(s^{+},t^{+})s=g(s^{+},t^{+})t}\big\}.

Since
f(s^{+},t^{+}),g(s^{+},t^{+})\in P^{1}
for any
s,t\in S
, we clearly have
\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma
.

Conversely, suppose
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
. Since
s^{+},t^{+}\in P
, we have
f(s^{+},t^{+})\cdot s^{+}=g(s^{+},t^{+})\cdot t^{+}
. Combining this with
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
, it follows from (
5.26
) that
f(s^{+},t^{+})\cdot s=g(s^{+},t^{+})\cdot t
, whence
s\mathrel{\sigma}^{\prime}t
.
∎

Remark 5.33
.

Since semilattices satisfy the identity
xy=yx
, it follows from Lemma
5.32
that

\displaystyle\sigma=\kappa

\displaystyle=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{t^{+}s=s^{+}t}\big\}

when
P
is a semilattice. Similarly,

\displaystyle\sigma=\kappa

\displaystyle=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{s^{+}t^{+}s=s^{+}t}\big\}

if
P
is a left-regular band.

If
P
satisfies an identity of the form
f(x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})x=g(x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})y
, then one can arbitrarily substitute
x
and/or
y
in place of the
z_{i}
to obtain an identity of the form
{f^{\prime}(x,y)x=g^{\prime}(x,y)y}
, and Lemma
5.32
then applies.

In Sections
5.4
–
5.7
we will be concerned with proper action pairs
(U,S)
, when
U
and
S
are both submonoids of the over-monoid
M
. The
\sigma
-class of the identity of
S
will play an important role on many occasions during these sections. The next lemma characterises the elements of this class, and shows that they act on
U^{1}
in a particularly simple way. In the statement, we do not assume that
U
is a submonoid of
M
, and we use the notation
\hat{1}
for consistency with later use.

Lemma 5.34
.

Let
(U,S)
be a proper action pair, with
S
a submonoid of the over-monoid
M
, and let
\hat{1}
be the
\sigma
-class of
1\in S
.

(i)

We have
\hat{1}=S\cap S^{+}=\{{s\in S}:{s=s^{+}}\}
.

(ii)

For any
s\in\hat{1}
, and for any
u\in U^{1}
, we have
{}^{s}u=su=s^{+}u
.

Proof.

(i)
. We prove this part by showing that for all
s\in S
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

s\mathrel{\sigma}1
,

(b)

s\in S^{+}
,

(c)

s=s^{+}
.

(a)
\ \Rightarrow\
(c)
. Taking
u=t=1
and
v=s^{+}
, we have
us^{+}=vt^{+}
and
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
. Since
(U,S)
is proper, it follows from (
5.26
) that
us=vt
: i.e.,
s=s^{+}
.

(c)
\ \Rightarrow\
(b)
. This is obvious.

(b)
\ \Rightarrow\
(a)
. Suppose
s=t^{+}
for some
t\in S
. Then since
1\cdot s=t^{+}\cdot 1
, with
1,t^{+}\in S^{+}\subseteq P
, it follows that
(s,1)\in\kappa\subseteq\sigma
; cf. Definition
5.21
.

(ii)
. We have
s=s^{+}
by part
(i)
, so of course
su=s^{+}u
. Combining
s=s^{+}
with
(A1)
and Lemma
3.1
(iv)
, we have
su={}^{s}u\cdot s={}^{s}u\cdot s^{+}={}^{s}u
.
∎

The next lemma provides a handy criterion for an action pair to be proper. For the statement, we say that a subsemigroup
V
of a semigroup
T
is
left-dense
in
T
if the following condition holds:

\displaystyle(\forall t\in T)\ (\exists a\in V)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptat\in V.

(5.35)

Note, for example, that any right ideal is left-dense, but the converse need not hold. (The term ‘left-dense’ is taken from
[
7
, p. 98]
, where the meaning is slightly different. There ‘
(\exists a\in V)
’ is replaced by ‘
(\exists a\in T)
’, and this results in a weaker condition. For example, take
T
to be an arbitrary group,
V
a proper subgroup, and
t\in T\setminus V
; any
a\in T
such that
at\in V
necessarily belongs to
T\setminus V
.) Before stating the lemma, we claim that left-density is equivalent to the ostensibly stronger condition:

\displaystyle(\forall s,t\in T^{1})\ (\exists a\in V)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptas,at\in V.

(5.36)

Obviously (
5.36
) implies (
5.35
). For the converse, suppose (
5.35
) holds, and let
s,t\in T^{1}
. There exists
c\in V
such that
ct\in V
(this is obvious if
t=1
, or follows from (
5.35
) otherwise). Since certainly
cs\in T
, there also exists
b\in V
such that
b\cdot cs\in V
. We then have
as,at\in V
for
a=bc\in V
.
(The weaker version of left-density from
[
7
]
does not imply the corresponding version of (
5.36
), as seen again with
T
a group,
V
a proper subgroup,
s\in V
and
t\in T\setminus V
.)

Lemma 5.37
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is an action pair.

(i)

If
P
is left-dense in
U
, then

us=vt\hskip 20.00003pt\Rightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptus^{+}=vt^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts\mathrel{\sigma}t\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U^{1}$ and $s,t\in S$.}

(ii)

If
\sigma=\Delta_{S}
, then

us=vt\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftarrow\hskip 20.00003ptus^{+}=vt^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts\mathrel{\sigma}t\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U^{1}$ and $s,t\in S$.}

(iii)

If
P
is left-dense in
U
, and if
\sigma=\Delta_{S}
, then
(U,S)
is proper.

Proof.

It clearly suffices to prove
(i)
and
(ii)
. For these, let
u,v\in U^{1}
and
s,t\in S
.

(i)
. Suppose
us=vt
. It follows from
(A2)
that
us^{+}=vt^{+}
. By (
5.36
) there exists
p\in P
such that
pu,pv\in P
, and it then follows from
pus=pvt
that
(s,t)\in\kappa\subseteq\sigma
.

(ii)
. Suppose
us^{+}=vt^{+}
and
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
. Since
\sigma=\Delta_{S}
, we have
s=t
. Combining this with
(

+

1)
, it follows that
us=us^{+}\cdot s=vt^{+}\cdot t=vt
. ∎

Consider again semigroups
S
and
U
for which
S
has a left action on
U^{1}
by semigroup morphisms, and assume that
S
acts monoidally if it happens to be monoid; cf. Assumption
5.13
. As in Section
5.2
, we have the semidirect products
U\rtimes S
and
U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}
, and the subsemigroups

\underline{U}=\{{\underline{u}}:{u\in U}\}\and\overline{S}=\{{\overline{s}}:{s\in S}\}

of
U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}
, where
\underline{u}=(u,1)
and
\overline{s}=(s^{+},s)
, for
u\in U
and
s\in S
. We showed in Proposition
5.16
that
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
is an action pair in the monoid

M=\big\{{(u,s)\in U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}}:{u=us^{+}}\big\},

and the next result takes this further. For the proof, we use the fact that
\overline{s}^{+}=\underline{s^{+}}
for
s\in S
; cf. (
5.17
). Together with the fact that
S\to\overline{S}:s\mapsto\overline{s}
is an isomorphism, it follows that

\overline{s}_{1}^{+}\cdots\overline{s}_{k}^{+}=\underline{s_{1}^{+}\cdots s_{k}^{+}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in S$.}

Proposition 5.38
.

Given Assumption
5.13
, and with the above notation, we have
\sigma(\underline{U},\overline{S})=\Delta_{\overline{S}}
. Moreover, the action pair
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
is proper, so the semigroup

\underline{U}\cdot\overline{S}=\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}

is
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
-proper.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we write
\kappa=\kappa(\underline{U},\overline{S})
and
\sigma=\sigma(\underline{U},\overline{S})
, and also

P=P(\underline{U},\overline{S})=\langle\overline{S}^{+}\rangle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{S}^{+}=\{{\overline{s}^{+}}:{s\in S}\}=\{{\underline{s^{+}}}:{s\in S}\}.

To show that
\sigma=\Delta_{\overline{S}}
we must show that
\kappa=\Delta_{\overline{S}}
, so suppose
\overline{s}\mathrel{\kappa}\overline{t}
for some
s,t\in S
. By definition, this means that
{\bf p}\cdot\overline{s}={\bf q}\cdot\overline{t}
for some
{\bf p},{\bf q}\in P
. Now,
{\bf p}=\overline{s}_{1}^{+}\cdots\overline{s}_{k}^{+}=\underline{s_{1}^{+}\cdots s_{k}^{+}}
for some
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in S
, so that
{\bf p}=\underline{u}
, where
u=s_{1}^{+}\cdots s_{k}^{+}\in U^{1}
. Similarly,
{\bf q}=\underline{v}
for some
v\in U^{1}
. But then

(us^{+},s)={\bf p}\cdot\overline{s}={\bf q}\cdot\overline{t}=(vt^{+},t).

It follows from this that
s=t
, whence
\overline{s}=\overline{t}
, and this completes the proof that
\kappa=\Delta_{\overline{S}}
.

Given Proposition
5.16
, it remains to show that
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
is proper: i.e., that

\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}\hskip 20.00003pt\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 20.00003pt\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}^{+}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{s}\mathrel{\sigma}\overline{t}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U^{1}$ and $s,t\in S$.}

The backwards implication follows from Lemma
5.37
(ii)
, as
\sigma(\underline{U},\overline{S})=\Delta_{\overline{S}}
. For the forwards implication, suppose
\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}
. Since
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
is an action pair, it follows from
(A2)
that
\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}^{+}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}^{+}
. Also,
(us^{+},s)=\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}=\underline{v}\cdot\overline{t}=(vt^{+},t)
gives
s=t
, so certainly
\overline{s}\mathrel{\sigma}\overline{t}
.
∎

We can now give the following covering theorem, which is the main result of this section. In essence, it says that any semigroup
US
arising from a weak action pair
(U,S)
is a homomorphic image of a semigroup
U^{\prime}S^{\prime}
arising from a
proper
action pair
(U^{\prime},S^{\prime})
, with
U^{\prime}\cong U
and
S^{\prime}\cong S
.

Theorem 5.39
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a weak action pair in a monoid
M
.

(i)

There exists a proper action pair
(U^{\prime},S^{\prime})
in a monoid
M^{\prime}
, with
U^{\prime}\cong U
and
S^{\prime}\cong S
, and a surmorphism
\psi:U^{\prime}S^{\prime}\to US
.

(ii)

If
S
is a submonoid of
M
, then such a pair and surmorphism exist for which:

•

S^{\prime}
is a submonoid of
M^{\prime}
,

•

U^{\prime}
is a subsemigroup of
U^{\prime}S^{\prime}
, and

•

the restriction
\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{U^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism
U^{\prime}\to U
.

(iii)

If
s^{+}\in U
for all
s\in S
(including the case that
U
is a submonoid of
M
), then such a pair and surmorphism exist for which:

•

S^{\prime}
is a subsemigroup of
U^{\prime}S^{\prime}
, and

•

the restriction
\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{S^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism
S^{\prime}\to S
.

Proof.

(i)
. Since
(U,S)
is a weak action pair,
S
(and hence also
S^{1}
) acts on
U^{1}
, and
S^{1}
acts monoidally (cf. Remark
4.4
). We then take

M^{\prime}=\big\{{(u,s)\in U^{1}\rtimes S^{1}}:{u=us^{+}}\big\},\ \ \ U^{\prime}=\underline{U}=\{{\underline{u}}:{u\in U}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptS^{\prime}=\overline{S}=\{{\overline{s}}:{s\in S}\},

as above. By Lemma
5.14
, we have
U^{\prime}\cong U
and
S^{\prime}\cong S
. By Proposition
5.38
,
(U^{\prime},S^{\prime})
is a proper action pair in
M^{\prime}
, and

U^{\prime}S^{\prime}=\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}.

Define the map
\psi:U^{\prime}S^{\prime}\to US:(u,s)\mapsto us
. Since this is the restriction to
U^{\prime}S^{\prime}
of the morphism
\pi:U\rtimes S\to US
from Proposition
4.46
, it follows that
\psi
is a morphism.
To demonstrate surjectivity, let
x\in US
, so that
x=us
for some
u\in U
and
s\in S
. As in Remark
4.4
, we have
s=s^{+}s
, and so
(\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s})\psi=(us^{+},s)\psi=us^{+}\cdot s=us=x
, with
\underline{u}\cdot\overline{s}\in U^{\prime}S^{\prime}
.

(ii)
. Suppose
S
is a submonoid of
M
. Clearly then
S^{\prime}=\overline{S}
is a submonoid of
M^{\prime}
, so that
{U^{\prime}=U^{\prime}\cdot\overline{1}\leq U^{\prime}S^{\prime}}
. Since
\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{U^{\prime}}
maps each
\underline{u}=(u,1)\mapsto u\cdot 1=u
, it is clearly an isomorphism
U^{\prime}\to U
.

(iii)
. The stated assumption ensures that
U^{\prime}S^{\prime}
contains each
\overline{s}=(s^{+},s)
, with
s\in S
: i.e., that
S^{\prime}\leq U^{\prime}S^{\prime}
. Since
\psi
maps each
\overline{s}=(s^{+},s)\mapsto s^{+}s=s
, the final assertion follows.
∎

Remark 5.40
.

We have already noted that a semigroup
US
arising from a (weak) action pair
(U,S)
need not contain
U
itself, or even an isomorphic copy of
U
; see Example
4.32
(iii)
. Moreover,
U^{\prime}
is not contained in
U^{\prime}S^{\prime}
(in the above construction) if
S
is not a submonoid of
M
. Thus,
S
being a submonoid is a necessary assumption in part
(ii)
of Theorem
5.39
, at least for the particular choice of
U^{\prime}=\underline{U}
and
S^{\prime}=\overline{S}
used in the proof.

In part
(iii)
, note that the stronger assumption of
U
being a submonoid of
M
implies that
U^{\prime}
is a submonoid of
M^{\prime}
.

As an application of Theorem
5.39
, we show how to deduce Theorem
5.11
. We begin with the case of left restriction
monoids
:

Corollary 5.41

(cf.
[
9
, Theorem 6.4]
)
.

Every left restriction monoid has a proper cover.

Proof.

Consider a left restriction monoid
M
, and the action pair
(P,M)
in
M
, where
P
is the semilattice of projections (cf. Proposition
4.37
). Let
(P^{\prime},M^{\prime})=(\underline{P},\overline{M})
be the proper action pair constructed in the proof of Theorem
5.39
, and let

C=P^{\prime}M^{\prime}=\big\{{(u,s)\in P\rtimes M}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}.

So
\psi:C\to PM=M:(u,s)\mapsto us
is a (monoid) surmorphism, and
\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{P^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism
{P^{\prime}\to P}
.

It is routine to check that
C
is left restriction under the operation
(u,s)^{+}=(u,1)
. Thus,
P(C)=\big\{{(u,1)}:{u\in P}\big\}=\{{\underline{u}}:{u\in P}\}=P^{\prime}
. Consequently,
\psi
is projection-separating, since
\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{P^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism.

It remains to check that
\psi
respects the unary operations of
S
and
C
. But for any
(u,s)\in C
we use Lemma
4.36
to calculate

(u,s)^{+}\psi=(u,1)\psi=u=us^{+}=(us)^{+}=((u,s)\psi)^{+},

as required.
∎

We now show how the general case follows.

Proof of Theorem
5.11
.

Let
S
be a left restriction semigroup. If
S
is a monoid, then Corollary
5.41
applies, so we assume otherwise. Let
M=S^{1}=S\sqcup\{1\}
, so that
M
is also left restriction (where we additionally define
1^{+}=1
). Let
C=P^{\prime}M^{\prime}
be the proper cover of
M
from the proof of Corollary
5.41
, with covering morphism
\psi:C\to M:(u,s)\mapsto us
. Since
M\setminus\{1\}=S\leq M
, is follows that

{\bf c}\psi=1\ \Leftrightarrow\ {\bf c}={\bf 1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf c}\in C$,}

(5.42)

where we write
{\bf 1}=(1,1)
for the identity of
C
.

Now we let
D=\{{{\bf c}\in C}:{{\bf c}^{+}\not={\bf 1}}\}
. By Lemma
4.42
(iii)
,
D
is a subsemigroup of
C
. Moreover,
D
is closed under
+
, as

{\bf c}\in D\ \Rightarrow\ ({\bf c}^{+})^{+}={\bf c}^{+}\not={\bf 1}\ \Rightarrow\ {\bf c}^{+}\in D,

by
(L6)
. It follows that
D
is a proper left restriction semigroup. By (
5.42
), the restriction
\Psi=\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{D}
maps
D
into
S=M\setminus\{1\}
, as

{\bf c}\psi=1\ \Rightarrow\ 1=1^{+}=({\bf c}\psi)^{+}={\bf c}^{+}\psi\ \Rightarrow\ {\bf c}^{+}={\bf 1}\ \Rightarrow\ {\bf c}\not\in D.

In fact,
\Psi:D\to S
is surjective, since for any
s\in S
we have
s={\bf c}\psi
for some
{\bf c}\in C
, and we must in fact have
{\bf c}\in D
; indeed, if
{\bf c}^{+}={\bf 1}
, then we would have
1={\bf 1}\psi={\bf c}^{+}\psi=({\bf c}\psi)^{+}=s^{+}
, contradicting the fact that
S=M\setminus\{1\}
is closed under
+
. Thus,
\Psi
is a surmorphism
D\to S
, and it is projection-separating since
\psi
is.
∎

Remark 5.43
.

Consider an
inverse
monoid
M
, regarded as a left restriction monoid under
s^{+}=ss^{-1}
. So
P=P(M)=E(M)
is the semilattice of (all) idempotents of
M
; as usual, we denote this semilattice by
E
. Let
C
and
\psi
be as in the proof of Corollary
5.41
, so that

C=\big\{{(e,s)\in E\rtimes M}:{e=es^{+}}\big\}=\big\{{(e,s)\in E\rtimes M}:{e\leq s^{+}}\big\}

is a proper left restriction monoid under
(e,s)^{+}=(e,1)
, and
\psi:C\to M:(e,s)\mapsto es
is a covering morphism. (In the above,
\leq
is the
natural partial order
on
M
, defined by
s\leq t\ \Leftrightarrow\ s\in Et
. Note that for
e,f\in E
we have
e\leq f\ \Leftrightarrow\ e=ef
.) It is routine to show the following:

•

C
is inverse, with inversion given by
(e,s)^{-1}=({}^{s^{-1}}e,s^{-1})
,

•

E(C)=\big\{{(e,s)}:{e,s\in E,\ e=es}\big\}=\big\{{(e,s)}:{e,s\in E,\ e\leq s}\big\}
.

(To show that
C
is inverse, one shows that it is regular, and
E(C)
is commutative. To verify the above claim about
(e,s)^{-1}
, one needs to note that

(e,s)\in C\ \Rightarrow\ e=es^{+}\ \Rightarrow\ {}^{s}({}^{s^{-1}}e)={}^{ss^{-1}}e={}^{s^{+}}e=s^{+}e(s^{+})^{-1}=s^{+}es^{+}=es^{+}s^{+}=e.)

We note, however, that
C
is not necessarily a proper inverse monoid, even though it is a proper left restriction monoid. In fact, one can easily show that
C
is a proper inverse monoid if and only if
S
is a proper inverse monoid.

The key point here is that
P(C)=\big\{{(e,1)}:{e\in E}\big\}
is generally not equal to
E(C)
, so that
(E(C),C)
might not be a proper action pair, even though
(P(C),C)
always is; cf. Proposition
4.37
. (In fact,
E(C)=P(C)\ \Leftrightarrow\ E=\{1\}
, as
(e,e)\in E(C)\setminus P(C)
for any
e\in E\setminus\{1\}
.) Similarly,
\psi
is generally not idempotent-separating, even though it is projection-separating, as we have
(e,s)\psi=e
for any idempotent
(e,s)
of
C
. (So again,
\psi
is idempotent-separating if and only if
E=\{1\}
. As we have noted on a number of occasions,
E=\{1\}
is equivalent to
M
being a group.)

5.4
Embedding theorems for proper monoids I

In this section, and the subsequent three sections, we turn to the task of finding an appropriate generalisation of Theorem
5.12
in the context of proper action pairs. That is, we consider a semigroup
US
arising from a proper pair
(U,S)
, and seek to embed
US
in a semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
, where
\mathcal{U}
is some semigroup containing
U
, and where
\sigma
is the congruence on
S
given in Definition
5.21
. For technical reasons, we will need to assume that
U
and
S
are both submonoids of the over-monoid
M
. However, this does not end up hampering us, in the sense that we can still derive (the full semigroup version of) Theorem
5.12
as a corollary.

The main result of the current section is Theorem
5.47
. This states that a
(U,S)
-proper monoid
M=US
embeds in a suitable semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
, under the assumption that the submonoid
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
of
U
(see Definition
5.21
) is central in
U
. This centrality condition is quite natural. For example, when
M
is a proper left restriction monoid, arising from the proper action pair
(P,M)
, we have already observed that
P=U
is the semilattice of projections of
M
, and is therefore commutative, and hence central in itself. After proving Theorem
5.47
, we deduce the monoid version of Theorem
5.12
in Corollary
5.58
, and then show that the general case follows.

The centrality assumption just discussed is in fact not necessary to obtain an embedding of the desired kind. Indeed, in the next section we prove Theorem
5.60
, which is essentially the same as Theorem
5.47
but with the centrality assumption removed. We do, however, have strong motivation for stating and proving the two separate embedding results. First, the construction of the monoid
\mathcal{U}
is somewhat more abstract in the general case. By contrast, the monoid
\mathcal{U}
used in the current section is more transparent, and the corresponding semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
is in fact an (unrestricted) wreath product
I\operatorname{wr}{}(S/\sigma)
, in the sense of
[
105
,
90
]
, for a suitable monoid
I
, as we explain in a little more detail below. Another advantage of the current construction is that when
M
is a proper left restriction monoid,
\mathcal{U}
is easily seen to be a semilattice, and as noted above this allows us to deduce Theorem
5.12
as a corollary.

In this section, and the next three, an important role will be played by certain special factorisations of elements of a semigroup
US
arising from a (weak) action pair
(U,S)
:

Definition 5.44
.

Let
(U,S)
be a weak action pair. A
natural factorisation
for an element
a\in US
is a pair
(u,s)\in U\times S
such that
a=us
and
u=us^{+}
.

The next result lists some special properties of these factorisations. Note that we can think of a natural factorisation of
a\in US
as an element of the semigroup

\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}

that maps to
a
under the surmorphism
\pi:U\rtimes S\to US
from Proposition
4.46
; cf. Remark
4.47
. The first two parts of the next result can be proved with reference to this viewpoint, but we give simple direct proofs for convenience.

Lemma 5.45
.

Let
(U,S)
be a weak action pair.

(i)

Every element of
US
has a natural factorisation.

(ii)

If
(u,s)
and
(v,t)
are natural factorisations of elements
a
and
b
of
US
, respectively, then
(u\cdot{}^{s}v,st)
is a natural factorisation of
ab
.

(iii)

If
(U,S)
is an action pair, and if
(u,s)
and
(v,t)
are natural factorisations for the same element of
US
, then
u=v
.

(iv)

If
(U,S)
is a proper action pair, and if
(u,s)
and
(v,t)
are natural factorisations for the same element of
US
, then
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
.

Proof.

(i)
. Let
a\in US
. We certainly have
a=vs
for some
v\in U
and
s\in S
, and we take
u=vs^{+}
. Then since
s=s^{+}s
(cf. Remark
4.4
) we have
us=vs^{+}s=vs=a
, while
us^{+}=vs^{+}s^{+}=vs^{+}=u
, by Lemma
3.1
(i)
.

(ii)
. We obtain
ab=u\cdot{}^{s}v\cdot st
right from
(A1)
. Combining Lemma
3.1
(ii)
with
v=vt^{+}
, we have

u\cdot{}^{s}v\cdot(st)^{+}=u\cdot{}^{s}v\cdot{}^{s}(t^{+})=u\cdot{}^{s}(vt^{+})=u\cdot{}^{s}v.

(iii)
. Suppose
us=vt
, where
(u,s)
and
(v,t)
are natural. By
(A2)
we have
us^{+}=vt^{+}
, and since
u=us^{+}
and
v=vt^{+}
(by naturality), it follows that
u=v
.

(iv)
. This follows immediately from (
5.26
).
∎

Remark 5.46
.

On many occasions, Lemma
5.45
will allow us to define a morphism
\psi
whose domain
US
arises from a proper action pair
(U,S)
. Given
a\in US
, parts
(i)
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
allow us to unambiguously define
a\psi
, by explaining how to construct it from
u
and the
\sigma
-class of
s
for
any
natural factorisation
(u,s)
of
a
. Part
(ii)
will be useful in showing that such a map
\psi
is a morphism.

We are now almost ready to state our first embedding theorem. The theorem concerns a proper action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
, where
U
and
S
are both submonoids. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
M=US
, so that
M
is
(U,S)
-proper. We continue to use the notation
S^{+}
,
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
and
\sigma=\kappa^{\sharp}
from Section
5.3
. So
P
is an idempotent-generated submonoid of
U
, and
\sigma
is a congruence on
S
.
For
s\in S
we write
\hat{s}
for the
\sigma
-class of
s
.

As we have already noted, the following theorem concerns the case in which
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
is central in
U
, meaning that
pu=up
for all
p\in P
and
u\in U
. This is of course equivalent to the generating set
S^{+}
being central in
U
.

Theorem 5.47
.

Let
M=US
be a
(U,S)
-proper monoid, and suppose
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
is central in
U
. Then there exists a semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
and an embedding
\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}
such that:

(i)

\mathcal{U}
contains a subsemigroup
U^{\prime}
isomorphic to
U
,

(ii)

\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{U}:U\to U^{\prime}\rtimes\{\hat{1}\}
is an isomorphism,

(iii)

\mathcal{U}
is commutative if
U
is commutative.

We build towards the proof of the theorem with a series of preliminary results. For the remainder of this section, we assume that
M=US
is a
(U,S)
-proper monoid (with
U
and
S
submonoids), and that
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
is central in
U
.

We begin with a result that gives a convenient equational formulation of
\sigma
.

Lemma 5.48
.

The monoid
P
is a semilattice, and consequently
\sigma=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{t^{+}s=s^{+}t}\big\}
.

Proof.

Commutativity of
P
follows from centrality. Since every element of
S^{+}
is an idempotent (cf. Lemma
3.1
(i)
), so too therefore is every element of
P
. The assertion regarding
\sigma
follows from Lemma
5.32
; cf. Remark
5.33
.
∎

In what follows, it will be convenient to write

\mathcal{S}=S/\sigma=\{{\hat{s}}:{s\in S}\}.

So we must construct a monoid
\mathcal{U}
, a semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
, and a suitable embedding
{\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}}
. The monoid
\mathcal{U}
will in fact be of the form
I^{\mathcal{S}}
for some monoid
I
, and then
\mathcal{U}\rtimes S
will be an
unrestricted wreath product

I\operatorname{wr}\mathcal{S}=I^{\mathcal{S}}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
in the sense of
[
105
,
90
]
. Since we will be studying a different kind of (transformational) wreath product in Chapter
9
, and since we wish to emphasise the monoid
\mathcal{U}
itself in our construction (rather than
I
), we will not speak explicitly of (any kind of) wreath products in the current chapter. However, in order to prepare the reader for our construction we briefly recall the definition of the unrestricted wreath product
V\operatorname{wr}T
, for semigroups
V
and
T
. First,
V^{T}
denotes as usual the semigroup of all functions
T\to V
under componentwise product, which we denote by
\star
. Given
f,g\in V^{T}
, the product
f\star g\in V^{T}
is defined by
x(f\star g)=xf\cdot xg
for
x\in T
(with the product
xf\cdot xg
taken in
V
). Next,
T
has a left action on
V^{T}
by semigroup morphisms, given by
(t,f)\mapsto{}^{t}\!f
, where the latter is defined by
x({}^{t}\!f)=(xt)f
for
x\in T
. This allows for the formation of the semidirect product
V^{T}\rtimes T
, as in Definition
3.4
, and this is taken as the definition of
V\operatorname{wr}T
. As noted above, we will no longer refer explicitly to this kind of wreath product, even though the semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes S
will be of this form.

The construction of
\mathcal{U}
begins with the
power semigroup
of
U
:

{\mathcal{P}}(U)=\{{V}:{V\subseteq U}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with set product}\hskip 20.00003ptV\cdot W=\{{vw}:{v\in V,\ w\in W}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $V,W\subseteq U$.}

(5.49)

We then take
I
to be the principal ideal of
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
generated by
P
. Since
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
is a monoid with identity
\{1\}
, the ideal
I={\mathcal{P}}(U)\cdot P\cdot{\mathcal{P}}(U)
consists of all subsets of
U
of the form
V\cdot P\cdot W
, with
V,W\subseteq U
. In fact, since
P
is a central submonoid of
U
, we have

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle={\mathcal{P}}(U)\cdot P=P\cdot{\mathcal{P}}(U)=\{{V\cdot P=P\cdot V}:{V\subseteq U}\},

and moreover

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=P\cdot{\mathcal{P}}(U)\cdot P=\{{V\in{\mathcal{P}}(U)}:{V=V\cdot P=P\cdot V}\}.

Thus,
I
is a (local) monoid with identity
P
(and zero
\varnothing
).
We note, however, that
I
is generally not a
submonoid
of
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
, as the respective identities of these monoids are
P
and
\{1\}
.

We now let

\mathcal{U}=I^{\mathcal{S}}

be the set of all functions
\mathcal{S}\to I
. We denote the componentwise product in
\mathcal{U}
by
\star
. So for
f,g\in\mathcal{U}
, we have
f\star g:\mathcal{S}\to I
, defined by

\hat{x}(f\star g)=\hat{x}f\cdot\hat{x}g\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for each $x\in S$.}

Note here that
\hat{x}f
and
\hat{x}g
are both elements of
I
, and in particular of
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
, so
\hat{x}f\cdot\hat{x}g
denotes the set product in
I
. It is easy to see that
\star
is associative, so that
\mathcal{U}
is a semigroup. In fact,
\mathcal{U}
is a monoid, whose identity
{\bf 1}
is the constant map with image
\{P\}
: i.e.,
\hat{x}{\bf 1}=P
for all
x\in S
.

The first part of the next result verifies the third item in Theorem
5.47
. The second part concerns the special case in which
U=P
; since
P
is central in
U
, certainly
U=P
is commutative in this case.

Lemma 5.50
.

(i)

If
U
is commutative, then so too is
\mathcal{U}
.

(ii)

If
U=P
(is commutative), then
U
and
\mathcal{U}
are both semilattices.

Proof.

(i)
. If
U
is commutative, then obviously
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
is as well. But then so too is its subsemigroup
I
, and hence also
\mathcal{U}=I^{\mathcal{S}}
.

(ii)
. We already observed in Lemma
5.48
that
P
is a semilattice. Since
\mathcal{U}=I^{\mathcal{S}}
, it is enough to show that
I
is a semilattice. An arbitrary element of
I
is a set of the form
P\cdot V\cdot P
for some
V\subseteq U=P
. These sets are precisely the ideals of
P
, and since the set product of two ideals of a semilattice is their intersection, we see that
I
is the
\cap
-semilattice of ideals of
P
.
∎

To define the semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
, we need an action of
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathcal{U}
, and this is also componentwise. For
s\in S
and
f\in\mathcal{U}
, we define
{}^{\hat{s}}\!f\in\mathcal{U}
by

\hat{x}({}^{\hat{s}}\!f)=(\hat{x}\hat{s})f\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $x\in S$.}

Clearly
{}^{\hat{s}}\!f
is well defined: i.e., the above definition does not depend on the choice of representative
s
of the
\sigma
-class
\hat{s}
. It is also a routine matter to check that we have a monoidal action by monoid morphisms: i.e., that

{}^{\hat{s}}({}^{\hat{t}}\!f)={}^{\hat{s}\hat{t}}\!f,\hskip 20.00003pt{}^{\hat{s}}(f\star g)={}^{\hat{s}}\!f\star{}^{\hat{s}}\!g,\hskip 20.00003pt{}^{\hat{1}}\!f=f\and{}^{\hat{s}}{\bf 1}={\bf 1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $s,t\in S$ and $f,g\in\mathcal{U}$.}

We may therefore form the semidirect product

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}=\big\{{(f,\hat{s})}:{f\in\mathcal{U},\ s\in S}\big\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with operation}\hskip 20.00003pt(f,\hat{s})\cdot(g,\hat{t})=(f\star{}^{\hat{s}}\!g,\hat{s}\hat{t}).

By Lemma
3.11
(iii)
,
\mathcal{M}
is a monoid with identity
({\bf 1},\hat{1})
. (As noted above, our monoid
\mathcal{M}
is an unrestricted wreath product
I\wr\mathcal{S}=I^{\mathcal{S}}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
.)

Now that we have defined the monoid
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes S
, we wish to construct an embedding
{\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}}
. As in Remark
5.46
,
\psi
will be defined in terms of natural factorisations. Specifically, given a natural factorisation
(u,s)
for an element
a\in M
, we will define
a\psi=(f_{u},\hat{s})
, for a certain
f_{u}\in\mathcal{U}
. We now turn to the definition of these elements
f_{u}
, and establish the important properties of the mapping
u\mapsto f_{u}
.

To this end, let
u\in U
. For
s\in S
, we define the subset

V_{\hat{s},u}=\{{{}^{t}u}:{t\in\hat{s}}\}\subseteq U.

(5.51)

We then define the function
f_{u}\in\mathcal{U}
by

\hat{s}f_{u}=V_{\hat{s},u}\cdot P\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $s\in S$.}

Finally, we define the map

\phi:U\to\mathcal{U}:u\mapsto f_{u}.

(5.52)

Lemma 5.53
.

The map
\phi:U\to\mathcal{U}
is a monomorphism.

Proof.

We begin by showing that
\phi
is injective. To do so, suppose
f_{u}=f_{v}
for some
u,v\in U
. In particular,
\hat{1}f_{u}=\hat{1}f_{v}
, so that

u={}^{1}u\cdot 1\in V_{\hat{1},u}\cdot P=\hat{1}f_{u}=\hat{1}f_{v}=V_{\hat{1},v}\cdot P.

It follows that
u={}^{t}v\cdot p
for some
t\in\hat{1}
and
p\in P
. Combining this with Lemma
5.34
(ii)
, and centrality of
P
, it follows that
u=t^{+}v\cdot p=t^{+}p\cdot v\in Pv
, as
t^{+}p\in P
. By symmetry,
v\in Pu
. Since
P
is a semilattice (cf. Lemma
5.48
), it follows from Lemma
2.5
that
u=v
, as required.

To show that
\phi
is a morphism, suppose
u,v\in U
. We must show that
f_{uv}=f_{u}\star f_{v}
: i.e., that

\displaystyle\hat{s}f_{uv}

\displaystyle=\hat{s}f_{u}\cdot\hat{s}f_{v}

for all
s\in S
.

By definition, and using centrality of
P
, this reduces to showing that

\displaystyle V_{\hat{s},uv}\cdot P

\displaystyle=V_{\hat{s},u}\cdot V_{\hat{s},v}\cdot P

for all
s\in S
.

(5.54)

For the fowards inclusion, let
x\in V_{\hat{s},uv}\cdot P
. Then
x={}^{t}(uv)\cdot p
for some
t\in\hat{s}
and
p\in P
, and so

x={}^{t}u\cdot{}^{t}v\cdot p\in V_{\hat{s},u}\cdot V_{\hat{s},v}\cdot P.

Conversely, let
x\in V_{\hat{s},u}\cdot V_{\hat{s},v}\cdot P
, so that
x={}^{t}u\cdot{}^{z}v\cdot p
for some
t,z\in\hat{s}
and
p\in P
. Since
t\mathrel{\sigma}z
(as
t,z\in\hat{s}
), it follows from Lemma
5.48
that
z^{+}\cdot t=t^{+}\cdot z
. Lemma
4.25
then gives

z^{+}\cdot{}^{t}v=t^{+}\cdot{}^{z}v.

(5.55)

But then

\displaystyle x={}^{t}u\cdot{}^{z}v\cdot p

\displaystyle={}^{t}u\cdot t^{+}\cdot{}^{z}v\cdot p

by Lemma
3.1
(iv)

\displaystyle={}^{t}u\cdot z^{+}\cdot{}^{t}v\cdot p

by (
5.55
)

\displaystyle={}^{t}u\cdot{}^{t}v\cdot z^{+}p

by centrality of
P

\displaystyle={}^{t}(uv)\cdot z^{+}p\in V_{\hat{s},uv}\cdot P.

This completes the proof of (
5.54
), and hence of the lemma.
∎

It follows that
\phi
is an isomorphism from
U
to its image, which is the subsemigroup

U^{\prime}=\{{f_{u}}:{u\in U}\}\leq\mathcal{U}.

Note that while
U^{\prime}
is a monoid (as
U
is), it is generally not a submonoid of
\mathcal{U}
, as the identities of
U^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{U}
are
f_{1}
and
{\bf 1}
, respectively.

We can now tie together the loose ends. The next proof uses the natural factorisations from Definition
5.44
.

Proof of Theorem
5.47
.

We define the map

\psi:M=US\to\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}

by

a\psi=(f_{u},\hat{s})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any natural factorisation $(u,s)$ of $a\in M=US$.}

This is well defined by Lemma
5.45
; cf. Remark
5.46
.

We begin by showing that
\psi
is injective. To do so, suppose
a\psi=b\psi
for some
a,b\in M
, and fix natural factorisations
(u,s)
and
(v,t)
for
a
and
b
, respectively. Then
(f_{u},\hat{s})=a\psi=b\psi=(f_{v},\hat{t})
, so that
f_{u}=f_{v}
and
\hat{s}=\hat{t}
. The former implies
u=v
, by Lemma
5.53
. Combining this with naturality, we obtain
us^{+}=u=v=vt^{+}
. Since also
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
(as
\hat{s}=\hat{t}
), and since
(U,S)
is proper, it follows from (
5.26
) that
us=vt
: i.e.,
a=b
.

Clearly
{u\psi=(f_{u},\hat{1})}
for all
u\in U
, so
\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{U}
maps
U
bijectively onto
U^{\prime}\rtimes\{\hat{1}\}
, and we have already observed that
U^{\prime}\cong U
. We also observed in Lemma
5.50
that
\mathcal{U}
is commutative if
U
is.

It remains to show that
\psi
is a morphism. So fix some
a,b\in M
, with natural factorisations
(u,s)
and
(v,t)
. We must show that
(ab)\psi=a\psi\cdot b\psi
. We first observe that

a\psi\cdot b\psi=(f_{u},\hat{s})\cdot(f_{v},\hat{t})=(f_{u}\star{}^{\hat{s}}\!f_{v},\hat{s}\hat{t}).

By Lemma
5.45
(ii)
,
ab
has natural factorisation
(u\cdot{}^{s}v,st)
, and so

(ab)\psi=(f_{u\cdot{}^{s}v},\hat{s}\hat{t}).

Thus, the proof will be complete if we can show that
f_{u}\star{}^{\hat{s}}\!f_{v}=f_{u\cdot{}^{s}v}
. To do so, let
x\in S
be arbitrary. Then

\hat{x}f_{u\cdot{}^{s}v}=V_{\hat{x},u\cdot{}^{s}v}\cdot P\and\hat{x}(f_{u}\star{}^{\hat{s}}\!f_{v})=\hat{x}f_{u}\cdot(\hat{x}\hat{s})f_{v}=V_{\hat{x},u}\cdot V_{\hat{x}\hat{s},v}\cdot P,

again using centrality of
P
. So we must show that

V_{\hat{x},u\cdot{}^{s}v}\cdot P=V_{\hat{x},u}\cdot V_{\hat{x}\hat{s},v}\cdot P.

(5.56)

For the forwards inclusion, let
c\in V_{\hat{x},u\cdot{}^{s}v}\cdot P
, so that
c={}^{y}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\cdot p
for some
y\in\hat{x}
and
p\in P
. Then
c={}^{y}u\cdot{}^{ys}v\cdot p
, with
{}^{y}u\in V_{\hat{x},u}
and
{}^{ys}v\in V_{\hat{x}\hat{s},v}
(as
y\mathrel{\sigma}x\ \Rightarrow\ ys\mathrel{\sigma}xs
).

Conversely, let
d\in V_{\hat{x},u}\cdot V_{\hat{x}\hat{s},v}\cdot P
, so that
d={}^{y}u\cdot{}^{z}v\cdot p
for some
y\in\hat{x}
,
z\in\hat{x}\hat{s}
and
p\in P
. Since
z\mathrel{\sigma}xs\mathrel{\sigma}ys
, Lemma
5.48
gives
(ys)^{+}\cdot z=z^{+}\cdot ys
. It then follows from Lemma
4.25
that

(ys)^{+}\cdot{}^{z}v=z^{+}\cdot{}^{ys}v.

(5.57)

But then

\displaystyle d={}^{y}u\cdot{}^{z}v\cdot p

\displaystyle={}^{y}(us^{+})\cdot{}^{z}v\cdot p

as
u=us^{+}
, by naturality

\displaystyle={}^{y}u\cdot{}^{y}(s^{+})\cdot{}^{z}v\cdot p

\displaystyle={}^{y}u\cdot(ys)^{+}\cdot{}^{z}v\cdot p

by Lemma
3.1
(ii)

\displaystyle={}^{y}u\cdot z^{+}\cdot{}^{ys}v\cdot p

by (
5.57
)

\displaystyle={}^{y}u\cdot{}^{ys}v\cdot z^{+}p

by centrality of
P

\displaystyle={}^{y}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\cdot z^{+}p.

This completes the proof of (
5.56
), and hence of the lemma, since
{}^{y}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\in V_{\hat{x},u\cdot{}^{s}v}
and
{z^{+}p\in P}
.
∎

This time we may deduce Theorem
5.12
as a corollary. Again we begin with the monoid case:

Corollary 5.58
.

Let
S
be a proper left restriction monoid with semilattice of projections
{P=P(S)}
. Then
S
can be embedded in a semidirect product
Q\rtimes(S/\sigma)
, where
Q
is a semilattice containing
P
.

Proof.

We aim to apply Theorem
5.47
to the pair
(U,S)
where
U=P
. We first observe that
(P,S)
is indeed a proper action pair, by Proposition
5.29
. Since
P
is a semilattice, it is certainly central in
U=P
. Theorem
5.47
then does indeed apply, and it tells us that
S=PS
embeds in a semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
, where
\mathcal{U}
is a monoid containing (an isomorphic copy) of
U=P
, and where
\mathcal{S}=S/\sigma
. By Lemma
5.50
(ii)
,
\mathcal{U}
is a semilattice.
∎

The general case now follows very quickly:

Proof of Theorem
5.12
.

Starting with an arbitrary proper left restriction
semigroup

S
, we embed this into
S^{1}
, and then apply Corollary
5.58
to
S^{1}
.
∎

Remark 5.59
.

Theorem
5.47
concerned the special case that the submonoid
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
is central in
U
. In the very special case that
U
is commutative, the monoid
\mathcal{U}
we constructed is also commutative. In the very very special case that
U
is a semilattice, unfortunately
\mathcal{U}
is not necessarily a semilattice; it
is
, however, in the very very very special case that
U=P
is a semilattice, as we showed in Lemma
5.50
(ii)
.

In the next section we prove Theorem
5.60
. In a sense, this is a more general version of Theorem
5.47
, as we drop the assumption that
P
is central in
U
. In the proof we construct a very different monoid
\mathcal{U}
to deal with this greater level of generality, and in fact
\mathcal{U}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle
is defined by a monoid presentation. This construction does not allow us to deduce that
\mathcal{U}
is commutative when
U
is commutative; thus, Theorem
5.60
does not completely subsume Theorem
5.47
. However, in Section
5.6
we prove Theorem
5.82
, which is a specialisation of Theorem
5.60
in the case that
U
is commutative. The monoid
\mathcal{U}
constructed in the proof of Theorem
5.82
is of the form
\mathcal{U}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R\cup C}\rangle
, where
C
is an additional set of relations forcing
\mathcal{U}
to be commutative. As we will see, this does have the additional benefit that
\mathcal{U}
is a semilattice when
U
is. Yet another specialisation is given in Theorem
5.102
, which treats the case that
U
is a left-regular band. Although a semilattice is a special case of a left-regular band, the differing technicalities in each case lead us to consider the two separately.

5.5
Embedding theorems for proper monoids II

Consider again a proper action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
, where
U
and
S
are submonoids, and assume again that
M=US
.
As discussed above, our aim now is to prove a stronger version of Theorem
5.47
, in which we drop the assumption that the submonoid
{P=\langle S^{+}\rangle}
is central in
U
.
In fact, the monoid
P
will play no role at all in the current section, apart from its involvement in the construction of the congruence
\sigma=\kappa^{\sharp}
from Definition
5.21
. In the following statement, we continue to write
\hat{s}
for the
\sigma
-class of
s\in S
.

Theorem 5.60
.

Let
M=US
be a
(U,S)
-proper monoid. Then there exists a semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
and an embedding
\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}
such that:

(i)

\mathcal{U}
contains a subsemigroup
U^{\prime}
isomorphic to
U
,

(ii)

\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{U}:U\to U^{\prime}\rtimes\{\hat{1}\}
is an isomorphism.

The proof of the theorem occupies the remainder of the section. Because we no longer assume that
P
is central in
U
, we are forced to adopt a completely different strategy. Accordingly, the construction of
\mathcal{U}
(and
\mathcal{M}
) is somewhat more involved than in the previous section.
As before, we write

\mathcal{S}=S/\sigma=\{{\hat{s}}:{s\in S}\}.

The monoid
\mathcal{U}
will in fact be defined by means of a presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle
, where
X
and
R
are defined as follows. First,

X=\{{x_{\hat{s},u}}:{s\in S,\ u\in U}\}

is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with the cartesian product
\mathcal{S}\times U
. We then define
R
to be the set consisting of all the following relations over
X
, displayed here as equations for clarity:

x_{\hat{t},u\cdot{}^{s}v}=x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t}\hat{s},v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $s,t\in S$ and $u,v\in U$ with $u=us^{+}$.}

(5.61)

These relations may appear mysterious at this stage, but the reader may notice a resemblance to (
5.56
). In any case, we will see that the relations contain (just) enough information about products in
U
and
\mathcal{S}
, and about the action of
S
on
U(=U^{1})
, to make everything work.

For the duration of this section we write
{\sim}=R^{\sharp}
for the congruence on the free monoid
X^{*}
generated by
R
, and we denote the
\sim
-class of
w\in X^{*}
by
\overline{w}
. (There should be no confusion with our earlier use of the over-line notation in Sections
5.2
and
5.3
, as we will never refer to the
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
construction here.)
We then define the monoid

\mathcal{U}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle=X^{*}/{\sim}=\{{\overline{w}}:{w\in X^{*}}\}.

It is worth noting at this point that

x_{\hat{t},uv}\sim x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t},v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $t\in S$ and $u,v\in U$.}

(5.62)

Indeed, taking
s=1
in (
5.61
), we see that
R
contains the relation
x_{\hat{t},uv}=x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t},v}
.

In order to define a semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
, we need an action of
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathcal{U}
. We begin by defining an action of
\mathcal{S}
on
X^{*}
. For this, let
s\in S
and
w\in X^{*}
. Then
w=x_{\hat{t}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{t}_{k},u_{k}}
for some
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in S
and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\in U
, and we define

{}^{\hat{s}}w=x_{\hat{s}\hat{t}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}\hat{t}_{k},u_{k}}.

(5.63)

This is trivially a monoidal action by monoid morphisms: i.e.,

{}^{\hat{s}}({}^{\hat{t}}w)={}^{\hat{s}\hat{t}}w,\ \ \ {}^{\hat{s}}(ww^{\prime})={}^{\hat{s}}w\cdot{}^{\hat{s}}w^{\prime},\ \ \ {}^{\hat{1}}w=w\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt{}^{\hat{s}}\iota=\iota\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $s,t\in S$ and $w,w^{\prime}\in X^{*}$.}

Here
\iota
denotes the empty word, which is the identity of
X^{*}
. Given any relation
(w,w^{\prime})
from
R
, as in (
5.61
), and any
s\in S
, it is easy to see that
({}^{\hat{s}}w,{}^{\hat{s}}w^{\prime})
is again a relation from
R
. It immediately follows that we have an induced action of
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathcal{U}
given by

{}^{\hat{s}}\overline{w}=\overline{{}^{\hat{s}}w}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $w\in X^{*}$ and $s\in S$.}

Of course this is still monoidal, and by monoid morphisms. Consequently, we may form the semidirect product

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes S.

By Corollary
3.13
,
\mathcal{M}
is a monoid with identity
(\overline{\iota},\hat{1})
.

We now define a function

\phi:U\to\mathcal{U}:u\mapsto\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}.

Taking
t=1
in (
5.62
), it follows quickly that
\phi
is a semigroup morphism. (But note that
\phi
is not a
monoid
morphism, as
1\phi=\overline{x}_{\hat{1},1}
, while the identity of
\mathcal{U}
is
\overline{\iota}
, the
\sim
-class of the empty word
\iota
. Since
R
consists entirely of relations with non-empty words on both sides (see (
5.61
)), it follows immediately that
\overline{\iota}=\{\iota\}
, and so
\overline{x}_{\hat{1},1}\not=\overline{\iota}
.)

The next result demonstrates a crucial relationship between the respective actions of
S
and
\mathcal{S}
on
U
and
\mathcal{U}
.

Lemma 5.64
.

If
u,v\in U
and
s\in S
are such that
u=us^{+}
, then

(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\phi=(u\phi)\cdot{}^{\hat{s}}(v\phi).

Proof.

Taking
t=1
in (
5.61
), we see that

\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u\cdot{}^{s}v}=\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}\cdot\overline{x}_{\hat{s},v}.

But of course
\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u\cdot{}^{s}v}=(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\phi
and
\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}=u\phi
, while
\overline{x}_{\hat{s},v}=\overline{x}_{\hat{s}\hat{1},v}={}^{\hat{s}}\overline{x}_{\hat{1},v}={}^{\hat{s}}(v\phi)
.
∎

The next result shows that
\mathcal{U}
and
\phi
are, in a sense, ‘universal’ with respect to the property just established.

Proposition 5.65
.

Let
M=US
be a
(U,S)
-proper monoid.

(i)

Suppose there is a semigroup
\mathcal{V}
on which
\mathcal{S}=S/\sigma
acts monoidally by semigroup morphisms, and a morphism
\xi:U\to\mathcal{V}
such that

(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\xi=(u\xi)\cdot{}^{\hat{s}}(v\xi)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U$ and $s\in S$ with $u=us^{+}$.}

(5.66)

Then there exists a morphism
\Xi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

(ii)

The morphism
\phi:U\to\mathcal{U}
is an embedding if and only if some
\mathcal{V}
and
\xi
exist as in part
(i)
, with
\xi
an embedding.

Proof.

(i)
. Suppose
\mathcal{V}
and
\xi:U\to\mathcal{V}
exist with the stated properties. We begin by defining a morphism

\zeta:X^{*}\to\mathcal{V}:x_{\hat{s},u}\mapsto{}^{\hat{s}}(u\xi).

Using (
5.66
), it is easy to check that
\zeta
preserves the relations from
R
: i.e., that

(x_{\hat{t},u\cdot{}^{s}v})\zeta=(x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t}\hat{s},v})\zeta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $s,t\in S$ and $u,v\in U$ with $u=us^{+}$.}

It follows that
R^{\sharp}\subseteq\ker(\zeta)
, so
\zeta
induces a morphism
\Xi:\mathcal{U}=X^{*}/R^{\sharp}\to\mathcal{V}:\overline{w}\mapsto w\zeta
. This
\Xi
is defined on generators of
\mathcal{U}
by

\Xi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}:\overline{x}_{\hat{s},u}\mapsto{}^{\hat{s}}(u\xi).

But then for any
u\in U
we have

u(\phi\circ\Xi)=\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}\Xi={}^{\hat{1}}(u\xi)=u\xi,

since
\mathcal{S}
acts monoidally on
\mathcal{V}
.

(ii)
. If
\phi
is an embedding, then we just take
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{U}
and
\xi=\phi
; cf. Lemma
5.64
. Conversely, if
\xi=\phi\circ\Xi
is an embedding, then of course
\phi
is as well.
∎

Recall that we wish to show that the
(U,S)
-proper monoid
M=US
embeds in the semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
. We will soon see that a certain very natural kind of embedding exists, and it is convenient to explicitly formulate what we mean by ‘natural’ here. The following definition refers to the natural factorisations from Definition
5.44
.

Definition 5.67
.

Let
M=US
be a
(U,S)
-proper monoid, and let
\mathcal{V}
be a semigroup on which
\mathcal{S}=S/\sigma
acts monoidally via semigroup morphisms. A morphism

\psi:M\to\mathcal{V}\rtimes\mathcal{S}

is
natural
if there exists a morphism
\xi:U\to\mathcal{V}
such that

a\psi=(u\xi,\hat{s})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any natural factorisation $(u,s)$ of $a\in M=US$.}

In this case we say
\psi
is
natural along
\xi
.

Strictly speaking, the next two results are not essential for our main purpose, which is to construct an embedding of
M=US
into
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
. However, we include them as they demonstrate that
\mathcal{U}
is somehow ‘canonical’ with respect to the existence of a
natural
such embedding.

Proposition 5.68
.

Let
M=US
be a
(U,S)
-proper monoid, and let
\mathcal{V}
be a semigroup on which
\mathcal{S}=S/\sigma
acts monoidally via semigroup morphisms. For a morphism
\xi:U\to\mathcal{V}
, define the map

\psi:M\to\mathcal{V}\rtimes\mathcal{S}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pta\psi=(u\xi,\hat{s})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any natural factorisation $(u,s)$ of $a\in M=US$.}

Then

(i)

\psi
is a morphism (and is hence natural along
\xi
) if and only if (
5.66
) holds,

(ii)

\psi
is injective if and only if
\xi
is injective.

Proof.

(i)
. Let
a
and
b
be elements of
US
, with natural factorisations
(u,s)
and
(v,t)
, respectively. By Lemma
5.45
(ii)
,
ab
has natural factorisation
(u\cdot{}^{s}v,st)
, so we have

(ab)\psi=((u\cdot{}^{s}v)\xi,\hat{s}\hat{t})\and a\psi\cdot b\psi=(u\xi,\hat{s})\cdot(v\xi,\hat{t})=((u\xi)\cdot{}^{\hat{s}}(v\xi),\hat{s}\hat{t}).

The claim follows quickly.

(ii)
. Suppose first that
\psi
is injective, and suppose
u,v\in U
are such that
u\xi=v\xi
. Now,
(u,1)
and
(v,1)
are clearly natural factorisations of
u,v\in U\subseteq M
, so

u\psi=(u\xi,\hat{1})=(v\xi,\hat{1})=v\psi.

It follows from injectivity of
\psi
that
u=v
.

Conversely, suppose
\xi
is injective, and suppose
a,b\in M
are such that
a\psi=b\psi
. Fix natural factorisations
(u,s)
and
(v,t)
of
a
and
b
, respectively. Then

(u\xi,\hat{s})=a\psi=b\psi=(v\xi,\hat{t}),

so it follows that
u\xi=v\xi
and
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
. From the former, and injectivity of
\xi
, we deduce
u=v
. By naturality, we have
us^{+}=u=v=vt^{+}
. Since
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
, and since
(U,S)
is proper, it follows from (
5.26
) that
us=vt
: i.e.,
a=b
.
∎

The next result summarises our progress so far:

Corollary 5.69
.

Let
M=US
be a
(U,S)
-proper monoid. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

there is a natural embedding of
M
in a semidirect product
\mathcal{V}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
for some semigroup
\mathcal{V}
,

(ii)

\phi:U\to\mathcal{U}
is an embedding,

(iii)

there is a natural embedding of
M
in the semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
along
\phi
.

Proof.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. If such a natural embedding exists, say along
\xi:U\to\mathcal{V}
, then by Proposition
5.68
,
\xi
is an embedding and satisfies (
5.66
). It then follows from Proposition
5.65
(ii)
that
\phi
is an embedding.

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. This follows immediately from Proposition
5.68
, taking
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{U}
and
\xi=\phi
, and keeping Lemma
5.64
in mind.

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
. This is obvious.
∎

Thus, the main step remaining in the proof of Theorem
5.60
is to show that the morphism
{\phi:U\to\mathcal{U}}
is injective: i.e., that

\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}=\overline{x}_{\hat{1},v}\ \Rightarrow\ u=v\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U$.}

This turns out to be quite involved, and is finally accomplished in Proposition
5.80
below. The proof involves a certain invariant associated to the
\sim
-class of (some) words over
X
, including those of the form
x_{\hat{1},u}
. This invariant comes from what we will call a
trace
. Working towards its definition, we begin by defining a map
\lambda:X^{+}\to\mathcal{S}
as follows. (Here, as usual,
X^{+}=X^{*}\setminus\{\iota\}
is the free
semigroup
over
X
.) For
{w=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}\in X^{+}}
, we define
w\lambda=\hat{s}_{1}
. So
w\lambda
is the ‘
\mathcal{S}
-coordinate’ of the first letter from
w
.

Lemma 5.70
.

If
w,w^{\prime}\in X^{+}
and
w\sim w^{\prime}
, then
w\lambda=w^{\prime}\lambda
.

Proof.

This is true for every relation
(w,w^{\prime})\in R
, as in (
5.61
), and the result quickly follows.
∎

We now define the set

W=\{{w\in X^{+}}:{w\lambda=\hat{1}}\}

of all (non-empty) words over
X
whose first letter has ‘
\mathcal{S}
-coordinate’
\hat{1}
. It follows from Lemma
5.70
that
W
is closed under
\sim
, in the sense that two
\sim
-equivalent words from
X^{*}
are either both contained in
W
or both not.
We now introduce the idea of a
trace
of (some) words from
W
.

Definition 5.71
.

Consider a word
w\in W
, so that

w=x_{\hat{1},u_{1}}x_{\hat{s}_{2},u_{2}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $k\geq 1$ and some $u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\in U$ and $s_{2},\ldots,s_{k}\in S$.}

Consider also a tuple
{\bf t}=(t_{2},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
, so that
t_{i}\in S
and
t_{i}\mathrel{\sigma}s_{i}
for all
2\leq i\leq k
. For each
1\leq i\leq k
, we define

p_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}\in U.

(As usual, we interpret
p_{1}=u_{1}
.) We say the tuple
{\bf p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\in U^{k}
is a
trace
of
w
if

p_{i-1}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $2\leq i\leq k$.}

In this case, we say the trace
{\bf p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
of
w
is
witnessed
by the tuple
{\bf t}=(t_{2},\ldots,t_{k})
.

Remark 5.72
.

It is not always obvious whether a given word from
W
has any trace at all. However, it is easy to see that a word
w=x_{\hat{1},u}
from
W
of length
1
has trace
{\bf p}=(u)
, as witnessed (vacuously) by the empty tuple
{\bf t}=\varnothing
.

On the other hand, consider a word
w=x_{\hat{1},u}x_{\hat{s},v}\in W
of length
2
. The possible tuples
{\bf t}
in Definition
5.71
have the form
{\bf t}=(t)
, where
t\in\hat{s}
. And then
{\bf p}=(u,u\cdot{}^{t}v)
is a trace for
w
(witnessed by
{\bf t}
) if and only if
p_{1}=p_{1}t_{2}^{+}
: i.e.,
u=ut^{+}
. To summarise,
w=x_{\hat{1},u}x_{\hat{s},v}
has a trace if and only if
u=ut^{+}
for some
t\in\hat{s}
.

Nevertheless, the next result shows that if a word has a trace, then this is unique.

Lemma 5.73
.

A word
w\in W
has at most one trace.

Proof.

Consider a word
w=x_{\hat{1},u_{1}}x_{\hat{s}_{2},u_{2}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}\in W
, and suppose
w
has traces
{{\bf p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})}
and
{\bf q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
, witnessed by tuples
{\bf t}=(t_{2},\ldots,t_{k})
and
{\bf z}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{k})
, respectively. So by definition,
{\bf t},{\bf z}\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
, and we have

\displaystyle p_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}

\displaystyle\and q_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}u_{i}

for all
1\leq i\leq k
,

and also

\displaystyle p_{i-1}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}

\displaystyle\and q_{i-1}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}

for all
2\leq i\leq k
.

We must show that
{\bf p}={\bf q}
, and for this we show by induction that
p_{i}=q_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. Certainly
p_{1}=u_{1}=q_{1}
. We now assume that
i\geq 2
, and that
p_{i-1}=q_{i-1}
. Then

p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}=p_{i-1}=q_{i-1}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}.

Since also
t_{i}\mathrel{\sigma}z_{i}
(as
t_{i},z_{i}\in\hat{s}_{i}
), and since
(U,S)
is proper, it follows from (
5.26
) that
{p_{i-1}t_{i}=q_{i-1}z_{i}}
. Lemma
4.25
then gives

p_{i-1}\cdot{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}=q_{i-1}\cdot{}^{z_{i}}u_{i}.

But by definition, we have
p_{i-1}\cdot{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}=p_{i}
and
q_{i-1}\cdot{}^{z_{i}}u_{i}=q_{i}
, so this completes the inductive step.
∎

Here is the main technical result we need:

Lemma 5.74
.

Suppose
w,w^{\prime}\in W
are such that
w\sim w^{\prime}
.

(i)

If either of
w
or
w^{\prime}
has a trace, then both do.

(ii)

If
w
and
w^{\prime}
have traces
{\bf p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
{\bf q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
, then
p_{k}=q_{l}
.

Proof.

We prove both parts at the same time, and it suffices to assume that
w
and
w^{\prime}
differ by a single application of a relation from
R
. That is, we assume that

w=w_{1}\cdot x_{\hat{t},u\cdot{}^{s}v}\cdot w_{2}\and w^{\prime}=w_{1}\cdot x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t}\hat{s},v}\cdot w_{2},

for some
w_{1},w_{2}\in X^{*}
, and some
s,t\in S
and
u,v\in U
with
u=us^{+}
. We also write

w_{1}=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}\and w_{2}=x_{\hat{s}_{m+2},u_{m+2}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where each $s_{i}\in S$ and $u_{i}\in U$,}

noting that either (or both) of
w_{1}
or
w_{2}
could be empty. It is also convenient to define

s_{m+1}=t\and u_{m+1}=u\cdot{}^{s}v.

So then

w=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}\and w^{\prime}=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}\cdot x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t}\hat{s},v}\cdot x_{\hat{s}_{m+2},u_{m+2}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}.

Since
w\in W
, we must have
\hat{s}_{1}=\hat{1}
; in the case that
w_{1}=\iota
(i.e.,
m=0
), this says that
\hat{t}=\hat{1}
. We also define

(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k+1})=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{m},u,v,u_{m+2},\ldots,u_{k}).

(5.75)

Note that
v_{i}
is the ‘
U
-coordinate’ of the
i
th letter of
w^{\prime}
.

We begin with the forwards implication.

(
\Rightarrow
). Suppose first that
w
has trace
{\bf p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
, witnessed by
{\bf t}=(t_{2},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
, meaning that

•

p_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
, and

•

p_{i-1}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}
for all
2\leq i\leq k
.

We claim that
w^{\prime}
has trace
{\bf q}
, witnessed by
{\bf z}
, for

{\bf z}=(t_{2},\ldots,t_{m},t_{m+1},t_{m+1}\cdot s,t_{m+2},\ldots,t_{k})\and{\bf q}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m},p_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u,p_{m+1},\ldots,p_{k}).

Note that
{\bf z}
and
{\bf q}
are obtained from
{\bf t}
and
{\bf p}
, respectively, by inserting single entries. Since the final entries of
{\bf p}
and
{\bf q}
are equal, this will complete the proof of the forwards implication. To assist with the following calculations, it is convenient to line up the entries of
{\bf z}
and
{\bf q}
, along with the letters of
w^{\prime}
:

1

2

\cdots

m

m+1

m+2

m+3

\cdots

k+1

{\bf z}

t_{2}

\cdots

t_{m}

t_{m+1}

t_{m+1}\cdot s

t_{m+2}

\cdots

t_{k}

{\bf q}

p_{1}

p_{2}

\cdots

p_{m}

p_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u

p_{m+1}

p_{m+2}

\cdots

p_{k}

w^{\prime}

x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}

x_{\hat{s}_{2},u_{2}}

\cdots

x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}

x_{\hat{t},u}

x_{\hat{t}\hat{s},v}

x_{\hat{s}_{m+2},u_{m+2}}

\cdots

x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}

To prove the claim, we follow Definition
5.71
carefully, and we proceed in three steps.

Step 1.
We first check that
{\bf z}\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{m}\times\hat{t}\times\hat{t}\hat{s}\times\hat{s}_{m+2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
.

•

For
2\leq i\leq m
we have
z_{i}=t_{i}\mathrel{\sigma}s_{i}
.

•

For
i=m+1
we have
z_{m+1}=t_{m+1}\mathrel{\sigma}s_{m+1}=t
.

•

For
i=m+2
we have
z_{m+2}=t_{m+1}\cdot s\mathrel{\sigma}s_{m+1}\cdot s=ts
.

•

For
m+3\leq i\leq k+1
we have
z_{i}=t_{i-1}\mathrel{\sigma}s_{i-1}
.

Step 2.
Next we check that
q_{i}=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k+1
, where the
v_{j}
are as in (
5.75
).

•

For
1\leq i\leq m
we have
q_{i}=p_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}
.

•

For
i=m+1
we have
q_{m+1}=p_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{m}}u_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{m}}v_{m}{}^{z_{m+1}}v_{m+1}
.

•

For
m+2\leq i\leq k+1
we have

\displaystyle q_{i}=p_{i-1}

\displaystyle=(u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{m}}u_{m})\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}\cdot({}^{t_{m+2}}u_{m+2}\cdots{}^{t_{i-1}}u_{i-1})

\displaystyle=(u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{m}}u_{m})\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\cdot({}^{t_{m+2}}u_{m+2}\cdots{}^{t_{i-1}}u_{i-1})

\displaystyle=(u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{m}}u_{m})\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}\cdot s}v\cdot({}^{t_{m+2}}u_{m+2}\cdots{}^{t_{i-1}}u_{i-1})

\displaystyle=(v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{m}}v_{m})\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}v_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+2}}v_{m+2}\cdot({}^{z_{m+3}}v_{m+3}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}).

Step 3.

Finally, we check that
q_{i-1}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}
for all
2\leq i\leq k+1
.

•

For
2\leq i\leq m+1
we have
q_{i-1}=p_{i-1}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}
.

•

For
i=m+2
we have

\displaystyle q_{m+1}\cdot z_{m+2}^{+}

\displaystyle=p_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u\cdot(t_{m+1}\cdot s)^{+}

\displaystyle=p_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}(s^{+})

by Lemma
3.1
(ii)

\displaystyle=p_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}(us^{+})

\displaystyle=p_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u

as
u=us^{+}

\displaystyle=q_{m+1}.

•

For
m+3\leq i\leq k+1
we have
q_{i-1}=p_{i-2}=p_{i-2}t_{i-1}^{+}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}
.

(
\Leftarrow
). Conversely, we now assume
w^{\prime}
has trace
{\bf q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k+1})
, witnessed by

{\bf z}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{k+1})\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{m}\times\hat{t}\times\hat{t}\hat{s}\times\hat{s}_{m+2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k},

(5.76)

meaning that

•

q_{i}=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k+1
, where again the
v_{j}
are as in (
5.75
), and

•

q_{i-1}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}
for all
2\leq i\leq k+1
.

We claim that
w
has trace
{\bf p}
, witnessed by
{\bf t}
, where

{\bf t}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{m+1},z_{m+3},\ldots,z_{k+1})\and{\bf p}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{m},q_{m+2},\ldots,q_{k+1}).

This time
{\bf t}
and
{\bf p}
are obtained by deleting an appropriate entry of
{\bf z}
and
{\bf q}
.
Again, since the final entries of
{\bf p}
and
{\bf q}
are equal, this will complete the proof of the backwards implication, and hence of the lemma.
(The reader might worry that
{\bf p}
and
{\bf q}
could perhaps not have the same final entry if
m+1=k+1
: i.e., if
{\bf p}
is obtained by deleting the
final
entry of
{\bf q}
. However, this case does not arise, as by the form of
w=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}\cdot x_{\hat{t},u\cdot{}^{s}v}\cdot x_{\hat{s}_{m+2},u_{m+2}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}
, we have
k\geq m+1
.)
This time the entries of
{\bf t}
and
{\bf p}
, and the letters from
w
, are as follows:

1

2

\cdots

m

m+1

m+2

\cdots

k

{\bf t}

z_{2}

\cdots

z_{m}

z_{m+1}

z_{m+3}

\cdots

z_{k+1}

{\bf p}

q_{1}

q_{2}

\cdots

q_{m}

q_{m+2}

q_{m+3}

\cdots

q_{k+1}

w

x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}

x_{\hat{s}_{2},u_{2}}

\cdots

x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}

x_{\hat{t},u\cdot{}^{s}v}

x_{\hat{s}_{m+2},u_{m+2}}

\cdots

x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}

=

x_{\hat{s}_{m+1},u_{m+1}}

Again we prove the claim in three steps.

Step 1.

We first note that
{\bf t}\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{m}\times\hat{t}\times\hat{s}_{m+2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
follows immediately from (
5.76
).

Step 2.

Next we check that
p_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}u_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
.

•

For
1\leq i\leq m
we have
p_{i}=q_{i}=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}
.

•

The
m+1\leq i\leq k
case is rather more involved. For this we first claim that

q_{m+1}z_{m+2}=q_{m+1}z_{m+1}s.

(5.77)

To prove this, it suffices by (
5.26
) to show that

q_{m+1}z_{m+2}^{+}=q_{m+1}(z_{m+1}s)^{+}\and z_{m+2}\mathrel{\sigma}z_{m+1}s.

(5.78)

The latter follows from
z_{m+2}\mathrel{\sigma}ts\mathrel{\sigma}z_{m+1}s
; cf. (
5.76
). For the former, we first note that
{q_{m+1}z_{m+2}^{+}=q_{m+1}}
. On the other hand, since
q_{m+1}=q_{m}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}v_{m+1}
, we have

\displaystyle q_{m+1}(z_{m+1}s)^{+}

\displaystyle=q_{m}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}v_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}(s^{+})

by Lemma
3.1
(ii)

\displaystyle=q_{m}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}(v_{m+1}s^{+})

\displaystyle=q_{m}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}v_{m+1}

as
v_{m+1}s^{+}=us^{+}=u=v_{m+1}

\displaystyle=q_{m+1}.

This completes the proof of (
5.78
), and hence of (
5.77
). We then have

\displaystyle q_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+2}}v_{m+2}

\displaystyle=q_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}s}v_{m+2}

by (
5.77
) and Lemma
4.25

\displaystyle=q_{m}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}v_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}s}v_{m+2}

\displaystyle=q_{m}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}(v_{m+1}\cdot{}^{s}v_{m+2})

\displaystyle=q_{m}\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)

\displaystyle=q_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}.

(5.79)

But then for any
m+1\leq i\leq k
we have

\displaystyle p_{i}=q_{i+1}

\displaystyle=q_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+2}}v_{m+2}\cdot{}^{z_{m+3}}v_{m+3}\cdots{}^{z_{i+1}}v_{i+1}

\displaystyle=q_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+3}}v_{m+3}\cdots{}^{z_{i+1}}v_{i+1}

by (
5.79
)

\displaystyle=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{m}}v_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+3}}v_{m+3}\cdots{}^{z_{i+1}}v_{i+1}

\displaystyle=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{m}}u_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}\cdot{}^{t_{m+2}}u_{m+2}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i},

as required.

Step 3.

Finally, we check that
p_{i-1}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}
for all
2\leq i\leq k
.

•

For
2\leq i\leq m+1
we have
p_{i-1}=q_{i-1}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}
.

•

For
m+2\leq i\leq k
we have
p_{i-1}=q_{i}=q_{i}z_{i+1}^{+}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}
.

We have now finally completed the proof.
∎

It is now relatively straightforward to show the following.

Proposition 5.80
.

The morphism
