\phi:U\to\mathcal{U}:u\mapsto\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}
is an embedding.

Proof.

Suppose
u,v\in U
are such that
\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}=\overline{x}_{\hat{1},v}
. This means that
w\sim w^{\prime}
, where
w=x_{\hat{1},u}
and
w^{\prime}=x_{\hat{1},v}
. As in Remark
5.72
,
w
and
w^{\prime}
have traces
(u)
and
(v)
, respectively, so it follows from Lemma
5.74
that
u=v
.
∎

And we can now finally tie together the loose ends.

Proof of Theorem
5.60
.

We take
\mathcal{U}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle
, as constructed above. By Corollary
5.69
and Proposition
5.80
, there is a natural embedding
\psi
of
M
in
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
along
\phi
. By definition, this
\psi
is defined by

a\psi=(u\phi,\hat{s})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any natural factorisation $(u,s)$ of $a\in M=US$.}

It remains to check items
(i)
and
(ii)
in the statement of the theorem. But

u\psi=(u\phi,\hat{1})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$,}

as
(u,1)
is a natural factorisation for any
u\in U
. Thus, we can take
U^{\prime}=\operatorname{im}(\phi)\cong U
.
∎

Remark 5.81
.

Theorem
5.47
(minus the clause concerning commutativity of
U
and
\mathcal{U}
) is of course a special case of Theorem
5.60
, but the proofs we have given for these two results are completely different. In particular, the monoids
\mathcal{U}
constructed in the proofs are not at all alike. One advantage of the proof of Theorem
5.47
is that the monoid
\mathcal{U}
turns out to be a semilattice when
M
is a proper left restriction monoid; see the proof of Corollary
5.58
.
This is not the case, however, for the monoid
\mathcal{U}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle
constructed in the current section, though it is worth noting that if
U
is a band, then
\mathcal{U}
is idempotent-generated. Indeed, taking
v=u(=u^{2})
in (
5.62
), we have
x_{\hat{t},u}\sim x_{\hat{t},u}\cdot x_{\hat{t},u}
for all
t\in S
and
u\in U
.

Nevertheless, some modifications may be made in certain special cases, which lead to specialised versions of Theorem
5.60
. Specifically, if we assume that
U
is commutative, or a semilattice, or a left-regular band, then we can modify the construction to ensure that
\mathcal{U}
has the same property (commutative, semilattice, left-regular band, respectively). This will be the subject of the next two sections.

5.6
Embedding theorems for proper monoids III

The main result of the previous section was Theorem
5.60
, which shows that any
(U,S)
-proper monoid
M=US
embeds naturally in a suitable semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
. In this section and the next we specialise this theorem in two important cases: when
U
is commutative (but see Remark
5.83
), and when
U
is a left-regular band, respectively.

Theorem 5.82
.

Let
M=US
be a
(U,S)
-proper monoid, with
U
commutative (respectively, a semilattice). Then there exists a semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}_{C}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
and an embedding
\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}
such that:

(i)

\mathcal{U}_{C}
is commutative (respectively, a semilattice),

(ii)

\mathcal{U}_{C}
contains a subsemigroup
U^{\prime}
isomorphic to
U
,

(iii)

\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{U}:U\to U^{\prime}\rtimes\{\hat{1}\}
is an isomorphism.

Remark 5.83
.

As explained in Remark
5.59
, if we removed the semilattice clauses from Theorem
5.82
then the result would follow from (or is indeed contained in) Theorem
5.47
. However, as explained in the same remark, the semilattice version of Theorem
5.82
does not follow from the proof of Theorem
5.47
given above. The proof of Theorem
5.82
that we give below assumes only that
U
is commutative, as it does not simplify at all if we assume that it is a semilattice.

There are two other reasons for including the coming proof of Theorem
5.82
. The first is to show how the method of the previous section can be adapted to the commutative case; we add additional relations to the presentation
\mathcal{U}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle
from the previous proof to obtain a commutative monoid
\mathcal{U}_{C}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R\cup C}\rangle
, which will be a semilattice if
U
is. The second additional reason is to pave the way for the next section, in which we carry out the same task in the more complex case that
U
is a left-regular band; there we construct a suitable left-regular band
\mathcal{U}_{L}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R\cup L}\rangle
.

For the rest of this section, we assume that
M=US
is a
(U,S)
-proper monoid, with
U
commutative. We will not assume that
U
is a semilattice, but will occasionally make a comment about the special case in which it is. It is possible to prove results analogous to Proposition
5.65
and Corollary
5.69
, demonstrating the ‘universality’ of our construction of
\mathcal{U}_{C}
. These are omitted, however, as the statements and proofs are almost identical to those of the results just quoted.

The next result refers to the submonoid
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle\leq U
. The proof is essentially the same as for Lemma
5.48
. (Note that
P
is commutative because
U
is.)

Lemma 5.84
.

The monoid
P
is a semilattice, and consequently
\sigma=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{t^{+}s=s^{+}t}\big\}
. ∎

As ever, we write

\mathcal{S}=S/\sigma=\{{\hat{s}}:{s\in S}\}.

The monoid
\mathcal{U}_{C}
will again be defined by means of a presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R\cup C}\rangle
. The alphabet
X
and the relations
R
will have the same meaning as in the previous section, but for convenience we repeat the definitions here. Specifically, we have

X=\{{x_{\hat{s},u}}:{s\in S,\ u\in U}\},

while
R
consists of all the relations

\displaystyle x_{\hat{t},u\cdot{}^{s}v}

\displaystyle=x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t}\hat{s},v}

for
s,t\in S
and
u,v\in U
with
u=us^{+}
.

(5.85)

We also define
C
to be the set of all relations

\displaystyle x_{\hat{s},u}x_{\hat{t},v}

\displaystyle=x_{\hat{t},v}x_{\hat{s},u}

for
s,t\in S
and
u,v\in U
.

(5.86)

For the duration of this section we write
{\sim}=(R\cup C)^{\sharp}
for the congruence on the free monoid
X^{*}
generated by
R\cup C
, and we denote the
\sim
-class of
w\in X^{*}
by
\overline{w}
.
We then define the monoid

\mathcal{U}_{C}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R\cup C}\rangle=X^{*}/{\sim}=\{{\overline{w}}:{w\in X^{*}}\}.

Lemma 5.87
.

(i)

The monoid
\mathcal{U}_{C}
is commutative.

(ii)

If
U
is a semilattice, then so too is
\mathcal{U}_{C}
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
2.9
. (As in Remark
5.81
, when
U
is a semilattice,
R
contains the relations
x_{\hat{t},u}=x_{\hat{t},u}\cdot x_{\hat{t},u}
for all
t\in S
and
u\in U
.)
∎

As before,
\mathcal{S}
acts monoidally on
X^{*}
by monoid morphisms via

{}^{\hat{s}}w=x_{\hat{s}\hat{t}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}\hat{t}_{k},u_{k}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $w=x_{\hat{t}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{t}_{k},u_{k}}\in X^{*}$ and $s\in S$.}

We noted previously that for any relation
(w,w^{\prime})
from
R
, and any
s\in S
,
({}^{\hat{s}}w,{}^{\hat{s}}w^{\prime})
is again a relation from
R
. This is also of course true if instead
(w,w^{\prime})
is a relation from
C
. So we have an induced action of
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathcal{U}_{C}
given by

{}^{\hat{s}}\overline{w}=\overline{{}^{\hat{s}}w}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $w\in X^{*}$ and $s\in S$,}

which is still monoidal, and by monoid morphisms. We therefore have the (monoid) semidirect product

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}_{C}\rtimes\mathcal{S}.

We now define a function

\phi_{C}:U\to\mathcal{U}_{C}:u\mapsto\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}.

Taking
s=t=1
in (
5.85
), we have

x_{\hat{1},uv}\sim x_{\hat{1},u}x_{\hat{1},v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U$,}

and it follows that
\phi_{C}
is a semigroup morphism. We also have the following, proved in the same way as Lemma
5.64
.

Lemma 5.88
.

If
u,v\in U
and
s\in S
are such that
u=us^{+}
, then

(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\phi_{C}=(u\phi_{C})\cdot{}^{\hat{s}}(v\phi_{C}).

We have now defined the monoid
\mathcal{U}_{C}
and the semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}_{C}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
, and we have verified part
(i)
of Theorem
5.82
. The next result defines the map
\psi
from the theorem.

Proposition 5.89
.

(i)

We have a morphism
\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}
given by

a\psi=(u\phi_{C},\hat{s})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any natural factorisation $(u,s)$ of $a\in M=US$.}

(ii)

\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}
is injective if and only if
\phi_{C}:U\to\mathcal{U}_{C}
is injective.

Proof.

(i)
. The map
\psi
is well defined because of Lemma
5.45
(cf. Remark
5.46
). The proof of Proposition
5.68
(i)
is easily adapted to show that
\psi
is a morphism.

(ii)
. This is essentially the same as the proof of Proposition
5.68
(ii)
.
∎

Thus, to complete the proof of Theorem
5.82
, it remains to show that
\phi_{C}
is injective: i.e., that

\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}=\overline{x}_{\hat{1},v}\ \Rightarrow\ u=v\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U$.}

Indeed, it will then follow that the restriction

\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{U}:U\to\mathcal{M}:u\mapsto(u\phi_{C},\hat{1})=(\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u},\hat{1})

maps
U
isomorphically onto its image, which is precisely
U^{\prime}\rtimes\{\hat{1}\}
for
U^{\prime}=\{{\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}}:{u\in U}\}\leq\mathcal{U}_{C}
.

The proof in the previous section that
\phi:U\to\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle
was injective was quite involved, relying on the concept of the
trace
of certain words over
X
; see Definition
5.71
. Because of the commutativity of
\mathcal{U}_{C}
, we can replace traces with the somewhat simpler notion of a
shadow
:

Definition 5.90
.

For a word
w=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}\in X^{*}
, we define the function

\varsigma_{w}:\hat{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}\to U\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pt\varsigma_{w}({\bf t})={}^{t_{1}}u_{1}\cdots{}^{t_{k}}u_{k}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for ${\bf t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}$.}

(Note that it is convenient to write
\varsigma_{w}
to the left of its argument, hence the notation
\varsigma_{w}({\bf t})
instead of
{\bf t}\varsigma_{w}
.)
We call
\varsigma_{w}({\bf t})
a
shadow
of
w
, and we say it is
witnessed
by
{\bf t}
. We write

\Sigma_{w}=\operatorname{im}(\varsigma_{w})=\{{\varsigma_{w}({\bf t})}:{{\bf t}\in\hat{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}}\}

for the set of all shadows of
w
.

Remark 5.91
.

When
k=0
, so that
w=\iota
is empty, the domain of
\varsigma_{\iota}
is
\hat{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}=\{\varnothing\}
, by the standard set-theoretic interpretation of a cartesian product of an empty family. By the usual convention regarding empty products we have
\varsigma_{\iota}(\varnothing)=1
. Thus,
\Sigma_{\iota}=\{1\}
.

It is also worth noting a relationship between shadows and traces. Recall from Definition
5.71
that the latter are defined for (some) words of the form
w=x_{\hat{1},u_{1}}x_{\hat{s}_{2},u_{2}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}
. A trace of such a word
w
is a certain special kind of tuple
{\bf p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\in U^{k}
, and we claim that if such a trace exists, then
p_{k}
is a shadow of
w
. Indeed, among the defining properties of the trace
{\bf p}
, we have

p_{k}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{k}}u_{k}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $(t_{2},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}$.}

But then
p_{k}={}^{1}u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{k}}u_{k}=\varsigma_{w}({\bf t})
for
{\bf t}=(1,t_{2},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{1}\times\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
.

Finally, we also note that
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
is precisely the set of all shadows of all words of the form
w=x_{\hat{s}_{1},1}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},1}
(
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in S
). Indeed, given any tuple
{{\bf t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}}
, we have

\varsigma_{w}({\bf t})={}^{t_{1}}1\cdots{}^{t_{k}}1=t_{1}^{+}\cdots t_{k}^{+}\in P.

Conversely, any element
z_{1}^{+}\cdots z_{l}^{+}
of
P
is a shadow of the word
x_{\hat{z}_{1},1}\cdots x_{\hat{z}_{l},1}
.

Since
\Sigma_{w}\subseteq U
for all
w\in X^{*}
, we can think of
\Sigma_{w}
as an element of the power semigroup
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
, as defined in (
5.49
).

Lemma 5.92
.

The map
\xi_{1}:X^{*}\to{\mathcal{P}}(U):w\mapsto\Sigma_{w}
is a monoid morphism.

Proof.

We observed in Remark
5.91
that
\Sigma_{\iota}=\{1\}
, which is the identity of
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
. So it remains to show that

\Sigma_{ww^{\prime}}=\Sigma_{w}\cdot\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $w,w^{\prime}\in X^{*}$.}

But this follows quickly upon writing
w=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}
and
w^{\prime}=x_{\hat{s}_{1}^{\prime},u_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{l}^{\prime},u_{l}^{\prime}}
, and noting that:

•

any shadow of
w
has the form
{}^{t_{1}}u_{1}\cdots{}^{t_{k}}u_{k}
for some
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
,

•

any shadow of
w^{\prime}
has the form
{}^{t_{1}^{\prime}}u_{1}^{\prime}\cdots{}^{t_{l}^{\prime}}u_{l}^{\prime}
for some
(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{l}^{\prime})\in\hat{s}_{1}^{\prime}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{l}^{\prime}
,

•

any shadow of
ww^{\prime}
has the form
{}^{t_{1}}u_{1}\cdots{}^{t_{k}}u_{k}\cdot{}^{t_{1}^{\prime}}u_{1}^{\prime}\cdots{}^{t_{l}^{\prime}}u_{l}^{\prime}
for some
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{s}_{1}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
and
(t_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{l}^{\prime})\in\hat{s}_{1}^{\prime}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{l}^{\prime}
. ∎

Since
U
is commutative, and since
P
is a submonoid, we have a morphism

\xi_{2}:{\mathcal{P}}(U)\to{\mathcal{P}}(U):V\mapsto P\cdot V.

The image of this morphism is contained in
P\cdot{\mathcal{P}}(U)=P\cdot{\mathcal{P}}(U)\cdot P
, the local monoid of
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
with identity
P
. (This local monoid played an important role in Section
5.4
as well, where it was denoted
I
.) Of particular importance to us is the composite
\xi_{1}\circ\xi_{2}
, where
\xi_{1}
is the morphism from Lemma
5.92
. We denote this composite by

\xi=\xi_{1}\circ\xi_{2}:X^{*}\to{\mathcal{P}}(U):w\mapsto P\cdot\Sigma_{w}.

Lemma 5.93
.

We have
R\cup C\subseteq\ker(\xi)
.

Proof.

Consider some
(w,w^{\prime})\in R\cup C
. We must show that
w\xi=w^{\prime}\xi
: i.e., that
P\cdot\Sigma_{w}=P\cdot\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}
. Since
P
is a submonoid of
U
, it is enough to show that

\Sigma_{w}\subseteq P\cdot\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}\and\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}\subseteq P\cdot\Sigma_{w}.

(5.94)

We consider separate cases according to whether
(w,w^{\prime})
belongs to
R
or
C
, starting with the simpler of the two.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
(w,w^{\prime})\in C
, so as in (
5.86
) we have

w=x_{\hat{s},u}x_{\hat{t},v}\and w^{\prime}=x_{\hat{t},v}x_{\hat{s},u}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $s,t\in S$ and $u,v\in U$.}

By symmetry, it is enough to establish the first inclusion in (
5.94
). Since
1\in P
, we can do this by showing that

\Sigma_{w}\subseteq\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}.

To do so, let
a\in\Sigma_{w}
be an arbitrary shadow of
w
; we must show that
a\in\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}
. Since
w
has length
2
, the shadow
a
of
w
is witnessed by a tuple of the form
{\bf z}=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\hat{s}\times\hat{t}
, and we have
a=\varsigma_{w}({\bf z})={}^{z_{1}}u\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v
. Since
U
is commutative it follows that
a={}^{z_{2}}v\cdot{}^{z_{1}}u=\varsigma_{w^{\prime}}({\bf z}^{\prime})
, where
{\bf z}^{\prime}=(z_{2},z_{1})\in\hat{t}\times\hat{s}
. This shows that
a\in\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}
, as required.

Case 2.
Next, suppose
(w,w^{\prime})\in R
, so as in (
5.85
) we have

w=x_{\hat{t},u\cdot{}^{s}v}\and w^{\prime}=x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t}\hat{s},v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $s,t\in S$ and $u,v\in U$ with $u=us^{+}$.}

This time we do not have symmetry, so we must demonstrate both inclusions in (
5.94
).

To establish the first of these inclusions, we will again show that

\Sigma_{w}\subseteq\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}.

To prove this, let
a\in\Sigma_{w}
. This time
a
is witnessed by a tuple of the form
{\bf z}=(z)
, meaning that
z\in\hat{t}
and
a=\varsigma_{w}({\bf z})={}^{z}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)
. Since
\sigma
is a congruence, we have

z\in\hat{t}\ \Rightarrow\ z\mathrel{\sigma}t\ \Rightarrow\ zs\mathrel{\sigma}ts\ \Rightarrow\ zs\in\hat{t}\hat{s},

so it follows that
{\bf z}^{\prime}=(z,zs)\in\hat{t}\times\hat{t}\hat{s}
. But then
w^{\prime}
has shadow

\varsigma_{w^{\prime}}({\bf z}^{\prime})={}^{z}u\cdot{}^{zs}v={}^{z}u\cdot{}^{z}({}^{s}v)={}^{z}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)=a,

showing that
a\in\Sigma_{w}^{\prime}
.

To demonstrate the second inclusion in (
5.94
), let
b\in\Sigma_{w^{\prime}}
be an arbitrary shadow of
w^{\prime}
. We must show that

b=pc\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $p\in P$ and some shadow $c\in\Sigma_{w}$.}

(5.95)

The shadow
b
of
w^{\prime}
is witnessed by some tuple
{\bf z}=(z_{1},z_{2})
. This means that
z_{1}\in\hat{t}
,
z_{2}\in\hat{t}\hat{s}
and

\displaystyle b=\varsigma_{w^{\prime}}({\bf z})

\displaystyle={}^{z_{1}}u\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v.

(5.96)

Now we put
{\bf z}^{\prime}=(z_{1})
. Since
z_{1}\in\hat{t}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\varsigma_{w}({\bf z}^{\prime})

\displaystyle={}^{z_{1}}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)

(5.97)

is a shadow of
w
. From
z_{1}\in\hat{t}
and
z_{2}\in\hat{t}\hat{s}
we deduce that
z_{2}\mathrel{\sigma}ts\mathrel{\sigma}z_{1}s
, so it follows from Lemma
5.84
that

\displaystyle z_{2}^{+}\cdot z_{1}s

\displaystyle=(z_{1}s)^{+}\cdot z_{2}.

Since
z_{2}^{+},(z_{1}s)^{+}\in U
and
z_{1}s,z_{2}\in S
(and
v\in U
), Lemma
4.25
then gives

\displaystyle z_{2}^{+}\cdot{}^{z_{1}s}v

\displaystyle=(z_{1}s)^{+}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v.

(5.98)

Putting everything together, and using properties of the action, we then have

\displaystyle z_{2}^{+}\cdot\varsigma_{w}({\bf z}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=z_{2}^{+}\cdot{}^{z_{1}}(u\cdot{}^{s}v)

by (
5.97
)

\displaystyle=z_{2}^{+}\cdot{}^{z_{1}}u\cdot{}^{z_{1}s}v

\displaystyle={}^{z_{1}}u\cdot z_{2}^{+}\cdot{}^{z_{1}s}v

by commutativity of
U

\displaystyle={}^{z_{1}}u\cdot(z_{1}s)^{+}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v

by (
5.98
)

\displaystyle={}^{z_{1}}u\cdot{}^{z_{1}}(s^{+})\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v

by Lemma
3.1
(ii)

\displaystyle={}^{z_{1}}(us^{+})\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v

\displaystyle={}^{z_{1}}u\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v

as
u=us^{+}

\displaystyle=b

by (
5.96
).

So (
5.95
) holds with
p=z_{2}^{+}\in P
and
c=\varsigma_{w}({\bf z}^{\prime})\in\Sigma_{w}
.
∎

Because of Lemma
5.93
, it follows that we have a well-defined morphism

\Xi:\mathcal{U}_{C}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R\cup C}\rangle\to{\mathcal{P}}(U)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{w}\Xi=w\xi=P\cdot\Sigma_{w}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $w\in X^{*}$.}

We can now prove the following:

Proposition 5.99
.

The morphism
\phi_{C}:U\to\mathcal{U}_{C}:u\mapsto\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}
is an embedding.

Proof.

We prove the proposition by showing that the composite

\phi_{C}\circ\Xi:U\to{\mathcal{P}}(U):u\mapsto P\cdot\Sigma_{x_{\hat{1},u}}

is injective. To do so, fix some
{(u,v)\in\ker(\phi_{C}\circ\Xi)}
, meaning that
u,v\in U
and

P\cdot\Sigma_{x_{\hat{1},u}}=P\cdot\Sigma_{x_{\hat{1},v}}.

Now,
u={}^{1}u
is a shadow of
x_{\hat{1},u}
(witnessed by
(1)
), and so
u=1\cdot u\in P\cdot\Sigma_{x_{\hat{1},u}}=P\cdot\Sigma_{x_{\hat{1},v}}
. It follows that
u=pa
for some
p\in P
and some shadow
a
of
x_{\hat{1},v}
. By definition, this shadow has the form
a={}^{t}v
for some
t\in\hat{1}
. By Lemma
5.34
(ii)
we have
a={}^{t}v=t^{+}v
, and so
u=pa=pt^{+}v\in Pv
, as
pt^{+}\in P
. By symmetry we also have
v\in Pu
. It then follows from Lemma
2.5
that
u=v
, and the proof is complete.
∎

As we have already observed, this completes the proof of Theorem
5.82
.

Remark 5.100
.

We noted above that the power semigroup
{\mathcal{P}}(U)
played an important role in Section
5.4
as well, as did its local monoid
P\cdot{\mathcal{P}}(U)\cdot P
. Comparing the above proof of Proposition
5.99
with that of Lemma
5.53
, there is a sense in which we have come full circle. Indeed, for
s\in S
and
u\in U
, the shadow set

\Sigma_{x_{\hat{s},u}}=\{{{}^{t}u}:{t\in\hat{s}}\}

is precisely the set
V_{\hat{s},u}
constructed in Section
5.4
; see (
5.51
). In the proof of Lemma
5.53
, the injectivity of the map
u\mapsto f_{u}
defined in (
5.52
) boiled down to the implication

\hat{1}f_{u}=\hat{1}f_{v}\ \Rightarrow\ u=v\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $u,v\in U$.}

This is of course equivalent to the injectivity of the map

U\to{\mathcal{P}}(U):u\mapsto\hat{1}f_{u}=V_{\hat{1},u}\cdot P.

(5.101)

Since

V_{\hat{1},u}\cdot P=P\cdot V_{\hat{1},u}=P\cdot\Sigma_{x_{\hat{1},u}}=u(\phi_{C}\circ\Xi),

it follows that the map in (
5.101
) is precisely
\phi_{C}\circ\Xi
from the proof of Proposition
5.99
. (But recall that we did not assume
U
itself was commutative in Section
5.4
, only that
P
was central in
U
.)

On the other hand, it does not seem possible to prove Theorem
5.102
(the main result of the next section, which concerns left-regular bands) using techniques similar to those of Sections
5.4
and
5.6
.

5.7
Embedding theorems for proper monoids IV

As already discussed, our main goal in this final section of the chapter is to prove the following:

Theorem 5.102
.

Let
M=US
be a
(U,S)
-proper monoid, with
U
a left-regular band. Then there exists a semidirect product
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}_{L}\rtimes(S/\sigma)
and an embedding
\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}
such that:

(i)

\mathcal{U}_{L}
is a left-regular band,

(ii)

\mathcal{U}_{L}
contains a subsemigroup
U^{\prime}
isomorphic to
U
,

(iii)

\psi\mathord{\restriction}_{U}:U\to U^{\prime}\rtimes\{\hat{1}\}
is an isomorphism.

Working towards the proof, for the rest of the section we fix a
(U,S)
-proper monoid
M=US
, and we assume that
U
is a left-regular band. As usual we write
\mathcal{S}=S/\sigma=\{{\hat{s}}:{s\in S}\}
. Since
U
is left-regular, so too is its submonoid
P=\langle S^{+}\rangle
. It then follows from Lemma
5.32
(cf. Remark
5.33
) that

\sigma=\big\{{(s,t)\in S\times S}:{s^{+}t^{+}s=s^{+}t}\big\}.

(5.103)

This time we define
\mathcal{U}_{L}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R\cup L}\rangle
, where again
X=\{{x_{\hat{s},u}}:{s\in S,\ u\in U}\}
,
R
consists of all the relations

\displaystyle x_{\hat{t},u\cdot{}^{s}v}

\displaystyle=x_{\hat{t},u}x_{\hat{t}\hat{s},v}

for
s,t\in S
and
u,v\in U
with
u=us^{+}
,

(5.104)

and additionally
L
is the set of all relations

\displaystyle x_{\hat{s},u}x_{\hat{t},v}x_{\hat{s},u}

\displaystyle=x_{\hat{s},u}x_{\hat{t},v}

for
s,t\in S
and
u,v\in U
.

(5.105)

By Lemma
2.9
(iii)
, and remembering that
R
contains the relations
x_{\hat{t},u}=x_{\hat{t},u}\cdot x_{\hat{t},u}
for each
t\in S
and
u\in U
(cf. Remark
5.81
),
\mathcal{U}_{L}
is a left-regular band. We write
{\sim}=(R\cup L)^{\sharp}
, and denote the
\sim
-class of
w\in X^{*}
by
\overline{w}
, so that

\mathcal{U}_{L}=\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{R\cup L}\rangle=X^{*}/{\sim}=\{{\overline{w}}:{w\in X^{*}}\}.

As ever, the action of
\mathcal{S}
on
X^{*}
given in (
5.63
) induces a monoidal action of
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathcal{U}_{L}
by monoid morphisms; indeed, we just need to observe that
(w,w^{\prime})\in L\ \Rightarrow\ ({}^{\hat{s}}w,{}^{\hat{s}}w^{\prime})\in L
for all
s\in S
. This then allows us to define the semidirect product

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{U}_{L}\rtimes\mathcal{S}.

We define the map

\phi_{L}:U\to\mathcal{U}_{L}:u\mapsto\overline{x}_{\hat{1},u}.

As in Lemma
5.88
, we have

(u\cdot{}^{s}v)\phi_{L}=(u\phi_{L})\cdot{}^{\hat{s}}(v\phi_{L})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $u,v\in U$ and $s\in S$ with $u=us^{+}$.}

As in Proposition
5.89
, this allows us to define a morphism

\psi:M\to\mathcal{M}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pta\psi=(u\phi_{L},\hat{s})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any natural factorisation $(u,s)$ of $a\in M=US$,}

which is injective if and only if
\phi_{L}
is.

Thus, we can complete the proof of Theorem
5.102
by showing that
\phi_{L}
is injective. The simpler method involving shadows from the previous section does not apply here, as several arguments there relied crucially on commutativity of
U
(see especially the proof of Lemma
5.93
). Thus, we will once again use the
traces
from Definition
5.71
. Recall that these are defined for (some) words from the set
W
, which consists of all non-empty words over
X
whose first letter has the form
x_{\hat{1},u}
for some
u\in U
. Examining (
5.104
) and (
5.105
), note that
W
is still closed under
{\sim}=(R\cup L)^{\sharp}
, in the sense that a pair of
\sim
-equivalent words either both belong to
W
or else both do not (cf. Lemma
5.70
). By Lemma
5.73
, any word from
W
has at most one trace. The main technical result we need is the following version of Lemma
5.74
:

Lemma 5.106
.

Suppose
w,w^{\prime}\in W
are such that
w\sim w^{\prime}
.

(i)

If either of
w
or
w^{\prime}
has a trace, then both do.

(ii)

If
w
and
w^{\prime}
have traces
{\bf p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
{\bf q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
, then
p_{k}=q_{l}
.

Proof.

As with Lemma
5.74
, it suffices to assume that
w
and
w^{\prime}
differ by a single application of a relation from
R\cup C
. Lemma
5.74
has already taken care of the case that the relation is from
R
, so for the rest of the proof we assume that the relation is from
C
. Thus, up to symmetry we have

w=w_{1}\cdot x_{\hat{s},u}x_{\hat{t},v}\cdot w_{2}\and w^{\prime}=w_{1}\cdot x_{\hat{s},u}x_{\hat{t},v}x_{\hat{s},u}\cdot w_{2},

for some
w_{1},w_{2}\in X^{*}
,
s,t\in S
and
u,v\in U
. We also write

w_{1}=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}\and w_{2}=x_{\hat{s}_{m+3},u_{m+3}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where each $s_{i}\in S$ and $u_{i}\in U$,}

noting that either (or both) of
w_{1}
or
w_{2}
could be empty. It is also convenient to define

s_{m+1}=s,\hskip 20.00003ptu_{m+1}=u,\hskip 20.00003pts_{m+2}=t\and u_{m+2}=v.

So then

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}

\displaystyle=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}\cdot x_{\hat{s}_{m+1},u_{m+1}}x_{\hat{s}_{m+2},u_{m+2}}\cdot x_{\hat{s}_{m+3},u_{m+3}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}},

and

\displaystyle w^{\prime}

\displaystyle=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}\cdot x_{\hat{s},u}x_{\hat{t},v}x_{\hat{s},u}\cdot x_{\hat{s}_{m+3},u_{m+3}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}

\displaystyle=x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{m},u_{m}}\cdot x_{\hat{s}_{m+1},u_{m+1}}x_{\hat{s}_{m+2},u_{m+2}}x_{\hat{s}_{m+1},u_{m+1}}\cdot x_{\hat{s}_{m+3},u_{m+3}}\cdots x_{\hat{s}_{k},u_{k}}.

Since
w\in W
, we must have
\hat{s}_{1}=\hat{1}
; in the case that
w_{1}=\iota
(i.e.,
m=0
), this says that
\hat{s}=\hat{1}
. We also define

\displaystyle(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k+1})

\displaystyle=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{m},u,v,u,u_{m+3},\ldots,u_{k})

\displaystyle=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{m},u_{m+1},u_{m+2},u_{m+1},u_{m+3},\ldots,u_{k}).

(5.107)

Note that
v_{i}
is the ‘
U
-coordinate’ of the
i
th letter of
w^{\prime}
.

We begin with the forwards implication.

(
\Rightarrow
). Suppose first that
w
has trace
{\bf p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
, witnessed by
{\bf t}=(t_{2},\ldots,t_{k})\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
, meaning that

•

p_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
, and

•

p_{i-1}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}
for all
2\leq i\leq k
.

We claim that
w^{\prime}
has trace
{\bf q}
, witnessed by
{\bf z}
, for

\displaystyle{\bf z}

\displaystyle=(t_{2},\ldots,t_{m},t_{m+1},t_{m+2},t_{m+1},t_{m+3},\ldots,t_{k})

\displaystyle\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt{\bf q}

\displaystyle=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m},p_{m+1},p_{m+2},p_{m+2},p_{m+3},\ldots,p_{k}).

Note that
{\bf z}
and
{\bf q}
are obtained from
{\bf t}
and
{\bf p}
, respectively, by inserting single entries. Since the final entries of
{\bf p}
and
{\bf q}
are equal, this will complete the proof of the forwards implication. To assist with the following calculations, it is convenient to line up the entries of

and
{\bf q}

x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}

x_{\hat{t},v}

x_{\hat{s},u}

To prove the claim, we follow Definition
5.71
carefully, and we proceed in three steps.

Step 1.
First, it is clear that
{\bf z}\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{m}\times\hat{s}\times\hat{t}\times\hat{s}\times\hat{s}_{m+3}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}
. For this, note that
t_{m+1}\in\hat{s}_{m+1}=\hat{s}
and

.

Step 2.
Next we check that
q_{i}=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}
for all

, where the

.

, and remembering that

\displaystyle q_{i}=p_{i-1}

\displaystyle=(u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{m}}u_{m})\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}{}^{t_{m+2}}u_{m+2}\cdot({}^{t_{m+3}}u_{m+3}\cdots{}^{t_{i-1}}u_{i-1})

\displaystyle=(u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{m}}u_{m})\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}{}^{t_{m+2}}u_{m+2}{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}\cdot({}^{t_{m+3}}u_{m+3}\cdots{}^{t_{i-1}}u_{i-1})

\displaystyle=(v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{m}}v_{m})\cdot{}^{z_{m+1}}v_{m+1}\cdot{}^{z_{m+2}}v_{m+2}{}^{z_{m+3}}v_{m+3}\cdot({}^{z_{m+4}}v_{m+4}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}).

Step 3.

Finally, we check that
q_{i-1}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}
for all

.

For

.

For

\displaystyle q_{m+2}=p_{m+2}

\displaystyle=p_{m}\cdot{}^{t_{m+1}}u_{m+1}{}^{t_{m+2}}u_{m+2}

by Lemma

(iv)

.

(
\Leftarrow
). Conversely, we now assume
w^{\prime}
has trace

meaning that

We will shortly construct a suitable trace for

q_{m+2}=q_{m+3}.

Working towards this, and defining

\displaystyle q_{i}

\displaystyle=q_{j-1}\cdot{}^{z_{j}}v_{j}\cdot{}^{z_{j+1}}{v_{j+1}}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}

(iv)

\displaystyle=q_{j-1}\cdot{}^{z_{j}}v_{j}\cdot z_{j}^{+}\cdot{}^{z_{j+1}}{v_{j+1}}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}\cdot z_{j}^{+}

\displaystyle=q_{j-1}\cdot{}^{z_{j}}v_{j}\cdot{}^{z_{j+1}}{v_{j+1}}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}\cdot z_{j}^{+}

by Lemma

again

\displaystyle=q_{i}z_{j}^{+}.

z_{m+1}\mathrel{\sigma}z_{m+3}.

).

We now claim that

.
This time the entries of

x_{\hat{s}_{1},u_{1}}

x_{\hat{s}_{2},u_{2}}

x_{\hat{s},u}

x_{\hat{t},v}

x_{\hat{s}_{m+3},u_{m+3}}

=

=

x_{\hat{s}_{m+1},u_{m+1}}

x_{\hat{s}_{m+2},u_{m+2}}

As ever, we proceed in three steps.

Step 1.

We first note that
{\bf t}\in\hat{s}_{2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{m}\times\hat{s}\times\hat{t}\times\hat{s}_{m+2}\times\cdots\times\hat{s}_{k}

p_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}u_{i}
for all

.

•

For
1\leq i\leq m+2
we have
p_{i}=q_{i}=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{i}}v_{i}=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i}
.

•

For
m+3\leq i\leq k
we have

\displaystyle p_{i}=q_{i+1}

\displaystyle=q_{m+3}\cdot{}^{z_{m+4}}v_{m+4}\cdots{}^{z_{i+1}}v_{i+1}

\displaystyle=q_{m+2}\cdot{}^{z_{m+4}}v_{m+4}\cdots{}^{z_{i+1}}v_{i+1}

by (
5.109
)

\displaystyle=v_{1}\cdot{}^{z_{2}}v_{2}\cdots{}^{z_{m+2}}v_{m+2}\cdot{}^{z_{m+4}}v_{m+4}\cdots{}^{z_{i+1}}v_{i+1}

\displaystyle=u_{1}\cdot{}^{t_{2}}u_{2}\cdots{}^{t_{m+2}}u_{m+2}\cdot{}^{t_{m+3}}u_{m+3}\cdots{}^{t_{i}}u_{i},

as required.

Step 3.

Finally, we check that
p_{i-1}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}
for all
2\leq i\leq k
.

•

For
2\leq i\leq m+2
we have
p_{i-1}=q_{i-1}=q_{i-1}z_{i}^{+}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}
.

•

For
i=m+3
, we use (
5.109
) to calculate

p_{m+2}=q_{m+2}=q_{m+3}=q_{m+3}z_{m+4}^{+}=p_{m+2}t_{m+3}^{+}.

•

For
m+4\leq i\leq k
we have
p_{i-1}=q_{i}=q_{i}z_{i+1}^{+}=p_{i-1}t_{i}^{+}
.

We have now finally completed the proof.
∎

As in Section
5.6
(cf. Proposition
5.80
) the injectivity of
\phi_{L}:U\to\mathcal{U}_{L}
follows quickly from Lemma
5.106
. As observed above, this completes the proof of Theorem
5.102
.

Remark 5.113
.

Theorem
5.82
(proved in the previous section) concerns two special cases:

•

U
is commutative, meaning that it satisfies the identity
xy=yx
,

•

U
is a semilattice, meaning that it satisfies the identities
xy=yx
and
x^{2}=x
.

Similarly, Theorem
5.102
concerns the special case in which:

•

U
is a left-regular band, meaning that it satisfies the identities
xyx=xy
and
x^{2}=x
.

The reader might wonder why we did not give a statement for the case that
U
is assumed only to satisfy
xyx=xy
. The simple reason for this is that
U
being a monoid means that the identity
xyx=xy
implies
x^{2}=x
upon substituting
y=1
. On the other hand, the identities
xy=yx
and
x^{2}=x
are independent of each other.

We leave it as an open problem to classify the (collections of) identities on
U
that lead to analogous embedding results. For example, if
M=US
is a
(U,S)
-proper monoid, with
U
a band, does there exist an embedding
M\to\mathcal{U}_{B}\rtimes\mathcal{S}
, with
\mathcal{U}_{B}
a band containing a copy of
U
?

Even in the case of a
right
-regular band (satisfying
xyx=yx
and
x^{2}=x
), it is not clear whether such an embedding will exist. If it does, then it is likely that a very different proof strategy would be required. Indeed, the method of Sections
5.4
,
5.6
and
5.7
relied crucially on
P
satisfying an identity of the form
f(x,y)x=g(x,y)y
, so that Lemma
5.32
applied and gave an equational formulation of the congruence
\sigma
. But neither of the defining identities for right-regular bands are of this form.

Part II
Presentations

This part of the paper explicitly focusses on presentations by generators and relations.
Since so many naturally occuring semigroups arise from action pairs, our purpose here is to develop a body of general results concerning presentations for such semigroups. We then apply these to a number of important examples.

Chapter
6
contains the main theoretical results of this part of the paper, each of which gives a presentation for a semigroup arising from an action pair, modulo various natural assumptions. Roughly speaking, stricter assumptions lead to smaller sets of relations. In particular, the results and/or proofs tend to be simpler when the semigroup in question is in fact a monoid.
Chapters
7
–
9
then apply the general machinery developed in Chapter
6
to several examples. Specifically, we treat:

•

free left restriction monoids in Chapter
7
,

•

several monoids and semigroups of (partial) endomorphisms of an independence algebra in Chapter
8
, and

•

a number of transformational wreath products in Chapter
9
.

The introductions to these chapters contain full summaries of the results they contain, so we now turn to the general results promised above.

6
Presentations

As just discussed, the purpose of this chapter is prove a number of general results on presentations. Each such result concerns a semigroup of the form
US
arising from an action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
. (In fact, some results hold more generally for
weak
action pairs.) In an ideal world, one would hope that a presentation for
US
could be ‘pieced together’ from presentations for
U
and
S
, in the following sense:

•

We start with presentations
\langle{X_{U}}:{R_{U}}\rangle
and
\langle{X_{S}}:{R_{S}}\rangle
for
U
and
S
.

•

We then hope to obtain a presentation for
US
of the form
\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R}\rangle
for some additional set of relations
R
over
X_{U}\cup X_{S}
.

This is indeed possible in some cases, a number of which we treat in the current chapter.

•

Sections
6.1
–
6.3
deal with the case in which
U
and
S
are both submonoids of
M
, the main results here being Theorems
6.5
,
6.13
,
6.28
,
6.30
and
6.32
. In Remark
6.6
we apply the results of Section
4.5
to give especially neat presentations when certain natural conditions hold, and in particular we are able to deduce the main results of
[
24
]
as a (very) special case.

•

The case in which only
U
is assumed to be a submonoid (and certain other conditions hold) is covered in Section
6.4
; see Theorems
6.36
and
6.44
. It turns out that this case, and the previous one, include a great many important and natural examples, several of which will be covered in subsequent chapters.

•

Section
6.5
considers the case in which only
S
is assumed to be a submonoid, and we will see that this is rather more complicated; see Theorem
6.50
.

Unfortunately, the hope described above cannot be realised in full generality. Indeed, Example
4.32
(iii)
shows that
US
might not even contain isomorphic copies of
U
or
S
. Moreover, even when
US
does contain copies of both
U
and
S
, the hope is still unrealistic, even for extremely simple semigroups
U
and
S
. For example, if
U
and
S
are both free monogenic semigroups (isomorphic to the positive integers under addition), then the direct product
U\times S
(the simplest possible case of the
(U\rtimes S)/\theta
construction) is not even finitely generated
[
65
,
106
,
37
]
.

Given the above considerations, the next result seems to be the most general one could hope to give concerning presentations for
US
. It is a special case of (the semigroup version of) Lemma
2.8
. The statement utilises the surmorphism
\pi:U\rtimes S\to US
from Proposition
4.46
, with kernel
\theta
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a weak action pair, and suppose the semidirect product
U\rtimes S
has presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle
via
\phi:X^{+}\to U\rtimes S
. Let
N:U\rtimes S\to X^{+}
be a normal form function, and suppose
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
. Then
US
has presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X}:{R\cup R_{\Omega}}\rangle
via
\phi\pi:X^{+}\to US
, where
R_{\Omega}=\big\{{(N({\bf a}),N({\bf b}))}:{({\bf a},{\bf b})\in\Omega}\big\}
. ∎

Remark 6.2
.

The previous result uses a presentation for the semidirect product
U\rtimes S
as a ‘black box’. Presentations for
U\rtimes S
are not always readily constructable from presentations for
U
and
S
, as already discussed (even for the simplest case of
direct
products). However, when
U\rtimes S
is a monoid, neat constructions are given by Lavers
[
74
]
; we will utilise these in Section
6.1
.

The case in which
U
is a monoid, and the semigroup
S
acts on
U(=U^{1})
by monoid morphisms, is considered in
[
41
]
, where a presentation for
U\rtimes S
is constructed from a presentation for
S
and the entire multiplication table of
U
. As we will see in Section
6.5
, similar presentations exist in the case that
S
is a monoid with a monoidal action on
U^{1}
; see Theorem
6.50
.

6.1
Two submonoids I

In this section we consider the simplest special case, where
U\rtimes S
is a monoid. By Corollary
3.13
, this occurs precisely when
U
and
S
are both monoids, with
S
acting on monoidally on
U(=U^{1})
by monoid morphisms.

For the duration of this section, we fix a weak action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
, with
U
and
S
submonoids, and we assume the action in
(A1)
is by monoid morphisms. In other words, we assume that
U
and
S
are submonoids of
M
, and that
(SA1)′
holds.
We will later additionally assume that
(U,S)
is an action pair, not just a weak action pair. In this case,
(U,S)
is strong, by Lemma
4.15
(ii)
.

We also fix monoid presentations for
U
and
S
:

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}}:{R_{U}}\rangle
via
\phi_{U}:X_{U}^{*}\to U
and
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{S}}:{R_{S}}\rangle
via
\phi_{S}:X_{S}^{*}\to S
.

For convenience, and without loss of generality, we assume that:

•

X_{U}
and
X_{S}
are disjoint,

•

no letter of
X_{U}
or
X_{S}
maps to
1
, and

•

\phi_{U}\mathord{\restriction}_{X_{U}}
and
\phi_{S}\mathord{\restriction}_{X_{S}}
are injective.

We write
\overline{w}=w\phi_{U}
and
\overline{v}=v\phi_{S}
for all
w\in X_{U}^{*}
and
v\in X_{S}^{*}
. There is no chance of confusion here, as the only word common to
X_{U}^{*}
and
X_{S}^{*}
is the empty word
\iota
, and we have
\iota\phi_{U}=\iota\phi_{S}=1
. We also fix normal form functions

N_{U}:U\to X_{U}^{*}\and N_{S}:S\to X_{S}^{*}.

We may assume that
N_{U}(1)=N_{S}(1)=\iota
, and also that
N_{U}(\overline{x})=x
and
N_{S}(\overline{y})=y
for all
x\in X_{U}
and
y\in X_{S}
.

Since
U
,
S
and
U\rtimes S
are all monoids, a presentation for the semidirect product
U\rtimes S
can be constructed using a result of Lavers
[
74
]
, which we state below. Lavers’ presentation is built out of the presentations
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}}:{R_{U}}\rangle
and
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{S}}:{R_{S}}\rangle
, and an additional set
R_{1}
of relations over
X_{U}\cup X_{S}
that capture the action of
S
on
U(=U^{1})
from
(A1)
. To motivate these additional relations, consider letters
x\in X_{S}
and
y\in X_{U}
. Since
\overline{x}\in S
and
\overline{y}\in U
, within
M
we have
{\overline{x}\cdot\overline{y}={}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y}\cdot\overline{x}}
, with
{}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y}\in U(=U^{1})
. The set
R_{1}
represents each such equation as a relation, and is defined by

R_{1}=\big\{{(xy,{}^{x}y\cdot x)}:{x\in X_{S},\ y\in X_{U}}\big\},

(6.3)

where for convenience we write
{}^{x}y=N_{U}({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y})\in X_{U}^{*}
.
The next result is
[
74
, Corollary 2]
; it is also a special case of Theorem
6.30
below.

Theorem 6.4
.

If
U
and
S
are monoids, and if
S
acts monoidally on
U(=U^{1})
by monoid morphisms, then with the above notation, the monoid
U\rtimes S
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}}\rangle

via

\phi:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}\to U\rtimes S:x\mapsto\begin{cases}(x\phi_{U},1)&\text{if $x\in X_{U}$,}\\
(1,x\phi_{S})&\text{if $x\in X_{S}$.}\end{cases}

By Proposition
4.46
,
US\cong(U\rtimes S)/\theta
is a homomorphic image of
U\rtimes S
. Thus, we can use Lemma
2.8
to extend the above presentation for
U\rtimes S
to a presentation for
US
via the surmorphism

\Phi=\phi\pi:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}\to US:x\mapsto\begin{cases}x\phi_{U}&\text{if $x\in X_{U}$,}\\
x\phi_{S}&\text{if $x\in X_{S}$.}\end{cases}

In the next statement we use the canonical normal form function
N:U\rtimes S\to(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}
defined by
N(u,s)=N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s)
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a weak action pair, with
U
and
S
both submonoids, and with
S
acting on
U(=U^{1})
by monoid morphisms. Suppose also that
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
. Then with the above notation, the monoid
US
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{\Omega}}\rangle

via
\Phi
, where
R_{\Omega}=\big\{{(N({\bf a}),N({\bf b}))}:{({\bf a},{\bf b})\in\Omega}\big\}
. ∎

Remark 6.6
.

In the special case that
(U,S)
is an action pair (and not just a weak action pair), we have already observed that
(U,S)
is in fact strong. In this case, we can take
\Omega
to be the generating set for
\theta
from Lemma
4.65
, and then

\displaystyle R_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s),N_{U}(u)N_{S}(t))}:{u\in U,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{u}}\big\},

(6.7)

where the
\Omega_{u}
generate the right congruences
\theta_{u}
from (
4.49
).

Lemmas
4.71
,
4.73
and
4.74
allow us to replace the above
R_{\Omega}
with a smaller set of relations if the pair
(U,S)
satisfies any of the additional conditions listed in these lemmas. When Lemma
4.71
(i)
applies, we can take

\displaystyle R_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(N_{U}(v)N_{S}(s),N_{U}(v)N_{S}(t))}:{v\in V,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{v}}\big\},

(6.8)

for a suitable subset
V\subseteq U
. If Lemma
4.71
(ii)
applies, then since
U=\langle\overline{X}_{U}\rangle
, we can take

\displaystyle R_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(xN_{S}(s),xN_{S}(t))}:{x\in X_{U},\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{\overline{x}}}\big\}.

(6.9)

In the (very) special case that
S
is a group,
(U,S)
is automatically strong by Lemma
4.23
(ii)
. Lemma
4.73
then applies, and we can take

\displaystyle R_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(N_{U}(u),N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s))}:{u\in U,\ s\in\Gamma_{u}}\big\},

(6.10)

where the
\Gamma_{u}
generate the subgroups
S_{u}
from (
4.72
). Again, this can be further simplified if either of the conditions of Lemma
4.74
hold. When Lemma
4.74
(i)
applies, we can take

\displaystyle R_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(N_{U}(v),N_{U}(v)N_{S}(s))}:{v\in V,\ s\in\Gamma_{v}}\big\}

(6.11)

for a suitable subset
V\subseteq U
. When Lemma
4.74
(ii)
applies, we can take

\displaystyle R_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(x,xN_{S}(s))}:{x\in X_{U},\ s\in\Gamma_{\overline{x}}}\big\}

(6.12)

In particular, these versions of Theorem
6.5
include the (very very) special case of factorisable inverse monoids (cf. Example
4.27
), where
U
and
S
are respectively the semilattice of idempotents and group of units of
US
, and we therefore obtain the main results of
[
24
]
as corollaries.

6.2
Two submonoids II

We now consider the more general case in which
U
and
S
are still submonoids of the over-monoid
M
, but we no longer assume
U\rtimes S
is a monoid. Even though
US
is still a submonoid of
M
in this case, we cannot hope to obtain a presentation for
US
via Lavers’ Theorem
6.4
, as this obviously does not apply when
U\rtimes S
is not a monoid. We will see in Theorem
6.13
, however, that we can still obtain a rather neat presentation for
US
that bears some resemblence to Theorem
6.5
, and the simplifications discussed in Remark
6.6
. We explore the reason behind this resemblance in Section
6.3
.

Throughout this section we fix an action pair
(U,S)
in a monoid
M
, and we assume that
U
and
S
are submonoids. Again we fix presentations for
U
and
S
:

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}}:{R_{U}}\rangle
via
\phi_{U}:X_{U}^{*}\to U
and
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{S}}:{R_{S}}\rangle
via
\phi_{S}:X_{S}^{*}\to S
.

We assume again that:

•

X_{U}
and
X_{S}
are disjoint,

•

no letter of
X_{U}
or
X_{S}
maps to
1
, and

•

\phi_{U}\mathord{\restriction}_{X_{U}}
and
\phi_{S}\mathord{\restriction}_{X_{S}}
are injective.

We again write
\overline{w}=w\phi_{U}
and
\overline{v}=v\phi_{S}
for
w\in X_{U}^{*}
and
v\in X_{S}^{*}
, and fix normal form functions

N_{U}:U\to X_{U}^{*}\and N_{S}:S\to X_{S}^{*},

assuming that
N_{U}(1)=N_{S}(1)=\iota
, and that
N_{U}(\overline{x})=x
and
N_{S}(\overline{y})=y
for
x\in X_{U}
and
y\in X_{S}
. Since
US=\langle\overline{X}_{U}\cup\overline{X}_{S}\rangle
, as
U
and
S
are submonoids, we still have a surmorphism

\Phi:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}\to US:x\mapsto\begin{cases}x\phi_{U}&\text{if $x\in X_{U}$,}\\
x\phi_{S}&\text{if $x\in X_{S}$.}\end{cases}

As in the previous section, we define

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(xy,{}^{x}y\cdot x)}:{x\in X_{S},\ y\in X_{U}}\big\},

where again we write
{}^{x}y=N_{U}({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y})\in X_{U}^{*}
. We also define

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s),N_{U}(u)N_{S}(t))}:{u\in U,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{u}}\big\},

where each
\Omega_{u}
generates
\theta_{u}
(
u\in U
) as a right congruence. This time, since
(U,S)
might not be strong, we also need the relations

\displaystyle R_{3}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(x,x^{+}x)}:{x\in X_{S},\ \overline{x}^{+}\not=1}\big\},

where here we write
x^{+}=N_{U}(\overline{x}^{+})
for each
x\in X_{S}
. Note that
x^{+}=\iota
if
\overline{x}^{+}=1
, so that we do not need to include a relation
(x,x^{+}x)
for such an
x
. In particular,
R_{3}=\varnothing
if
(U,S)
is strong; cf. Lemma
4.15
(ii)
. It is also worth noting that
\overline{x^{+}}=\overline{x}^{+}
, by definition of
N_{U}
.

Theorem 6.13
.

If
(U,S)
is an action pair, and if
U
and
S
are both submonoids, then with the above notation, the monoid
US
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup R_{3}}\rangle

via
\Phi
.

Proof.

We have already noted that
\Phi
is a surmorphism, and it is routine to check that

R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup R_{3}\subseteq\ker(\Phi).

It therefore remains to show that
\ker(\Phi)\subseteq(R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup R_{3})^{\sharp}
, and for this we require some technical lemmas. But first we fix notation.

We write
\overline{w}=w\Phi
for
w\in(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}
. We also use the abbreviations

{}^{w}v=N_{U}({}^{\overline{w}}\overline{v})\and w^{+}=N_{U}(\overline{w}^{+})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $w\in X_{S}^{*}$ and $v\in X_{U}^{*}$.}

In particular,
{}^{\iota}v=N_{U}({}^{1}\overline{v})=N_{U}(\overline{v})
for all
v\in X_{U}^{*}
. By definition of
N_{U}
, we have

\overline{{}^{w}v}={}^{\overline{w}}\overline{v}\and\overline{w^{+}}=\overline{w}^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $w\in X_{S}^{*}$ and $v\in X_{U}^{*}$.}

(6.14)

We also note that

w^{+}=N_{U}(\overline{w}^{+})=N_{U}({}^{\overline{w}}1)={}^{w}\iota\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $w\in X_{S}^{*}$.}

(6.15)

We write
{\sim}=(R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup R_{3})^{\sharp}
for the congruence on
(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}
generated by the relations. We also write
{\sim_{U}}=R_{U}^{\sharp}
, and similarly for
\sim_{S}
,
\sim_{1}
and so on. This will allow us to indicate which of the various sets of relations are used in the manipulations of words to follow.

Lemma 6.16
.

For any
x\in X_{S}
and
v\in X_{U}^{*}
, we have
xv\sim{}^{x}v\cdot x
.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
k=\ell(v)
, the length (number of letters) of
v
. If
k=0
, then
v=\iota
, and we have
xv=x\sim_{3}x^{+}x={}^{x}\iota\cdot x={}^{x}v\cdot x
, where we used (
6.15
) in the third step. (In the second step, note that
x=x^{+}x
if
\overline{x}^{+}=1
.)

We now assume that
k\geq 1
, so that
v=wy
for some
w\in X_{U}^{*}
and
y\in X_{U}
with
\ell(w)=k-1
. Then by induction,
xv=xwy\sim{}^{x}w\cdot xy\sim_{1}{}^{x}w\cdot{}^{x}y\cdot x
, so it remains to show that
{}^{x}w\cdot{}^{x}y\sim{}^{x}v
. But this follows quickly from properties of the action and of normal form functions (cf. (
2.7
)), as

{}^{x}w\cdot{}^{x}y=N_{U}({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{w})\cdot N_{U}({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y})\sim_{U}N_{U}({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{w}\cdot{}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y})=N_{U}({}^{\overline{x}}(\overline{w}\cdot\overline{y}))=N_{U}({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{v})={}^{x}v.\qed

Lemma 6.17
.

For any
w\in X_{S}^{*}
and
v\in X_{U}^{*}
, we have
wv\sim{}^{w}v\cdot w
.

Proof.

We use induction on
k=\ell(w)
. The
k=0
case is trivial, as
{{}^{\iota}v=N_{U}(\overline{v})\sim_{U}v}
. For
k\geq 1
, we write
w=w^{\prime}x
, where
w^{\prime}\in X_{S}^{*}
and
x\in X_{S}
with
\ell(w^{\prime})=k-1
. Then by induction and Lemma
6.16
, we have
wv=w^{\prime}xv\sim w^{\prime}\cdot{}^{x}v\cdot x\sim{}^{w^{\prime}}\!({}^{x}v)\cdot w^{\prime}x={}^{w^{\prime}}\!({}^{x}v)\cdot w
, so it remains to show that
{}^{w^{\prime}}\!({}^{x}v)\sim{}^{w}v
. For this we have

{}^{w^{\prime}}\!({}^{x}v)=N_{U}\big({}^{\overline{w}^{\prime}}\!(\overline{{}^{x}v})\big)=N_{U}\big({}^{\overline{w}^{\prime}}\!({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{v})\big)=N_{U}({}^{\overline{w}^{\prime}\overline{x}}\overline{v})=N_{U}({}^{\overline{w}}\overline{v})={}^{w}v,

where we used (
6.14
) in the second step.
∎

Lemma 6.18
.

For any
w\in X_{S}^{*}
, we have
w\sim w^{+}w
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.17
and (
6.15
), we have
w=w\cdot\iota\sim{}^{w}\iota\cdot w=w^{+}w
.
∎

Remark 6.19
.

For later reference, it is important to note that the proof so far has not used any of the relations from
R_{2}
.

Returning now to the proof of the theorem, we recall that the last remaining step is to show that
\ker(\Phi)\subseteq{\sim}
. To do this, let
(w,v)\in\ker(\Phi)
: i.e.,
w,v\in(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}
and
\overline{w}=\overline{v}
. We must show that
w\sim v
.

We first observe that

w\sim_{1}w_{1}w_{2}\and v\sim_{1}v_{1}v_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $w_{1},v_{1}\in X_{U}^{*}$ and $w_{2},v_{2}\in X_{S}^{*}$.}

(6.20)

It follows from Lemma
6.18
that

w\sim w_{1}w_{2}^{+}w_{2}\and v\sim v_{1}v_{2}^{+}v_{2}.

(6.21)

From (
6.20
), we have

\overline{w}_{1}\overline{w}_{2}=\overline{w}=\overline{v}=\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with $\overline{w}_{1},\overline{v}_{1}\in U$ and $\overline{w}_{2},\overline{v}_{2}\in S$.}

It follows from
(A2)
that
\overline{w}_{1}\overline{w}_{2}^{+}=\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}^{+}
, and we denote this element of
U
by
u
. Now,

\overline{w_{1}w_{2}^{+}}=\overline{w}_{1}\overline{w}_{2}^{+}=u\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{v_{1}v_{2}^{+}}=u,

so it follows that
w_{1}w_{2}^{+}\sim N_{U}(u)\sim v_{1}v_{2}^{+}
. Combining this with (
6.21
), we have

w\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot w_{2}\and v\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot v_{2},

(6.22)

so it remains to show that

N_{U}(u)\cdot w_{2}\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot v_{2}.

(6.23)

Applying
\Phi
to (
6.22
), we have
u\cdot\overline{w}_{2}=\overline{w}=\overline{v}=u\cdot\overline{v}_{2}
, so that
(\overline{w}_{2},\overline{v}_{2})\in\theta_{u}
. Since
\theta_{u}
is generated as a right congruence by
\Omega_{u}
, it follows that there is a sequence

\overline{w}_{2}=s_{1}\to s_{2}\to\cdots\to s_{k}=\overline{v}_{2}

where
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in S
, and such that for each
1\leq i<k
,

s_{i}=a_{i}c_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts_{i+1}=b_{i}c_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $(a_{i},b_{i})\in\Omega_{u}\cup\Omega_{u}^{-1}$ and $c_{i}\in S(=S^{1})$.}

Since
N_{U}(u)\cdot w_{2}\sim_{S}N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{1})
and
N_{U}(u)\cdot v_{2}\sim_{S}N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{k})
, we can complete the proof of (
6.23
), and hence of the theorem, by showing that

N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{i})\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{i+1})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for each $1\leq i<k$.}

But for any such
i
we have

N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{i})\sim_{S}N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(a_{i})\cdot N_{S}(c_{i})\sim_{2}N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(b_{i})\cdot N_{S}(c_{i})\sim_{S}N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{i+1}),

as required.
∎

Remark 6.24
.

When the pair
(U,S)
is strong, Theorem
6.13
reduces to Theorem
6.5
, or more specifically to the version of Theorem
6.5
where
R_{\Omega}
is the set of relations in (
6.7
).

Remark 6.25
.

The presentation

\displaystyle\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup R_{3}}\rangle

(6.26)

for
US
from Theorem
6.13
contains/extends the presentation

\displaystyle\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}}\rangle.

(6.27)

Lavers’ Theorem
6.4
says that (
6.27
) is a presentation for the semidirect product
U\rtimes S
in the case that the latter is a monoid. This is obviously not true when
{U\rtimes S}
is not a monoid, so the reader may wonder ‘why’ our presentation (
6.26
) for
US
still extends the presentation (
6.27
). We will explore this question in Section
6.3
, where we will in fact see (in Theorem
6.30
) that the intermediate

\displaystyle\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{3}}\rangle

is a presentation for the (local) monoid
\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}\leq U\rtimes S
.

In the presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup R_{3}}\rangle
from Theorem
6.13
, the sets of relations
R_{1}
and
R_{3}
are quantified over letters from
X_{U}\cup X_{S}
: i.e., generators for
U
and
S
. However,
R_{2}
is quantified over all of
U
. Ideally, one would like to replace
R_{2}
with a set of relations quantified over
X_{U}
. This is sometimes possible, but not always. The second part of the next result describes one such situation, and the first part describes a more general situation in which some degree of simplification of
R_{2}
is still possible. The conditions are similar to those discussed in Section
4.5
, but note that we do not need to assume the pair
(U,S)
is strong here. In the statement,
\preceq
is the relation on
U
given in (
4.67
).

Theorem 6.28
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is an action pair, with
U
and
S
submonoids.

(i)

If there exists a subset
V\subseteq U
such that

\theta_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop v\preceq u}\theta_{v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$,}

then
US
has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}^{\prime}\cup R_{3}}\rangle
via
\Phi
, where

R_{2}^{\prime}=\big\{{(N_{U}(v)N_{S}(s),N_{U}(v)N_{S}(t))}:{v\in V,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{v}}\big\}.

(ii)

If
U
is commutative, and if
\theta_{uv}=\theta_{u}\vee\theta_{v}
for all
u,v\in U
, then
US
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}^{\prime\prime}\cup R_{3}}\rangle

via
\Phi
, where
R_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\big\{{(xN_{S}(s),xN_{S}(t))}:{x\in X_{U},\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{\overline{x}}}\big\}
.

Proof.

As with Lemma
4.71
, it suffices to prove
(i)
. For this, we proceed in similar fashion to the proof of Lemma
4.71
(i)
.

By Theorem
6.13
, and since
R_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq R_{2}
, it suffices to show that
R_{2}\subseteq{\approx}
, where this time we write
{\approx}=(R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}^{\prime}\cup R_{3})^{\sharp}
. For this, let
u\in U
and
(s,t)\in\Omega_{u}
. We must show that
N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s)\approx N_{U}(u)N_{S}(t)
.
Again we write
V_{u}=\{{v\in V}:{v\preceq u}\}
. Since
(s,t)\in\theta_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in V_{u}}\theta_{v}
, there is a sequence

s=s_{1}\to s_{2}\to\cdots\to s_{k}=t,

such that each
(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in\bigcup_{v\in V_{u}}(\Omega_{v}\cup\Omega_{v}^{-1})
, and it is enough to show that

N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s_{i})\approx N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s_{i+1})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i<k$.}

Fix some such
i
, so that
(s_{i},s_{i+1})\in\Omega_{v}\cup\Omega_{v}^{-1}
for some
v\in V_{u}
. Then
u=wv
for some
w\in U(=U^{1})
, and

N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s_{i})\approx N_{U}(w)\cdot N_{U}(v)N_{S}(s_{i})\approx N_{U}(w)\cdot N_{U}(v)N_{S}(s_{i+1})\approx N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s_{i+1}).\qed

Remark 6.29
.

In the very special case that
S
is additionally assumed to be a group, the pair
(U,S)
is then strong. Simplifications arising in this case have already been discussed in Remark
6.6
.

6.3
Two submonoids III

In this section we briefly discuss an alternative way to prove Theorem
6.13
, and also give a generalisation of Theorem
6.5
; see Theorem
6.32
. This involves proving an additional general result, Theorem
6.30
, which is of independent interest, and which will in fact be directly applicable when we consider free left restriction monoids in Chapter
7
.

So again we consider a weak action pair
(U,S)
, where
U
and
S
are both submonoids of the over-monoid (which shall not be named in this section). By Proposition
4.46
, the monoid
US
is a homomorphic image of
U\rtimes S
, though the latter need not be a monoid; cf. Lemma
3.11
. In the case that
U\rtimes S

is
a monoid, Theorem
6.5
built a presentation for
US
, starting with Lavers’ (monoid) presentation for
U\rtimes S
from Theorem
6.4
. As we noted in the previous section, however, this approach is obviously not available in the case that
U\rtimes S
is not a monoid. Despite this, the presentation for
US
from Theorem
6.13
(which applies when
(U,S)
is an action pair, not just a weak action pair) certainly ‘looks like’ it has been built in this way (cf. Remark
6.25
), and we explore the reason for this in the current section.

Recall from Remark
4.47
that the natural surmorphism
\pi:U\rtimes S\to US:(u,s)\mapsto us
factors through the local monoid

M=(1,1)\cdot(U\rtimes S)\cdot(1,1)=\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}\leq U\rtimes S,

so that
US\cong M/\vartheta
, where
\vartheta=\ker(\pi\mathord{\restriction}_{M})
. It follows that one could obtain a presentation for
US
by first finding a (monoid) presentation for
M
, then finding a generating set for the congruence
\vartheta
, and then finally applying Lemma
2.8
. We show here how to do the first of these tasks; see Theorem
6.30
.

The next result concerns a monoid
S
acting monoidally on a monoid
U
by semigroup morphisms. In particular, this is the case whenever
(U,S)
is a weak action pair; cf. Remark
4.4
.
For the statement and proof we keep the notation of Section
6.2
, in particular:

•

the presentations and normal form functions for
U
and
S
,

•

the over-line notation for words over
X_{U}
and
X_{S}
, and

•

the additional sets of relations
R_{1}
and
R_{3}
(the relations
R_{2}
are not required).

We also define the morphism

\varphi:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}\to M:x\mapsto\begin{cases}(\overline{x},1)&\text{if $x\in X_{U}$,}\\
(\overline{x}^{+},\overline{x})&\text{if $x\in X_{S}$.}\end{cases}

Theorem 6.30
.

If the monoid
S
acts monoidally on the monoid
U
by semigroup morphisms, then the (local) monoid
M=\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{3}}\rangle

via
\varphi
.

Proof.

First, for any
(u,s)\in M
we have

(u,s)=(us^{+},s)=(u,1)\cdot(s^{+},s)=N_{U}(u)\varphi\cdot N_{S}(s)\varphi=(N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s))\varphi,

and this shows that
\varphi
is surjective. (In the above calculation, it is clear that
N_{U}(u)\varphi=(u,1)
. For
N_{S}(s)\varphi=(s^{+},s)
, we apply (
5.15
).)

Next, it is easy to check that
\varphi
preserves the relations, so that
{\approx}\subseteq\ker(\varphi)
, where we write
{{\approx}=(R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{3})^{\sharp}}
. Here we just show that
R_{1}\subseteq\ker(\varphi)
. For this, let
x\in X_{S}
and
y\in X_{U}
. Then, using Lemma
3.1
(iv)
, we have

(xy)\varphi=(\overline{x}^{+},\overline{x})\cdot(\overline{y},1)=(\overline{x}^{+}\cdot{}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y},\overline{x})=({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y},\overline{x})=({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y}\cdot\overline{x}^{+},\overline{x})=({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y},1)\cdot(\overline{x}^{+},\overline{x})=({}^{x}y\cdot x)\varphi.

To complete the proof we must show that
\ker(\varphi)\subseteq{\approx}
, so suppose
(w,v)\in\ker(\varphi)
. As in Lemmas
6.16
–
6.18
(cf. Remark
6.19
), we have

w\approx w_{1}w_{2}\approx w_{1}w_{2}^{+}w_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptv\approx v_{1}v_{2}\approx v_{1}v_{2}^{+}v_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $w_{1},v_{1}\in X_{U}^{*}$ and $w_{2},v_{2}\in X_{S}^{*}$.}

But then

w\varphi=w_{1}\varphi\cdot w_{2}\varphi=(\overline{w}_{1},1)\cdot(\overline{w}_{2}^{+},\overline{w}_{2})=(\overline{w}_{1}\overline{w}_{2}^{+},\overline{w}_{2})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003ptv\varphi=(\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}^{+},\overline{v}_{2}).

Since
w\varphi=v\varphi
, it follows that
\overline{w}_{1}\overline{w}_{2}^{+}=\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}^{+}
and
\overline{w}_{2}=\overline{v}_{2}
, so that
w_{1}w_{2}^{+}\sim_{U}v_{1}v_{2}^{+}
and
w_{2}\sim_{S}v_{2}
. But then

w\approx w_{1}w_{2}^{+}\cdot w_{2}\approx v_{1}v_{2}^{+}\cdot v_{2}\approx v,

and the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 6.31
.

When the action of
S
on
U
is additionally by monoid morphisms (i.e., when each
s^{+}=1
), the semidirect product
U\rtimes S
is a monoid, and
M=U\rtimes S
; cf. Corollary
3.13
and Proposition
3.32
. In this case we have
R_{3}=\varnothing
, and Theorem
6.30
reduces to Lavers’ Theorem
6.4
.

An application of Lemma
2.8
yields the following more general version of Theorem
6.5
. In the statement we use the surmorphism

\Phi=\varphi\circ\pi\mathord{\restriction}_{M}:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}\to US:x\mapsto\begin{cases}x\phi_{U}&\text{if $x\in X_{U}$,}\\
x\phi_{S}&\text{if $x\in X_{S}$,}\end{cases}

where
\varphi:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}\to M
is as in Theorem
6.30
. We also refer to the congruence
\vartheta=\ker(\pi\mathord{\restriction}_{M})
, and the canonical normal form function
N:M\to(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{*}
defined by
N(u,s)=N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s)
.

Theorem 6.32
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is a weak action pair, with
U
and
S
both submonoids, and suppose also that
\vartheta=\Omega^{\sharp}
. Then with the above notation, the monoid
US
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{3}\cup R_{\Omega}}\rangle

via
\Phi
, where
R_{\Omega}=\big\{{(N({\bf a}),N({\bf b}))}:{({\bf a},{\bf b})\in\Omega}\big\}
. ∎

6.4
One submonoid I

Theorem
6.13
gave a presentation for the monoid
US
arising from an action pair
(U,S)
, when
U
and
S
are both submonoids of the over-monoid. However, in some of our intended applications,
S
and
US
are not a submonoids, even though
U
is a (commutative) submonoid. This section deals with such cases, where we must make some additional assumptions. It is convenient to list these up front:

Assumption 6.33
.

We assume that
(U,S)
is an action pair in a monoid
M
such that:

(i)

U
is a submonoid of
M
(which implies that
S\subseteq US
),

(ii)

U\setminus\{1\}
is a subsemigroup of
U
, and
U\setminus\{1\}\subseteq US
,

(iii)

(U,S^{1})
is an action pair extending
(U,S)
, in the sense described before Lemma
4.19
.

Remark 6.34
.

Item
(ii)
of Assumption
6.33
is a fairly strong condition, but holds in all of our motivating examples in later chapters. For example, it is known that
U\setminus\{1\}\leq U
if
U
is an idempotent-generated monoid
[
20
, Lemma 4.9]
. In particular, this holds for monoid bands, including monoid semilattices. An important special case therefore occurs when
U=P(M)
is the semilattice of projections of a left restriction monoid
M
; cf. Propositions
4.37
and
4.44
(and Remark
4.45
).

By Lemma
4.19
, item
(iii)
of Assumption
6.33
holds if and only if:

•

us=v\ \Rightarrow\ us^{+}=v
for all
u,v\in U^{1}
and
s\in S
.

By the same lemma, this holds automatically if
(U,S)
is strong, and then
(U,S^{1})
is also strong.

It is also worth noting that (under Assumption
6.33
)
US
is a submonoid of
M
if and only if
S
is a submonoid, as follows from Lemma
4.20
. Of course if
S
(and hence
US
) happens to be a submonoid, then we could just apply Theorem
6.13
to obtain a (monoid) presentation for
US
.

For the duration of this section, we fix a pair
(U,S)
satisfying Assumption
6.33
.
We have already noted in Remark
6.2
that presentations for
U\rtimes S
exist in the case that
S
acts on
U(=U^{1})
by monoid morphisms
[
41
]
: i.e., when the pair
(U,S)
is strong. However, since the presentation from
[
41
]
utilises the entire multiplication table for
U
, attempting to use this (and Proposition
6.1
) to obtain presentations for
US\cong(U\rtimes S)/\theta
in this case is undesirable. It also does not apply to the more general situation in which
(U,S)
is not strong.
In any case, we will see in Theorem
6.36
that it is still possible to construct a presentation for
US
(under Assumption
6.33
) very much like that from Theorem
6.13
.

For the rest of this section, we fix semigroup presentations for
U\setminus\{1\}
and
S
:

\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{U}}:{R_{U}}\rangle
via
\phi_{U}:X_{U}^{+}\to U\setminus\{1\}
and
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{S}}:{R_{S}}\rangle
via
\phi_{S}:X_{S}^{+}\to S
.

(The assumption that
U\setminus\{1\}\leq U
means that
U
itself has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}}:{R_{U}}\rangle
.)
Again we assume without loss of generality that
X_{U}
and
X_{S}
are disjoint, that
\phi_{U}\mathord{\restriction}_{X_{U}}
and
\phi_{S}\mathord{\restriction}_{X_{S}}
are both injective, and we write
\overline{w}=w\phi_{U}
and
\overline{v}=v\phi_{S}
for
w\in X_{U}^{+}
and
v\in X_{S}^{+}
. We also fix normal form functions

N_{U}:U\setminus\{1\}\to X_{U}^{+}\and N_{S}:S\to X_{S}^{+},

assuming that
N_{U}(\overline{x})=x
and
N_{S}(\overline{y})=y
for
x\in X_{U}
and
y\in X_{S}
. It is important to note the following convenitions regarding the identity
1
.

•

Even though
1
obviously does not belong to
U\setminus\{1\}
, it will still be convenient to write
N_{U}(1)=\iota
, which as usual denotes the empty word. In the calculations to follow,
N_{U}(1)=\iota
will only ever appear as a sub-word of a non-empty word.

•

It is possible that
1\in S
, in which case
N_{S}(1)
is (by definition) a fixed non-empty word over
X_{S}
mapping to
1
.

•

On the other hand, if
1\not\in S
, it is convenient to write
N_{S}(1)=\iota
as well. In this case,
N_{S}(1)=\iota
will again only ever appear below as a sub-word of a non-empty word.

Since
US
contains both
U\setminus\{1\}
and
S
, there is a well-defined morphism

\Phi:(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{+}\to US:x\mapsto\begin{cases}x\phi_{U}&\text{if $x\in X_{U}$,}\\
x\phi_{S}&\text{if $x\in X_{S}$,}\end{cases}

which is easily seen to be surjective.

As in Section
6.2
, we define

\displaystyle R_{1}=\big\{{(xy,{}^{x}y\cdot x)}:{x\in X_{S},\ y\in X_{U}}\big\}\and R_{3}=\big\{{(x,x^{+}x)}:{x\in X_{S},\ \overline{x}^{+}\not=1}\big\},

where again we write

{}^{x}y=N_{U}({}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y})\in X_{U}^{*}\and x^{+}=N_{U}(\overline{x}^{+})\in X_{U}^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $x\in X_{S}$ and $y\in X_{U}$.}

Note that it is possible to have
{}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y}=1
for some
x\in X_{S}
and
y\in X_{U}
, in which case
{{}^{x}y=N_{U}(1)=\iota}
, and the corresponding relation from
R_{1}
is simply
(xy,x)
.
Similarly, it is possible to have
x^{+}=\iota
for some
x\in X_{S}
(when
\overline{x}^{+}=1\not\in U
), but in this case
R_{3}
does
not
contain the relation
(x,x^{+}x)
.

To define the final set of relations, we first require another piece of notation. For each
u\in U
we extend the relation
\theta_{u}
from (
4.49
) to

\Theta_{u}=\big\{{(s,t)\in S^{1}\times S^{1}}:{us=ut}\big\},

(6.35)

which is again a right congruence on
S^{1}
. The reason we need to work with
\Theta_{u}
in place of
\theta_{u}
is that it is possible to have
u=us
for
u\in U\setminus\{1\}
and
s\in S
, even if
S
is not itself a monoid. In fact, such an equation holds for
every

u\in U
. Indeed, due to the assumption
U\setminus\{1\}\subseteq US
, each
u\in U\setminus\{1\}
satisfies
u=vs
for some
v\in U
and
s\in S
. Since
(U,S^{1})
is an action pair by Assumption
6.33
(iii)
, it follows from Lemma
4.19
(ii)
that
u=vs^{+}
, and so
u=vs=vs^{+}s=us
. Of course if
S
is also a submonoid, then
\Theta_{u}=\theta_{u}
for all
u
, and
u=us\ \Leftrightarrow\ (1,s)\in\theta_{u}
; but in this case we already have the (monoid) presentation for
US
in Theorem
6.13
.

For each
u\in U\setminus\{1\}
we fix a set of pairs
\Omega_{u}\subseteq S^{1}\times S^{1}
that generates
\Theta_{u}
as a right congruence, and we define

R_{2}=\big\{{(N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s),N_{U}(u)N_{S}(t))}:{u\in U\setminus\{1\},\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{u}}\big\}.

Note that it is possible that
s=1
(or
t=1
) for some
(s,t)\in\Omega_{u}
, and we might have
1\not\in S
. In this case, we interpret
N_{S}(s)=\iota
as above, and we note that the word
N_{U}(u)N_{S}(s)
is still non-empty.

Theorem 6.36
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is an action pair satisfying Assumption
6.33
. Then with the above notation, the semigroup
US
has presentation

\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup R_{3}}\rangle

via
\Phi
.

Proof.

The proof is mostly the same as for Theorem
6.13
, but with a little extra care required in some places since we do not assume
S
is a submonoid.

Since
\Phi
is a surmorphism and preserves the relations, it remains to show that
{\ker(\Phi)\subseteq{\sim}}
, where
{\sim}=(R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup R_{3})^{\sharp}
.
We again write
{\sim_{U}}=R_{U}^{\sharp}
, and similarly for
\sim_{S}
,
\sim_{1}
, and so on. By convention, we also allow ourselves to write
\iota\sim\iota
.

We write
\overline{w}=w\Phi
for
w\in(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{+}
. It will also be convenient to define
\overline{\iota}=1
, even though
\iota
does not belong to
(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{+}
, and
1
might not belong to
US=\operatorname{im}(\Phi)
. We also use the abbreviations

{}^{w}v=N_{U}({}^{\overline{w}}\overline{v})\and w^{+}=N_{U}(\overline{w}^{+})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $w\in X_{S}^{*}$ and $v\in X_{U}^{*}$,}

keeping in mind the above conventions regarding adjoined identities and empty words. In particular, we again have

\overline{{}^{w}v}={}^{\overline{w}}\overline{v},\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{w^{+}}=\overline{w}^{+},\hskip 20.00003pt{}^{\iota}v=N_{U}(\overline{v})\and w^{+}={}^{w}\iota\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $w\in X_{S}^{*}$ and $v\in X_{U}^{*}$.}

We then have the following three lemmas, whose proofs are exactly as for Lemmas
6.16
–
6.18
:

Lemma 6.37
.

For any
x\in X_{S}
and
v\in X_{U}^{*}
, we have
xv\sim{}^{x}v\cdot x
. ∎

Lemma 6.38
.

For any
w\in X_{S}^{*}
and
v\in X_{U}^{*}
, we have
wv\sim{}^{w}v\cdot w
. ∎

Lemma 6.39
.

For any
w\in X_{S}^{*}
, we have
w\sim w^{+}w
. ∎

Returning now to the proof of the theorem, let
(w,v)\in\ker(\Phi)
: i.e.,
w,v\in(X_{U}\cup X_{S})^{+}
and
\overline{w}=\overline{v}
. We must show that
w\sim v
.

We first observe that

w\sim_{1}w_{1}w_{2}\and v\sim_{1}v_{1}v_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $w_{1},v_{1}\in X_{U}^{*}$ and $w_{2},v_{2}\in X_{S}^{*}$.}

(6.40)

Note that one of
w_{1}
or
w_{2}
might be empty, but not both (and similarly for
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
). It follows from Lemma
6.39
that

w\sim w_{1}w_{2}^{+}w_{2}\and v\sim v_{1}v_{2}^{+}v_{2}.

(6.41)

From (
6.40
), and keeping
\overline{\iota}=1
in mind, we have

\overline{w}_{1}\overline{w}_{2}=\overline{w}=\overline{v}=\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with $\overline{w}_{1},\overline{v}_{1}\in U(=U^{1})$ and $\overline{w}_{2},\overline{v}_{2}\in S^{1}$.}

Since
(U,S^{1})
is an action pair (cf. Assumption
6.33
), it follows that
\overline{w}_{1}\overline{w}_{2}^{+}=\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}^{+}
. Keeping in mind that
U\setminus\{1\}
is a semigroup, this implies that the words
w_{1}w_{2}^{+}
and
v_{1}v_{2}^{+}
(both from
X_{U}^{*}
) are either both empty, or else both non-empty. We consider these possibilities separately.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
w_{1}w_{2}^{+}=\iota=v_{1}v_{2}^{+}
. Then from (
6.41
) we have
w\sim w_{2}
and
v\sim v_{2}
, with
{w_{2},v_{2}\in X_{S}^{+}}
. But then
\overline{w}_{2}=\overline{w}=\overline{v}=\overline{v}_{2}
, so that
w_{2}\sim_{S}v_{2}
, and so
w\sim v
.

Case 2.
Now suppose
w_{1}w_{2}^{+}\not=\iota\not=v_{1}v_{2}^{+}
. Let
u=\overline{w}_{1}\overline{w}_{2}^{+}=\overline{v}_{1}\overline{v}_{2}^{+}\in U\setminus\{1\}
, so that
{w_{1}w_{2}^{+}\sim_{U}N_{U}(u)\sim_{U}v_{1}v_{2}^{+}}
. Combined with (
6.41
) it follows that

w\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot w_{2}\and v\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot v_{2}.

(6.42)

Applying
\Phi
to (
6.42
), we have
u\cdot\overline{w}_{2}=\overline{w}=\overline{v}=u\cdot\overline{v}_{2}
, so that
(\overline{w}_{2},\overline{v}_{2})\in\Theta_{u}
. Since
\Theta_{u}
is generated as a right congruence by
\Omega_{u}
, it follows that there is a sequence

\overline{w}_{2}=s_{1}\to s_{2}\to\cdots\to s_{k}=\overline{v}_{2}

where
s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in S^{1}
, and such that for each
1\leq i<k
,

s_{i}=a_{i}c_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts_{i+1}=b_{i}c_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $(a_{i},b_{i})\in\Omega_{u}\cup\Omega_{u}^{-1}$ and $c_{i}\in S^{1}$.}

Since
w\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot w_{2}\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{1})
and similarly
v\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{k})
, it suffices to show that

N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{i})\sim N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{i+1})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for each $1\leq i<k$.}

But for any such
i
we have

N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{i})\sim_{S}N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(a_{i})\cdot N_{S}(c_{i})\sim_{2}N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(b_{i})\cdot N_{S}(c_{i})\sim_{S}N_{U}(u)\cdot N_{S}(s_{i+1}),

as required.
∎

Remark 6.43
.

Despite the obvious similarities, Theorem
6.13
does not follow from Theorem
6.36
(in the special case that
S
is a submonoid), as the former does not assume that
U\setminus\{1\}
is a subsemigroup of
U
.

As with Theorem
6.28
, and with an essentially identical proof, the presentation from Theorem
6.36
can be simplified in certain special cases:

Theorem 6.44
.

Suppose
(U,S)
is an action pair satisfying Assumption
6.33
.

(i)

If there exists a subset
V\subseteq U\setminus\{1\}
such that

\Theta_{u}=\bigvee_{v\in V,\atop v\preceq u}\Theta_{v}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U\setminus\{1\}$,}

then
US
has presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}^{\prime}\cup R_{3}}\rangle
via
\Phi
, where

R_{2}^{\prime}=\big\{{(N_{U}(v)N_{S}(s),N_{U}(v)N_{S}(t))}:{v\in V,\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{v}}\big\}.

(ii)

If
U
is commutative, and if
\Theta_{uv}=\Theta_{u}\vee\Theta_{v}
for all
u,v\in U
, then
US
has presentation

\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{U}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{U}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}^{\prime\prime}\cup R_{3}}\rangle

via
\Phi
, where
R_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\big\{{(xN_{S}(s),xN_{S}(t))}:{x\in X_{U},\ (s,t)\in\Omega_{\overline{x}}}\big\}
. ∎

6.5
One submonoid II

In the previous section we gave presentations for the semigroup
US
arising from an action pair
(U,S)
in which only
U
was assumed to be a submonoid of the over-monoid
M
, modulo other conditions listed in Assumption
6.33
. In this section we consider the case in which only
S
is assumed to be a submonoid. As we will see, this is a much more complicated matter, and the ‘hope’ described at the beginning of Chapter
6
is far from realisable.

An example of a semigroup of this form is
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n})=\mathcal{I}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{n}
, the singular part of a finite symmetric inverse monoid; cf. Example
4.30
. Here
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n})=US
arises from the (strong) action pair
{(U,S)=(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n}),\mathcal{G}_{n})}
. Two different presentations for
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n})
may be found in
[
26
,
33
]
, and it is important to note that these presentations are not ‘built’ from presentations for
U
and
S
. Neither
could
any such presentation be built in this way, as
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n})
does not contain any copy of
{S=\mathcal{G}_{n}}
. On the other hand,
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n})
does of course contain
U=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n})
, and also (isomorphic copies of)
\mathcal{G}_{n-1}
, which played an important role in both papers. Another example is
{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{n}}
, the semigroup of all strictly partial transformations
[
30
]
, which arises from the (strong) action pair
(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{n}),\mathcal{T}_{n})
. These two examples generalise to
almost-(left-)factorisable
inverse and left restriction semigroups; cf. Examples
4.27
and
4.30
and Remark
4.45
.

Although it is not possible to give general results for building presentations for
US
out of presentations for
U
and
S
when we assume only
S
is a submonoid, it is however possible to adapt ideas from
[
41
]
in order to give a general presentation for
U\rtimes S
in this case; see Theorem
6.50
below. (Note that
[
41
, Theorem 3.1]
applies to semidirect products
U\rtimes S
where
U
is a monoid, which is the opposite of our current focus.) One can then apply Proposition
6.1
to extend this to a presentation for
US\cong(U\rtimes S)/\theta
, modulo a generating set for the congruence
\theta
.

We assume throughout this section that the monoid
S
acts monoidally on
U^{1}
for some semigroup
U
, allowing for the formation of the semidirect product
U\rtimes S
as in Definition
3.4
. (This monoidal assumption is indeed satisfied when
(U,S)
is a weak action pair; cf. Remark
4.4
.) We write
1
for the identity of both
S
and
U^{1}
. So
{}^{1}u=u
for all
u\in U^{1}
.

We also assume that
U
has presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X}:{R}\rangle
via
\phi:X^{+}\to U
, assuming as usual that
\phi\mathord{\restriction}_{X}
is injective. (We will not be referring to a presentation for
S
.) For
w\in X^{+}
, we write
\overline{w}=w\phi\in U
, and we write
{\sim}=R^{\sharp}
. We also fix a normal form function
N:U\to X^{+}
, assuming that
N(\overline{x})=x
for all
x\in X
. If
1\in U
, then by definition
N(1)
is some non-empty word over
X
mapping to
1
. If
1\not\in U
, then by convention we define
N(1)=\iota
, even though this does not belong to
X^{+}
.

We now introduce an alphabet

Y=\{{x_{s}}:{x\in X,\ s\in S}\}

in one-one correspondence with the cartesian product
X\times S
, and we define the morphism

\Phi:Y^{+}\to U\rtimes S:x_{s}\mapsto(\overline{x},s).

We identify each
x\in X
with
x_{1}\in Y
, and in this way identify
X
with the subset
\{{x_{1}}:{x\in X}\}
of
Y
. Note then that
x\Phi=x_{1}\Phi=(\overline{x},1)
for all
x\in X
.

For any (non-empty) word
w=x_{1}\cdots x_{k}\in X^{+}
, and for any
s\in S
, we write

w_{s}=x_{1}\cdots x_{k-1}(x_{k})_{s}\in Y^{+}.

Note that
(wv)_{s}=w\cdot v_{s}
for any
w\in X^{*}
,
v\in X^{+}
and
s\in S
. We define two sets of relations as follows:

R_{1}=\big\{{(w_{s},v_{s})}:{(w,v)\in R,\ s\in S}\big\}\and R_{2}=\big\{{(x_{s}y_{t},(xN({}^{s}\overline{y}))_{st})}:{x,y\in X,\ s,t\in S}\big\}.

By identifying
X
with a subset of
Y
, as above, we also have
R\subseteq R_{1}
, as
(w,v)\equiv(w_{1},v_{1})\in R_{1}
for
(w,v)\in R
.
Note also that in
R_{2}
, we have

(xN({}^{s}\overline{y}))_{st}=\begin{cases}x_{st}&\text{if ${}^{s}\overline{y}=1\not\in U$, as then $N({}^{s}\overline{y})=\iota$,}\\
x\cdot N({}^{s}\overline{y})_{st}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In the first case, the corresponding relation from
R_{2}
is just
(x_{s}y_{t},x_{st})
.

Lemma 6.45
.

For any
w\in X^{+}
and
s\in S
, we have
w_{s}\Phi=(\overline{w},s)
. Consequently,
\Phi
is a surmorphism.

Proof.

We prove the first assertion by induction on
k=\ell(w)
, the length of
w
. If
k=1
, then
w\in X
and
w_{s}\in Y
, so
w_{s}\Phi=(\overline{w},s)
by definition. We now assume that
k\geq 2
, so that
w=xv
for some
x\in X
and
v\in X^{+}
with
\ell(v)=k-1
, and we note that
w_{s}=x\cdot v_{s}=x_{1}\cdot v_{s}
