. By induction, and monoidality of the action, it follows that

w_{s}\Phi=(x_{1}\Phi)\cdot(v_{s}\Phi)=(\overline{x},1)\cdot(\overline{v},s)=(\overline{x}\cdot\overline{v},s)=(\overline{w},s).

The second assertion now follows, because
(u,s)=(\overline{N(u)},s)=N(u)_{s}\Phi
for all
u\in U
and
{s\in S}
.
∎

Lemma 6.46
.

We have
R_{1}\cup R_{2}\subseteq\ker(\Phi)
.

Proof.

Consider first a relation
(w_{s},v_{s})
from
R_{1}
. So
s\in S
and
(w,v)\in R
. The latter gives
\overline{w}=\overline{v}
, and it then follows from Lemma
6.45
that
w_{s}\Phi=(\overline{w},s)=(\overline{v},s)=v_{s}\Phi
.

Now consider a relation
(x_{s}y_{t},(xN({}^{s}\overline{y}))_{st})
from
R_{2}
. Then again using Lemma
6.45
we have

(xN({}^{s}\overline{y}))_{st}\Phi=(\overline{xN({}^{s}\overline{y})},st)=(\overline{x}\cdot\overline{N({}^{s}\overline{y})},st)=(\overline{x}\cdot{}^{s}\overline{y},st)=(\overline{x},s)\cdot(\overline{y},t)=(x_{s}y_{t})\Phi.\qed

We now write
{\approx}=(R_{1}\cup R_{2})^{\sharp}
for the congruence on
Y^{+}
generated by the relations
R_{1}\cup R_{2}
. We also write
{\approx_{1}}=R_{1}^{\sharp}
and
{\approx_{2}}=R_{2}^{\sharp}
. Recall that
{\sim}=R^{\sharp}
is the congruence on
X^{+}
generated by
R
.

Lemma 6.47
.

If
w,v\in X^{+}
and
w\sim v
, then
w_{s}\approx v_{s}
for any
s\in S
.

Proof.

It suffices to assume that
w
and
v
differ by a single application of a relation from
R
. So by symmetry we have
w=aw^{\prime}b
and
v=av^{\prime}b
for some
a,b\in X^{*}
and
(w^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in R
. Now,
R_{1}
contains the relations
(w^{\prime}_{s},v^{\prime}_{s})
and
(w^{\prime},v^{\prime})\equiv(w^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{1})
. So in the cases
b=\iota
and
b\not=\iota
, we have

w_{s}=a\cdot w^{\prime}_{s}\approx_{1}a\cdot v^{\prime}_{s}=v_{s}\and w_{s}=a\cdot w^{\prime}\cdot b_{s}\approx_{1}a\cdot v^{\prime}\cdot b_{s}=v_{s},

respectively.
∎

Lemma 6.48
.

If
w\in Y^{+}
, then
w\approx v_{t}
for some
v\in X^{+}
and
t\in S
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on
k=\ell(w)
, the length of
w
. If
k=1
, then
w\in Y
, so
w=x_{s}
for some
x\in X
and
s\in S
, so we just take
v=x
and
t=s
. We now assume that
k\geq 2
, so that
w=w^{\prime}y_{s}
for some
w^{\prime}\in Y^{+}
,
y\in X
and
s\in S
. By induction, since
\ell(w^{\prime})=k-1
, we have
w^{\prime}\approx v^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
for some
v^{\prime}\in X^{+}
and
t^{\prime}\in S
. By definition,
v^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}=v^{\prime\prime}x_{t^{\prime}}
for some
v^{\prime\prime}\in X^{*}
and
x\in X
. But then

w=w^{\prime}y_{s}\approx v^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}y_{s}=v^{\prime\prime}x_{t^{\prime}}y_{s}\approx_{2}v^{\prime\prime}(xN({}^{t^{\prime}}\overline{y}))_{t^{\prime}s}=(v^{\prime\prime}xN({}^{t^{\prime}}\overline{y}))_{t^{\prime}s},

and we take
v=v^{\prime\prime}xN({}^{t^{\prime}}\overline{y})
and
t=t^{\prime}s
.
∎

Lemma 6.49
.

If
w\in Y^{+}
and
w\Phi=(u,s)
, then
w\approx N(u)_{s}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.48
, we have
w\approx v_{t}
for some
v\in X^{+}
and
t\in S
. It then follows from Lemmas
6.45
and
6.46
that

(u,s)=w\Phi=v_{t}\Phi=(\overline{v},t),

so that
t=s
and
\overline{v}=u=\overline{N(u)}
. It follows from the latter that
v\sim N(u)
. Combining all of this with Lemma
6.47
we obtain

w\approx v_{t}\approx N(u)_{t}=N(u)_{s}.\qed

We can now prove the main result of this section:

Theorem 6.50
.

Suppose
U
is a semigroup, and
S
a monoid with a monoidal action on
U^{1}
. Then with the above notation, the semidirect product
U\rtimes S
has presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{Y}:{R_{1}\cup R_{2}}\rangle
via
\Phi
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
6.45
and
6.46
, it remains only to show that
\ker(\Phi)\subseteq{\approx}
. So suppose
{(w,v)\in\ker(\Phi)}
, and write
(u,s)=w\Phi=v\Phi
. Then by Lemma
6.49
we have
w\approx N(u)_{s}\approx v
.
∎

Remark 6.51
.

The presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{Y}:{R_{1}\cup R_{2}}\rangle
from Theorem
6.50
is not as ‘compact’ as that of Lavers’ Theorem
6.4
(which only applies in much more special circumstances):

•

The generating set
Y
is essentially
|S|
copies of
X
, and
R_{1}
is
|S|
copies of
R
.

•

Moreover,
R_{2}
contains
|X|^{2}
copies of the entire multiplication table of
S
, in the sense that for every
x,y\in X
and
s,t\in S
, we have a relation of the form
(x_{s}y_{t},w_{st})
.

In general, however, one cannot hope to obtain a presentation for
U\rtimes S
in terms of a smaller generating set. For example, consider the
direct
product
U\times S
, where
U=X^{+}
is a free semigroup over a non-empty set
X
, and
S
is an arbitrary monoid. For any
x\in X
and
s\in S
, it is clear that an expression
(x,s)=(u_{1},t_{1})\cdots(u_{k},t_{k})
, with each
u_{i}\in U
and
t_{i}\in S
can only exist with
k=1
(and
(u_{1},t_{1})=(x,s)
). This shows that any generating set for
U\times S
must contain
X\times S
.

Remark 6.52
.

We have not included any applications of Theorem
6.50
in the current paper. While we believe it would be very worthwhile to investigate such applications, especially to almost-(left-)factorisable inverse or left restriction semigroups (cf. Example
4.27
and Remark
4.45
), it is beyond the scope of the current work.
For example, the papers
[
26
,
33
,
30
]
, which concern the semigroups
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n})
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{n}
discussed above, total more than
60
pages.
As another relevant comparison, the 41-page paper
[
41
]
is essentially devoted to a single semigroup of the form
U\rtimes S
, with
U
a monoid and
S
a non-monoid semigroup (the opposite configuration to that covered by Theorem
6.50
).
A number of wreath products including
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n})
are treated in
[
15
]
, using entirely different methods.
We leave it as an open problem to investigate the above-mentioned applications of Theorem
6.50
.

We conclude the chapter with the following observation.

Corollary 6.53
.

Suppose
U
is a semigroup, and
S
a monoid with a monoidal action on
U^{1}
. If
U
is finitely presented, and if
S
is finite, then
U\rtimes S
is finitely presented.

Proof.

Examining the definitions, we see that the assumptions ensure that
Y
,
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are all finite, so the result follows immediately from Theorem
6.50
.
∎

Remark 6.54
.

Corollary
6.53
gave a sufficient condition for a semidirect product
U\rtimes S
(with
S
a monoid, acting monoidally on
U^{1}
) to be finitely presented. We leave it as an open problem to find necessary and sufficient conditions; for
direct
products
U\times S
, see
[
106
]
.

7
Free left restriction monoids

We now come to the first of our applications of the general results of Chapter
6
. Our goal in this chapter is to obtain a monoid presentation for the free left restriction monoid
\mathcal{L}_{X}
over an arbitrary set
X
. (Of course
\mathcal{L}_{X}
has trivial presentation
\langle{X}:{\varnothing}\rangle
in the signature of left restriction monoids. Monoid presentations will inevitably require more relations, yet presentations in this simpler signature are valuable.)

We recall the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{X}
in Section
7.1
, where we also discuss its structure and decomposition in terms of action pairs. We then give the presentation in Section
7.2
; see Theorem
7.13
. As we note in Remark
7.14
, this presentation utilises the unique minimum (monoid) generating set for
\mathcal{L}_{X}
. Theorem
7.12
gives a presentation for the semilattice of projections of
\mathcal{L}_{X}
.

7.1
Preliminaries

Left restriction semigroups
were defined in Section
2.3
as a variety of unary semigroups, and consequently free objects exist. It transpires that the free left restriction semigroup on a set
X
coincides with the free left ample semigroup on
X
; see for example
[
52
]
. The latter form a quasi-variety, and a sub-class of the class of left restriction semigroups. Note that left ample semigroups were originally called
left type
A
semigroups
in the literature. The original description of free
right
type
A
semigroups was given by Fountain in
[
45
]
, and these are in fact subsemigroups of the free inverse semigroup
[
94
,
108
]
over the same base set. See
[
55
,
52
,
47
,
68
]
for connections with free objects in other varieties and quasi-varieties. An equivalent form of Fountain’s construction, but taking the left-right dual, is given below. The free left restriction
monoid
is simply the monoid completion of the corresponding semigroup, so we treat monoids here for convenience. All of the results in this section can be translated to results for free left restriction
semigroups
by replacing monoid presentations with semigroup presentations (with the same generators and relations).

Let
X
be an arbitrary set, and as usual let
X^{*}
be the free monoid over
X
, with empty word denoted
\iota
. For
w\in X^{*}
we write
w^{\downarrow}
for the set of all prefixes of
w
(including
\iota
and
w
), and for
A\subseteq X^{*}
we write
A^{\downarrow}=\bigcup_{w\in A}w^{\downarrow}
for the set of all prefixes of all words from
A
. Let

{\mathcal{P}}_{X}=\{{A\subseteq X^{*}}:{A^{\downarrow}=A,\ 0<|A|<\aleph_{0}}\}

be the set of all non-empty, finite, prefix-closed subsets of
X^{*}
. Then
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
is a monoid semilattice under
\cup
, with identity
\{\iota\}
. The
free left restriction monoid over
X
is the set

\mathcal{L}_{X}=\big\{{(A,w)}:{A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X},\ w\in A}\big\},

with:

•

product
(A,w)\cdot(B,v)=(A\cup wB,wv)
, where
wB=\{{wv}:{v\in B}\}
, and

•

unary operation
(A,w)^{+}=(A,\iota)
.

The monoid
\mathcal{L}_{X}
contains isomorphic copies of both
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
and
X^{*}
, which we identify with the submonoids

{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\equiv P(\mathcal{L}_{X})=\big\{{(A,\iota)}:{A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}}\big\}\and X^{*}\equiv\big\{{(w^{\downarrow},w)}:{w\in X^{*}}\big\}.

By identifying
A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
and
w\in X^{*}
with
A\equiv(A,\iota)
and
w\equiv(w^{\downarrow},w)
, we have

\displaystyle A\cdot w

\displaystyle\equiv(A\cup w^{\downarrow},w)

for all
A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
and
w\in X^{*}
.

(7.1)

In particular,

\displaystyle(A,w)

\displaystyle\equiv A\cdot w

for all
(A,w)\in\mathcal{L}_{X}
.

It follows that
\mathcal{L}_{X}={\mathcal{P}}_{X}\cdot X^{*}
.

It of course follows from Proposition
4.37
(and the identification
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\equiv P(\mathcal{L}_{X})
) that
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},\mathcal{L}_{X})
is an action pair in
\mathcal{L}_{X}
, though this does not help us to find a presentation; cf. Remark
4.40
. However, Proposition
4.44
(i)
immediately gives us the following action pair, which will prove to be much more useful:

Proposition 7.2
.

For any set
X
,
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})
is an action pair in
\mathcal{L}_{X}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{X}={\mathcal{P}}_{X}\cdot X^{*}
. ∎

Before turning to presentations, we conclude this section with a series of remarks considering how the monoid
\mathcal{L}_{X}
fits into the context of the various results and constructions from Part
I
of the paper.

Remark 7.3
.

It is instructive to consider a direct proof of Proposition
7.2
, starting from Definition
4.2
:

(A1)

The required action of
X^{*}
on
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
is given by

{}^{w}\!A=(wA)^{\downarrow}=w^{\downarrow}\cup wA\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}$ and $w\in X^{*}$.}

(7.4)

It is then routine to show that

{}^{wv}\!A={}^{w}({}^{v}\!A),\hskip 20.00003pt{}^{w}(A\cup B)={}^{w}\!A\cup{}^{w}\!B\and w\cdot A={}^{w}\!A\cdot w,

for all
A,B\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
and
w,v\in X^{*}
. (We also of course have
{}^{\iota}\!A=A
for all
A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
.)

(A2)

As noted above, the identity of
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
is
\{\iota\}
, and we have

w^{+}={}^{w}\{\iota\}=w^{\downarrow}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any $w\in X^{*}$.}

(7.5)

We then quickly deduce the required implication

A\cdot w=B\cdot v\ \Rightarrow\ A\cdot w^{+}=B\cdot v^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $A,B\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}$ and $w,v\in X^{*}$}

from the expansions
A\cdot w=(A\cup w^{\downarrow},w)
,
A\cdot w^{+}=(A\cup w^{\downarrow},\iota)
, and so on.

It also follows from (
7.5
) that the associated map
{X^{*}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{X}:w\mapsto w^{+}}
from Proposition
4.7
is given by
{w^{+}=w^{\downarrow}\equiv(w^{\downarrow},\iota)}
. This is of course just the restriction to
X^{*}
of the
+
operation on
\mathcal{L}_{X}
.

Remark 7.6
.

It follows from Lemma
4.15
(ii)
that the pair
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})
is not strong, as
w^{\downarrow}\not=\{\iota\}
when
w\in X^{*}
is non-empty.
Hence, the left-uniqueness property
(SA2)
does not hold. In contrast to this, it follows immediately from (
7.1
) that the pair
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})
has the
right-uniqueness
property:

A\cdot w=B\cdot v\ \Rightarrow\ w=v\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $A,B\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}$ and $w,v\in X^{*}$.}

One important consequence of this is that the congruence
\sigma=\sigma({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})
on
X^{*}
from Definition
5.21
is trivial: i.e.,
\sigma=\Delta_{X^{*}}
. Together with Lemma
5.37
(and
P=U={\mathcal{P}}_{X}
), it follows immediately from this that the pair
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})
is proper. Consequently,
\mathcal{L}_{X}={\mathcal{P}}_{X}\cdot X^{*}
is
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})
-proper.

Remark 7.7
.

It does not follow from Remark
7.6
that
\mathcal{L}_{X}
is a proper left restriction monoid in the sense of Definition
5.7
, although this does turn out to be the case. (To the best of our knowledge, this fact has not been explicitly stated in the literature, but it follows quickly from known results; see for example
[
52
, Proposition 3.3]
.) To demonstrate this we could show that the pair
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},\mathcal{L}_{X})
is proper, and then apply Proposition
5.29
. But this is no easier than directly using Definition
5.7
. To use this, we need to understand the congruence
\sigma=\sigma_{\mathcal{L}_{X}}(=\sigma({\mathcal{P}}_{X},\mathcal{L}_{X}))
on
\mathcal{L}_{X}
, and it is easy to see that for any elements
(A,w)
and
(B,v)
of
\mathcal{L}_{X}
,

(A,w)\mathrel{\sigma}(B,v)\ \Leftrightarrow\ w=v.

(7.8)

Indeed, the forwards implication is clear, while if
w=v
, then
(B,\iota)\cdot(A,w)=(A,\iota)\cdot(B,v)
. We then have

\displaystyle(A,w)=(B,v)\hskip 10.00002pt\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 10.00002ptA=B\text{ and }w=v\hskip 10.00002pt\ \Leftrightarrow\ \hskip 10.00002pt(A,w)^{+}=(B,v)^{+}\text{ and }(A,w)\mathrel{\sigma}(B,v).

Remark 7.9
.

Consider again the pair
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})
. It follows from Proposition
4.46
that
\mathcal{L}_{X}
is a homomorphic image of the semidirect product

{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\rtimes X^{*}=\big\{{(A,w)}:{A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X},\ w\in X^{*}}\big\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with operation}\hskip 20.00003pt(A,w)\cdot(B,v)=(A\cup{}^{w}\!B,wv),

where the action is given in (
7.4
). Explicitly, the surmorphism
\pi:{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\rtimes X^{*}\to\mathcal{L}_{X}
from Proposition
4.46
is given by

(A,w)\pi=A\cdot w=(A\cup w^{\downarrow},w).

Note that
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\rtimes X^{*}
is not a monoid, even though
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
and
X^{*}
both are. This is because the action of
X^{*}
on
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
is not by monoid morphisms (cf. (
7.5
)), even though it is of course monoidal; cf. Lemma
3.11
.
On the other hand, it follows immediately from its definition that
\mathcal{L}_{X}
is also the
subsemigroup
of
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\rtimes X^{*}
consisting of all pairs
(A,w)
with
w\in A
. (For this, note that when
w\in A
, we have
{A\cup{}^{w}\!B=A\cup wB}
.)

This is of course reminiscent of the embedding theorems from Chapter
5
. Indeed, Theorem
5.60
tells us that
\mathcal{L}_{X}={\mathcal{P}}_{X}\cdot X^{*}
embeds in a semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes X^{*}
for some semigroup
\mathcal{U}
containing
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
. (For this we also need to remember that
\sigma({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})=\Delta_{X^{*}}
; cf Remark
7.6
.) As noted in the previous paragraph, we can in fact take
\mathcal{U}
to be
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
itself.

Alternatively, we could apply Theorem
5.60
to the pair
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},\mathcal{L}_{X})
, or even apply Theorem
5.12
to
\mathcal{L}_{X}
itself. In either case, the relevant theorem tells us that
\mathcal{L}_{X}(={\mathcal{P}}_{X}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{X})
embeds in a semidirect product
\mathcal{U}\rtimes(\mathcal{L}_{X}/\sigma)
for some semigroup
\mathcal{U}
containing
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
, where
\sigma=\sigma_{\mathcal{L}_{X}}=\sigma({\mathcal{P}}_{X},\mathcal{L}_{X})
. It is easy to see from (
7.8
) that
\mathcal{L}_{X}/\sigma\cong X^{*}
, as has also been shown in
[
10
, Theorem 5.1(iii)]
.

Remark 7.10
.

Finally, we consider the covering Theorem
5.39
in the context of
\mathcal{L}_{X}
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{X}
is proper, it is trivially covered by itself, and it turns out that this is precisely what we recover from our proof of Theorem
5.39
. Indeed, the
(\underline{U},\overline{S})
construction applied to the pair
{(U,S)=({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})}
leads to the (monoid) subsemigroups

\underline{{\mathcal{P}}_{X}}=\big\{{(A,\iota)}:{A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}}\big\}\equiv{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\and\overline{X^{*}}=\big\{{(w^{\downarrow},w)}:{w\in X^{*}}\big\}\equiv X^{*}

of the semidirect product
U\rtimes S(=U^{1}\rtimes S^{1})={\mathcal{P}}_{X}\rtimes X^{*}
. The proper monoid

\underline{U}\cdot\overline{S}=\big\{{(u,s)\in U\rtimes S}:{u=us^{+}}\big\}=\big\{{(A,w)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\rtimes X^{*}}:{A=A\cup w^{+}}\big\}

is then precisely
\mathcal{L}_{X}
itself, since

A=A\cup w^{+}\ \Leftrightarrow\ A=A\cup w^{\downarrow}\ \Leftrightarrow\ w\in A\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}$ and $w\in X^{*}$.}

7.2
Presentations

Our goal now is to give a monoid presentation for the free left restriction monoid
\mathcal{L}_{X}
over an arbitrary set
X
. We keep the notation from the previous section. In particular,
\mathcal{L}_{X}={\mathcal{P}}_{X}\cdot X^{*}
arises from the action pair
({\mathcal{P}}_{X},X^{*})
, where
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
and
X^{*}
are identified with submonoids of
\mathcal{L}_{X}
, as explained above. We
could
apply Theorem
6.13
to this pair, but it actually follows from Remark
7.10
that Theorem
6.30
applies, and this will lead us to a slightly quicker derivation. In any case, we need presentations for
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
and
X^{*}
; with the latter of course being trivial, we turn to the former.

The next result gives a monoid generating set for
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
. Recall that an
atom
of a semigroup
S
is an element
a
such that
a=xy\ \Rightarrow\ a\in\{x,y\}
for all
x,y\in S
. It is easy to see that any generating set for a semigroup must contain all atoms.

Proposition 7.11
.

For any set
X
,
\Gamma_{X}=\{{w^{\downarrow}}:{w\in X^{+}}\}
is the (unique) minimum monoid generating set for
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
.

Proof.

If
A\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
, then

A=A^{\downarrow}=\bigcup_{w\in A\setminus\{\iota\}}w^{\downarrow}\in\langle\Gamma_{X}\rangle,

keeping
|A|<\aleph_{0}
in mind. This shows that
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}=\langle\Gamma_{X}\rangle
.

To complete the proof, it suffices to show that every element of
\Gamma_{X}
is an atom. So let
w\in X^{+}
, and suppose
w^{\downarrow}=A\cup B
for some
A,B\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
. Then
A,B\subseteq w^{\downarrow}
.
Since
w\in w^{\downarrow}=A\cup B
, we may assume by symmetry that
w\in A
, and then
w^{\downarrow}\subseteq A^{\downarrow}=A
, so that
A=w^{\downarrow}
.
∎

We now wish to give a presentation for
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
in terms of the generating set
\Gamma_{X}
. With this in mind, we define an alphabet

A_{X}=\{{a_{w}}:{w\in X^{+}}\}

in one-one correspondence with
\Gamma_{X}
. By Proposition
7.11
, we have a surmorphism

\phi_{X}:A_{X}^{*}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{X}:a_{w}\mapsto w^{\downarrow}.

We then define
R_{X}=R_{X}^{1}\cup R_{X}^{2}
, where

R_{X}^{1}=\big\{{(a_{w}a_{v},a_{v}a_{w})}:{w,v\in X^{+}}\big\}\and R_{X}^{2}=\big\{{(a_{wv}a_{w},a_{wv})}:{w\in X^{+},\ v\in X^{*}}\big\}.

Note that we have the alternative description

R_{X}^{2}=\big\{{(a_{w}a_{v},a_{w})}:{w,v\in X^{+},\ v\text{ is a prefix of }w}\big\}.

Theorem 7.12
.

For any set
X
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{A_{X}}:{R_{X}}\rangle
via
\phi_{X}
.

Proof.

We have already noted that
\phi_{X}
is a surmorphism, and it is clear that
{R_{X}\subseteq\ker(\phi_{X})}
. Indeed, we have
R_{X}^{1}\subseteq\ker(\phi_{X})
because
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
is commutative, and
R_{X}^{2}\subseteq\ker(\phi_{X})
follows from the observation that

(wv)^{\downarrow}\cup w^{\downarrow}=(wv)^{\downarrow}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $w,v\in X^{*}$.}

It therefore remains to show that
\ker(\phi_{X})\subseteq{\sim}
, where
{\sim}=R_{X}^{\sharp}
. So suppose
(p,q)\in\ker(\phi_{X})
, and write

p=a_{w_{1}}\cdots a_{w_{k}}\and q=a_{v_{1}}\cdots a_{v_{l}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptw_{1},\ldots,w_{k},v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\in X^{+}.

For any
1\leq i\leq l
, we have

v_{i}\in v_{i}^{\downarrow}\subseteq v_{1}^{\downarrow}\cup\cdots\cup v_{l}^{\downarrow}=q\phi_{X}=p\phi_{X}=w_{1}^{\downarrow}\cup\cdots\cup w_{k}^{\downarrow}.

It follows from this that
v_{i}\in w_{j}^{\downarrow}
for some
1\leq j\leq k
, which means that
v_{i}
is a prefix of
w_{j}
. But then
R_{X}^{2}
contains the relation
(a_{w_{j}}a_{v_{i}},a_{w_{j}})
. Combining this with
R_{X}^{1}
, it follows that
p\sim pa_{v_{i}}
. Since this is true for all
1\leq i\leq l
, it follows that
p\sim pa_{v_{1}}\cdots a_{v_{l}}=pq
. By symmetry we also have
q\sim qp
. Since
pq\sim qp
by
R_{X}^{1}
, it follows that
p\sim q
, as required.
∎

We now apply Theorem
6.30
to obtain a (monoid) presentation for
\mathcal{L}_{X}
.

Theorem 7.13
.

For any set
X
, the free left restriction monoid
\mathcal{L}_{X}
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{A_{X}\cup X}:{R_{X}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}}\rangle

via

\Phi_{X}:(A_{X}\cup X)^{*}\to\mathcal{L}_{X}:\begin{cases}a_{w}\mapsto w^{\downarrow}\equiv(w^{\downarrow},\iota)&\text{for $w\in X^{+}$,}\\
x\mapsto x\equiv(x^{\downarrow},x)&\text{for $x\in X$,}\end{cases}

where

R_{1}=\big\{{(xa_{w},a_{xw}x)}:{x\in X,\ w\in X^{+}}\big\}\and R_{2}=\big\{{(x,a_{x}x)}:{x\in X}\big\}.

Proof.

We begin with the presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{A_{X}}:{R_{X}}\rangle
for
{\mathcal{P}}_{X}
from Theorem
7.12
, and the canonical presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X}:{\varnothing}\rangle
for
X^{*}
(via the identity map
X^{*}\to X^{*}
). Theorem
6.30
then tells us that
\mathcal{L}_{X}=\big\{{(A,w)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{X}\rtimes X^{*}}:{A=A\cup w^{+}}\big\}
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{A_{X}\cup X}:{R_{X}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}}\rangle

via
\Phi_{X}
, where

R_{1}=\big\{{(xa_{w},{}^{x}(a_{w})\cdot x)}:{x\in X,\ w\in X^{+}}\big\}\and R_{2}=\big\{{(x,x^{+}x)}:{x\in X}\big\}.

(The second set of relations was denoted
R_{3}
in Theorem
6.30
.)
In
R_{1}
,
{}^{x}(a_{w})
is a word mapping to
{}^{x}(w^{\downarrow})=(xw)^{\downarrow}
, so of course we can take
{}^{x}(a_{w})=a_{xw}
. Similarly, we can take
x^{+}=a_{x}
in
R_{2}
.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

The presentation in Theorem
7.13
involves the generating set
\Gamma_{X}\cup X
for
\mathcal{L}_{X}
. It is easy to see (using Proposition
7.11
) that this is the unique minimum generating set.

8
Independence algebras

In this chapter we apply the theory developed so far to a number of monoids and semigroups of partial endomorphisms of independence algebras. In Sections
8.1
and
8.2
we recall the necessary definitions, and prove some preliminary results on independence algebras, and the partial endomorphism monoid
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
of such an algebra
A
. A key point is that
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
is a left restriction monoid. Section
8.3
contains a number of results concerning maximal subalgebras, and Section
8.4
treats subalgebras of arbitrary finite codimension; the main result here is Theorem
8.40
, which gives a presentation for the
\cap
-semilattice
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
of all finite-codimensional subalgebras of a strong independence algebra
A
. Section
8.5
provides natural generating sets for the automorphism groups
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
in the case that
A
is finite-dimensional, and also for the group
\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
of finitary automorphisms of an arbitrary strong independence algebra.
In Section
8.6
we identify six strong action pairs
(E,S)
in
M=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, each leading in the usual way to a naturally occurring subsemigroup
ES
of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, the elements of which we also characterise. We then look, in Section
8.7
, at the various ‘congruence conditions’ from Section
4.5
and Chapter
6
, as they apply to each of our pairs. Finally, we discuss presentations for the semigroups
ES
in Section
8.8
. Presentations for
S
are not known in general (even for relatively simple classes of the algebra
A
), so it is not possible to give explicit presentations for
ES
in full generality. However, a presentation for
E
can be deduced from Theorem
8.40
in the case that
A
is finite-dimensional and strong, as
E
is then isomorphic to
\operatorname{Sub}(A)=\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
. We conclude by explaining how the presentation for
E
can be extended to a presentation for
ES
modulo an appropriate presentation for
S
. For background on universal algebra, see
[
12
]
.

8.1
Preliminaries

Let
A
be a (universal) algebra. We typically identify
A
with its underlying set, and as usual we identify any nullary operation on
A
with a constant: i.e., the unique element in the image of the operation.
If
X\subseteq A
then
\langle X\rangle
denotes the subalgebra generated by
X
. The least subalgebra of
A
will be denoted

\mathcal{C}(A)=\langle\varnothing\rangle.

Note that
\mathcal{C}(A)=\varnothing
if and only if
A
has no constants.
We write
B\leq A
(
B<A
) to indicate that
B
is a (proper) subalgebra of
A
, and we write
\operatorname{Sub}(A)=\{{B}:{B\leq A}\}
for the lattice of all subalgebras. The meet and join of
B,C\in\operatorname{Sub}(A)
are
B\cap C
and
B\vee C=\langle B\cup C\rangle
, respectively.

A subset
X\subseteq A
is
independent
if for any
x\in X
we have
x\notin\langle X\setminus x\rangle
. (Here and elsewhere in this chapter, we avoid clutter by identifying one-element subsets of
A
with their unique element, so
X\setminus x
stands for
X\setminus\{x\}
, and so on.) A
basis
is an independent generating set for
A
.
Note that a singleton subset
\{x\}
is independent if and only if
x\not\in\mathcal{C}(A)
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
A
be a (universal) algebra. Then
A
is an
independence algebra
if it satisfies the
exchange property
and the
free basis property
:

(EP)

For all
X\subseteq A
and
x,y\in A
, if
x\in\langle X\cup y\rangle
and
x\notin\langle X\rangle
, then
y\in\langle X\cup x\rangle
.

(FBP)

If
X
is basis for
A
, then any map
X\rightarrow A
can be extended (uniquely) to an endomorphism of
A
.

Archetypal examples of independence algebras include:

•

vector spaces over division rings, where we have a binary operation (addition), a unary operation (negation), a nullary operation (the zero), and an additional unary operation for each element of the ground ring (scalar multiplication),

•

sets, regarded as algebras with no operations, and

•

free group-acts, including the special case of sets when the group is trivial; see Example
9.9
.

As noted in
[
54
]
, independence algebras were first studied under the name of
v^{*}
-algebras
. They appear first in an article of Narkiewicz
[
96
]
, and were inspired by Marczewski’s study of notions of independence, initiated in
[
80
]
. A classification into five families, together with one finite exception, was given by Urbanik in
[
117
]
.

The next (well known) result is a simple consequence of
(EP)
.

Lemma 8.2
.

If
X
is an independent subset of an independence algebra
A
, then
X\cup y
is independent for any
y\in A\setminus\langle X\rangle
. ∎

The next result follows from
[
54
, Lemma 3.7]
, and will typically be used without explicit reference.

Lemma 8.3
.

If
X
and
Y
are bases of an independence algebra
A
, then any endomorphism of
A
extending a bijection
X\to Y
is an automorphism. ∎

The following facts concerning an arbitrary independence algebra
A
are well known; see for example
[
54
, Section 3]
.

•

Every independent subset of
A
is contained in a maximal independent subset. Since
\varnothing
is independent, maximal independent subsets exist. Any maximal independent subset generates
A
, and is hence a basis. So any independent set can be extended to a basis.

•

All bases for
A
have the same cardinality, which we call the
dimension
of
A
, and denote
\dim(A)
. This coincides with the usual meaning of dimension when
A
is a vector space; when
A
is a set (with no operations),
\dim(A)=|A|
. Note that the dimension of
A
is also called the
rank
of
A
in the literature, and denoted
\operatorname{rank}(A)
.

•

Any subalgebra
B\leq A
is also an independence algebra, and any basis of
B
may be extended to a basis for
A
. Thus,
\dim(B)
exists and
\dim(B)\leq\dim(A)
.

•

If
\dim(A)=n<\aleph_{0}
, then
A
is the unique subalgebra of dimension
n
, and the maximal (proper) subalgebras are precisely those of dimension
n-1
. (Recall that
B\leq A
is maximal if
B<C\leq A\ \Rightarrow\ C=A
.) When
\dim(A)\geq\aleph_{0}
we require the notion of
codimension
to describe the maximal subalgebras; see Definition
8.6
and Proposition
8.22
.

•

If
X\subseteq A
is any independent set, then any map
X\to A
can be extended to an endomorphism of
A
. (First extend
X
to a basis
X\sqcup Y
of
A
, and extend the given map
X\to A
arbitrarily to
X\sqcup Y\to A
; by
(FBP)
, the latter extends to an endomorphism.)

Here and elsewhere,
\sqcup
denotes disjoint union.

The notion of
codimension
of subalgebras was used in
[
48
]
, and goes back to ideas from Jones
[
67
]
. The following definition is a special case of that given in
[
67
]
, which involved more general
closure operators
on arbitrary sets.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
B
be a subalgebra of an independence algebra
A
. A subset
X
of
A
is
B
-independent
if
x\not\in\big\langle B\cup(X\setminus x)\big\rangle
for all
x\in X
. The set
X
is then called a
B
-basis
for
\langle B\cup X\rangle
.

Clearly any
B
-independent set is disjoint from
B
. Note that independent sets are precisely the
\mathcal{C}(A)
-independent sets in the sense of Definition
8.4
, where as usual
\mathcal{C}(A)=\langle\varnothing\rangle
. The next result is a special case of the main theorem of
[
67
]
:

Lemma 8.5
.

For any subalgebra
B
of an independence algebra
A
, all
B
-bases for
A
have the same cardinality. ∎

We may therefore make the following definition:

Definition 8.6
.

Given a subalgebra
B
of an independence algebra
A
, the
codimension of
B
in
A
, denoted
\operatorname{codim}(B)
, is defined to be the cardinality of any
B
-basis of
A
.

Strictly speaking, one should perhaps use notation such as
\operatorname{codim}_{A}(B)
, to explicitly indicate the over-algebra
A
. But since the context should always be clear, we simply write
\operatorname{codim}(B)
to avoid clutter.

We will also need the following basic facts.

Lemma 8.7
.

If
B
is a subalgebra of an independence algebra
A
, then
X\cup Y
is a basis of
A
for any basis
X
of
B
, and any
B
-basis
Y
of
A
.

Proof.

Since
A=\langle B\cup Y\rangle=\langle\langle X\rangle\cup Y\rangle=\langle X\cup Y\rangle
, it remains to show that
X\cup Y
is independent. For this, and aiming for a contradiction, suppose
z\in\big\langle(X\cup Y)\setminus z\big\rangle
for some
z\in X\cup Y
.

Case 1.
If
z\in Y
, then
z\in\big\langle X\cup(Y\setminus z)\big\rangle=\big\langle B\cup(Y\setminus z)\big\rangle
, contradicting
B
-independence of
Y
.

Case 2.
Now suppose
z\in X
, so that
z\in\big\langle(X\setminus z)\cup Y\big\rangle
.
Choose finite subsets
X^{\prime}\subseteq X\setminus z
and
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
such that
z\in\langle X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}\rangle
, and suppose
|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|
is minimal among all such
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
. Since
X
is a basis of
B
, it follows from independence that
z\not\in\langle X^{\prime}\rangle
, and so
Y^{\prime}\not=\varnothing
. Then for any
y\in Y^{\prime}
, and with
Z=(X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime})\setminus y
, we have

z\in\langle X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}\rangle=\langle Z\cup y\rangle\and z\not\in\langle Z\rangle,

using minimality of
|X^{\prime}|+|Y^{\prime}|
for the latter. It then follows from
(EP)
that

y\in\langle Z\cup z\rangle\subseteq\big\langle(X\cup Y)\setminus y\big\rangle.

Since
y\in Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
, we have therefore reduced to Case 1.
∎

It follows that
\dim(B)+\operatorname{codim}(B)=\dim(A)
for all
B\leq A
. In particular, if
\dim(A)=n<\aleph_{0}
, then
\operatorname{codim}(B)=n-\dim(B)
for all
B\leq A
.
We will also use the following converse of Lemma
8.7
, often without reference.

Lemma 8.8
.

If
B
is a subalgebra of an independence algebra
A
, and if
X\sqcup Y
is independent for some basis
X
of
B
, then
Y
is
B
-independent.

Proof.

Aiming to prove the contrapositive, suppose
Y
is not
B
-independent, so
{y\in\big\langle B\cup(Y\setminus y)\big\rangle}
for some
y\in Y
. But
B=\langle X\rangle
, so
y\in\big\langle B\cup(Y\setminus y)\big\rangle=\big\langle X\cup(Y\setminus y)\big\rangle=\big\langle(X\cup Y)\setminus y\big\rangle
, where we used the fact that
X
and
Y
are disjoint in the last equality. This shows that
X\cup Y
is not independent.
∎

The first part of the next result is
[
48
, Lemma 1.3]
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra.

(i)

If
B\leq C\leq A
, then
\operatorname{codim}(B)\geq\operatorname{codim}(C)
.

(ii)

If
B\leq C\leq A
and
\operatorname{codim}(B)=\operatorname{codim}(C)<\aleph_{0}
, then
B=C
.

Proof.

Let
X
be a basis of
B
, and extend this to bases
X\sqcup Y
and
X\sqcup Y\sqcup Z
of
C
and
A
, respectively.
By Lemma
8.8
,
Y\sqcup Z
is a
B
-basis of
A
, and
Z
is a
C
-basis of
A
, so

\operatorname{codim}(B)=|Y\sqcup Z|\and\operatorname{codim}(C)=|Z|.

Part
(i)
immediately follows. For
(ii)
, we have

\operatorname{codim}(B)=\operatorname{codim}(C)<\aleph_{0}\ \Rightarrow\ |Y\sqcup Z|=|Z|<\aleph_{0}\ \Rightarrow\ Y=\varnothing\ \Rightarrow\ B=\langle X\rangle=\langle X\sqcup Y\rangle=C.\qed

Some of the results in Chapter
8
apply only to so-called
strong
independence algebras:

Definition 8.10
.

An independence algebra
A
is
strong
if it satisfies the
strong property
:

(SP)

For independent subsets
X,Y\subseteq A
, if
\langle X\rangle\cap\langle Y\rangle=\mathcal{C}(A)
, then
X\cup Y
is independent.

Remark 8.11
.

Sets, vector spaces and free group-acts are all strong independence algebras. It is also easy to show that every independence algebra of dimension
\leq 2
is strong.

A specific example of a three-dimensional non-strong independence algebra is given in
[
48
, Example 1.5]
, and it will be convenient to recall the construction here. Let
A=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\}
, with a ternary operation
f
defined by

\displaystyle f(a_{i},a_{i},a_{i})

\displaystyle=a_{i}

for all
i

\displaystyle f(a_{i},a_{i},a_{j})=f(a_{i},a_{j},a_{i})=f(a_{j},a_{i},a_{i})

\displaystyle=a_{j}

if
i,j
are distinct

\displaystyle f(a_{i},a_{j},a_{k})

\displaystyle=a_{l}

if
i,j,k,l
are distinct.

All proper subsets of
A
are independent, but
A
is not itself independent, and
(SP)
fails (for example) for
X=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
Y=\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
.

Of crucial importance to us is the following result, which is
[
48
, Lemma 1.6]
. In fact, the property in the lemma can be taken as an equivalent definition of strong independence algebras.

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
A
be a strong independence algebra, and let
B,C\leq A
. Let
X
be a basis for
B\cap C
, and extend
X
to bases
X\sqcup Y
and
X\sqcup Z
for
B
and
C
, respectively. Then
X\sqcup Y\sqcup Z
is a basis for
B\vee C
. ∎

We will also need the following simple result, which is a generalisation of the inclusion-exclusion formula for sets.

Lemma 8.13
.

If
B
and
C
are subalgebras of a strong independence algebra
A
, then

(i)

\dim(B\vee C)+\dim(B\cap C)=\dim(B)+\dim(C)
,

(ii)

\operatorname{codim}(B\vee C)+\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)=\operatorname{codim}(B)+\operatorname{codim}(C)
.

Proof.

Let
X
be a basis for
B\cap C
, and extend this to bases
X\sqcup Y
and
X\sqcup Z
for
B
and
C
. By Lemma
8.12
,
X\sqcup Y\sqcup Z
is a basis for
B\vee C
, and we extend this to a basis
X\sqcup Y\sqcup Z\sqcup W
for
A
. Both parts follow quickly upon writing down expressions for the various (co)dimensions in terms of the cardinalities of
X
,
Y
,
Z
and
W
.
∎

Remark 8.14
.

The assumption that
A
is strong cannot be removed from Lemma
8.13
. For example, let
A
be the algebra from
[
48
]
discussed in Remark
8.11
. Then for the subalgebras
B=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
C=\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
, we have

\dim(B)=\dim(C)=2,\hskip 20.00003pt\dim(B\cap C)=0\and\dim(B\vee C)=\dim(A)=3,

which witnesses the failure of the first identity from Lemma
8.13
in
A
. (The same (sub)algebras work to demonstrate the failure of the second identity.)

8.2
(Partial) endomorphisms

A
partial endomorphism
of an algebra
A
is a morphism
B\to A
for some subalgebra
B\leq A
; if this morphism is injective, it is a
partial automorphism
, and it is then an isomorphism from its domain onto its image. We also have
endomorphisms
and
automorphisms
, with their usual meaning. We write

\operatorname{PEnd}(A),\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{End}(A),\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{PAut}(A)\and\operatorname{Aut}(A)

(8.15)

for the sets of all partial endomorphisms, endomorphisms, partial automorphisms and automorphisms of
A
, respectively. These are all monoids under composition, with identity element
\mathrm{id}_{A}
. Note that for
\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, we have

\operatorname{dom}(\alpha\beta)=(\operatorname{im}(\alpha)\cap\operatorname{dom}(\beta))\alpha^{-1}\and\operatorname{im}(\alpha\beta)=(\operatorname{im}(\alpha)\cap\operatorname{dom}(\beta))\beta,

both of which are subalgebras of
A
by standard facts from universal algebra.

The inclusions among the monoids listed in (
8.15
) are indicated as follows:

\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
\operatorname{End}(A)
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
\operatorname{Aut}(A)

Moreover,
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
is an inverse monoid, and
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
is the group of units of all of the above monoids.
If
A
is an independence algebra, then we also have
\operatorname{End}(A)\vee\operatorname{PAut}(A)=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, as follows from Theorem
8.56
(i)
below. On the other hand,
\operatorname{End}(A)\cap\operatorname{PAut}(A)
consists of all injective endomorphisms of
A
; this is equal to
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
if and only if
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
.
When
A
is simply a set (an algebra with no operations), the monoids listed in (
8.15
) are
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{A}
,
\mathcal{T}_{A}
,
\mathcal{I}_{A}
and
\mathcal{G}_{A}
, respectively. These monoids of (partial) transformations were introduced in Section
2.2
.

The next result is of fundamental importance. It is well known, and follows immediately from the fact that left restriction monoids form a variety. Specifically,
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{A}
is a left restriction monoid under
\alpha^{+}=\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}
, and the submonoid
{\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{A}}
is closed under
+
.

Proposition 8.16
.

For any algebra
A
, the partial endomorphism monoid
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
is a left restriction monoid under the unary operation
\alpha^{+}=\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}
. ∎

Recall from Section
4.3
that for any left restriction monoid
M
we have a number of important submonoids, such as
P(M)=\{{s^{+}}:{s\in M}\}
and
T(M)=\{{s\in M}:{s^{+}=1}\}
.
For any algebra
A
, we denote the semilattice of projections of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
by

\mathcal{E}_{A}=P(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))=\{{\alpha^{+}}:{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}\}=\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B\leq A}\}.

(8.17)

So
\mathcal{E}_{A}
is precisely the semilattice of
partial identities
of
A
: i.e., all identity maps on subalgebras of
A
. Since

\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{C}=\mathrm{id}_{B\cap C}\and\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{C}\ \Leftrightarrow\ B=C\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $B,C\leq A$,}

it follows that
\mathcal{E}_{A}
is in fact isomorphic to
\operatorname{Sub}(A)=\{{B}:{B\leq A}\}
, considered as an
\cap
-semilattice.

It is also worth noting that for any algebra
A
,

T(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}:{\alpha^{+}=\mathrm{id}_{A}}\}=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}:{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)=A}\}=\operatorname{End}(A).

(8.18)

For an independence algebra
A
, we also define the subsets

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{Aut}(A),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{End}(A)\setminus\operatorname{Aut}(A),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PAut}(A))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{PAut}(A)\setminus\operatorname{Aut}(A),

and we refer to the elements of these sets as
singular
. These are subsemigroups (indeed, two-sided ideals) of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
,
\operatorname{End}(A)
and
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
, respectively, if and only if
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{End}(A)

\displaystyle=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}:{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)<A}\}

is a subsemigroup (indeed, right ideal) of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, consisting of all
strictly
partial endomorphisms, for any algebra
A
. However,

\displaystyle\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{PAut}(A)

\displaystyle=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}:{\alpha\text{ is not injective}}\}

is not a subsemigroup if
\dim(A)\geq 3
. For example, let
\{x,y,z\}\subseteq A
be independent, let
B=\langle x,y\rangle
and
C=\langle y,z\rangle
, and consider
\alpha:B\to A
and
\beta:C\to A
defined by
x\alpha=y\alpha=x
and
y\beta=z\beta=y
; then
\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{PAut}(A)
, yet
\alpha\beta=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}(A)}\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)
.

Now suppose
A
is an independence algebra. If
\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, then the dimension of the subalgebra
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)\leq A
is taken as the definition of the
rank
of
\alpha
:

\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=\dim(\operatorname{im}(\alpha)).

Note that
0\leq\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)\leq\dim(A)
. We also have

\operatorname{rank}(\alpha\beta)\leq\min(\operatorname{rank}(\alpha),\operatorname{rank}(\beta))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)$.}

(8.19)

Indeed, this was proved for
\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{End}(A)
in
[
54
, Lemma 4.1]
, and the argument there works unmodified for partial endomorphisms. At times it will also be convenient to speak of the
corank
of
\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, defined by

\operatorname{corank}(\alpha)=\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{im}(\alpha)).

When
\dim(A)=n<\aleph_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Aut}(A)

\displaystyle=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=n}\}

\displaystyle=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{End}(A)}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=n}\}

\displaystyle=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=n}\},

so that
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)<n}\}
, with similar statements for
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PAut}(A))
.

At this point it is worth recording some observations concerning small ranks and dimensions.

Remark 8.20
.

For the duration of this remark, we write
C=\mathcal{C}(A)=\langle\varnothing\rangle
for the unique subalgebra of
A
of dimension
0
. We have already noted that
C
is non-empty if and only if
A
has constants (nullary operations).

(i)

Since every subalgebra of
A
contains
C
, and since every (partial) endomorphism fixes each constant, every (partial) endomorphism fixes
C
pointwise.

(ii)

A (partial) endomorphism has rank
0
if and only if its image is
C
. It follows that if
A
has no constants, then
A
has no rank-
0
endomorphisms (unless
A
is itself empty). On the other hand,
\mathrm{id}_{C}
is always a rank-
0

partial
endomorphism.

(iii)

If
A
has constants, then any function from a basis of
A
into
C
can be extended to an endomorphism of rank
0
.

(iv)

By (
8.19
), the sets

I_{0}=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{End}(A)}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=0}\}\and J_{0}=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}:{\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=0}\}

are ideals of
\operatorname{End}(A)
and
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
. As above
I_{0}=\varnothing
if
A
is non-empty and has no constants; in this case we also have
J_{0}=\{\mathrm{id}_{C}=\mathrm{id}_{\varnothing}=\varnothing\}
.

(v)

If
\alpha\in J_{0}
and
\beta\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, then since
\beta
fixes
C=\operatorname{im}(\alpha)
pointwise,
\alpha\beta=\alpha
. That is, each element of
J_{0}
is a left zero for
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
. Consequently, the ideals
I_{0}
and
J_{0}
are left zero semigroups.

(vi)

If
\dim(A)=0
, then
A=C
and
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)=\operatorname{End}(A)=\operatorname{PAut}(A)=\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\{\mathrm{id}_{A}\}
.

(vii)

If
\dim(A)=1
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{End}(A)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Aut}(A)\cup I_{0},

\displaystyle\operatorname{PEnd}(A)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Aut}(A)\cup J_{0}=\operatorname{End}(A)\cup\{\mathrm{id}_{C}\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{PAut}(A)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Aut}(A)\cup\{\mathrm{id}_{C}\}.

In particular, if
\dim(A)=1
and
A
has no constants, then
C=\varnothing
,
I_{0}=\varnothing
and

\operatorname{End}(A)=\operatorname{Aut}(A)\and\operatorname{PEnd}(A)=\operatorname{PAut}(A)=\operatorname{Aut}(A)\cup\{\varnothing\}.

It will also be convenient to prove the following:

Lemma 8.21
.

For any independence algebra
A
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\operatorname{End}(A)=\operatorname{Aut}(A)
,

(ii)

\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))=\varnothing
,

(iii)

\dim(A)=0
or
[\dim(A)=1
and
A
has no constants
]
.

Proof.

Clearly
(i)
and
(ii)
are equivalent.

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
. This implication was covered in items
(vi)
and
(vii)
of Remark
8.20
.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. Aiming to prove the contrapositive, suppose
(iii)
does not hold, so that either
\dim(A)\geq 2
or else
\dim(A)=1
and
A
has constants. In either case, there exists
x\in A
such that
\langle x\rangle<A
. It follows that for any basis
X
of
A
, the endomorphism
\alpha
extending the constant map
X\to\{x\}
is not an automorphism.
∎

8.3
Maximal subalgebras

In this section we prove a number of results concerning maximal (proper) subalgebras of an independence algebra
A
. These will be used in a number of places in the remainder of Chapter
8
. We denote the set of all maximal subalgebras of
A
by
\operatorname{Max}(A)
.

Proposition 8.22
.

For a proper subalgebra
B
of an independence algebra
A
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

A=\langle B\sqcup x\rangle
for some
x\in A\setminus B
,

(ii)

A=\langle B\sqcup x\rangle
for all
x\in A\setminus B
(i.e.,
B
is maximal),

(iii)

\operatorname{codim}(B)=1
.

Consequently,
\operatorname{Max}(A)=\{{B\leq A}:{\operatorname{codim}(A)=1}\}
.

Proof.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. Suppose
A=\langle B\sqcup x\rangle
for some
x\in A\setminus B
, and let
y\in A\setminus B
be arbitrary; we must show that
A=\langle B\sqcup y\rangle
. But
y\in A\setminus B=\langle B\sqcup x\rangle\setminus\langle B\rangle
, so
(EP)
gives
x\in\langle B\sqcup y\rangle
. It then follows that
A=\langle B\sqcup x\rangle\subseteq\langle B\sqcup y\rangle
.

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. Since
B<A
, we have
\operatorname{codim}(B)\geq 1
. It follows directly from
(ii)
that
\operatorname{codim}(B)\leq 1
.

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
. This is clear.
∎

In particular, if
\dim(A)=n<\aleph_{0}
, then
\operatorname{Max}(A)=\{{B\leq A}:{\dim(B)=n-1}\}
, as observed in Section
8.1
. Our next result shows that every finite-codimensional subalgebra of
A
is a finite intersection of maximal subalgebras.
In the statement, the expression
C_{1}\cap\cdots\cap C_{k}
is understood to represent
A
if
k=0
: i.e., if the list
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
is empty.

Lemma 8.23
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra, and let
B\leq A
with
\operatorname{codim}(B)=k<\aleph_{0}
. Then
B=C_{1}\cap\cdots\cap C_{k}
for some
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
.

Proof.

Let
X
be a basis of
B
, and
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}\}
a
B
-basis of
A
, so that
Y=X\sqcup\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}\}
is a basis of
A
by Lemma
8.7
. For each
1\leq i\leq k
, let
C_{i}=\langle Y\setminus y_{i}\rangle
; it follows from Proposition
8.22
that each
C_{i}
is maximal. We will show by induction on
k
that
B=C_{1}\cap\cdots\cap C_{k}
. This is vacuously true for
k=0
, so we assume that
k\geq 1
.

Let
X^{\prime}=X\sqcup y_{k}
, and put
B^{\prime}=\langle X^{\prime}\rangle
, so that
\operatorname{codim}(B^{\prime})=k-1
, and
Y=X^{\prime}\sqcup\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1}\}
. It follows inductively that
B^{\prime}=C_{1}\cap\cdots\cap C_{k-1}
, so it remains to show that
B=B^{\prime}\cap C_{k}
.
From
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
and
X\subseteq Y\setminus y_{k}
, we have
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
and
B\subseteq C_{k}
, so that
B\leq B^{\prime}\cap C_{k}\leq B^{\prime}
. Since
B^{\prime}=\langle B\sqcup y_{k}\rangle
, it follows from Proposition
8.22
that
B
is maximal in
B^{\prime}
, so we can complete the proof that
B=B^{\prime}\cap C_{k}
by showing that
B^{\prime}\cap C_{k}<B^{\prime}
. But this is clear: we obviously have
y_{k}\in B^{\prime}
, yet
y_{k}\not\in\langle Y\setminus y_{k}\rangle=C_{k}
, as
Y
is independent.
∎

Remark 8.24
.

In the case that
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
, it follows from Lemma
8.23
that
\operatorname{Sub}(A)
is generated (as an
\cap
-monoid) by the set
\operatorname{Max}(A)
. We will see in Proposition
8.39
that
\operatorname{Max}(A)
is in fact the unique minimum generating set. Actually, Proposition
8.39
also treats the case that
A
is infinite-dimensional and strong, but with
\operatorname{Sub}(A)
replaced by the lattice of finite-codimensional subalgebras.

For subalgebras of arbitrary codimension we have the following:

Lemma 8.25
.

If
A
is an independence algebra, and if
B\leq A
, then

B=\bigcap_{C\in\Sigma}C\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\Sigma=\{{C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}:{B\leq C}\}.

Proof.

Write
D=\bigcap_{C\in\Sigma}C
. Since
B\leq C
for each
C\in\Sigma
, certainly
B\leq D
.

To show that
D\leq B
, it suffices to show that
A\setminus B\subseteq A\setminus D
, so fix some
y\in A\setminus B
. We will show that
y\not\in D
by finding
C\in\Sigma
such that
y\not\in C
. Fix a basis
X
of
B
, so that
X\sqcup y
is independent, by Lemma
8.2
. Extend
X\sqcup y
to a basis
X\sqcup y\sqcup Z
of
A
, and define
C=\langle X\sqcup Z\rangle
. By Proposition
8.22
,
C
is maximal, and certainly
B=\langle X\rangle\subseteq C
, so that
C\in\Sigma
. Since
X\sqcup y\sqcup Z
is independent,
y\not\in\langle X\sqcup Z\rangle=C
.
∎

The next result concerns the situation in which
A
has subalgebras missing only one element, and shows that this is an extremely restrictive property. Since every subalgebra contains
\mathcal{C}(A)
, we could only possibly have
A\setminus x\leq A
if
x\not\in\mathcal{C}(A)
. Such an element
x
could only exist if
A\not=\mathcal{C}(A)
: i.e., if
\dim(A)\not=0
.

Proposition 8.26
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra with
\dim(A)\not=0
, and write
C=\mathcal{C}(A)
and
X=A\setminus C
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

A\setminus x\leq A
for some
x\in X
,

(ii)

A\setminus x\leq A
for all
x\in X
,

(iii)

X
is independent,

(iv)

X
is the unique basis of
A
,

(v)

\operatorname{Sub}(A)=\{{B}:{C\subseteq B\subseteq A}\}
,

(vi)

\operatorname{Max}(A)=\{{A\setminus x}:{x\in X}\}
.

Proof.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. Suppose
A\setminus x\leq A
for some
x\in X
, and let
y\in X
be arbitrary; we must show that
A\setminus y\leq A
. Since
x,y\not\in C
, we may extend
x
and
y
to bases
U\sqcup x
and
V\sqcup y
for
A
, noting that
|U|=|V|
. We then let
\alpha
be the endomorphism of
A
extending any bijection
U\sqcup x\to V\sqcup y
that maps
x\mapsto y
. Then
\alpha
is an automorphism (as it maps a basis bijectively to a basis), and since
A\setminus x\leq A
, it follows that
A\setminus y=(A\setminus x)\alpha\leq A
.

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. If we had
x\in\langle X\setminus x\rangle
for some
x\in X
, then since
A\setminus x\leq A
, it would follow that
x\in\langle X\setminus x\rangle\subseteq\langle A\setminus x\rangle=A\setminus x
, a contradiction.

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iv)
. If
X
is independent, then it is clearly the maximum independent subset of
A
.

(iv)
\ \Rightarrow\
(v)
. Since every subalgebra contains
C
, it suffices to show that
B\leq A
for any
C\subseteq B\subseteq A
, so fix some such
B
. To show that
B\leq A
, it suffices to show that no element of
A\setminus B
belongs to
\langle B\rangle
. But for any
x\in A\setminus B
, we have
(B\setminus C)\sqcup x\subseteq A\setminus C=X
. Since
X
is independent, so too is
(B\setminus C)\sqcup x
. Thus,
x\not\in\langle B\setminus C\rangle=\langle B\rangle
.

(v)
\ \Rightarrow\
(vi)
and
(vi)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
. These are both clear.
∎

Consider an independence algebra
A
, and again write
C=\mathcal{C}(A)
and
X=A\setminus C
. Any automorphism of
A
fixes
C
pointwise, and is therefore of the form
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
for some permutation
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
. It follows that
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
is a subgroup of
\{{\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha}:{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}}\}
, and the latter is of course isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}_{X}
.
It turns out that the conditions of Proposition
8.26
lead to the greatest ‘freedom’ in automorphisms, in the sense that
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
is precisely this copy of
\mathcal{G}_{X}
. Indeed, if
X
is a basis for
A
, then any permutation
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
extends to an automorphism (cf. Lemma
8.3
), which must be
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
.
However, it turns out that
X
being independent is not (quite) necessary for this ‘maximum freedom’ to occur, and the next result characterises the algebras for which it does.

Proposition 8.27
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra with
\dim(A)\not=0
, and write
C=\mathcal{C}(A)
and
X=A\setminus C
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

X\setminus x
is independent for some
x\in X
,

(ii)

X\setminus x
is independent for all
x\in X
,

(iii)

\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\{{\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha}:{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}}\}
.

When the above conditions hold, we have
\operatorname{Aut}(A)\cong\mathcal{G}_{X}
.

Proof.

If
X
is independent, then all three conditions hold, so we assume otherwise for the rest of the proof. In particular, it follows from Proposition
8.26
that
A\setminus x\not\leq A
for all
x\in A
.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. Suppose
X\setminus x
is independent for some
x\in X
, and let
y\in X
be arbitrary; we must show that
X\setminus y
is independent. This is clear if
x=y
, so suppose otherwise, and write
U=X\setminus\{x,y\}
. Since
U\sqcup y=X\setminus x
is independent we have
y\not\in\langle U\rangle
.

Next we claim that
x\not\in\langle U\rangle
. To prove this, suppose to the contrary that
x\in\langle U\rangle
. Then
\langle U\rangle=\langle U\sqcup x\rangle=\langle C\sqcup U\sqcup x\rangle=\langle A\setminus y\rangle=A
, since
A\setminus y\not\leq A
. It follows that
y\in A=\langle U\rangle
, a contradiction.
Now that the claim has been proved, it follows from Lemma
8.2
(and the fact that
U
is independent) that
U\sqcup x
is independent. But
U\sqcup x=X\setminus y
, so we are done.

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. It suffices to show that
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
is an automorphism for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
. To do so, fix some such
\alpha
, and let
Y=X\setminus x
for any
x\in X
. Since
Y
is independent, and
X
is not, it follows that
Y
is a basis of
A
. Since
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
, we have
Y\alpha=X\setminus x\alpha
, and again this is a basis. It follows from Lemma
8.3
that the unique endomorphism
\gamma
of
A
extending
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{Y}
is an automorphism. But
\gamma\mathord{\restriction}_{C}=\mathrm{id}_{C}
and
\gamma\mathord{\restriction}_{Y}=\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{Y}
, so
\gamma
and
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
agree on
C\cup Y=A\setminus x
. Since
\gamma
and
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
agree on the basis
Y
of
A
, it follows that
\gamma=\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
. This completes the proof that
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
is an automorphism.

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
. Aiming to prove the contrapositive, we assume that
(i)
does not hold; we must show that
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha\not\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
for some
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
. Fix a basis
Y
of
A
. So
Y\subseteq X
, and by assumption
|X\setminus Y|\geq 2
. The only automorphism extending
\mathrm{id}_{Y}
is
\mathrm{id}_{A}=\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\mathrm{id}_{X}
. So we may take
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
to be any permutation that maps
Y
identically, but permutes
X\setminus Y
non-trivially.
∎

Remark 8.28
.

One may wonder how the conditions of Proposition
8.27
relate to the monoids
\operatorname{End}(A)
,
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
and
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
, and to what extent these have the same ‘freedom’ as
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
, as discussed above. We will treat
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
in the next result. The situation for the other two monoids is a little more complex, as (partial) endomorphisms can map elements of
X
into
C
, in the above notation. But, for example, when
X
is independent (so the conditions of Proposition
8.26
are satisfied), a transformation
\alpha
of
A
belongs to
\operatorname{End}(A)
if and only if
\alpha
fixes
C
pointwise, so that

\operatorname{End}(A)=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{T}_{A}}:{\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{C}=\mathrm{id}_{C}}\}.

These submonoids of
\mathcal{T}_{A}
have been studied extensively, and are typically denoted
\operatorname{Fix}(A,C)
; see for example
[
63
]
. In the case that
C=\varnothing
we of course have
\operatorname{End}(A)=\mathcal{T}_{A}
. If
|C|=1
, say with
C=\{c\}
, then
\operatorname{End}(A)\cong\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, via the standard trick of identifying a partial transformation
\alpha
of
X
with the transformation of
A=X\sqcup c
mapping as
\alpha
on
\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
, and sending all elements of
A\setminus\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
to
c
.

Here is the promised version of Proposition
8.27
, concerning ‘maximum freedom’ for
partial
automorphisms.

Proposition 8.29
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra, and write
C=\mathcal{C}(A)
and
X=A\setminus C
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

X
is independent,

(ii)

\operatorname{PAut}(A)=\{{\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha}:{\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{X}}\}
.

When the above conditions hold, we have
\operatorname{PAut}(A)\cong\mathcal{I}_{X}
.

Proof.

The result is trivial if
\dim(A)=0
, as then
X=\varnothing
, so we assume that
\dim(A)\not=0
.

(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. Since any element of
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
is injective and maps
C
identically, the forwards containment always holds. Conversely, let
\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{X}
, and write
B=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
and
D=\operatorname{im}(\alpha)
. We must show that
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)
; in fact, since
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
is obviously injective, it is enough to check that it is a morphism
C\cup B\to C\cup D
. Now,
C\cup B,C\cup D\leq A
by Proposition
8.26
. Since
B
is independent (as
X
is), it is a basis for
C\cup B
, so there is therefore a unique morphism
C\cup B\to C\cup D
extending
\alpha:B\to D
, and this is of course
\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha
.

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(i)
. By Proposition
8.26
it suffices to show that
B\leq A
for any
C\subseteq B\subseteq A
. But for any such
B
we have
\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\mathrm{id}_{B\setminus C}\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)
, so that
B=\operatorname{dom}(\mathrm{id}_{B})\leq A
.
∎

We have already noted that the conditions in Proposition
8.26
imply those in Proposition
8.27
. The next result concerns algebras that satisfy the latter but not the former. An example of such an algebra is the two-dimensional vector space over the two-element field; another example is the one from
[
48
]
discussed in Remark
8.11
; see also Proposition
8.32
below.

Proposition 8.30
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra with
\dim(A)\not=0
, write
C=\mathcal{C}(A)
and
X=A\setminus C
, and suppose
A
does not satisfy the conditions of Proposition
8.26
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

X\setminus x
is independent for some
x\in X
,

(ii)

X\setminus x
is independent for all
x\in X
,

(iii)

A\setminus\{x,y\}\leq A
for all
x,y\in X
with
x\not=y
,

(iv)

\operatorname{Sub}(A)=\{{B}:{C\subseteq B\subseteq A,\ |A\setminus B|\not=1}\}
,

(v)

\operatorname{Max}(A)=\{{A\setminus\{x,y\}}:{x,y\in X,\ x\not=y}\}
.

Proof.

(i)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(ii)
. This was proved in Proposition
8.27
.

(ii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iv)
. Since
A
does not satisfy the conditions of Proposition
8.26
, it has no subalgebras missing just one element. This gives the forwards containment.

Conversely, suppose
C\subseteq B\subseteq A
is such that
|A\setminus B|\not=1
; we must show that
B\leq A
. If
|A\setminus B|=0
then
B=A\leq A
, so suppose instead that
|A\setminus B|\geq 2
.
Aiming for a contradiction, suppose
B\not\leq A
, so that
x\in\langle B\rangle
for some
x\in A\setminus B
. Since
|A\setminus B|\geq 2
, we may fix another element
y\in A\setminus B
.
Since
x,y\not\in B
and
C\subseteq B
, we have
x,y\in X
.
Then
x\in\langle B\rangle=\langle B\setminus C\rangle\subseteq\big\langle X\setminus\{x,y\}\big\rangle
; but this implies that
X\setminus y
is not independent, and contradicts
(ii)
.

(iv)
\ \Rightarrow\
(v)
and
(v)
\ \Rightarrow\
(iii)
. These are clear.

(iii)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. Aiming to prove the contrapositive, suppose
X\setminus x
is not independent for some
x\in X
, so that
y\in\big\langle X\setminus\{x,y\}\big\rangle
for some
y\in X\setminus x
. But then
y\in\big\langle X\setminus\{x,y\}\big\rangle\subseteq\big\langle A\setminus\{x,y\}\big\rangle
, so that
A\setminus\{x,y\}\not\leq A
.
∎

Remark 8.31
.

Comparing Propositions
8.26
and
8.30
, one might wonder why the following condition was not listed in the latter:

•

A\setminus\{x,y\}\leq A
for
some

x,y\in X
with
x\not=y
.

The reason is that this is strictly weaker than condition
(iii)
of Proposition
8.30
in general.

For example, let
A=\{a,b,c,d\}
with a single unary operation mapping
a\leftrightarrow b
and
c\leftrightarrow d
. Then
A
is an independence algebra (it is term-equivalent to the free two-dimensional
\mathcal{G}_{2}
-act, as in Example
9.9
), and it is easy to see that the lattice
\operatorname{Sub}(A)
is as follows:

A
\{a,b\}
\{c,d\}
\varnothing

In particular,
\{a,b\}
and
\{c,d\}
are the only two-element subalgebras of
A
. This means that condition
(iii)
of Proposition
8.30
fails, even though the above weaker condition holds. The conditions of Propositions
8.26
and
8.27
also fail for
A
.

Because of this, it follows from Proposition
8.27
that
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
is a proper subgroup of
\mathcal{G}_{A}
(note that
X=A\setminus\mathcal{C}(A)=A
since
A
has no nullary operations), and we can also see this by direct computation. Indeed, any basis of
A
contains one element each from
\{a,b\}
and
\{c,d\}
, and it follows that
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
consists precisely of the following permutations, which are specified in two ways, by indicating the action on the basis
\{a,c\}
, and in standard cycle notation:

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}_{A}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
a&c\end{smallmatrix}\right),

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
b&c\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(a,b),

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
c&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(a,c)(b,d),

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
a&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(c,d),

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
c&b\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(a,c,b,d),

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
b&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(a,b)(c,d),

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
d&b\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(a,d)(b,c),

\displaystyle\beta_{4}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\
d&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(a,d,b,c).

Consequently,
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
is dihedral of order
8
, or equivalently a wreath product
\mathcal{G}_{2}\wr\mathcal{G}_{2}
(see Chapter
9
).

We have already noted that the two-dimensional vector space over the two-element field satisfies the conditions of Proposition
8.27
but not those of Proposition
8.26
. The next result shows that this is unusual among such examples, as it is strong; note that
|X|=3
for this example.

Proposition 8.32
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra, write
C=\mathcal{C}(A)
and
X=A\setminus C
, and suppose
|X|\geq 4
. If
A
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
8.27
but not those of Proposition
8.26
, then
A
is not strong.

Proof.

Fix a partition
X=Y\sqcup Z
where
|Y|,|Z|\geq 2
. Then
Y
and
Z
are both independent (as
X\setminus x
is independent for all
x\in X
). By Proposition
8.30
,
C\cup Y=A\setminus Z\leq A
, so
\langle Y\rangle=\langle C\cup Y\rangle=C\cup Y
, and similarly
\langle Z\rangle=C\cup Z
. Certainly then
\langle Y\rangle\cap\langle Z\rangle=C
, yet
Y\cup Z=X
is not independent.
∎

Remark 8.33
.

By contrast, if
A
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
8.26
, then
A
is trivially strong, for then the independent sets are just the subsets of
X=A\setminus\mathcal{C}(A)
.

If the conditions of Proposition
8.26
hold for an independence algebra
A
, then it quickly follows that
A
is equal to the union of any two distinct maximal subalgebras. In Section
8.5
we will be concerned with the case in which this never happens. In fact, we only need a weaker assumption, but the next result shows that these are equivalent when
A
is strong.

Lemma 8.34
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra, and consider the following two conditions:

(i)

for any
B,C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
, we have
B\cup C\not=A
,

(ii)

for any basis
X
of
A
, and for any
x,y\in X
, we have
\langle X\setminus x\rangle\cup\langle X\setminus y\rangle\not=A
.

Then
(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
. If
A
is strong, then
(i)
\ \Leftrightarrow\
(ii)

Proof.

By Proposition
8.22
,
\langle X\setminus x\rangle
is maximal for any basis
X
of
A
and any
x\in X
, so it is clear that
(i)
\ \Rightarrow\
(ii)
.

We now assume that
A
is strong, and that
(ii)
holds. Let
B,C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
be arbitrary; we must show that
B\cup C\not=A
. This is clear if
B=C
, so suppose
B\not=C
. Together with maximality it follows that
B\not\subseteq C
and
C\not\subseteq B
, and also that
B\vee C=A
. From the latter, we have
\operatorname{codim}(B\vee C)=0
, so it follows from Lemma
8.13
(ii)
and Proposition
8.22
that
\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)=2
.

Fix a basis
U
for
B\cap C
, and extend this to bases
U\sqcup Y
and
U\sqcup Z
for
B
and
C
, respectively. Since
B\not=C
we have
|Y|\geq 1
and
|Z|\geq 1
. By Lemma
8.12
,
U\sqcup Y\sqcup Z
is a basis of
B\vee C=A
. But then
|Y|+|Z|=\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)=2
, so in fact
|Y|=|Z|=1
, and we may write
Y=\{y\}
and
Z=\{z\}
. This all shows that
X=U\sqcup\{y,z\}
is a basis of
A
. We also have
B=\langle U\sqcup y\rangle=\langle X\setminus z\rangle
, and similarly
C=\langle X\setminus y\rangle
. It then follows from
(ii)
that
B\cup C=\langle X\setminus z\rangle\cup\langle X\setminus y\rangle\not=A
.
∎

Remark 8.35
.

The algebra
A
from
[
48
]
discussed in Remark
8.11
satisfies condition
(ii)
of Lemma
8.34
, but not
(i)
. Indeed, each two-element subalgebra is maximal, and for example
{A=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\cup\{a_{3},a_{4}\}}
. However, any basis has the form
X=\{a_{i},a_{j},a_{k}\}
for distinct
i,j,k
, and
\langle X\setminus x\rangle\cup\langle X\setminus y\rangle=(X\setminus x)\cup(X\setminus y)\subseteq X\not=A
for any
x,y\in X
.

Remark 8.36
.

We have already noted that the conditions in Proposition
8.26
and Lemma
8.34
are mutually exclusive. They are not, however, exhaustive. For example, let
A
be the four-element algebra from Remark
8.31
. We have already observed that
A
does not satisfy the conditions in Proposition
8.26
. On the other hand, the maximal subalgebras of
A
are precisely
B=\{a,b\}
and
C=\{c,d\}
, and of course
B\cup C=A
. In fact,
B=\langle X\setminus c\rangle
and
C=\langle X\setminus a\rangle
for the basis
X=\{a,c\}
. Thus,
A
does not satisfy either of the conditions of Lemma
8.34
.
(This extends to arbitrary free
G
-acts of dimension
\geq 2
for non-trivial
G
.)

Remark 8.37
.

Any vector space
A
of dimension
\geq 2
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
8.34
. Indeed, if
X
is a basis, and if
x,y\in X
are distinct, then
x+y\not\in\langle X\setminus x\rangle\cup\langle X\setminus y\rangle
. (To see this, note that if
x+y\in\langle X\setminus x\rangle
, then since also
y\in X\setminus x
, we would have
x=(x+y)-y\in\langle X\setminus x\rangle
, contradicting independence of
X
.)

It follows that such a vector space
A
cannot satisfy the conditions of Proposition
8.26
. The only such
A
satisfying the conditions of Proposition
8.27
is two-dimensional over the two-element field, which we have already discussed.

8.4
Finite-codimensional subalgebras

The previous section concerned maximal subalgebras of an independence algebra
A
: i.e., subalgebras of codimension
1
; cf. Proposition
8.22
. We now consider the more general case of subalgebras of arbitrary finite codimension, the set of which we denote by

\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)=\{{B\leq A}:{\operatorname{codim}(B)<\aleph_{0}}\}.

Our first result shows that
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
is a sublattice of
\operatorname{Sub}(A)
in the case that
A
is finite-dimensional or strong, and Theorem
8.40
gives a presentation for
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
in this case, considered as an
\cap
-semilattice.

Lemma 8.38
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra, and let
B,C\in\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
. Then

(i)

B\vee C\in\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
,

(ii)

B\cap C\in\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
if
A
is finite-dimensional or strong.

Proof.

(i)
. This follows immediately from Lemma
8.9
(i)
, as
B\leq B\vee C\leq A
and
\operatorname{codim}(B)<\aleph_{0}
.

(ii)
. Since
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)=\operatorname{Sub}(A)
when
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
, we only need to consider the case that
A
is strong. But here Lemma
8.13
(ii)
gives
\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)\leq\operatorname{codim}(B)+\operatorname{codim}(C)<\aleph_{0}
.
∎

Recall that
\operatorname{Max}(A)=\{{B\leq A}:{\operatorname{codim}(B)=1}\}
is the set of maximal subalgebras of
A
.

Proposition 8.39
.

If
A
is a finite-dimensional or strong independence algebra, then
\operatorname{Max}(A)
is the (unique) minimum monoid generating set for
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
, considered as an
\cap
-semilattice.

Proof.

It follows immediately from Lemma
8.23
that
\operatorname{Max}(A)
is a generating set.

It remains to show that any generating set for
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
contains
\operatorname{Max}(A)
, and for this it suffices to show that every element of
\operatorname{Max}(A)
is an atom. To do so, suppose
B=C\cap D
, with
B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
and
C,D\in\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
; we must show that
B\in\{C,D\}
. If
C=A
, then
B=A\cap D=D
. Otherwise,
B=C\cap D\leq C<A
, so
B=C
by maximality.
∎

Now that we know
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
contains a unique minimum generating set (when
A
is finite-dimensional or strong), this is of course the generating set we wish to use in constructing a presentation. We can do this in the case that
A
is assumed to be strong. We begin by defining an alphabet

X_{E}=\big\{{x_{B}}:{B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\big\}

in one-one correspondence with
\operatorname{Max}(A)
. (The reason for choosing the
E
subscript will become clear in later sections.) By Proposition
8.39
we have a surmorphism

\Phi_{E}:X_{E}^{*}\to\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A):x_{B}\mapsto B.

We define the set of relations
R_{E}=R_{E}^{1}\cup R_{E}^{2}\cup R_{E}^{3}
, where

\displaystyle R_{E}^{1}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(x_{B}^{2},x_{B})}:{B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\big\},

\displaystyle R_{E}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(x_{B}x_{C},x_{C}x_{B})}:{B,C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\big\},

\displaystyle R_{E}^{3}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(x_{B}x_{C},x_{B}x_{D})}:{B,C,D\in\operatorname{Max}(A),\ B\cap C=B\cap D}\big\}.

It is clear that
R_{E}\subseteq\ker(\Phi_{E})
.

Theorem 8.40
.

If
A
is a strong independence algebra, then with the above notation,
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{E}}:{R_{E}}\rangle
via
\Phi_{E}
.

Proof.

We begin with three technical lemmas, the first of which is rather simple. The second may appear to be
exceedingly
technical, but it turns out to be exactly what we need in the proof of the third, which leads to a swift conclusion to the proof of the theorem.

Lemma 8.41
.

If
B\leq A
and
C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
, then
\operatorname{codim}(B)\leq\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)\leq\operatorname{codim}(B)+1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.13
(ii)
and Proposition
8.22
, we have

\operatorname{codim}(B\vee C)+\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)=\operatorname{codim}(B)+1.

Since
C\leq B\vee C\leq A
, Lemma
8.9
(i)
gives
0\leq\operatorname{codim}(B\vee C)\leq 1
, and the result follows.
∎

Lemma 8.42
.

If
B,C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
and
D\in\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
are such that
B\not=C
,
D\not\subseteq B
and
D\cap C\subseteq B
, then there exists
B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
such that
D\subseteq B^{\prime}
and
C\cap B=C\cap B^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We first claim that
D\cap B=D\cap C
. To prove this, first note that
D\cap C\not=D
(as
D\not\subseteq B
but
D\cap C\subseteq B
), so Lemma
8.41
gives
\operatorname{codim}(D\cap C)=\operatorname{codim}(D)+1
. Since also
D\cap B\not=D
(as
D\not\subseteq B
), Lemma
8.41
gives
\operatorname{codim}(D\cap B)=\operatorname{codim}(D)+1
as well. In particular,
\operatorname{codim}(D\cap C)=\operatorname{codim}(D\cap B)<\aleph_{0}
(as
D\in\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
). By assumption we have
D\cap C\subseteq B
, so of course
D\cap C\subseteq D\cap B
. It then follows from Lemma
8.9
(ii)
that
D\cap C=D\cap B
, as claimed.

We now fix a basis
X
of
D\cap B(=D\cap C)
, and extend this to a basis
X\sqcup Y
for
B\cap C
. Since
\operatorname{codim}(B)=\operatorname{codim}(C)=1
and
B\not=C
, Lemma
8.41
gives
\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)=2
. Since
D\not\subseteq B
, we may also fix some
z\in D\setminus B(=D\setminus C)
. Since
z\not\in B
, certainly
z\not\in B\cap C=\langle X\sqcup Y\rangle
, so it follows from Lemma
8.2
that
X\sqcup Y\sqcup z
is independent. Let
B^{\prime}=\langle X\sqcup Y\sqcup z\rangle
. Since
B\cap C=\langle X\sqcup Y\rangle
and
\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)=2
, we have
B^{\prime}<A
and so
\operatorname{codim}(B^{\prime})\geq 1
. Since
B\cap C\subseteq B^{\prime}
, we have
\operatorname{codim}(B^{\prime})\leq\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)=2
. If
\operatorname{codim}(B^{\prime})=2
, then Lemma
8.9
(ii)
would give
B^{\prime}=B\cap C
, contradicting
z\not\in B\cap C
. So it follows that
\operatorname{codim}(B^{\prime})=1
, so that
B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
.

Next let
D^{\prime}=\langle X\sqcup z\rangle
. Since
D\cap C=\langle X\rangle
, and since
X\sqcup z\subseteq D
, we have

D\cap C\subseteq D^{\prime}\subseteq D.

(8.43)

Combined with
\operatorname{codim}(D\cap C)=\operatorname{codim}(D)+1
(shown above), it follows that

\operatorname{codim}(D)+1\geq\operatorname{codim}(D^{\prime})\geq\operatorname{codim}(D),

and this of course implies that

\operatorname{codim}(D^{\prime})=\operatorname{codim}(D)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{codim}(D^{\prime})=\operatorname{codim}(D)+1=\operatorname{codim}(D\cap C).

In these two cases, and since
\operatorname{codim}(D)<\aleph_{0}
, Lemma
8.9
(ii)
and (
8.43
) give
D^{\prime}=D
or
{D^{\prime}=D\cap C}
, respectively. But the latter is impossible, since
D\cap C=\langle X\rangle
and
D^{\prime}=\langle X\sqcup z\rangle
, and since
X(\sqcup Y)\sqcup z
is independent. Thus,
D=D^{\prime}
, and it follows that
D=\langle X\sqcup z\rangle\subseteq\langle X\sqcup Y\sqcup z\rangle=B^{\prime}
.

It remains to show that
C\cap B=C\cap B^{\prime}
, and for this it is enough to show that

C\cap B\subseteq B^{\prime}\and C\cap B^{\prime}\subseteq B.

For the first, we have
C\cap B=\langle X\sqcup Y\rangle\subseteq B^{\prime}
. For the second, suppose to the contrary that there exists
w\in(C\cap B^{\prime})\setminus B
. In particular we have
w\in B^{\prime}=\langle X\sqcup Y\sqcup z\rangle
. On the other hand, since
w\not\in B
, certainly
w\not\in B\cap C=\langle X\sqcup Y\rangle
. It then follows from
(EP)
that
z\in\langle X\sqcup Y\sqcup w\rangle\subseteq C
(as
X\sqcup Y\subseteq B\cap C\subseteq C
and
w\in C\cap B^{\prime}\subseteq C
), and this contradicts
z\in D\setminus C
.
∎

For the rest of the proof (of the theorem) we write
{\sim}=R_{E}^{\sharp}
.

Lemma 8.44
.

If
w\in X_{E}^{*}
, and if
B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
is such that
w\Phi_{E}\subseteq B
, then
w\sim wx_{B}
.

Proof.

Given the relations in
R_{E}^{1}\cup R_{E}^{2}
, it suffices to show that
w
is
\sim
-equivalent to some word that involves the letter
x_{B}
.

Write
w=x_{C_{1}}\cdots x_{C_{k}}
, where each
C_{i}\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
, and note that
w\Phi_{E}=C_{1}\cap\cdots\cap C_{k}
. Since
w\Phi_{E}\subseteq B<A
, we must have
k\geq 1
. We now proceed by induction on
k
. If
k=1
, then
C_{1}=w\Phi_{E}\subseteq B
, and it follows by maximality that
C_{1}=B
; thus,
w=x_{B}
in this case.

Now suppose
k\geq 2
, and let
u=x_{C_{1}}\cdots x_{C_{k-1}}
and
D=u\Phi_{E}=C_{1}\cap\cdots\cap C_{k-1}\in\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
. If
D\subseteq B
, then by induction we have
u\sim ux_{B}
, and so
w=ux_{C_{k}}\sim ux_{B}x_{C_{k}}
, and we are done. So now we assume that
D\not\subseteq B
, and we also write
C=C_{k}
. If
B=C
, then
w=ux_{B}
, and again we are done, so we also assume that
B\not=C
. Since also
D\cap C=w\Phi_{E}\subseteq B
, the conditions of Lemma
8.42
are all satisfied, so by that lemma there exists
B^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
such that
D\subseteq B^{\prime}
and
C\cap B=C\cap B^{\prime}
. By the latter,
R_{E}^{3}
contains the relation
(x_{C}x_{B},x_{C}x_{B^{\prime}})
. Since
u
has length
k-1
, and since
u\Phi_{E}=D\subseteq B^{\prime}
, it follows by induction that
u\sim ux_{B^{\prime}}
. But then

w=ux_{C}\sim ux_{B^{\prime}}x_{C}\sim ux_{C}x_{B^{\prime}}\sim ux_{C}x_{B},

and the proof is complete.
∎

We now return to the main proof.
We have already observed that
\Phi_{E}
is surjective, and that
R_{E}\subseteq\ker(\Phi_{E})
, so it remains to show that
\ker(\Phi_{E})\subseteq R_{E}^{\sharp}
. So suppose
(u,v)\in\ker(\Phi_{E})
; we must show that
u\sim v
. For
w\in X_{E}^{*}
, we have
w\Phi_{E}=A\ \Leftrightarrow\ w=\iota
(the empty word), so it suffices to assume that
u
and
v
are both non-empty. Let
x_{B}\in X_{E}
be any letter appearing in
v
, so that
v\sim vx_{B}
(by
R_{E}^{1}\cup R_{E}^{2}
). Then
u\Phi_{E}=v\Phi_{E}=(vx_{B})\Phi_{E}=v\Phi_{E}\cap B\subseteq B
, so it follows from Lemma
8.44
that
u\sim ux_{B}
. Since this is the case for every letter appearing in
v
, it follows that
u\sim uv
. By symmetry we also have
v\sim vu
, and since
uv\sim vu
(by
R_{E}^{2}
), it follows that
u\sim v
.
∎

Remark 8.45
.

Lemma
8.41
can fail for non-strong independence algebras. Indeed, consider the example from
[
48
]
discussed in Remark
8.11
. Then with
B=\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
C=\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
, both maximal, we have
\operatorname{codim}(B)=1
, yet
\operatorname{codim}(B\cap C)=3
.

Theorem
8.40
also does not (quite) hold for this algebra
A
. According to GAP
[
50
,
91
]
,
{\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{E}}:{R_{E}}\rangle}
has size
15
in this case, while
\operatorname{Sub}(A)
has size
12
; it contains all subsets of size
{\not=3}
. We do, however, obtain a presentation for this
\operatorname{Sub}(A)
by enlarging
R_{E}^{3}
to
