\big\{{(x_{B}x_{C},x_{B^{\prime}}x_{C^{\prime}})}:{B,C,B^{\prime},C^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Max}(A),\ B\cap C=B^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime}}\big\},

as again confirmed by GAP. We leave it as an open problem to determine whether a similar presentation exists for
\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
for an arbitrary (non-strong) independence algebra
A
.

8.5
Generating (finitary) automorphisms

The main purpose of this section is to prove results concerning (monoid) generating sets for the automorphism group of a finite-dimensional independence algebra; in fact, we prove more general results concerning
finitary
automorphisms, which we define below.

Let
A
be an independence algebra. As usual, the
fix set
of a partial endomorphism
{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}
is the subalgebra

\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)=\{{x\in\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}:{x\alpha=x}\}.

We also write

\operatorname{fix}(\alpha)=\dim(\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha))\and\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)=\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)).

Note that
\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)
is called the
shift
of
\alpha
in
[
48
]
, and denoted
s(\alpha)
.
We say
\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
is
finitary
if
\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)<\aleph_{0}
, and we write

\operatorname{PEnd}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}:{\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)<\aleph_{0}}\}

for the set of all finitary partial endomorphisms of
A
, and similarly for
\operatorname{End}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
,
\operatorname{PAut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
and
\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
.
Of course we have

\operatorname{PEnd}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\ \Leftrightarrow\ \dim(A)<\aleph_{0},

and similarly with
\operatorname{End}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
and so on.
When
A
is simply a set (with no operations),
{\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)=\mathcal{G}^{\operatorname{f}}_{A}}
is the
finitary symmetric group over
A
, consisting of all finitary permutations of
A
: i.e., the permutations that move only finitely many elements of
A
.

Lemma 8.46
.

If
A
is a finite-dimensional or strong independence algebra, then
\operatorname{PEnd}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
is a submonoid of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
.

Proof.

The result is clear for
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
. When
A
is strong it follows from the identity

\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha\beta)\leq\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)+\operatorname{cofix}(\beta)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)$.}

This was proved in
[
48
, Lemma 2.7]
for (full) endomorphisms, and the proof there works unchanged for partial endomorphisms.
∎

For any cardinal
\kappa
, we define the set

\Gamma_{\kappa}=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)}:{\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)=\kappa}\}.

For example,
\Gamma_{0}=\{\mathrm{id}_{A}\}
. If
\kappa>\dim(A)
then
\Gamma_{\kappa}=\varnothing
.

Theorem 8.47
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra, and let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
. Then for any
{\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)}
, we have
\alpha=\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}
for some
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\Gamma
with
{\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\beta_{i})}
for each
i
. Consequently,
\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)\subseteq\langle\Gamma\rangle
, with equality if
A
is finite-dimensional or strong.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the first claim, and for this we use induction on
d=\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)
. If
d=0
then
\alpha=\mathrm{id}_{A}
, and we take
k=0
. If
d=1
, then
\alpha\in\Gamma
, and we take
k=1
and
\beta_{1}=\alpha
. (We could include
d=2
in the base case(s), but we prefer not to, as it allows us to only use certain elements of
\Gamma_{2}
, which will then be used in the next proof.)

We now assume that
d\geq 2
. We will show that there exists
\beta\in\Gamma
such that

\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha\beta^{-1})\leq d-1\and\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\beta).

(8.48)

It follows from the latter that
\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha\beta^{-1})
. By induction, it follows from the former that
\alpha\beta^{-1}=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{l}
for some
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{l}\in\Gamma
such that each
\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma_{i})\supseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha\beta^{-1})\supseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)
. Since
\alpha=\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{l}\cdot\beta
, the inductive step will be complete.

For the rest of the proof, we fix a basis
X
for
\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)
. Since
d>0
we may also fix some
y\in A\setminus\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)
. Since
y\not\in\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)=\langle X\rangle
, and since
X
is independent, it follows from Lemma
8.2
that
X\sqcup y
is independent.

Case 1.
Suppose first that
y\alpha\in\langle X\sqcup y\rangle
. Note also that
y\alpha\not\in\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)=\langle X\rangle
. (Otherwise,
(y\alpha)\alpha=y\alpha
would imply
y\alpha=y
, contradicting
y\not\in\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)
.) It then follows from
(EP)
that
y\in\langle X\sqcup y\alpha\rangle
. Consequently,
\langle X\sqcup y\rangle=\langle X\sqcup y\alpha\rangle
. Since
X\sqcup y
is independent, we may extend it to a basis
X\sqcup y\sqcup Z
of
A
, and we note that
|y\sqcup Z|=\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)=d
. So
Z
is a
B
-basis of
A
, where
B=\langle X\sqcup y\rangle=\langle X\sqcup y\alpha\rangle
. It then follows from Lemma
8.7
that
X\sqcup y\alpha\sqcup Z
is also a basis of
A
. Now let
\beta
be the endomorphism of
A
satisfying

x\beta=x,\hskip 20.00003pty\beta=y\alpha\and z\beta=z\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $x\in X$ and $z\in Z$.}

Since
\beta
maps a basis bijectively to a basis, it is an automorphism. And moreover, the maximal subalgebra
\langle X\sqcup Z\rangle
is contained in
\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)
, so it follows that
\operatorname{cofix}(\beta)\leq 1
. Since
{y\beta=y\alpha\not=y}
, we cannot have
\operatorname{cofix}(\beta)=0
, so in fact
\beta\in\Gamma_{1}\subseteq\Gamma
. Certainly
{\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)=\langle X\rangle\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)}
, and since
X\sqcup y\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha\beta^{-1})
, we also have
\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha\beta^{-1})\leq|Z|=d-1
. This completes the proof that (
8.48
) holds in this case.

Case 2.
Now suppose
y\alpha\not\in\langle X\sqcup y\rangle
, and for convenience write
y^{\prime}=y\alpha
. It follows from Lemma
8.2
that
X\sqcup\{y,y^{\prime}\}
is independent, so we may extend it to a basis
X\sqcup\{y,y^{\prime}\}\sqcup Z
of
A
. This time we have
\big|\{y,y^{\prime}\}\sqcup Z\big|=\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha)=d
, and we define
\beta\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
by

x\beta=x,\hskip 20.00003pty\beta=y^{\prime},\hskip 20.00003pty^{\prime}\beta=y\and z\beta=z\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $x\in X$ and $z\in Z$.}

Since
X\sqcup Z\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)
but
y\not\in\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)
, we have
1\leq\operatorname{cofix}(\beta)\leq 2
, so
\beta\in\Gamma
, and again we have
\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)
. Also, since
X\sqcup y\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha\beta^{-1})
we have
\operatorname{cofix}(\alpha\beta^{-1})\leq|Z\sqcup y^{\prime}|=d-1
, and again (
8.48
) holds.
∎

Remark 8.49
.

In the above proof, we noted that the automorphism
\beta
constructed during Case 2 satisfied
\operatorname{cofix}(\beta)\leq 2
. It is actually possible that
\operatorname{cofix}(\beta)=1
, so that in fact
\beta\in\Gamma_{1}
. For example, if
A
is a vector space, then
X\sqcup\{y,y+y^{\prime}\}\sqcup Z
is also a basis of
A
, and
(y+y^{\prime})\beta=y+y^{\prime}
. It follows that
\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)=\langle\Gamma_{1}\rangle
if
A
is a vector space.

Among other things, the next result shows that the situation just described is true of a wider class of algebras:

Theorem 8.50
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra.

(i)

If
A
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
8.26
, then Theorem
8.47
holds with
\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
replaced by
\Gamma_{2}
.

(ii)

If
A
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
8.27
, but not those of Proposition
8.26
, then Theorem
8.47
holds with
{\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}}
replaced by
\Gamma_{1}
.

(iii)

If
A
satisfies either of the conditions of Lemma
8.34
, then Theorem
8.47
holds with
{\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}}
replaced by
\Gamma_{1}
.

Proof.

(i)
. In this case, the subalgebras of codimension
1
are of the form
A\setminus x
, and if an automorphism fixes
A\setminus x
pointwise, it must also fix
x
. So
\Gamma_{1}=\varnothing
, and the claim follows.

For later reference, it is worth discussing an alternative proof of this part. We know from Proposition
8.27
that
{\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\{{\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha}:{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}}\}}
, where again we write
X=A\setminus\mathcal{C}(A)
, and of course it follows that

\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)=\{{\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha}:{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{\operatorname{f}}_{X}}\}.

It is well known that finite symmetric groups are generated by transpositions. So too therefore is the finitary symmetric group
\mathcal{G}^{\operatorname{f}}_{X}
, and so
\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
is generated by automorphisms of the form
\beta=\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup(x,y)
for distinct
x,y\in X
(again using standard cycle notation). But for this
\beta
we have
\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)=A\setminus\{x,y\}=\big\langle X\setminus\{x,y\}\big\rangle
. Since
X
is independent, so too is
X\setminus\{x,y\}
, so
\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)
has codimension
2
.

(ii)
. This is similar to the second proof of
(i)
. Again we have
{\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)=\{{\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup\alpha}:{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{\operatorname{f}}_{X}}\}}
, where
X=A\setminus\mathcal{C}(A)
. So
\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
is generated by automorphisms of the form
\beta=\mathrm{id}_{C}\cup(x,y)
for distinct
x,y\in X
, and we still have
\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)=A\setminus\{x,y\}=\big\langle X\setminus\{x,y\}\big\rangle
. Since
X
is not independent, but
X\setminus x
and
X\setminus y
both are, it follows that
\operatorname{Fix}(\beta)
is maximal, and hence has codimension
1
.

(iii)
. Looking at the proof of Theorem
8.47
, it suffices to show that the automorphism
\beta
constructed during Case 2 can be expressed as
\beta=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}
, where each
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma_{1}
satisfies
\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\gamma_{i})
. To do so, we keep the notation of that part of the proof, and it will be convenient to write
U=X\sqcup Z
. So
U\sqcup\{y,y^{\prime}\}
is a basis of
A
, and
\beta\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
acts via

u\beta=u,\hskip 20.00003pty\beta=y^{\prime}\and y^{\prime}\beta=y\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u\in U$.}

By Lemma
8.34
(ii)
, we may choose some
w\in A
such that
w\not\in\langle U\sqcup y\rangle\cup\langle U\sqcup y^{\prime}\rangle
. It follows quickly that
U\sqcup\{y,w\}
and
U\sqcup\{y^{\prime},w\}
are both bases of
A
. We then define
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in\Gamma_{1}
by their action on the various bases by

\displaystyle u\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=u,

\displaystyle u\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=u,

\displaystyle u\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=u,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=y,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=y^{\prime},

\displaystyle y^{\prime}\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=y^{\prime},

\displaystyle y^{\prime}\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=w,

\displaystyle w\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=w,

\displaystyle w\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=y,

for each
u\in U
. Verification that
\beta=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}
is straightforward, and we have
\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)=\langle X\rangle
, and
X\subseteq U
.
∎

Remark 8.51
.

As we observed in the proof, parts
(i)
and
(ii)
of Theorem
8.50
boil down to the fact that finite symmetric groups are generated by transpositions. The standard Coxeter presentation for a finite symmetric group involves (simple) transpositions
[
92
]
; this is stated in Theorem
9.55
below. We believe it would be interesting to look for presentations for
\operatorname{Aut}^{\operatorname{f}}(A)
in the cases that
A
does not satisfy the conditions of Propositions
8.26
or
8.27
, but this is beyond the scope of the current work. To our knowledge, such presentations are not even known in the case that
A
is a finite-dimensional vector space.

For infinite
\dim(A)
, generating sets for the full automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
can be very complicated, even when
A
is simply a set (since
\mathcal{G}_{A}
is uncountable).

Remark 8.52
.

Since vector spaces of dimension
2\leq n<\aleph_{0}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
8.34
(cf. Remark
8.37
), Theorem
8.50
(iii)
implies the well-known fact that general linear groups are generated by matrices whose fix spaces have codimension
1
. Indeed, it is a basic fact of linear algebra that every invertible matrix is a product of ‘elementary row operation’ matrices, and one can easily see that the fix space of each such matrix has codimension
1
. This is clear for row operations of the form
R_{i}\to\lambda R_{i}
or
R_{i}\to R_{i}+R_{j}
; for a row swap
R_{i}\leftrightarrow R_{j}
, we use the argument in Remark
8.49
, with
y
and
y^{\prime}
the
i
th and
j
th standard basis vectors.

Remark 8.53
.

Consider again the algebra
A=\{a,b,c,d\}
from Remark
8.31
. Using the notation of that remark, we have

\Gamma_{0}=\{\mathrm{id}_{A}\},\hskip 20.00003pt\Gamma_{1}=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}\and\Gamma_{2}=\{\alpha_{3},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\beta_{4}\}.

Then
A
satisfies none of the conditions of Proposition
8.26
, Proposition
8.27
or Lemma
8.34
, and one can check that

\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\langle\Gamma_{2}\rangle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{but}\hskip 20.00003pt\langle\Gamma_{1}\rangle=\{\mathrm{id}_{A},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\rangle\not=\operatorname{Aut}(A).

8.6
Action pairs and subsemigroups

Consider a partial endomorphism
\alpha:B\to A
, where
A
is an independence algebra. Since any basis for
B
can be extended to a basis of
A
, it follows from
(FBP)
that
\alpha
can be extended to an endomorphism
\beta:A\to A
. Note then that
\alpha=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\beta
, so that every partial endomorphism is a restriction of an endomorphism. This and more can be expressed in terms of (strong) action pairs, as we explore in this section.

In what follows, we continue to write

\mathcal{E}_{A}=\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B\leq A}\}

for the semilattice of all partial identities of
A
. We observed in Section
8.2
that
\mathcal{E}_{A}
is isomorphic to the
\cap
-semilattice
\operatorname{Sub}(A)
.
We also write

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})=\mathcal{E}_{A}\setminus\{\mathrm{id}_{A}\}=\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B<A}\},

and we note that
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})
is a subsemilattice of
\mathcal{E}_{A}
, regardless of the dimension of
A
. We have
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})=\varnothing
if
\dim(A)=0
.

Proposition 8.54
.

For any independence algebra
A
, the following are all strong action pairs in
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
:

(i)

(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{End}(A))
,

(ii)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A}),\operatorname{End}(A))
,

(iii)

(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A)))
if
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
,

(iv)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A}),\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A)))
if
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
,

(v)

(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{Aut}(A))
,

(vi)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A}),\operatorname{Aut}(A))
.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
4.44
and
8.16
, given (
8.17
) and (
8.18
).
∎

Remark 8.55
.

In fact the previous result holds for an arbitrary algebra
A
(with the same proof), but note that parts
(iii)
and
(iv)
hold precisely when
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
is a subsemigroup of
\operatorname{End}(A)
. (When
A
is not an independence algebra, there might not be a meaningful notion of dimension.)

Each of the pairs
(E,S)
listed in Proposition
8.54
leads as usual to the subsemigroup
ES
of
{M=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}
. The next result identifies the set
ES
for each pair. Note that we do not assume
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
in the third and fourth parts; even though the stated equality still holds when
\dim(A)\geq\aleph_{0}
, the subsets
S
and
ES
are not semigroups in this case.

Theorem 8.56
.

For any independence algebra
A
, we have:

(i)

\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{End}(A)=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
,

(ii)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})\cdot\operatorname{End}(A)=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{End}(A)
,

(iii)

\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))\subseteq\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))
, with equality unless
\dim(A)=1
and
A
has no constants,

(iv)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))\subseteq\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{End}(A)
, with equality unless
\dim(A)=1
and
A
has no constants,

(v)

\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)\subseteq\operatorname{PAut}(A)
, with equality if and only if
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
,

(vi)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)\subseteq\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PAut}(A))
, with equality if and only if
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
.

Proof.

The forwards inclusions are straightforward in each case (and trivial for
(i)
), given that

\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\alpha=\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\alpha\in\operatorname{End}(A)$ and $B\leq A$.}

For cases
(ii)
–
(vi)
, we additionally rely on (combinations of) the following simple observations:

•

If
B\not=A
(i.e.,
\mathrm{id}_{B}\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})
), then
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
is not an endomorphism (and of course not an automorphism).

•

If
\alpha
is not an automorphism (i.e.,
\alpha\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
), then neither is
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
. (Indeed, this is clear if
B=A
, and follows from the previous point if
B<A
.)

•

If
\alpha
is injective, then so too is
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
.

We now work towards the reverse inclusions. For this, we fix some
{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}
, and write
B=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
. Let
X
be a basis of
B
, and extend this to a basis
X\sqcup Y
of
A
. Let
f:Y\to A
be an arbitrary function, so that
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{X}\sqcup f:X\sqcup Y\to A
extends to an endomorphism
\beta:A\to A
. For any
x\in X
, we have
x\beta=x\alpha
, so since
X
is a basis it follows that
b\beta=b\alpha
for all
b\in B
. Consequently, we have

\alpha=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\beta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with $\mathrm{id}_{B}\in\mathcal{E}_{A}$ and $\beta\in\operatorname{End}(A)$.}

This completes the proof of part
(i)
. For the other parts, we need to ensure that the additional constraints on
\alpha
(by virtue of belonging to the appropriate right-hand set) allow us to define
f
in such a way that the appropriate conditions on
\beta
(and
\mathrm{id}_{B}
) hold, or else explain why the backwards inclusions do not hold in the relevant cases.

(ii)
. If
\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{End}(A)
, then
B\not=A
, so
\mathrm{id}_{B}\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})
.

(iii)
. First suppose
\dim(A)=1
and
A
has no constants. Then by Remark
8.20
(vii)
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))=\varnothing

(8.57)

and

\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{End}(A)=\{\varnothing\},

(8.58)

so we have strict inclusion in this case.

We now assume that
\dim(A)\not=1
and/or
A
has constants.

Suppose
\alpha\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))
. We must show that
\beta
can be chosen so that
\beta\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
. First, if
\alpha\in\operatorname{End}(A)
, then
\alpha\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
and
B=A
, so that
\beta=\alpha\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
. So now suppose
\alpha\not\in\operatorname{End}(A)
. In particular,
B<A
, which implies
Y\not=\varnothing
, and so
\dim(A)\geq 1
. Since we have also assumed that
\dim(A)\not=1
and/or
A
has constants, it then follows from Lemma
8.21
that
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))\not=\varnothing
.

If
X\not=\varnothing
, then we fix some
x\in X
, and simply ensure that
yf=x\alpha
for all
y\in Y
; in this case,
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{X}\sqcup f
is not injective, and so
\beta\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
.

If
X=\varnothing
, then
B=\mathcal{C}(A)
and
\alpha=\mathrm{id}_{B}
(since every partial endomorphism fixes
\mathcal{C}(A)
pointwise). So in fact,
\alpha=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\beta
for
any

\beta\in\operatorname{End}(A)
. In particular, we can take
\beta
to be any element of
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
, which we noted above is non-empty.

(iv)
. If
\dim(A)=1
and
A
has no constants, then strict inclusion again follows from (
8.57
) and (
8.58
). So now suppose
\dim(A)\not=1
and/or
A
has constants, and let
{\alpha\in\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{End}(A)}
. As in
(ii)
, we have
\mathrm{id}_{B}\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})
. As in
(iii)
, we can choose
\beta
so that
\beta\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
.

(v)
. First suppose
\dim(A)\geq\aleph_{0}
, and fix any basis
Z
for
A
. Let
z\in Z
, and let
\beta
be an endomorphism extending some bijection
Z\to Z\setminus z
. Then in fact
\beta\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PAut}(A))
. But if
\beta=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\gamma
for some
B\leq A
and
\gamma\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
, then from
\beta=\gamma\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
it follows that
B=\operatorname{dom}(\beta)=A
, and so
\beta=\gamma\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
, a contradiction.

Now suppose
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
. Since
\alpha\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)
, it follows that
X\alpha
is a basis for
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)
and
|X\alpha|=|X|
. We then extend
X\alpha
to a basis
X\alpha\sqcup W
for
A
, noting that
|W|=|Y|
, and let
f:Y\to W
be any bijection. Clearly then
\beta\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
.

(vi)
. The proof is essentially the same as
(v)
. The same
\beta\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PAut}(A))
deals with the case
\dim(A)\geq\aleph_{0}
. When
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
, we define
f
as above, and note that
\alpha\not\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
implies
B<A
.
∎

Remark 8.59
.

One might wonder if any of the pairs
(E,S)
in Proposition
8.54
are proper, in the sense of Definition
5.25
. By Proposition
5.29
this is equivalent to the left restriction semigroup
ES
being proper, in the sense of Definition
5.7
. But it is easy to see that this is never the case (apart from trivially small exceptions). Indeed,
\mathrm{id}_{C}
is a left zero element of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, where
C=\mathcal{C}(A)
, and we have
\mathrm{id}_{C}\in E=P(ES)
for each of the pairs
(E,S)
listed in the proposition. It follows immediately that
\sigma=\sigma_{ES}=\nabla_{S}
(the universal relation) in each case. The equivalence (
5.8
) becomes

\alpha=\beta\ \Leftrightarrow\ \operatorname{dom}(\alpha)=\operatorname{dom}(\beta)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $\alpha,\beta\in ES$,}

and this clearly does not hold (apart from trivially small exceptions).

On the other hand, it follows from Theorem
5.39
(cf. Theorem
5.11
) that the left restriction semigroup
ES
is covered by the proper left restriction semigroup

\big\{{(\mathrm{id}_{B},\alpha)\in E\rtimes S}:{\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\alpha^{+}}\big\}=\big\{{(\mathrm{id}_{B},\alpha)\in E\rtimes S}:{B\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}\big\}.

Remark 8.60
.

Part
(ii)
of Theorem
8.56
is a special case of the right ideal
P^{\flat}T=M\setminus T
discussed in Remark
4.45
; cf. (
8.17
) and (
8.18
).

By Proposition
8.54
,
(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{Aut}(A))
and
(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A}),\operatorname{Aut}(A))
are strong action pairs in
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
, for arbitrary
A
. Theorem
8.56
characterises the resulting subsemigroups
\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)
in the case that
\dim(A)
is finite. For the infinite case, we need the notion of
codimension
from Definition
8.6
:

Theorem 8.61
.

For any independence algebra
A
, we have

(i)

\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\big\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)}:{\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{dom}(\alpha))=\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{im}(\alpha))}\big\}
,

(ii)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\big\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)}:{\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{dom}(\alpha))=\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{im}(\alpha))\geq 1}\big\}
.

Proof.

Since

\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\big({\operatorname{Sing}}(\mathcal{E}_{A})\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)\big)\sqcup\operatorname{Aut}(A),

and since

\operatorname{Aut}(A)=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)}:{\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{dom}(\alpha))=\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{im}(\alpha))=0}\},

it suffices to prove
(i)
.

Beginning with the forwards inclusion, let
\alpha\in\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)
, so that
\alpha=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\beta=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
for some
B\leq A
and
\beta\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
. Fix some
B
-basis
X
of
A
, and also write
C=\operatorname{im}(\alpha)=B\beta
and
Y=X\beta
. We first claim that
Y
is a
C
-basis of
A
. To see this, first note that since
\beta
is an automorphism, we have
A=\langle B\cup X\rangle=\langle(B\cup X)\beta\rangle=\langle C\cup Y\rangle
. It remains (for the claim) to show that
Y
is
C
-independent, and this is also straightforward. If
y\in\big\langle C\cup(Y\setminus y)\big\rangle
for some
y\in Y
, then applying the automorphism
\beta^{-1}
, we have
y\beta^{-1}\in\big\langle B\cup(X\setminus y\beta^{-1})\big\rangle
, with
y\beta^{-1}\in X
, contradicting
B
-independence of
X
. With the claim established, and since
\beta
is injective, we then have

\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{dom}(\alpha))=\operatorname{codim}(B)=|X|=|X\beta|=|Y|=\operatorname{codim}(C)=\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{im}(\alpha)).

Conversely, suppose
\alpha\in\operatorname{PAut}(A)
is such that
\operatorname{codim}(B)=\operatorname{codim}(C)
, where we have written
B=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
and
C=\operatorname{im}(\alpha)
. Let
X
be a basis for
B
, and let
Y
and
Z
be
B
- and
C
-bases for
A
, respectively. Since
\alpha:B\to C
is an isomorphism,
X\alpha
is a basis of
C
, and it follows from Lemma
8.7
that
X\cup Y
and
X\alpha\cup Z
are bases for
A
.
Since
{|Y|=\operatorname{codim}(B)=\operatorname{codim}(C)=|Z|}
, we may fix a bijection
\phi:Y\to Z
. Since
X\cup Y
is a basis of
A
, we may define
\beta\in\operatorname{End}(A)
to be the endomorphism extending
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{X}\cup\phi:X\cup Y\to X\alpha\cup Z
. Since
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{X}\cup\phi
is a bijection between bases, it follows that
\beta\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
. For any
x\in X
we have
x\beta=x\alpha
, so since
B=\langle X\rangle
it follows that
b\beta=b\alpha
for all
b\in B
. We then have
\alpha=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\beta\in\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)
.
∎

Remark 8.62
.

Theorem
8.61
holds for arbitrary
\dim(A)
. When
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
, we have

\dim(B)=\dim(C)\ \Rightarrow\ \operatorname{codim}(B)=\operatorname{codim}(C)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $B,C\leq A$},

so parts
(v)
and
(vi)
of Theorem
8.56
follow from Theorem
8.61
.

Remark 8.63
.

Note that
\mathcal{E}_{A}
and
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
respectively consist of all idempotents and units of the inverse monoid
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
. It follows that
\mathcal{E}_{A}\cdot\operatorname{Aut}(A)
is the largest factorisable inverse submonoid of
\operatorname{PAut}(A)
. Theorem
8.61
(i)
is therefore a generalisation of the main result of
[
14
]
, which concerns the case that
A
is a set, and says that the largest factorisable inverse submonoid of the symmetric inverse monoid
\mathcal{I}_{A}
is

\mathcal{F}_{A}=\big\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{A}}:{|A\setminus{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}|=|A\setminus{\operatorname{im}(\alpha)}|}\big\}.

(In this case, an arbitrary subset
B\subseteq A
is a subalgebra, and
\operatorname{codim}(B)=|A\setminus B|
.)

Remark 8.64
.

It is worth considering Proposition
8.54
and Theorems
8.56
and
8.61
in the special case that
A=X
is simply a set with no operations. As explained above, we then have
{\operatorname{PEnd}(A)=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}}
, and so on. We then obtain the following strong action pairs in
M=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
:

(i)

(\mathcal{E}_{X},\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(ii)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X}),\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(iii)

(\mathcal{E}_{X},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))
if
|X|<\aleph_{0}
,

(iv)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X}),\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))
if
|X|<\aleph_{0}
,

(v)

(\mathcal{E}_{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
,

(vi)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X}),\mathcal{G}_{X})
.

These action pairs give rise, respectively, to the following product semigroups:

(i)

\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{X}=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
,

(ii)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})\cdot\mathcal{T}_{X}=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{X}
,

(iii)

\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
if
1\not=|X|<\aleph_{0}
,

(iv)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{X}
if
1\not=|X|<\aleph_{0}
,

(v)

\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{X}=\mathcal{F}_{X}
,

(vi)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})\cdot\mathcal{G}_{X}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{F}_{X})
.

The results of the current chapter all have interpretations for these subsemigroups of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
. We will not explicitly state these, however, as they are more readily deduced as special cases of the results of Chapter
9
.

8.7
Congruence conditions

The action pairs
(E,S)
listed in Proposition
8.54
lead to the subsemigroups
ES
of
{M=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)}
, which are characterised in Theorems
8.56
and
8.61
. In each case we have
ES\cong(E\rtimes S)/\theta
, as in Proposition
4.46
. Section
4.5
gave a number of conditions under which relatively small generating sets exist for the congruence
\theta
; such conditions also featured in a number of results of Chapter
6
concerning presentations for
ES
. The purpose of the current section is to identify which of these conditions hold for the pairs in Proposition
8.54
.

Lemma 8.65
.

Let
(E,S)
be one of the (strong) action pairs in
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
listed in Proposition
8.54
, with the exception of
(E,S)=(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A}),\operatorname{End}(A))
in the case that
\dim(A)\geq\aleph_{0}
. Then
(E,S)
satisfies at least one of the conditions listed in Lemma
4.61
.

Proof.

The pairs in parts
(i)
,
(iii)
or
(v)
of Proposition
8.54
satisfy
all
the conditions of Lemma
4.61
, since
\mathcal{E}_{A}
is a submonoid of
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
; cf. Remark
4.62
.

The other pairs (not including the stated exception) satisfy condition
(viii)
, and hence also
(x)
. To prove this, we need to show that every element of
ES
has a right identity from
E
. So let
\alpha\in ES
, and put
B=\operatorname{im}(\alpha)
. Since we clearly have
\alpha=\alpha\cdot\mathrm{id}_{B}
, it remains to show that
B\not=A
. For this, we consider the three cases (as listed in Proposition
8.54
) separately.

(ii)
. Here Theorem
8.56
gives
ES=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{End}(A)
, and
\dim(A)
is finite (because the infinite case is excluded in the lemma). Since
\alpha
is therefore not an endomorphism,
\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
is a proper subalgebra of
A
, and hence
\dim(\operatorname{dom}(\alpha))<\dim(A)
, as the latter is finite. It follows that
\dim(B)=\dim(\operatorname{im}(\alpha))\leq\dim(\operatorname{dom}(\alpha))<\dim(A)
, and so
B\not=A
.

(iv)
. The proof is essentially the same as for
(ii)
, except that finiteness of
\dim(A)
is built into the assumption of this case in Proposition
8.54
.

(vi)
. Here we use Theorem
8.61
(ii)
, which tells us that
\operatorname{codim}(B)\geq 1
, whence
B\not=A
.
∎

Remark 8.66
.

When
\dim(A)\geq\aleph_{0}
,
(E,S)=(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A}),\operatorname{End}(A))
does not satisfy any of the conditions of Lemma
4.61
. Indeed, in this case Theorem
8.56
(ii)
gives
ES=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)\setminus\operatorname{End}(A)
, and if
\alpha
is any partial endomorphism with
\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)\not=A=\operatorname{im}(\alpha)
, then no element of
{E=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})}
is a right identity for
\alpha
. This shows that condition
(viii)
of Lemma
4.61
does not hold, and hence neither do any of the other conditions. (No such
\alpha
exists if
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
.)

Remark 8.67
.

In the above proof we showed that condition
(viii)
of Lemma
4.61
was satisfied for the cases in which
E=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})
, apart from the excluded case; condition
(x)
therefore holds as well. On the other hand, condition
(vii)
is never satisfied (in these cases), apart from trivially small exceptions. Examining the flow of implications in Lemma
4.61
, it follows that none of
(i)
,
(iii)
–
(vii)
or
(ix)
hold. Condition
(ii)
is satisfied for
{(E,S)=(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A}),\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))}
with
\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}
, but not for the other two pairs.

It follows from Lemma
8.65
that Lemma
4.65
applies to each of the action pairs listed in Proposition
8.54
, with the exception of
(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A}),\operatorname{End}(A))
in the case that
\dim(A)\geq\aleph_{0}
. More powerful results from Section
4.5
—such as Lemmas
4.71
,
4.73
and
4.74
, and also Theorems
6.28
and
6.44
from Chapter
6
—involve pairs
(E,S)
with
E
a (commutative) monoid, satisfying various conditions on joins of the right congruences
\theta_{e}
(or
\Theta_{e}
) or sub(semi)groups
S_{e}
, for
e\in E
. The pairs
(E,S)
from Proposition
8.54
with
E=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{A})
are therefore excluded. Thus, the remainder of Section
8.7
concerns pairs
(E,S)
where:

•

E=\mathcal{E}_{A}=\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B\leq A}\}
, and

•

S
is one of
\operatorname{End}(A)
,
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
or
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
, where we must additionally assume that
{\dim(A)<\aleph_{0}}
in the second case.

Our main goal here is to determine which of the above-mentioned conditions hold for these pairs.

Recall that for
B\leq A
, we have the right congruence
\theta_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}
on
S
, defined by

\theta_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}=\big\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in S\times S}:{\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\alpha=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\beta}\big\}=\big\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in S\times S}:{\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}}\big\}.

(8.68)

(Since this depends on
S
, we will be careful to specify which of the above choices of
S
statements apply to.) To simplify notation, we write
\theta_{B}
for
\theta_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}
. It is clear that (for any
S
)

B\subseteq C\ \Rightarrow\ \theta_{B}\supseteq\theta_{C}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $B,C\leq A$.}

(8.69)

It is also worth noting that
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
(i.e.,
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{B}
) if and only if
\alpha
and
\beta
agree on a basis of
B
.

The next two results apply to
strong
independence algebras (cf. Definition
8.10
). The first shows that Lemma
4.71
(ii)
applies to the pair
(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{End}(A))
for strong
A
:

Proposition 8.70
.

If
S=\operatorname{End}(A)
for a strong independence algebra
A
, then
\theta_{B}\vee\theta_{C}=\theta_{B\cap C}
for all
B,C\leq A
.

Proof.

By (
8.69
), we have
\theta_{B},\theta_{C}\subseteq\theta_{B\cap C}
, and hence
\theta_{B}\vee\theta_{C}\subseteq\theta_{B\cap C}
. Thus, it remains to show that
{\theta_{B\cap C}\subseteq\theta_{B}\vee\theta_{C}}
. To do so, fix some
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{B\cap C}
, meaning that

\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{End}(A)\and\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B\cap C}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B\cap C}.

Fix a basis
X
for
B\cap C
, and extend this to bases
X\sqcup Y
and
X\sqcup Z
for
B
and
C
, respectively. By Lemma
8.12
,
X\sqcup Y\sqcup Z
is independent, so there exists an endomorphism
\gamma\in\operatorname{End}(A)
extending the map

X\sqcup Y\sqcup Z\to A:\begin{cases}x\mapsto x\alpha=x\beta&\text{for $x\in X$,}\\
y\mapsto y\alpha&\text{for $y\in Y$,}\\
z\mapsto z\beta&\text{for $z\in Z$.}\end{cases}

Since
\gamma
agrees with
\alpha
on the basis
X\sqcup Y
of
B
we have
(\alpha,\gamma)\in\theta_{B}
. Similarly,
(\gamma,\beta)\in\theta_{C}
, so it follows that
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{B}\vee\theta_{C}
, as required.
∎

The situation for the pair
(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A)))
is almost the same as for
(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{End}(A))
, with one exception (when
\dim(A)=2
and
A
has no constants). The next result concerns the right congruences
\Theta_{B}=\Theta_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}
on
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))\cup\{\mathrm{id}_{A}\}
, defined in (
6.35
). It is again clear that
B\subseteq C\ \Rightarrow\ \Theta_{B}\supseteq\Theta_{C}
for all
B,C\leq A
; cf. (
8.69
).

Proposition 8.71
.

If
S=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
for a finite-dimensional strong independence algebra
A
, and if
[\dim(A)\not=2
or
A
has constants
]
, then
\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C}=\Theta_{B\cap C}
for all
B,C\leq A
.

Proof.

Again we must show that
{\Theta_{B\cap C}\subseteq\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C}}
. This is clear if
B
and
C
are comparable in the inclusion order, so suppose instead that
B
and
C
are incomparable, and let
(\alpha,\beta)\in\Theta_{B\cap C}
. Fix a basis
X
for
B\cap C
, and extend this to bases
X\sqcup Y
and
X\sqcup Z
for
B
and
C
, respectively. So again
X\sqcup Y\sqcup Z
is independent. While it is possible for
X
to be empty, both
Y
and
Z
are non-empty (by incomparability). We now consider separate cases. In each one, we define three singular endomorphisms
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
such that

\alpha\mathrel{\Theta}_{B}\gamma_{1}\mathrel{\Theta}_{C}\gamma_{2}\mathrel{\Theta}_{B}\gamma_{3}\mathrel{\Theta}_{C}\beta,

(8.72)

which leads to
(\alpha,\beta)\in\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C}
. The verification that
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
are indeed singular, and that (
8.72
) holds, is left to the reader.

Case 1.
If
X\not=\varnothing
, then we fix some
x_{0}\in X
, and we let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
be any endomorphisms such that for
x\in X
,
y\in Y
and
z\in Z
,

\displaystyle x\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=x\alpha=x\beta,

\displaystyle x\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=x\alpha=x\beta,

\displaystyle x\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=x\alpha=x\beta,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=y\alpha,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=x_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=x_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=x_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=x_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=z\beta.

Case 2.
If
X=\varnothing
and
A
has a constant
c
, then we let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
be any endomorphisms such that for
y\in Y
and
z\in Z
,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=y\alpha,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=c,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=c,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=c,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=c,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=z\beta.

Case 3.
Finally, suppose
X=\varnothing
and
A
has no constants. By the assumption in the statement of the lemma, we have
\dim(A)\not=2
, and so since
\dim(A)\geq|Y|+|Z|\geq 2
, we must have
\dim(A)\geq 3
. By symmetry we may assume that
|Y|\geq|Z|
, and we fix some
y_{0}\in Y
. We also extend
Y\sqcup Z
to a basis
Y\sqcup Z\sqcup W
for
A
, noting that
W
could be empty. We then let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
be any endomorphisms such that for
y\in Y
,
z\in Z
and
w\in W
,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=y\alpha,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=y_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle y\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=y_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=y_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=y_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle z\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=z\beta,

\displaystyle w\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=y_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle w\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=y_{0}\alpha,

\displaystyle w\gamma_{3}

\displaystyle=y_{0}\alpha.

(Singularity of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
is clear, and also of
\gamma_{3}
when
W\not=\varnothing
. If
W=\varnothing
, then
{3\leq\dim(A)=|Y|+|Z|}
and
|Y|\geq|Z|
force
|Y|\geq 2
, and singularity of
\gamma_{3}
quickly follows.)
∎

Proposition
8.71
excluded the case in which
\dim(A)=2
and
A
has no constants. We will soon show, in Proposition
8.74
, that a weaker (but equally powerful) condition holds in this case; cf. Remark
8.75
. We begin with a lemma.

Lemma 8.73
.

Suppose
A
is a two-dimensional independence algebra with no constants. Then for any
\alpha\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))\cup\{\mathrm{id}_{A}\}
we have
(\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{A})\in\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C}
for some
B,C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
.

Proof.

Only the case
\alpha\not=\mathrm{id}_{A}
needs proof.
Fix a basis
\{x,y\}
for
A
, and write
u=x\alpha
and
v=y\alpha
. Since
\alpha
is singular, and since
A
has no constants, we have
\operatorname{rank}(\alpha)=1
, and it quickly follows that
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)=\langle u\rangle=\langle v\rangle
.
Let
B_{1}=\langle x\rangle
,
B_{2}=\langle y\rangle
and
C=\langle u\rangle=\langle v\rangle
; each is maximal, by Proposition
8.22
. Since
C\not=A=\langle x,y\rangle
, we may assume by symmetry that
x\not\in C=\langle u\rangle
. It follows from Lemma
8.2
that
\{x,u\}
is independent, and hence a basis for
A
. In this case we define
\beta\in\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
with
x\beta=u\beta=u
. Then
\alpha\mathrel{\Theta}_{B_{1}}\beta\mathrel{\Theta}_{C}\mathrm{id}_{A}
, so that
(\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{A})\in\theta_{B_{1}}\vee\theta_{C}
.
∎

Proposition 8.74
.

If
S=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
for a two-dimensional independence algebra
A
with no constants, then for any
B<A
we have

\Theta_{B}=\bigvee_{C\in\operatorname{Max}(A),\atop B\subseteq C}\Theta_{C}.

Proof.

Fix
B<A
, and write
\Sigma=\{{C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}:{B\subseteq C}\}
. By the assumptions on
A
, either
B=\varnothing(=\mathcal{C}(A))
or else
B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
. In the latter case we have
\Sigma=\{B\}
, and the result is trivial. So now we assume that
B=\varnothing
, and here we have
\Sigma=\operatorname{Max}(A)
. Since
\Theta_{B}=\Theta_{\varnothing}=\nabla_{S^{1}}
, we must show that
\bigvee_{C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\Theta_{C}=\nabla_{S^{1}}
. For this it suffices to show that any
\alpha\in S=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
is
\sigma
-related to
\mathrm{id}_{A}
, where
\sigma=\bigvee_{C\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\Theta_{C}
, and this follows immediately from Lemma
8.73
.
∎

Remark 8.75
.

Proposition
8.71
feeds into Theorem
6.44
(ii)
, and shows that (in the notation of Theorems
6.36
and
6.44
) relations
R_{2}
may be replaced by
R_{2}^{\prime\prime}
for the pair
(U,S)=(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A)))
. Note that
R_{2}^{\prime\prime}
is defined with respect to a fixed generating set
V=X_{U}\phi_{U}
for
U=\mathcal{E}_{A}
, coming from a presentation
U\cong\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{U}}:{R_{U}}\rangle
. When
A
is finite-dimensional, Proposition
8.22
(cf. Theorem
8.40
) tells us that the most obvious generating set
V
for
{U=\mathcal{E}_{A}\cong(\operatorname{Sub}(A),\cap)}
is

V=\big\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\big\}.

Proposition
8.71
excluded the case in which
A
is two-dimensional and has no constants. However, Proposition
8.74
shows that Theorem
6.44
(i)
applies in this case with respect to the same set
V=\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\}
, so that relations
R_{2}
may be replaced by
R_{2}^{\prime}
in the notation of that theorem. It follows that in fact
R_{2}^{\prime}=R_{2}^{\prime\prime}
in this case.

Remark 8.76
.

Even though Proposition
8.71
excluded the case in which
\dim(A)=2
and
A
has no constants, the conclusion of the proposition may still hold in this case. For example, suppose
A=\{1,2\}
with no operations, so that
S=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{2})=\{\alpha,\beta\}
, where
\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\beta=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\
2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Recall that Proposition
8.71
only needs proof when
B
and
C
are incomparable, and up to symmetry this is only the case when
B=\{1\}
and
C=\{2\}
. But here we have
\alpha\mathrel{\Theta}_{B}\mathrm{id}_{A}
and
\beta\mathrel{\Theta}_{C}\mathrm{id}_{A}
, and it quickly follows that
\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C}=\nabla_{S^{1}}=\Theta_{\varnothing}=\Theta_{B\cap C}
.

Keeping Remark
8.75
in mind, in order to give presentations for
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))
, we will need to have generating sets for the right congruences
\theta_{B}
and
\Theta_{B}
for
B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
. It turns out that these are extremely simple:

Lemma 8.77
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra with
\dim(A)\geq 1
, and additionally assume that
\dim(A)\geq 2
if
A
has no constants. Then for any
{B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}
, the right congruence
\theta_{B}
on
\operatorname{End}(A)
is generated by the pair
(\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{A})
for any idempotent
\alpha\in\operatorname{End}(A)
with
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)=B
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we write
\sigma
for the right congruence on
\operatorname{End}(A)
generated by
(\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{A})
.

Since
\alpha
maps
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)=B
identically (as it is an idempotent), we have
\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\alpha=\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{A}
. This shows that
(\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{A})\in\theta_{B}
, and so
\sigma\subseteq\theta_{B}
.

For the reverse inclusion, fix some
(\beta,\gamma)\in\theta_{B}
. For any
x\in A
we have
x\alpha\in B
(as
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)=B
), so since
\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\gamma\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
it follows that
x(\alpha\beta)=(x\alpha)\beta=(x\alpha)\gamma=x(\alpha\gamma)
, so that
\alpha\beta=\alpha\gamma
. But also, since
\mathrm{id}_{A}\mathrel{\sigma}\alpha
, and since
\sigma
is a right congruence, we have
\beta=\mathrm{id}_{A}\cdot\beta\mathrel{\sigma}\alpha\beta=\alpha\gamma\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{A}\cdot\gamma=\gamma
.
∎

The next result has essentially the same proof:

Lemma 8.78
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra with
\dim(A)\geq 1
, and additionally assume that
\dim(A)\geq 2
if
A
has no constants. Then for any
{B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}
, the right congruence
\Theta_{B}
on
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))\cup\{\mathrm{id}_{A}\}
is generated by the pair
(\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{A})
for any idempotent
\alpha\in\operatorname{End}(A)
with
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)=B
. ∎

Remark 8.79
.

Lemmas
8.77
and
8.78
excluded the case in which
\dim(A)=1
and
A
has no constants. But in this case
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)=\operatorname{Aut}(A)\cup\{\varnothing\}
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))=\{\varnothing\}
, as in Remark
8.20
(vii)
. So it is trivial to derive a presentation for
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
from a presentation for
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
, and of course a presentation for
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))
is trivial.

We now move on to the pair
(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{Aut}(A))
, and here we do not need to assume
A
is strong. Since
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
is a group, Lemma
4.73
applies, and shows that a generating set for the congruence
\theta
may be constructed using (generating sets for) the subgroups

S_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)}:{\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\alpha}\}=\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)}:{\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $B\leq A$.}

(8.80)

For simplicity again, we will abbreviate
S_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}
to
S_{B}
. Note that we have the alternative formulation in terms of fix sets:

S_{B}=\big\{{\alpha\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)}:{B\subseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)}\big\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $B\leq A$.}

Lemma
4.74
gives conditions on these subgroups under which the generating set for
\theta
from Lemma
4.73
can be reduced further. The simpler of the two conditions is in Lemma
4.74
(ii)
, and says (in this case) that
{S_{B}\vee S_{C}=S_{B\cap C}}
for all
B,C\leq A
. Unfortunately, this does not hold in general, even in the simple case that
A
is a set of size
\geq 2
(with no operations), where
\operatorname{Aut}(A)
is the symmetric group
\mathcal{G}_{A}
. For example, if
A=B\sqcup C
, with
B
and
C
both non-empty, then each element of
S_{B}
and
S_{C}
fixes
B
and
C
setwise; so too therefore does each element of
S_{B}\vee S_{C}
. However,
S_{B\cap C}=S_{\varnothing}
is all of
\mathcal{G}_{A}
.
Despite this, we will now show that the weaker assumption of Lemma
4.74
(i)
does hold for the pair
(\mathcal{E}_{A},\operatorname{Aut}(A))
when
\dim(A)=n<\aleph_{0}
, with respect to the set

V=\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B\leq A,\ n-2\leq\dim(B)\leq n-1}\}.

Proposition 8.81
.

Let
A
be an independence algebra with
\dim(A)=n<\aleph_{0}
, and let

Q=\{{B\leq A}:{n-2\leq\dim(B)\leq n-1}\}.

Then for any
B<A
we have

S_{B}=\bigvee_{C\in Q,\atop B\subseteq C}S_{C}.

Proof.

As explained in Remark
4.75
, we only need to demonstrate the forwards inclusion. So let
\alpha\in S_{B}
, and fix a factorisation
\alpha=\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}
, for
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
, as in Theorem
8.47
. Also write
C_{i}=\operatorname{Fix}(\beta_{i})\in Q
for each
i
, so that each
C_{i}\supseteq\operatorname{Fix}(\alpha)\supseteq B
. But then

\alpha=\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}\in S_{C_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee S_{C_{k}}\subseteq\bigvee_{C\in Q,\atop B\subseteq C}S_{C}.\qed

Remark 8.82
.

As in Theorem
8.50
, the set
Q
in Proposition
8.81
can be replaced by

\{{B\leq A}:{\dim(B)=n-2}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003pt\{{B\leq A}:{\dim(B)=n-1}\},

as appropriate, if the various conditions of Propositions
8.26
and
8.27
, or Lemma
8.34
hold.

8.8
Presentations

We conclude Chapter
8
by discussing presentations for the semigroups

T_{1}=\operatorname{PEnd}(A),\hskip 20.00003ptT_{2}=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))\and T_{3}=\operatorname{PAut}(A),

where
A
is a finite-dimensional strong independence algebra.
As we have seen, each arises as a product
T_{i}=ES_{i}
, where
E=\mathcal{E}_{A}
and

S_{1}=\operatorname{End}(A),\hskip 20.00003ptS_{2}=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))\and S_{3}=\operatorname{Aut}(A).

We have already noted that presentations are not known for the
S_{i}
in general. However, it is possible to describe presentations for each
T_{i}
modulo a presentation for the corresponding
S_{i}
.

For example, suppose
S=\operatorname{End}(A)
has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{S}}:{R_{S}}\rangle
via
\phi_{S}:X_{S}^{*}\to\operatorname{End}(A)
. We know from Theorem
8.40
that
E=\mathcal{E}_{A}(\cong\operatorname{Sub}(A)=\operatorname{Sub}^{\operatorname{f}}(A))
has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{E}}:{R_{E}}\rangle
via
\phi_{E}:X_{E}^{*}\to E:x_{B}\mapsto\mathrm{id}_{B}
. It then follows from Theorem
6.5
that
ES=\operatorname{PEnd}(A)
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{E}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{E}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{\Omega}}\rangle,

where the additional sets of relations
R_{1}
and
R_{\Omega}
are as follows. For the former (and making the respective notational substitutions
z
and
x_{B}
for the letters
x\in X_{S}
and
y\in X_{E}(=X_{U})
in the original definition of
R_{1}
in (
6.3
)), we have

R_{1}=\big\{{(zx_{B},{}^{z}x_{B}\cdot z)}:{z\in X_{S},\ B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\big\},

where
{}^{z}x_{B}
is some word over
X_{E}
mapping to
{}^{z\phi_{S}}\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B(z\phi_{S})^{-1}}
.
As explained in Remark
6.6
(see (
6.9
), by Proposition
8.70
and Lemma
8.77
(and excluding trivial small cases), we can take

R_{\Omega}=\big\{{(x_{B},x_{B}N_{S}(\alpha_{B}))}:{B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)}\big\},

where for each
B\in\operatorname{Max}(A)
,
\alpha_{B}\in\operatorname{End}(A)
is some fixed idempotent with image
B
, and where
{N_{S}:\operatorname{End}(A)\to X_{S}^{*}}
is a normal form function.

The situation for
S=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
is very similar. We first note that
(E,S)
satisfies Assumption
6.33
, with
E
commutative. We may therefore apply Theorem
6.44
, Propositions
8.71
and
8.74
, and Remark
8.75
. The latter explains whether to use part
(i)
or
(ii)
of Theorem
6.44
, but in either case the resulting presentation for
\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{PEnd}(A))
has the form

\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{E}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{E}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle,

where this time
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{S}}:{R_{S}}\rangle
is a presentation for
S=\operatorname{Sing}(\operatorname{End}(A))
. The sets
R_{1}
and
R_{2}^{\prime\prime}
here have the same form as
R_{1}
and
R_{\Omega}
above (for
S=\operatorname{End}(A)
). Note that
{}^{z}x_{B}\in X_{E}^{*}
might be empty, but
N_{S}(\alpha_{B})\in X_{S}^{+}
is always non-empty.

In the case that
S=\operatorname{Aut}(A)
, Theorem
6.5
and Remark
6.6
apply, drawing from Lemma
4.74
(i)
and Proposition
8.81
. These yield the presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{E}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{E}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{\Omega}}\rangle,

where
R_{1}
is yet again as above, and

\displaystyle R_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(x_{B},x_{B}\cdot N_{S}(\alpha))}:{B\in\operatorname{Max}(A),\ \alpha\in\Gamma_{B}}\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\cup\big\{{(x_{B}x_{C},x_{B}x_{C}\cdot N_{S}(\alpha))}:{B,C\in\operatorname{Max}(A),\ B\not=C,\ \alpha\in\Gamma_{B\cap C}}\big\},

(8.83)

where each
\Gamma_{B}
is a generating set for
S_{B}=S_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}
. Such generating sets can be deduced from Theorems
8.47
and
8.50
. As in Remark
8.82
, we only need one of the two sets in the union (
8.83
) if any of the conditions of Theorem
8.50
hold.

9
Wreath products and free acts

This final chapter concerns a number of (transformational) wreath products
M\wr S
, where
M
is an arbitrary monoid, and
S
is a subsemigroup of some partial transformation semigroup
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
. The standard way to define the wreath product
M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
is as a semidirect product
M^{X}\rtimes\mathcal{T}_{X}
, where
M^{X}
is the direct product of
|X|
copies of
M
, and where
\mathcal{T}_{X}
acts on the coordinates of tuples from
M^{X}
; the full definitions are given below. (This distinguishes the
transformational
wreath products from the
unrestricted
wreath products considered implicitly in Section
5.4
. The unrestricted wreath product of
M
with
\mathcal{T}_{X}
is the semidirect product
M^{\mathcal{T}_{X}}\rtimes\mathcal{T}_{X}
.)

There are (at least) two standard ways to generalise this construction of
M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
in order to define
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
: either as a suitable subsemigroup or quotient of a semidirect product
M_{0}^{X}\rtimes\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, where
M_{0}
is
M
with a zero adjoined. These definitions are of course interchangeable, but among other things we will see in this chapter that action pairs provide a natural mechanism for passing between the two viewpoints. When
M
is a group,
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
is isomorphic to the partial endomorphism monoid of a free
M
-act of rank
|X|
. Since free group-acts are (strong) independence algebras, it follows that the results of this chapter and those of Chapter
8
have a common specialisation. We will sometimes be able to use results from Chapter
8
to assist in proofs in the current chapter.

We begin in Section
9.1
with definitions and basic results, including that
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
is a left restriction monoid, and we comment on the above connection with free group-acts. The rest of Chapter
9
then proceeds in two largely parallel strands. In Sections
9.2
and
9.5
we identify two families of action pairs in
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, leading to natural subsemigroups such as
M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
,
M\wr\mathcal{I}_{X}
,
M\wr(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{X})
,
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
for finite
X
, and so on. Sections
9.3
and
9.6
explore ‘congruence conditions’ that feed into general results from Chapters
4
and
6
. In Sections
9.4
and
9.7
we apply these general results to obtain presentations for several of our wreath products; see Theorems
9.33
,
9.59
,
9.68
,
9.69
and
9.70
.
Taking
M=\{1\}
, these theorems reduce to well-known presentations for various (partial) transformation semigroups
[
32
,
1
,
92
,
100
,
73
,
28
,
27
,
25
]
.

Throughout this chapter we will again often identify one-element subsets of
X
with their unique elements, and so use abbreviations such as
X\setminus x\equiv X\setminus\{x\}
.

9.1
Preliminaries

Let
M
be a monoid with identity
1
, and
X
an arbitrary set. Let
M_{0}=M\sqcup\{0\}
, where
0
is a symbol not belonging to
M
, acting as an adjoined zero element, even if
M
already had a zero element.

We write
M_{0}^{X}
for the set of all
X
-tuples
{\bf a}=(a_{x})_{x\in X}
over
M_{0}
; we usually abbreviate such a tuple to
{\bf a}=(a_{x})
. As usual,
M_{0}^{X}
is a monoid under the componentwise product. The identity of
M_{0}^{X}
is
{\bf 1}=(1)_{x\in X}
, the
X
-tuple with all entries equal to
1
. Before we define our wreath products, we fix some basic notation.

The
support
of
{\bf a}=(a_{x})\in M_{0}^{X}
is the set
\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})=\{{x\in X}:{a_{x}\not=0}\}
. It is worth noting that

\operatorname{supp}({\bf a}{\bf b})=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})\cap\operatorname{supp}({\bf b})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf a},{\bf b}\in M_{0}^{X}$.}

(9.1)

For
B\subseteq X
, we write
{\bf 1}_{B}\in M_{0}^{X}
for the
indicator function
of
B
, defined by

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{B}=(b_{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptb_{x}=\begin{cases}\mathrlap{1}\hphantom{a_{x\alpha}}&\text{if $x\in B$,}\\
\mathrlap{0}\hphantom{a_{x\alpha}}&\text{if $x\in X\setminus B$.}\end{cases}

For
{\bf a}=(a_{x})\in M_{0}^{X}
, and for
B\subseteq X
, we write
{\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}\in M_{0}^{X}
for the
restriction of
{\bf a}
to
B
, defined by

\displaystyle{\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}={\bf a}\cdot{\bf 1}_{B}={\bf 1}_{B}\cdot{\bf a}=(b_{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptb_{x}=\begin{cases}\mathrlap{a_{x}}\hphantom{a_{x\alpha}}&\text{if $x\in B$,}\\
\mathrlap{0}\hphantom{a_{x\alpha}}&\text{if $x\in X\setminus B$.}\end{cases}

Note that
\operatorname{supp}({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B})=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})\cap B
.

The partial transformation monoid
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
has a left action on
M_{0}^{X}
by semigroup morphisms, defined for
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
{\bf a}=(a_{x})\in M_{0}^{X}
by

\displaystyle{}^{\alpha}{\bf a}=(b_{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptb_{x}=\begin{cases}a_{x\alpha}&\text{if $x\in\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(9.2)

It is worth noting that there are two ways for
b_{x}
to equal
0
in (
9.2
); either
x\not\in\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
or else
x\in\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
and
a_{x\alpha}=0
. This means that

\operatorname{supp}({}^{\alpha}{\bf a})=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})\alpha^{-1}=\big\{{x\in\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}:{x\alpha\in\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})}\big\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}$ and $\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}$.}

(9.3)

The action from (
9.2
) has a natural diagrammatic interpretation, as shown in Figure
2
with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
and
\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3&4&5&6\\
2&-&3&2&6&6\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

\alpha
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{6}
{\bf a}
\alpha
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{2}
a_{6}
a_{6}
0
{}^{\alpha}{\bf a}

Figure 2:
The action of
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
on
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
from (
9.2
), with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
.

It is clear that the action in (
9.2
) is monoidal: i.e., that
{}^{\mathrm{id}_{X}}{\bf a}={\bf a}
for all
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
. It is not, however, by monoid morphisms, as for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, we have
{}^{\alpha}{\bf 1}={\bf 1}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}
. It follows from this that

{}^{\alpha}{\bf 1}={\bf 1}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \alpha\in\mathcal{T}_{X}.

(9.4)

For any subsemigroup
S\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, the action in (
9.2
) leads as usual to the semidirect product

M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S=\big\{{({\bf a},\alpha)}:{{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X},\ \alpha\in S}\big\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with operation}\hskip 20.00003pt({\bf a},\alpha)\cdot({\bf b},\beta)=({\bf a}\cdot{}^{\alpha}{\bf b},\alpha\beta).

If
S
is a submonoid of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, then
({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X})
is a left identity for
M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S
, as the action is monoidal, but it is only a right identity when the action of
S
on
M_{0}^{X}
is by monoid morphisms; by (
9.4
), this is equivalent to having
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
. This all follows from Lemma
3.11
, or by examining products of the form

({\bf a},\alpha)\cdot({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X})=({\bf a}\cdot{}^{\alpha}{\bf 1},\alpha)=({\bf a}\cdot{\bf 1}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\alpha)=({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\alpha).

(9.5)

In any case, when
S
is a submonoid of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, the local monoid of
M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S
with identity
({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X})
is
({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X})\cdot(M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S)\cdot({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X})=(M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S)\cdot({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X})
. Using (
9.5
) it is easy to see that this is

(M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S)\cdot({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X})=\big\{{({\bf a},\alpha)\in M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S}:{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}\big\}.

(9.6)

This leads us naturally to the following:

Definition 9.7
.

For any set
X
, any subsemigroup
S
of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, and any monoid
M
, the
(transformational) wreath product

M\wr S
is the set

M\wr S=\big\{{({\bf a},\alpha)\in M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S}:{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}\big\}.

It is routine to check that
M\wr S
is a subsemigroup of
M_{0}^{X}\rtimes S
. Indeed, given
({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta)\in M\wr S
, we have
({\bf a},\alpha)\cdot({\bf b},\beta)=({\bf a}\cdot{}^{\alpha}{\bf b},\alpha\beta)
, and (
9.1
) and (
9.3
) give

\operatorname{supp}({\bf a}\cdot{}^{\alpha}{\bf b})=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})\cap\operatorname{supp}({\bf b})\alpha^{-1}=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)\cap\operatorname{dom}(\beta)\alpha^{-1}=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha\beta).

Wreath products of this kind (and similar) have been studied by numerous authors. See for example
[
18
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
11
,
109
,
15
,
85
,
79
]
. The introduction to
[
70
]
discusses some of the early history of the idea, going back to the work of Specht
[
110
]
.

Remark 9.8
.

If
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
, then every element
({\bf a},\alpha)\in M\wr S
satisfies
{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)=X}
: i.e.,
{\bf a}\in M^{X}
. In this case, we have
M\wr S=M^{X}\rtimes S
.

Given (
9.5
), it is clear that
M\wr S
is a monoid whenever
S
is a submonoid of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
.

The elements of
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, and their products, have a natural diagrammatic representation, which will be useful in all the calculations to follow.
Figure
3
gives an example with
{X=\{1,\ldots,6\}}
, and

\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3&4&5&6\\
2&-&3&2&6&6\end{smallmatrix}\right)\and\beta=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3&4&5&6\\
1&1&-&4&-&4\end{smallmatrix}\right).

When drawing an element
({\bf a},\alpha)
of
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, we omit the label
a_{x}
of upper vertex
x
if this label is
0
. We will also often omit the label if it is
1
. One can tell whether an omitted label is
0
or
1
by the non/existence of an edge at that vertex. See Figures
4
–
10
.

a_{1}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{6}
b_{1}

b_{4}
