a_{1}b_{2}
a_{4}b_{2}
a_{5}b_{6}
a_{6}b_{6}

Figure 3:
Elements of
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
(left) and their product (right), with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
.

An important special case arises when
M
is a group, when wreath products can be viewed as (partial) endomorphism monoids.

Example 9.9
.

For an arbitrary monoid
M
, the class of
M
-acts forms a variety. Consequently, free
M
-acts exist, and a number of equivalent characterisations exist; see for example
[
69
]
. Roughly speaking, the free
M
-act of rank
\rho
can be thought of as
\rho
disjoint copies of
M
, with the action coinciding with multiplication in
M
. More formally, given a set
X
, the
free (left)
M
-act over
X
is the algebra
F_{X}(M)
with:

•

underlying set
M\times X
, and

•

a unary operation
f_{a}
for each
a\in M
, defined by
f_{a}(b,x)=(ab,x)
.

It is known
[
117
]
that
F_{X}(M)
is an independence algebra (of dimension
|X|
) if and only if
M
is a group. In this case, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{PEnd}(F_{X}(M))

\displaystyle\cong M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X},

\displaystyle\operatorname{PAut}(F_{X}(M))

\displaystyle\cong M\wr\mathcal{I}_{X},

\displaystyle\operatorname{End}(F_{X}(M))

\displaystyle\cong M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Aut}(F_{X}(M))

\displaystyle\cong M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}.

For example, the isomorphism
\operatorname{PAut}(F_{X}(M))\cong M\wr\mathcal{I}_{X}
(with
X
finite) was demonstrated in
[
11
, Theorem 2.2]
, and the argument there works virtually unchanged for the others (for any
X
). Thus, the group case of any result proved in this chapter can also be viewed as a special case of a result from Chapter
8
(when
A
is a free group-act).

When
M
is not a group, we do not have the above isomorphisms. Indeed, the proof in the group case relies on the fact that the subalgebras of
F_{X}(M)
are all of the form
F_{Y}(M)=M\times Y
for some
Y\subseteq X
, but this is no longer true when
M
is not a group. For example,
I\times X
is a subalgebra of
F_{X}(M)
for any left ideal
I\subseteq M
. More generally, one can show that the subalgebras of
F_{X}(M)
are precisely the subsets of the form
\bigcup_{y\in Y}(I_{y}\times\{y\})
, where
Y
is an arbitrary subset of
X
, and where each
I_{y}
(
y\in Y
) is a left ideal of
M
. (When
M
is a group, each
I_{y}=M
, as groups have no proper non-empty left ideals, and then the subalgebra in question is just
M\times Y=F_{Y}(M)
.)

Returning now to the more general discussion of wreath products, we fix a monoid
M
, a set
X
, and a subsemigroup
S\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
.
It is important to note that
M\wr S
contains a natural copy of
S
, namely:

S\equiv\big\{{({\bf 1}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\alpha)}:{\alpha\in S}\big\}.

In this way,
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
contains the semilattice

\mathcal{E}_{X}=\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B\subseteq X}\}\equiv\big\{{({\bf 1}_{B},\mathrm{id}_{B})}:{B\subseteq X}\big\}

of partial identities. As in Chapter
8
, we also write
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})=\mathcal{E}_{X}\setminus\{\mathrm{id}_{X}\}
.

It is easy to see that for all
({\bf a},\alpha)\in M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
B\subseteq X
, we have

\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)=({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B},\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B})\and({\bf a},\alpha)\cdot\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B\alpha^{-1}}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha).

(9.10)

Figures
4
and
5
illustrate these identities, with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
,
B=\{1,2,4,5\}
, and
\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3&4&5&6\\
2&-&3&2&6&6\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

a_{1}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{6}
a_{1}
a_{4}
a_{5}

Figure 4:
An example of
\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)=({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B},\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B})
in
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
.

a_{1}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{6}
a_{1}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{6}
a_{1}
a_{4}

Figure 5:
An example of
({\bf a},\alpha)\cdot\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B\alpha^{-1}}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)
in
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
.

The next result is crucial in all that follows. As far as we are aware, it has not been stated explicitly in the literature. Of course it follows from Proposition
8.16
in the special case that
M
is a group, when
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\cong\operatorname{PEnd}(F_{X}(M))
is the partial endomorphism monoid of the (independence) algebra
F_{X}(M)
.

Proposition 9.11
.

For any set
X
and monoid
M
, the wreath product
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
is a left restriction monoid under the unary operation
({\bf a},\alpha)^{+}=\alpha^{+}=\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}\equiv({\bf 1}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)})
.

Proof.

The identities
(L1)
and
(L2)
are clear. For
(L3)
and
(L4)
, we use (
9.10
). We just give the details for
(L4)
, as
(L3)
is easier. So consider elements
x=({\bf a},\alpha)
and
y=({\bf b},\beta)
from
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, and write
B=\operatorname{dom}(\beta)
. Then

xy^{+}=({\bf a},\alpha)\cdot\mathrm{id}_{B}\and(xy)^{+}x=\mathrm{id}_{D}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptD=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha\beta)=B\alpha^{-1},

so
xy^{+}=(xy)^{+}x
follows from the second identity in (
9.10
).
∎

In the notation of Section
4.3
, we have

\displaystyle P(M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})

\displaystyle=\big\{{({\bf a},\alpha)^{+}}:{({\bf a},\alpha)\in M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}}\big\}=\mathcal{E}_{X}

(9.12)

\displaystyle\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptT(M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})

\displaystyle=\big\{{({\bf a},\alpha)\in M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}}:{({\bf a},\alpha)^{+}=\mathrm{id}_{X}}\big\}=M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}.

(9.13)

Next we note that we may also identify
M_{0}^{X}
with a submonoid of
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
:

M_{0}^{X}\equiv\big\{{({\bf a},\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})})}:{{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}}\big\}.

For
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, we have

\displaystyle{\bf a}\cdot\alpha

\displaystyle=({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B},\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{C})

where
B=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
and
C=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})

\displaystyle=({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{D},\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{D})

where
D=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)\cap\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})
.

(9.14)

This is illustrated in Figure
6
, with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
,
C=\{1,2,4,5\}
and
\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3&4&5&6\\
2&-&3&2&6&6\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
In particular, if
({\bf a},\alpha)\in M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, then
\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
, and so
({\bf a},\alpha)={\bf a}\cdot\alpha
. It is also important to note that

\alpha\cdot{\bf a}={}^{\alpha}{\bf a}\cdot\alpha\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}$ and $\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}$.}

(9.15)

Indeed, this can be verified by directly showing that both sides evaluate to
(({}^{\alpha}{\bf a})\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({}^{\alpha}{\bf a})})
, keeping in mind the identifications
\alpha\equiv({\bf 1}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\alpha)
, etc. It is also easy to see diagrammatically; cf. Figure
2
.

a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{1}
a_{4}
a_{5}

Figure 6:
An example of
{\bf a}\cdot\alpha=({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})})
in
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
.

The remainder of this chapter will be split into two largely parallel strands, each dealing with one family of action pairs:

•

Sections
9.2
–
9.4
concern pairs of the form
(E,S)
, where
E=\mathcal{E}_{X}
or
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})
, and where
S\leq M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
.

•

Sections
9.5
–
9.7
concern pairs of the form
(U,S)
, where
U=M_{0}^{X}
or
M^{X}
, and where
S\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
.

The pairs of the first type are all strong, as are some (but not all) of the second type. No pair of the first type is proper (apart from trivially small exceptions), but some of the second type are proper.

9.2
Action pairs and subsemigroups I

Here is the first family of action pairs in
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
:

Proposition 9.16
.

For any set
X
and monoid
M
, the following are all strong action pairs in
{M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}}
:

(i)

(\mathcal{E}_{X},M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(ii)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X}),M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(iii)

(\mathcal{E}_{X},M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))
if
|X|<\aleph_{0}
,

(iv)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X}),M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))
if
|X|<\aleph_{0}
,

(v)

(\mathcal{E}_{X},M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X})
,

(vi)

(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X}),M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X})
.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
4.44
and
9.11
, together with (
9.12
) and (
9.13
).
∎

The next result characterises the semigroups
ES
arising from the pairs
(E,S)
in Proposition
9.16
. Again, parts
(iii)
and
(iv)
do not assume that
X
is finite, even though
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})=\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{X}
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{X}
are not semigroups for infinite
X
; in these cases,
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
simply refers to the relevant
subset
of
M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
, and similarly for
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
. (The
|X|=1
cases are excluded in these parts because the left-hand sets are empty, and the right-hand sets contain the empty map.)
For parts
(v)
and
(vi)
, we again write

\mathcal{F}_{X}=\big\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{I}_{X}}:{|X\setminus\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)|=|X\setminus\operatorname{im}(\alpha)|}\big\}.

As discussed in Remark
8.63
,
\mathcal{F}_{X}
is the largest factorisable inverse submonoid of
\mathcal{I}_{X}

[
14
]
. Note that
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{F}_{X})=\mathcal{F}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{X}
is always an (almost-factorisable inverse) subsemigroup of
\mathcal{F}_{X}
, even when
X
is infinite; cf. Example
4.27
.

Proposition 9.17
.

For any set
X
and monoid
M
, we have:

(i)

\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot(M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X})=M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
,

(ii)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})\cdot(M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X})=M\wr(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(iii)

\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot(M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
if
|X|\not=1
,

(iv)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})\cdot(M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))=M\wr(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{X})
if
|X|\not=1
,

(v)

\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot(M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X})=M\wr\mathcal{F}_{X}\subseteq M\wr\mathcal{I}_{X}
, with equality if and only if
|X|<\aleph_{0}
,

(vi)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})\cdot(M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X})=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{F}_{X})\subseteq M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{X})
, with equality if and only if
|X|<\aleph_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward, and in each case we can use the relevant part of Theorems
8.56
and
8.61
, applied to
\operatorname{PEnd}(A)=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, where
A=X
is viewed as an independence algebra with no operations.

For example, in
(iii)
, the main work is in showing the backwards inclusion. For this, consider some
({\bf a},\alpha)
from
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
, where
|X|\not=1
. By Theorem
8.56
(iii)
we have
\alpha=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\beta
for some
\beta\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
, where
B=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
. And then for any
{\bf b}\in M^{X}
with
{\bf a}={\bf b}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
, we have
({\bf a},\alpha)=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot({\bf b},\beta)
, with
({\bf b},\beta)\in M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
.
∎

Remark 9.18
.

As in Remark
8.59
, and again excluding trivially small exceptions, none of the action pairs
(E,S)
listed in Proposition
9.16
are proper. Hence (cf. Proposition
5.29
), none of the left restriction semigroups
ES
in Proposition
9.17
are proper. But by Theorem
5.39
each
ES
is covered by the proper left restriction semigroup

\big\{{(\mathrm{id}_{B},({\bf a},\alpha))\in E\rtimes S}:{\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)^{+}}\big\}=\big\{{(\mathrm{id}_{B},({\bf a},\alpha))\in E\rtimes S}:{B\subseteq\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}\big\}.

9.3
Congruence conditions I

In Section
9.4
we will use the results of Chapter
6
to give presentations for the semigroups arising from (some of) the pairs from Proposition
9.16
. We first examine which of the simplifying conditions on the right congruences hold for these pairs.

Lemma 9.19
.

Let
(E,S)
be one of the (strong) action pairs in
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
listed in Proposition
9.16
, with the exception of
(E,S)=(\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X}),M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X})
in the case that
|X|\geq\aleph_{0}
. Then
(E,S)
satisfies at least one of the conditions listed in Lemma
4.61
.

Proof.

This is proved in similar fashion to Lemma
8.65
. Again, the cases in which
E=\mathcal{E}_{X}
are clear, and for those involving
E=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{E}_{X})
it reduces to checking (using Proposition
9.17
) that any element
({\bf a},\alpha)
of the semigroup
ES
satisfies
\operatorname{im}(\alpha)\not=X
.
∎

As in Section
8.7
, we now restrict our attention to the pairs
(E,S)
from Proposition
9.16
in which

•

E=\mathcal{E}_{X}
, and

•

S
is one of
M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
,
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
or
M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
, where we must additionally assume that
|X|<\aleph_{0}
in the second case.

We are particularly concerned with determining which of the stronger congruence conditions considered in Section
4.5
and Chapter
6
are satisfied by these pairs. Recall that these conditions were stated in terms of the right congruences on
S
or
S^{1}
defined, for each
B\subseteq X
, by

\displaystyle\theta_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta))\in S\times S}:{\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot({\bf b},\beta)}\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta))\in S\times S}:{{\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}={\bf b}\mathord{\restriction}_{B},\ \alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}}\big\}

for
S=M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
S=M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
, and

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{id}_{B}}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta))\in S^{1}\times S^{1}}:{\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot({\bf b},\beta)}\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta))\in S^{1}\times S^{1}}:{{\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}={\bf b}\mathord{\restriction}_{B},\ \alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}}\big\}

for
S=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
, where
S^{1}=(M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))\cup\{\mathrm{id}_{X}\}
.
Again we will abbreviate these to
\theta_{B}
and
\Theta_{B}
. Note that we do not use the sub(semi)groups
S_{B}
in the case of
S=M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
, since this is not a group in general, even though
\mathcal{G}_{X}
is.

Lemma 9.20
.

If
S=M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
for any
X
, then
\theta_{B}\vee\theta_{C}=\theta_{B\cap C}
for any
B,C\subseteq X
.

Proof.

This follows quickly from Proposition
8.70
.
We need to show that any pair
(({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta))
from
\theta_{B\cap C}
belongs to
\theta_{B}\vee\theta_{C}
, so fix some such pair. Since
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B\cap C}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B\cap C}
, the pair
(\alpha,\beta)
belongs to the ‘
\theta_{B\cap C}
relation of
\mathcal{T}_{X}
’. So we let
\gamma\in\mathcal{T}_{X}
be as in the proof of Proposition
8.70
, so that
\gamma\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
and
\gamma\mathord{\restriction}_{C}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{C}
. Since
{\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B\cap C}={\bf b}\mathord{\restriction}_{B\cap C}
, we may also define

{\bf c}=(c_{x})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptc_{x}=\begin{cases}a_{x}&\text{if $x\in B$,}\\
b_{x}&\text{if $x\in C$,}\\
1&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.21)

and then
({\bf a},\alpha)\mathrel{\theta}_{B}({\bf c},\gamma)\mathrel{\theta}_{C}({\bf b},\beta)
.
∎

Lemma 9.22
.

If
S=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
for
|X|<\aleph_{0}
, then
\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C}=\Theta_{B\cap C}
for any
B,C\subseteq X
.

Proof.

This is similar to the proof of Lemma
9.20
, with a little care taken for the
|X|=2
case (cf. Proposition
8.71
and Remark
8.76
).

Beginning with the case
|X|\not=2
, we fix some
(({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta))
from
\Theta_{B\cap C}
, aiming to show it belongs to
\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C}
. We let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
be as in the proof of Proposition
8.71
(Case 1 or 3), and take
{\bf c}
as in (
9.21
), and we have
({\bf a},\alpha)\mathrel{\Theta}_{B}({\bf c},\gamma_{1})\mathrel{\Theta}_{C}({\bf c},\gamma_{2})\mathrel{\Theta}_{B}({\bf c},\gamma_{3})\mathrel{\Theta}_{C}({\bf b},\beta)
.

For the
|X|=2
case, we assume without loss of generality that
X=\{1,2\}
. We need only show that
\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C}=\Theta_{B\cap C}
when
B
and
C
are incomparable, and by symmetry the only case to consider is
B=\{1\}
and
C=\{2\}
. Since
B\cap C=\varnothing
and
\Theta_{\varnothing}
is the universal relation, we must show that every element of
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
is
(\Theta_{B}\vee\Theta_{C})
-related to
\mathrm{id}_{X}
. So fix some such
({\bf a},\alpha)\in M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
. By symmetry, we may assume that
\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Write
{\bf a}=(a_{1},a_{2})
, and set
{\bf b}=(1,a_{2})
. Then
({\bf a},\alpha)\mathrel{\Theta}_{C}({\bf b},\alpha)\mathrel{\Theta}_{B}\mathrm{id}_{X}
.
∎

Lemmas
9.20
and
9.22
allow us to simplify presentations for
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
arising from Theorems
6.5
and
6.36
; see Remark
6.6
and Theorem
6.44
(ii)
.
To utilise these results, we need generators for the right congruences
\theta_{B}
and
\Theta_{B}
in the case that
|X\setminus B|=1
, and for these we first recall the definition of certain idempotents from
\mathcal{T}_{X}
. Given distinct
x,y\in X
, we denote by
\varepsilon_{xy}\in\mathcal{T}_{X}
the unique idempotent with image
X\setminus y
and mapping
y\mapsto x
. That is,

z\varepsilon_{xy}=\begin{cases}z&\text{if $z\in X\setminus y$,}\\
x&\text{if $z=y$.}\end{cases}

(9.23)

See Figure
7
for
\varepsilon_{24}
and
\varepsilon_{42}
, with
X=\{1,\ldots,6\}
, but ignore the vertex labels.

Lemma 9.24
.

If
S=M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
for any
X
, and if
B=X\setminus y
for some
y\in X
, then
\theta_{B}
is generated as a right congruence by any pair
(\varepsilon_{xy},\mathrm{id}_{X})
with
x\in B
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be the right congruence generated by the stated pair. Since
\mathrm{id}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\cdot\varepsilon_{xy}
, we have
\sigma\subseteq\theta_{B}
. For the reverse inclusion, let
(({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta))\in\theta_{B}
. So
{\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}={\bf b}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
and
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
, and from these it quickly follows that
\varepsilon_{xy}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)=\varepsilon_{xy}\cdot({\bf b},\beta)
. Since
\mathrm{id}_{X}\mathrel{\sigma}\varepsilon_{xy}
, and since
\sigma
is a right congruence, we have

({\bf a},\alpha)=\mathrm{id}_{X}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)\mathrel{\sigma}\varepsilon_{xy}\cdot({\bf a},\alpha)=\varepsilon_{xy}\cdot({\bf b},\beta)\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}\cdot({\bf b},\beta)=({\bf b},\beta).\qed

The proof of the next result is essentially identical.

Lemma 9.25
.

If
S=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
for
|X|<\aleph_{0}
, and if
B=X\setminus y
for some
y\in X
, then
\Theta_{B}
is generated as a right congruence by any pair
(\varepsilon_{xy},\mathrm{id}_{X})
with
x\in B
. ∎

Remark 9.26
.

In Lemmas
9.24
and
9.25
, since the right congruences
\theta_{B}
and
\Theta_{B}
are generated by the pair
(\varepsilon_{xy},\mathrm{id}_{X})
for
any

x\in B=X\setminus y
, they are also generated by the set
\big\{{(\varepsilon_{xy},\mathrm{id}_{X})}:{x\in B}\big\}
of
all
such pairs.

We now move on to the case of
S=M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
, for which we must assume
X
is finite. It will be convenient to first give generators for the right congruences
\theta_{B}
for arbitrary
B\subseteq X
. To describe these, we begin with some notation. For
x\in X
and
a\in M
, we write
{\bf t}_{x;a}\in M^{X}
for the
X
-tuple whose
x
th coordinate is
a
, and all other entries are
1
. For distinct
x,y\in X
, we write
\tau_{xy}\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
for the transposition that interchanges
x
and
y
.

For the next lemma and its proof, recall that we identify a tuple
{\bf a}\in M^{X}
with the element
({\bf a},\mathrm{id}_{X})
of
M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
. The statement gives a generating set
\Omega_{B}
for the right congruence
\theta_{B}
, and
\Omega_{B}
is a union of two sets of pairs. A typical pair from the first has the form
({\bf t}_{x;a},\mathrm{id}_{X})
, and as just mentioned this is shorthand for the pair
({\bf t}_{x;a},\mathrm{id}_{X})\equiv(({\bf t}_{x;a},\mathrm{id}_{X}),({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X}))
. Similar comments apply to the second set, where we identify
(\tau_{xy},\mathrm{id}_{X})\equiv(({\bf 1},\tau_{xy}),({\bf 1},\mathrm{id}_{X}))
.

Lemma 9.27
.

Suppose
S=M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
for
|X|<\aleph_{0}
, and let
\Gamma
be a (monoid) generating set for
M
. Then for any
B\subseteq X
,
\theta_{B}
is generated as a right congruence by the set

\Omega_{B}=\big\{{({\bf t}_{x;a},\mathrm{id}_{X})}:{x\in X\setminus B,\ a\in\Gamma}\big\}\cup\big\{{(\tau_{xy},\mathrm{id}_{X})}:{x,y\in X\setminus B,\ x\not=y}\big\}.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be the right congruence generated by
\Omega_{B}
. It is easy to check that
\Omega_{B}\subseteq\theta_{B}
, so we have
\sigma\subseteq\theta_{B}
. It remains to show that
\theta_{B}\subseteq\sigma
.

First we claim that

{\bf t}_{x;a}\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $x\in X\setminus B$ and $a\in M$.}

(9.28)

To prove this, write
a=c_{1}\cdots c_{k}
, where
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\in\Gamma
. Since
({\bf t}_{x;c_{i}},\mathrm{id}_{X})\in\Omega_{B}\subseteq\sigma
for all
i
, it follows from Lemma
2.1
that
{\bf t}_{x;a}={\bf t}_{x;c_{1}}\cdots{\bf t}_{x;c_{k}}\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}
, and (
9.28
) is proved.

Now let
(({\bf a},\alpha),({\bf b},\beta))\in\theta_{B}
, so that
{\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}={\bf b}\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
and
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
. We must show that
({\bf a},\alpha)\mathrel{\sigma}({\bf b},\beta)
.
We begin by defining

{\bf c}\in M^{X}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptc_{x}=\begin{cases}a_{x}=b_{x}&\text{if $x\in B$,}\\
1&\text{if $x\in X\setminus B$.}\end{cases}

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
X\setminus B=\{1,\ldots,k\}
for some
k
, and we note that (
9.28
) gives
{\bf t}_{i;a_{i}}\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. It follows from Lemma
2.1
that
{\bf t}_{1;a_{1}}\cdots{\bf t}_{k;a_{k}}\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}
. Since
\sigma
is a right congruence, it follows from this that
{\bf a}={\bf t}_{1;a_{1}}\cdots{\bf t}_{k;a_{k}}\cdot{\bf c}\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}\cdot{\bf c}={\bf c}
, and then in turn that
({\bf a},\alpha)={\bf a}\cdot\alpha\mathrel{\sigma}{\bf c}\cdot\alpha
.

A symmetrical calculation gives
({\bf b},\beta)\mathrel{\sigma}{\bf c}\cdot\beta
, so we can complete the proof by showing that
{{\bf c}\cdot\alpha\mathrel{\sigma}{\bf c}\cdot\beta}
. Since
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
, we have
{}^{\alpha^{-1}}{\bf c}={}^{\beta^{-1}}{\bf c}
(the action is defined in (
9.2
)), so if we write
{\bf d}
for this tuple, then we have
{\bf c}\cdot\alpha=\alpha\cdot{\bf d}
and
{\bf c}\cdot\beta=\beta\cdot{\bf d}
. (Indeed, for the former we have
\alpha\cdot{\bf d}={}^{\alpha}{\bf d}\cdot\alpha={}^{\alpha}({}^{\alpha^{-1}}{\bf c})\cdot\alpha={\bf c}\cdot\alpha
, and the latter is similar.) Since
\sigma
is a right congruence, it therefore suffices to show that
\alpha\mathrel{\sigma}\beta
.
In fact, since
\alpha=\alpha\beta^{-1}\cdot\beta
and
\beta=\mathrm{id}_{X}\cdot\beta
, it is enough to show that
\alpha\beta^{-1}\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}
.

From
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
, we see that
\alpha\beta^{-1}
fixes
B
pointwise, so we may write
\alpha\beta^{-1}=\tau_{y_{1}z_{1}}\cdots\tau_{y_{l}z_{l}}
as a product of transpositions, where
y_{i},z_{i}\in X\setminus B
for each
i
. Since
(\tau_{y_{i}z_{i}},\mathrm{id}_{X})\in\Omega_{B}\subseteq\sigma
for all
i
, it follows from Lemma
2.1
that
\alpha\beta^{-1}=\tau_{y_{1}z_{1}}\cdots\tau_{y_{l}z_{l}}\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}
, as required.
∎

In Lemma
9.30
we show that the right congruences
\theta_{B}
on
S=M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
satisfy condition
(i)
of Lemma
4.71
. In order to do this, it is first convenient to prove the next lemma, which shows that the stronger condition
(ii)
of the same lemma
almost
holds.

Lemma 9.29
.

If
S=M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
for
|X|<\aleph_{0}
, then
\theta_{B}\vee\theta_{C}=\theta_{B\cap C}
for any
B,C\subseteq X
with
(X\setminus B)\cap(X\setminus C)\not=\varnothing
.

Proof.

Write
\sigma=\theta_{B}\vee\theta_{C}
; as usual, it is enough to show that
\theta_{B\cap C}\subseteq\sigma
. For this, it suffices to show that the generating set
\Omega_{B\cap C}
is contained in
\sigma
(cf. Lemma
9.27
). Much of
\Omega_{B\cap C}
is in fact contained in
\Omega_{B}\cup\Omega_{C}
(keeping
X\setminus(B\cap C)=(X\setminus B)\cup(X\setminus C)
in mind). Up to symmetry, any pair belonging to
\Omega_{B\cap C}\setminus(\Omega_{B}\cup\Omega_{C})
has the form
(\tau_{xy};\mathrm{id}_{X})
, for some
x\in X\setminus B
and
y\in X\setminus C
. By assumption we may fix some
z\in(X\setminus B)\cap(X\setminus C)
, and we note that
(\tau_{xz};\mathrm{id}_{X})\in\Omega_{B}\subseteq\sigma
and
(\tau_{yz};\mathrm{id}_{X})\in\Omega_{C}\subseteq\sigma
. It then follows from Lemma
2.1
that
\tau_{xy}=\tau_{xz}\tau_{yz}\tau_{xz}\mathrel{\sigma}\mathrm{id}_{X}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 9.30
.

Let
S=M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
for
|X|=n<\aleph_{0}
, and let
Q=\{{B\subseteq X}:{n-2\leq|B|\leq n-1}\}
. Then for any
B\subseteq X
, we have

\theta_{B}=\bigvee_{C\in Q,\atop B\subseteq C}\theta_{C}.

Proof.

If
B=X
, then
\theta_{B}=\Delta_{S}
, the stated join is empty, and the result is trivial. If
|B|=n-1
then we take
k=1
and
C_{1}=B
.

Now suppose
|B|\leq n-2
, and write
X\setminus B=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}\}
, noting that
l\geq 2
. For
1\leq i<l
let
C_{i}=X\setminus\{x_{i},x_{i+1}\}\in Q
, so that
B=C_{1}\cap\cdots\cap C_{l-1}
. Iterating Lemma
9.29
, we obtain

\theta_{B}=\theta_{C_{1}\cap\cdots\cap C_{l-1}}=\theta_{C_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\theta_{C_{l-1}}\subseteq\bigvee_{C\in Q,\atop B\subseteq C}\theta_{C}.

By (
4.70
), this completes the proof.
∎

9.4
Presentations I

It is now possible to give explicit presentations for the three semigroups

•

M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}=\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot(M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

•

M\wr\mathcal{I}_{X}=\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot(M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X})
, and

•

M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})=\mathcal{E}_{X}\cdot(M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))
,

where
M
is an arbitrary monoid, and where
X
is finite. Without loss of generality we may assume that
X={\bf n}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for some fixed integer
n
, and to avoid trivialities we assume that
n\geq 2
. As usual, we denote the semigroups involved by
\mathcal{E}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}
, and so on.

In what follows, we just give the full details for
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
, and indicate how to proceed for
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
M\wr\mathcal{I}_{X}
in Remark
9.39
; these other two (and more) will be treated fully in Section
9.7
.

To deal with
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})=\mathcal{E}_{n}\cdot(M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
, we need presentations for
\mathcal{E}_{n}
and
{M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})}
.
The following is well known (see for example
[
89
, p. 115]
), and is also a special case of Theorem
8.40
.

Theorem 9.31
.

For
n\geq 0
, the semilattice
\mathcal{E}_{n}=\{{\mathrm{id}_{A}}:{A\subseteq{\bf n}}\}
has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{E}}:{R_{E}}\rangle
via
\phi_{E}:X_{E}^{*}\to\mathcal{E}_{n}
, where:

•

X_{E}=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\}
,

•

t_{i}\phi_{E}=\mathrm{id}_{{\bf n}\setminus i}
for all
i
,

•

R_{E}=\big\{{(t_{i}^{2},t_{i})}:{i\in{\bf n}}\big\}\cup\big\{{(t_{i}t_{j},t_{j}t_{i})}:{i,j\in{\bf n}}\big\}
. ∎

Next we recall the presentation for
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
from
[
41
]
. For distinct
i,j\in{\bf n}
, and for
a,b\in M
, we define

\varepsilon_{ij;ab}=({\bf c},\varepsilon_{ij})\in M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptc_{k}=\begin{cases}a&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
b&\text{if $k=j$,}\\
1&\text{if $k\in{\bf n}\setminus\{i,j\}$.}\end{cases}

See Figure
7
for two examples with
n=6
.

a
b
b
a

Figure 7:
The generators
\varepsilon_{24;ab}
(left) and
\varepsilon_{42;ab}
(right) from
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{6})
.

The following is Theorem 5.2 of
[
41
]
. Theorem 5.9 of the same paper gives a presentation in terms of the smaller (all-idempotent) generating set

\{{\varepsilon_{ij;1b}}:{i,j\in{\bf n},\ i\not=j,\ b\in M}\}

in the special case that the underlying monoid
M
has the property that its left ideals are totally ordered under inclusion. In particular this property holds for groups, as they have no proper non-empty left ideals. In the next statement, we abbreviate
e_{ij;11}
to
e_{ij}
in the last two relations. We display relations as equations, so writing
u=v
instead of
(u,v)
, for readability; we also do this for several subsequent presentations. (Note that
{\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})=\varnothing}
if
n<2
.)

Theorem 9.32
.

For
n\geq 2
, the wreath product
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
has presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{S}}:{R_{S}}\rangle
via
\phi_{S}:X_{S}^{+}\to M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
, where:

•

X_{S}=\{{e_{ij;ab}}:{i,j\in{\bf n},\ i\not=j,\ a,b\in M}\}
,

•

e_{ij;ab}\phi_{S}=\varepsilon_{ij;ab}
for all
i,j,a,b
,

•

R_{S}
consists of the following relations, with
{a,b,c,d\in M}
arbitrary, and
i,j,k,l\in{\bf n}
distinct, in each:

\displaystyle e_{ij;ab}e_{ij;cd}=e_{ij;ac,bc}

\displaystyle=e_{ji;ba}e_{ij;dc},

\displaystyle e_{ij;ab}e_{kl;cd}

\displaystyle=e_{kl;cd}e_{ij;ab},

\displaystyle e_{ik;ab}e_{jk;1c}

\displaystyle=e_{ik;ab},

\displaystyle e_{ik;ab}e_{jk;c1}

\displaystyle=e_{ki;ba}e_{ji;c1}e_{ik;11},

\displaystyle e_{ik;aa}e_{jk;b1}

\displaystyle=e_{ik;11}e_{jk;b1}e_{ik;a1},

\displaystyle e_{ij;ab}e_{ik;cd}=e_{ik;ac,d}e_{ij;1,bc}

\displaystyle=e_{jk;bc,d}e_{ij;ac,1},

\displaystyle e_{ij;c,ad}e_{ik;1,bd}=e_{ik;c,bd}e_{ij;1,ad}

\displaystyle=e_{jk;ab}e_{ij;cd},

\displaystyle e_{ki}e_{ij}e_{jk}

\displaystyle=e_{ik}e_{kj}e_{ji}e_{ik},

\displaystyle e_{ki}e_{ij}e_{jk}e_{kl}

\displaystyle=e_{ik}e_{kl}e_{li}e_{ij}e_{jl}.

∎

Note that in the above relations, products in
M
sometimes appear in subscripts of letters from
X_{S}
, and additional commas are included to avoid any ambiguity. For example, in the first relation, the two subscripts from
M
appearing in
e_{ij;ac,bc}
are
ac
and
bc
.

Here then is the promised presentation for
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
.

Theorem 9.33
.

For
n\geq 2
, and with the notation of Theorems
9.31
and
9.32
, the wreath product
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
has presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{E}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{E}\cup R_{S}\cup R}\rangle
via

\Phi:(X_{E}\cup X_{S})^{+}\to M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}):\begin{cases}e_{ij;ab}\mapsto\varepsilon_{ij;ab},\\
t_{i}\mapsto\mathrm{id}_{{\bf n}\setminus i},\end{cases}

where
R
consists of the following relations, with
a,b\in M
arbitrary, and
i,j,k\in{\bf n}
with
i,j
distinct, in each:

\displaystyle e_{ij;ab}t_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e_{ij;ab}&\text{if $k=j$,}\\
t_{i}t_{j}e_{ij;ab}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
t_{k}e_{ij;ab}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.34)

\displaystyle t_{j}e_{ij}

\displaystyle=t_{j}.

(9.35)

Proof.

This is obtained from Theorems
6.44
(ii)
,
9.31
and
9.32
, and Lemmas
9.22
and
9.25
. Explicitly, these results lead to the presentation
\textup{{Sgp}}\langle{X_{E}\cup X_{S}}:{R_{E}\cup R_{S}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle
via
\Phi
, where

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(e_{ij;ab}t_{k},{}^{e_{ij;ab}}t_{k}\cdot e_{ij;ab})}:{i,j,k\in{\bf n},\ i\not=j,\ a,b\in M}\big\}

(9.36)

\displaystyle\and R_{2}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\big\{{(t_{j}N(\alpha),t_{j}N(\beta))}:{j\in{\bf n},\ (\alpha,\beta)\in\Omega_{j}}\big\},

(9.37)

where

•

{}^{e_{ij;ab}}t_{k}
is a (possibly empty) word over
X_{E}
mapping to
{}^{\varepsilon_{ij;ab}}\mathrm{id}_{{\bf n}\setminus k}
,

•

N:M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})\to X_{S}^{+}
is a normal form function, and additionally
N(\mathrm{id}_{n})=\iota
, and

•

\Omega_{j}
generates
\Theta_{{\bf n}\setminus j}=\Theta_{\mathrm{id}_{{\bf n}\setminus j}}
as a right congruence.

(Note that
R_{3}
from Theorem
6.44
(ii)
is empty because the action pair
(\mathcal{E}_{n},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n}))
is strong.)

By direct computation, we can take

{}^{e_{ij;ab}}t_{k}=\begin{cases}\iota&\text{if $k=j$,}\\
t_{i}t_{j}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
t_{k}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and then
R_{1}
is precisely the set of relations in (
9.34
).

By Remark
9.26
, we can take
\Omega_{j}=\big\{{(\varepsilon_{ij},\mathrm{id}_{X})}:{i\in{\bf n}\setminus j}\big\}
, and then
R_{2}^{\prime\prime}
is precisely the set of relations in (
9.35
).
∎

Remark 9.38
.

The
k=i
case of (
9.34
) says
e_{ij;ab}t_{i}=t_{i}t_{j}e_{ij;ab}
. One can show that (in the presence of the other relations) this is equivalent the slightly simpler
{e_{ij;ab}t_{i}=t_{i}t_{j}}
.

Taking
M
to be the trivial monoid,
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})\equiv\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
, and the above presentation reduces to the main result of
[
32
]
.

Remark 9.39
.

One can adapt the above method to deal with

M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{n}\cdot(M\wr\mathcal{T}_{n})\and M\wr\mathcal{I}_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{n}\cdot(M\wr\mathcal{G}_{n}).

Here, in addition to the presentation for
\mathcal{E}_{n}
from Theorem
9.31
, we need presentations for
{M\wr\mathcal{T}_{n}}
and
M\wr\mathcal{G}_{n}
. Since the latter are (monoid) semidirect products
M\wr\mathcal{T}_{n}=M^{n}\rtimes\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
{M\wr\mathcal{G}_{n}=M^{n}\rtimes\mathcal{G}_{n}}
, one could obtain such presentations from Lavers’ Theorem
6.4
. However, since we will deal with such wreath products in Section
9.7
, we do not provide the details here; see Theorems
9.59
and
9.68
–
9.70
.

9.5
Action pairs and subsemigroups II

We now move on to the second family of action pairs in
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
. Recall that
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
contains natural copies of
M_{0}^{X}
and
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, via the identifications

{\bf a}\equiv({\bf a},\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})})\and\alpha\equiv({\bf 1}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\alpha)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for ${\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}$ and $\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}$.}

Proposition 9.40
.

For any set
X
and monoid
M
, the following are all action pairs in
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, with respect to the action (
9.2
)
:

(i)

(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(ii)

(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{I}_{X})
,

(iii)

(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(iv)

(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
,

(v)

(M^{X},\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(vi)

(M^{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
.

For finite
X
, the following are also action pairs:

(vii)

(M_{0}^{X},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}))
,

(viii)

(M_{0}^{X},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{X}))
,

(ix)

(M_{0}^{X},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))
,

(x)

(M^{X},\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}))
.

Proof.

Given Lemma
4.18
, it suffices to show that
(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
is an action pair.
For
(A1)
, we have already noted in (
9.15
) that
\alpha\cdot{\bf a}={}^{\alpha}{\bf a}\cdot\alpha
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
. For
(A2)
we need to show that

{\bf a}\cdot\alpha={\bf b}\cdot\beta\ \Rightarrow\ {\bf a}\cdot\alpha^{+}={\bf b}\cdot\beta^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf a},{\bf b}\in M_{0}^{X}$ and $\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}$,}

where
\alpha^{+}={}^{\alpha}{\bf 1}={\bf 1}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}\equiv\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)}
. By (
9.14
), we have

\displaystyle{\bf a}\cdot\alpha

\displaystyle=({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)},\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})}),

\displaystyle{\bf a}\cdot\alpha^{+}

\displaystyle=({\bf a}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{dom}(\alpha^{+})},\alpha^{+}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})}),

\displaystyle{\bf b}\cdot\beta

\displaystyle=({\bf b}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{dom}(\beta)},\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf b})}),

\displaystyle{\bf b}\cdot\beta^{+}

\displaystyle=({\bf b}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{dom}(\beta^{+})},\beta^{+}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf b})}).

So the required implication quickly follows from:

•

the identities
\operatorname{dom}(\alpha^{+})=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)
and
\operatorname{dom}(\alpha^{+}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})})=\operatorname{dom}(\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})})
,

•

the analogous identities for
{\bf b}
and
\beta
, and

•

the fact that
\mathrm{id}_{C}=\mathrm{id}_{D}\ \Leftrightarrow\ C=D
for
C,D\subseteq X
, noting that
\alpha^{+}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})}
and
\beta^{+}\mathord{\restriction}_{\operatorname{supp}({\bf b})}
are both partial identities. ∎

Remark 9.41
.

Pairs
(v)
,
(vi)
and
(x)
from Proposition
9.40
have the form
(M^{X},S)
. Any such pair
(M^{X},S)
necessarily satisfies
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
, since
M^{X}
is not closed under the action of elements from
{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{X}}
. Any such pair also has the
right-
uniqueness property. Indeed, it quickly follows from (
9.14
) that

{\bf a}\cdot\alpha={\bf b}\cdot\beta\ \Rightarrow\ \alpha=\beta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf a},{\bf b}\in M^{X}$ and $\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}$.}

But this is to be expected, since for
S\leq\mathcal{T}_{X}
we have
M\wr S=M^{X}\rtimes S
; cf. Remark
9.8
.

We now identify the strong and proper pairs from Proposition
9.40
.

Proposition 9.42
.

Of the action pairs listed in Proposition
9.40
, and apart from trivially small exceptions:

(i)

the strong pairs are precisely
(iii)
–
(vi)
,
(ix)
and
(x)
,

(ii)

the proper pairs are precisely
(v)
,
(vi)
and
(x)
.

Proof.

(i)
. The stated pairs are precisely those of the form
(U,S)
with
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
, so this part follows immediately from Lemma
4.15
(ii)
and (
9.4
).

(ii)
. The stated pairs are precisely those of the form
(M^{X},S)
with
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
. For each of these, we have
P(U,S)=\{\mathrm{id}_{X}\equiv{\bf 1}\}
, so that
\sigma=\Delta_{S}
is the trivial relation. The required equivalence (
5.26
) then becomes

{\bf a}\cdot\alpha={\bf b}\cdot\beta\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt{\bf a}={\bf b}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha=\beta\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf a},{\bf b}\in M^{X}$ and $\alpha,\beta\in S$,}

and this again follows immediately from (
9.14
).

The pairs not listed are all of the form
(M_{0}^{X},S)
with
S\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
. To show that these are not proper we consider separate cases according to whether or not
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
. We must show that the following fails for some
{\bf a},{\bf b}\in M_{0}^{X}
and
\alpha,\beta\in S
:

{\bf a}\cdot\alpha={\bf b}\cdot\beta\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt{\bf a}\cdot\alpha^{+}={\bf b}\cdot\beta^{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha\mathrel{\sigma}\beta.

(9.43)

Case 1.
If
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
, then as above we have
\sigma=\Delta_{S}
, and
\alpha^{+}={\bf 1}
for all
\alpha\in S
. In this case, the forwards implication in (
9.43
) fails for
{\bf a}={\bf b}={\bf 0}
and distinct
\alpha,\beta\in S
, where
{\bf 0}=(0)_{x\in X}\in M_{0}^{X}
is the all-zero
X
-tuple.

Case 2.
Now suppose
S\not\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
. By the form of the pairs in question, we have
\varnothing=\mathrm{id}_{\varnothing}\in S
, and so
{\bf 0}=\varnothing^{+}\in P
, so that
\sigma=\nabla_{S}
is the universal relation in this case. Here the backwards implication in (
9.43
) fails for
{\bf a}={\bf b}={\bf 1}
and any
\alpha,\beta\in S
with
\operatorname{dom}(\alpha)=\operatorname{dom}(\beta)
but
\alpha\not=\beta
.
∎

Remark 9.44
.

As suggested by Case 1 in the above proof, one can make a general statement about proper action pairs in a certain special case concerning zero elements. Indeed, suppose
(U,S)
is a proper action pair in a monoid
M
with zero
0
, and suppose additionally that
0\in U
. Since
0\cdot s=0\cdot t
(and
0\cdot s^{+}=0\cdot t^{+}
) for all
s,t\in S
, it follows from (
5.26
) that
s\mathrel{\sigma}t
for all
s,t\in S
, meaning that
\sigma=\nabla_{S}
for such pairs.

On the other hand, if
(U,S)
is a weak action pair with
\sigma=\nabla_{S}
, then it follows from Definition
5.25
that
(U,S)
is proper precisely when

us=vt\ \Leftrightarrow\ us^{+}=vt^{+}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $u,v\in U^{1}$ and $s,t\in S$.}

This is of course an if-and-only-if version of the defining property
(A2)
from Definition
4.2
.

It is a routine matter to determine the subsemigroups
US
of
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
corresponding to the action pairs
(U,S)
in Proposition
9.40
. In the next statement, we again do not assume
X
is finite in the parts involving singular subsets/subsemigroups, and we again refer to the subsemigroup
\mathcal{F}_{X}\leq\mathcal{I}_{X}
from Remark
8.63
; recall that
\mathcal{F}_{X}=\mathcal{I}_{X}
when
X
is finite.

Proposition 9.45
.

For any set
X
and monoid
M
, we have:

(i)

M_{0}^{X}\cdot\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}=M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
,

(ii)

M_{0}^{X}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{X}=M\wr\mathcal{I}_{X}
,

(iii)

M_{0}^{X}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{X}=M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
,

(iv)

M_{0}^{X}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{X}=M\wr\mathcal{F}_{X}
,

(v)

M^{X}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{X}=M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
,

(vi)

M^{X}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{X}=M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
,

(vii)

M_{0}^{X}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(viii)

M_{0}^{X}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{X})=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{X})
,

(ix)

M_{0}^{X}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(x)

M^{X}\cdot\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})=M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X})
. ∎

9.6
Congruence conditions II

We now examine the congruence conditions for the pairs from Proposition
9.40
. Each such pair is of the form
(U,S)
, where
S\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, and
U
is either
M_{0}^{X}
or
M^{X}
, the latter only when
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{X}
. Since
U
is a monoid in each case, all such pairs satisfy all the conditions of Lemma
4.61
, and hence Lemma
4.65
applies to each pair, which then feeds into Proposition
6.1
.

The stronger congruence conditions involve the behaviour of the right congruences and sub(semi)groups

\theta_{\bf a}=\big\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in S\times S}:{{\bf a}\cdot\alpha={\bf a}\cdot\beta}\big\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptS_{\bf a}=\{{\alpha\in S}:{{\bf a}\cdot\alpha={\bf a}}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for ${\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}$,}

(9.46)

or the related right congruences
\Theta_{\bf a}
on
S^{1}
, as appropriate. Such conditions then feed into one of Theorems
6.5
,
6.13
,
6.28
,
6.36
or
6.44
, as applicable to the pair
(U,S)
. Theorem
6.13
applies when
U
and
S
are both submonoids, which obviously excludes the pairs with
S\subseteq\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})
. Theorem
6.44
(and indeed even Theorem
6.36
) is not generally applicable to
any
of the pairs, as
M_{0}^{X}\setminus\{{\bf 1}\}
and
M^{X}\setminus\{{\bf 1}\}
are typically not subsemigroups. Thus, for the remainder of the current section, we will only be concerned with the first six pairs from Proposition
9.40
.
By Proposition
9.45
, the four excluded pairs produce the following three product semigroups:

M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{X}),\hskip 20.00003ptM\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X})\and M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{X}).

Presentations for the first two (for finite
X
) are stated above in Theorems
9.32
and
9.33
. For the third (again for finite
X
), see
[
15
]
.

We have so far restricted our attention to the first six pairs
(U,S)
from Proposition
9.40
. In fact, since we are primarily concerned with finding presentations for the corresponding subsemigroup
US
(for finite
X
), and since the same subsemigroup can arise from different pairs, as seen in Proposition
9.45
(and recalling that
\mathcal{F}_{X}=\mathcal{I}_{X}
when
X
is finite), we can further restrict our attention to the following four pairs:

(a)

(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(b)

(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
,

(c)

(M^{X},\mathcal{T}_{X})
,

(d)

(M^{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
.

As in Proposition
9.45
, these give rise to the following subsemigroups (respectively), where we additionally assume
X
is finite in
(b)
:

(a)

M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
,

(b)

M\wr\mathcal{I}_{X}
,

(c)

M\wr\mathcal{T}_{X}
,

(d)

M\wr\mathcal{G}_{X}
.

Since the pairs
(c)
and
(d)
have the right-uniqueness property (cf. Remark
9.41
), the congruence
\theta
is trivial, as are the right congruences
\theta_{\bf a}
and sub(semi)groups
S_{\bf a}
. So in fact, we need only focus on pairs
(a)
and
(b)
in the current section, though some of the results we prove are more general.

Lemma 9.47
.

Let
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
, and write
B=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})
. Then for any subsemigroup
S\leq\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
we have

\theta_{\bf a}=\theta_{{\bf 1}_{B}}=\big\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in S\times S}:{\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}}\big\}.

Proof.

Since
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}={\bf 1}_{B}\cdot\alpha
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, we have
\theta_{{\bf 1}_{B}}=\big\{{(\alpha,\beta)\in S\times S}:{\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B}}\big\}
by definition.

Next, let
\alpha,\beta\in S
. Then since
{\bf a}={\bf a}\cdot{\bf 1}_{B}
, we have

(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{{\bf 1}_{B}}\ \Rightarrow\ {\bf 1}_{B}\cdot\alpha={\bf 1}_{B}\cdot\beta\ \Rightarrow\ {\bf a}\cdot\alpha={\bf a}\cdot{\bf 1}_{B}\cdot\alpha={\bf a}\cdot{\bf 1}_{B}\cdot\beta={\bf a}\cdot\beta\ \Rightarrow\ (\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{\bf a}.

The converse follows quickly from (
9.14
) and the definition of
\theta_{\bf a}
.
∎

Lemma 9.48
.

If
S=\mathcal{T}_{X}
or
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
, then

\theta_{{\bf a}{\bf b}}=\theta_{\bf a}\vee\theta_{\bf b}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf a},{\bf b}\in M_{0}^{X}$.}

Proof.

Throughout the proof we write
B=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})
and
C=\operatorname{supp}({\bf b})
, so
\operatorname{supp}({\bf a}{\bf b})=B\cap C
. By Lemma
9.47
, we need to show that

\theta_{{\bf 1}_{B\cap C}}=\theta_{{\bf 1}_{B}}\vee\theta_{{\bf 1}_{C}}.

Since
{\bf 1}_{B}
and
{\bf 1}_{C}
commute (and
{\bf 1}_{B}{\bf 1}_{C}={\bf 1}_{B\cap C}
), it follows from (
4.70
) that we only need to show the forwards inclusion.
To do so, suppose
(\alpha,\beta)\in\theta_{{\bf 1}_{B\cap C}}
, so that
\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B\cap C}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{B\cap C}
. We then let
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
be such that
\gamma\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}
and
\gamma\mathord{\restriction}_{C}=\beta\mathord{\restriction}_{C}
; in the case
S=\mathcal{T}_{X}
we also define
x\gamma=x
for all
x\in X\setminus(B\cup C)
. We then have
\gamma\in S
, and
\alpha\mathrel{\theta}_{B}\gamma\mathrel{\theta}_{C}\beta
.
∎

Remark 9.49
.

The previous result excluded the cases
S=\mathcal{I}_{X}
and
S=\mathcal{G}_{X}
since injectivity of
\alpha
and
\beta
does not imply that the
\gamma
constructed in the proof is injective.

Lemma
9.48
concerns the congruence condition from Lemma
4.71
(ii)
. However, that result can only be applied if
U
is commutative, and since
U=M_{0}^{X}
or
M^{X}
, this is only the case if
M
is commutative, and we do not need or want to assume this is the case. It turns out that while part
(ii)
of Lemma
4.71
does not apply in general, part
(i)
always does. While this might seem less desirable, it is in fact an improvement in this case, since we can choose the relevant subset
V\subseteq U
to be rather a lot smaller than a generating set for
U
, at least when
X
is finite, as we now explain.

For
x\in X
, we write
{\bf v}_{x}={\bf 1}_{X\setminus x}\equiv\mathrm{id}_{X\setminus x}
, and we write

V=\{{{\bf v}_{x}}:{x\in X}\}.

When
X
is finite we have

\langle V\rangle=\{{{\bf 1}_{B}}:{B\subseteq X}\}\equiv\{{\mathrm{id}_{B}}:{B\subseteq X}\}=\mathcal{E}_{X}.

Again writing
\preceq
for the relation in (
4.67
), note that

{\bf v}_{x}\preceq{\bf a}\ \Leftrightarrow\ x\not\in\operatorname{supp}({\bf a})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all ${\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}$ and $x\in X$.}

The following therefore verifies the conditions of Lemma
4.71
(i)
with respect to this
V
.

Lemma 9.50
.

If
S=\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{X}
or
\mathcal{T}_{X}
for
|X|<\aleph_{0}
, and if
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
, then

\theta_{\bf a}=\bigvee_{x\in X\setminus B}\theta_{{\bf v}_{x}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptB=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a}).

Proof.

By Lemma
9.47
we have
\theta_{\bf a}=\theta_{{\bf 1}_{B}}
. Since
{\bf 1}_{B}=\prod_{x\in X\setminus B}{\bf v}_{x}
, it follows from Lemma
9.48
that
\theta_{{\bf 1}_{B}}=\bigvee_{x\in X\setminus B}\theta_{{\bf v}_{x}}
.
∎

The proof of the next result is similar to (but easier than) that of Lemma
9.24
. For this result we do not need to assume that
X
is finite.

Lemma 9.51
.

If
S=\mathcal{T}_{X}
, and if
y\in X
, then
\theta_{{\bf v}_{y}}
is generated as a right congruence by any pair
(\varepsilon_{xy},\mathrm{id}_{X})
with
x\in X\setminus y
. ∎

We now have all the information we need for the pair
(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{T}_{X})
, so we now turn our attention to
(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
for finite
X
. For
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
, we have
\alpha\in S_{\bf a}\ \Leftrightarrow\ (\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{X})\in\theta_{\bf a}
, so by Lemma
9.47
we have

S_{\bf a}=\{{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}_{X}}:{\alpha\mathord{\restriction}_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptB=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a}).

The next result immediately follows, where again
\tau_{xy}
denotes the transposition
(x,y)
.

Lemma 9.52
.

If
S=\mathcal{G}_{X}
for
|X|<\aleph_{0}
, and if
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
, then
S_{\bf a}
is generated by the set
\{{\tau_{xy}}:{x,y\in X\setminus B,\ x\not=y}\}
. ∎

As in the proof of Lemma
9.29
, it is then easy to show that

S_{{\bf a}{\bf b}}=S_{\bf a}\vee S_{\bf b}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any ${\bf a},{\bf b}\in M_{0}^{X}$ with $(X\setminus\operatorname{supp}({\bf a}))\cap(X\setminus\operatorname{supp}({\bf b}))\not=\varnothing$.}

(9.53)

For distinct
x,y\in X
, we write
{\bf v}_{xy}={\bf v}_{x}{\bf v}_{y}={\bf 1}_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}\equiv\mathrm{id}_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}
. The next result shows that Lemma
4.74
(i)
applies to the pair
(M_{0}^{X},\mathcal{G}_{X})
, with respect to the set

V=\{{{\bf v}_{xy}}:{x,y\in X,\ x\not=y}\}.

Lemma 9.54
.

If
S=\mathcal{G}_{X}
for
|X|<\aleph_{0}
, and if
{\bf a}\in M_{0}^{X}
, then

S_{\bf a}=\bigvee_{x,y\in X\setminus B\atop x\not=y}S_{{\bf v}_{xy}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptB=\operatorname{supp}({\bf a}).

Proof.

If
|X\setminus B|\leq 1
, then
S_{\bf a}=\{\mathrm{id}_{X}\}
, and the stated join is empty. If
|X\setminus B|\geq 2
, then we argue as in the proof of Lemma
9.30
, applying (
9.53
) in place of Lemma
9.29
.
∎

9.7
Presentations II

We now assume that
X={\bf n}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for some integer
n\geq 2
, with the goal of giving presentations for the wreath products

M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}=M_{0}^{n}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n},\hskip 20.00003ptM\wr\mathcal{I}_{n}=M_{0}^{n}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{n},\hskip 20.00003ptM\wr\mathcal{T}_{n}=M^{n}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n}\and M\wr\mathcal{G}_{n}=M^{n}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{n}.

The wreath products
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{T}_{n})
and
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{I}_{n})
were treated in
[
41
,
15
]
, and
M\wr\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n})
in Theorem
9.33
.
For the remaining wreath products listed above, we need presentations for
M^{n}
,
M_{0}^{n}
,
\mathcal{G}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}_{n}
. For
\mathcal{G}_{n}
we have the following classical result, in which we continue to use the notation
\tau_{xy}
for the transposition
(x,y)\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
:

Theorem 9.55

(Moore
[
92
]
)
.

For
n\geq 2
, the symmetric group
\mathcal{G}_{n}
has presentation
{\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{G}}:{R_{G}}\rangle}
via
\phi_{G}:X_{G}^{*}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}
, where

•

X_{G}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\}
,

•

s_{i}\phi_{G}=\tau_{i,i+1}
for all
i
, and

•

R_{G}
consists of the following relations:

s_{i}^{2}=\iota\text{ for all $i$},\hskip 20.00003pts_{i}s_{j}=s_{j}s_{i}\text{ if $|i-j|>1$},\hskip 20.00003pts_{i}s_{j}s_{i}=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j}\text{ if $|i-j|=1$.}

The first presentation for
\mathcal{T}_{n}
was given by Aĭzenštat
[
1
]
, but it is convenient here to use the following formulation from
[
36
]
; see also
[
73
]
. The statement (and following ones) uses the maps
\varepsilon_{ij}
from (
9.23
).

Theorem 9.56

(Aĭzenštat
[
1
]
)
.

For
n\geq 2
, the full transformation semigroup
\mathcal{T}_{n}
has presentation
{\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{T}}:{R_{T}}\rangle}
via
\phi_{T}:X_{T}^{*}\to\mathcal{T}_{n}
, where

•

X_{T}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\}\cup\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n-1}\}\cup\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n-1}\}
,

•

s_{i}\phi_{T}=\tau_{i,i+1}
,
\lambda_{i}\phi_{T}=\varepsilon_{i,i+1}
and
\rho_{i}\phi_{T}=\varepsilon_{i+1,i}
for all
i
,

•

R_{T}
consists of the following relations:

\displaystyle s_{i}^{2}=\iota_{n},\hskip 20.00003pt\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{2}=\rho_{i}\lambda_{i}=s_{i}\lambda_{i}=\rho_{i}s_{i},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{i}=\rho_{i}^{2}=\lambda_{i}\rho_{i}=s_{i}\rho_{i}=\lambda_{i}s_{i},

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i+1}=\lambda_{i}s_{i+1},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{i+1}\rho_{i}=\rho_{i+1}s_{i},\hskip 20.00003pt\lambda_{i}\rho_{i+1}=\lambda_{i},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{i+1}\lambda_{i}=\rho_{i+1},

\displaystyle\lambda_{i+1}\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i+1}\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i+1}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i+1},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{i}\rho_{i+1}=\rho_{i}\rho_{i+1}\rho_{i}=\rho_{i+1}\rho_{i}\rho_{i+1},

\displaystyle\lambda_{i+1}s_{i}=s_{i}s_{i+1}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i+1},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{i}s_{i+1}=s_{i+1}s_{i}\rho_{i+1}\rho_{i},

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{j}=s_{j}s_{i},\hskip 20.00003pt\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{j}\lambda_{i},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{i}\rho_{j}=\rho_{j}\rho_{i},

if
|i-j|>1
,

\displaystyle s_{i}\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{j}s_{i},\hskip 20.00003pts_{i}\rho_{j}=\rho_{j}s_{i},

if
|i-j|>1
,

\displaystyle s_{i}s_{j}s_{i}=s_{j}s_{i}s_{j},

if
|i-j|=1
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\rho_{j}=\rho_{j}\lambda_{i},

if
j\not=i,i+1
.

∎

The generators for
\mathcal{G}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}_{n}
from Theorems
9.55
and
9.56
are pictured in Figure
8
.

Figure 8:
The generators
\lambda_{i}\phi_{T}
(left),
s_{i}\phi_{G}=s_{i}\phi_{T}
(middle) and
\rho_{i}\phi_{T}
(right), for
1\leq i\leq n-1
.

Presentations for
M^{n}
and
M_{0}^{n}
are easy to obtain (either directly, or by iterating Theorem
6.4
in the special case of direct products), so we fix notation for these as follows. For
a\in M
and
i\in{\bf n}
, we denote by
a^{(i)}\in M^{n}
the
n
-tuple over
M
whose
i
th entry is
a
, and all other entries are
1
. (This tuple was denoted
{\bf t}_{i;a}
in Section
9.3
, but the
a^{(i)}
notation is more convenient for our present purposes.) If
{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in M^{n}
, then
{\bf a}=a_{1}^{(1)}\cdots a_{n}^{(n)}
.

We assume the monoid
M
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M}}:{R_{M}}\rangle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{via}\hskip 20.00003pt\phi_{M}:X_{M}^{*}\to M.

For
w\in X_{M}^{*}
, we write
\overline{w}=w\phi_{M}
.
For each
i\in{\bf n}
, we define an alphabet
X_{M}^{(i)}=\{{x^{(i)}}:{x\in X_{M}}\}
in one-one correspondence with
X_{M}
. For a word
w=x_{1}\cdots x_{k}\in X_{M}^{*}
, we write
w^{(i)}=x_{1}^{(i)}\cdots x_{k}^{(i)}
, and we also define
{R_{M}^{(i)}=\big\{{(w^{(i)},v^{(i)})}:{(w,v)\in R_{M}}\big\}}
. We then define the alphabet

X_{M^{n}}=X_{M}^{(1)}\cup\cdots\cup X_{M}^{(n)},

and the set of relations

R_{M^{n}}=R_{M}^{(1)}\cup\cdots\cup R_{M}^{(n)}\cup\big\{{(x^{(i)}y^{(j)},y^{(j)}x^{(i)}}:{x,y\in X_{M},\ i,j\in{\bf n},\ i\not=j}\big\}.

Theorem 9.57
.

With the above notation,
M^{n}
has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M^{n}}}:{R_{M^{n}}}\rangle
via

\phi_{M^{n}}:X_{M^{n}}^{*}\to M^{n}:x^{(i)}\mapsto\overline{x}^{(i)}.

A presentation for
M_{0}^{n}
can be derived as a special case of Theorem
9.57
, beginning instead with a presentation for
M_{0}
. However, it is convenient to give a more explicit formulation, making use of the fact that
M
is a subsemigroup of
M_{0}
by definition. For this, we additionally use the notation of Theorem
9.31
, in particular the alphabet
X_{E}
and the relations
R_{E}
. We define the alphabet

X_{M_{0}^{n}}=X_{M^{n}}\cup X_{E},

and set of relations

\displaystyle R_{M_{0}^{n}}=R_{M^{n}}\cup R_{E}

\displaystyle\cup\big\{{(t_{i}x^{(j)},x^{(j)}t_{i})}:{x\in X_{M},\ i,j\in{\bf n},\ i\not=j}\big\}

\displaystyle\cup\big\{{(t_{i}x^{(i)},t_{i}),(x^{(i)}t_{i},t_{i})}:{x\in X_{M},\ i\in{\bf n}}\big\}.

Theorem 9.58
.

With the above notation,
M_{0}^{n}
has presentation
\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M_{0}^{n}}}:{R_{M_{0}^{n}}}\rangle
via

\phi_{M_{0}^{n}}:X_{M_{0}^{n}}^{*}\to M_{0}^{n}:x^{(i)}\mapsto\overline{x}^{(i)},\ t_{i}\mapsto{\bf 1}_{{\bf n}\setminus i}.

The generators for
M^{n}
and
M_{0}^{n}
from Theorems
9.57
and
9.58
are pictured in Figure
9
, each identified with a pair from
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
in the usual way.

(left) and

(right).

We can now give the main results of this section, which are presentations for the wreath products
M\wr S
, where
S
is one of
\mathcal{G}_{n}
,
\mathcal{T}_{n}
,
\mathcal{I}_{n}
or
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
. It is convenient to begin with the largest of these,
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
.

Theorem 9.59
.

For
n\geq 2
, the wreath product
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup X_{T}}:{R_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup R_{T}\cup R}\rangle

via

\Psi:(X_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup X_{T})^{*}\to M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}:\begin{cases}x^{(i)}\mapsto\overline{x}^{(i)},\\
t_{i}\mapsto\mathrm{id}_{{\bf n}\setminus i},\\
s_{i}\mapsto\tau_{i,i+1},\\
\lambda_{i}\mapsto\varepsilon_{i,i+1},\\
\rho_{i}\mapsto\varepsilon_{i+1,i},\end{cases}

where
R
consists of the following relations, with
x\in X_{M}
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
1\leq k\leq n
in each:

\displaystyle s_{i}x^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrlap{x^{(i+1)}s_{i}}\hphantom{XXXXXX}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
x^{(i)}s_{i}&\text{if $k=i+1$,}\\
x^{(k)}s_{i}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.60)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}x^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrlap{x^{(i)}x^{(i+1)}\lambda_{i}}\hphantom{XXXXXX}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
\lambda_{i}&\text{if $k=i+1$,}\\
x^{(k)}\lambda_{i}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.61)

\displaystyle\rho_{i}x^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrlap{\rho_{i}}\hphantom{XXXXXX}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
x^{(i)}x^{(i+1)}\rho_{i}&\text{if $k=i+1$,}\\
x^{(k)}\rho_{i}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.62)

\displaystyle s_{i}t_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrlap{t_{i+1}s_{i}}\hphantom{XXXXXX}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
t_{i}s_{i}&\text{if $k=i+1$,}\\
t_{k}s_{i}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.63)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}t_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrlap{t_{i}t_{i+1}\lambda_{i}}\hphantom{XXXXXX}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
\lambda_{i}&\text{if $k=i+1$,}\\
t_{k}\lambda_{i}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.64)

\displaystyle\rho_{i}t_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrlap{\rho_{i}}\hphantom{XXXXXX}&\text{if $k=i$,}\\
t_{i}t_{i+1}\rho_{i}&\text{if $k=i+1$,}\\
t_{k}\rho_{i}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(9.65)

\displaystyle t_{i}\rho_{i}

\displaystyle=t_{i}.

(9.66)

Proof.

By Proposition
9.40

(M_{0}^{n},\mathcal{T}_{n})
is a strong action pair, and by Proposition
9.45
we have
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}=M_{0}^{n}\cdot\mathcal{T}_{n}
. Theorem
6.5
therefore applies, and tells us that
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup X_{T}}:{R_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup R_{T}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{\Omega}}\rangle

via
\Psi
, where:

•

R_{1}=\big\{{(xy,{}^{x}y\cdot x)}:{x\in X_{T},\ y\in X_{M_{0}^{n}}}\big\}
, and

•

R_{\Omega}=\big\{{(N({\bf a}),N({\bf b}))}:{({\bf a},{\bf b})\in\Omega}\big\}
for any generating set
\Omega
for the congruence
\theta
on
M_{0}^{n}\rtimes\mathcal{T}_{n}
.

In
R_{1}
,
{}^{x}y
is a word over
X_{M_{0}^{n}}
mapping to
{}^{\overline{x}}\overline{y}
. So we must find a suitable set of such words
{}^{x}y
, for each combination of
x=s_{i}
,
\lambda_{i}
or
\rho_{i}
, and
y=x^{(k)}
or
t_{k}
. This has been carried out, and the resulting relations are precisely (
9.60
)–(
9.65
), as can be easily checked. We give a sample calculation in Figure
10
for the
k=i,i+1
cases of (
9.61
).

For the relations
R_{\Omega}
, we begin by defining the tuples
{\bf v}_{i}={\bf 1}_{{\bf n}\setminus i}\equiv\mathrm{id}_{{\bf n}\setminus i}=t_{i}\Psi
for each
i\in{\bf n}
.
By Lemmas
9.50
and
4.71
(i)
, we have
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
for

\Omega=\big\{{(({\bf v}_{i},\alpha),({\bf v}_{i},\beta))}:{i\in{\bf n},\ (\alpha,\beta)\in\Omega_{{\bf v}_{i}}}\big\},

where each
\Omega_{{\bf v}_{i}}
generates
\theta_{{\bf v}_{i}}
as a right congruence. By Lemma
9.51
we can take
\Omega_{{\bf v}_{i}}
to be the set consisting of the single pair
(\mathrm{id}_{\bf n},\varepsilon_{i+1,i})
. That is, we can take

\Omega=\big\{{(({\bf v}_{i},\varepsilon_{i+1,i}),({\bf v}_{i},\mathrm{id}_{\bf n}))}:{i\in{\bf n}}\big\}.

The resulting set
R_{\Omega}
therefore consists precisely of the relations (
9.66
).
∎

n
\overline{x}
=
\overline{x}

Remark 9.67
.

The relations in Theorem
9.59
contain a fair few redundancies. For example, the following relations could all be deleted:

(i)

the
k=i+1
(or
k=i
) cases of (
9.60
) and (
9.63
),

(ii)

all of (
9.61
) and (
9.64
), or alternatively all of (
9.62
) and (
9.65
).

Indeed, for
(i)
, we use
R_{G}
and the
k=i
case of (
9.63
) to transform
s_{i}t_{i+1}
into
t_{i}s_{i+1}
as follows:

s_{i}t_{i+1}\to s_{i}t_{i+1}s_{i}s_{i}\to s_{i}s_{i}t_{i}s_{i}\to t_{i}s_{i}.

(A similar calculation works for
s_{i}x^{(i+1)}\to x^{(i)}s_{i}
.) As an example calculation for
(ii)
, we transform
\lambda_{i}t_{i}
into
t_{i}t_{i+1}\lambda_{i}
using
R_{T}
, (
9.63
) and (
9.65
) as follows:

\lambda_{i}t_{i}\to\rho_{i}s_{i}t_{i}\to\rho_{i}t_{i+1}s_{i}\to t_{i}t_{i+1}\rho_{i}s_{i}\to t_{i}t_{i+1}\lambda_{i}.

Each of the remaining results will involve a restriction of the surmorphism
\Psi
from Theorem
9.59
, and a subset of the relations (
9.60
)–(
9.66
). The proofs of the next two results are exactly as for Theorem
9.59
, but with a little less to check.

Theorem 9.68
.

For
n\geq 2
, the wreath product
M\wr\mathcal{G}_{n}
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M^{n}}\cup X_{G}}:{R_{M^{n}}\cup R_{G}\cup R}\rangle

via
\Psi\mathord{\restriction}_{(X_{M^{n}}\cup X_{G})^{*}}
, where
R
consists of the relations (
9.60
). ∎

Theorem 9.69
.

For
n\geq 2
, the wreath product
M\wr\mathcal{T}_{n}
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M^{n}}\cup X_{T}}:{R_{M^{n}}\cup R_{T}\cup R}\rangle

via
\Psi\mathord{\restriction}_{(X_{M^{n}}\cup X_{T})^{*}}
, where
R
consists of the relations (
9.60
)–(
9.62
). ∎

For the final result, we need to work just a little harder.

Theorem 9.70
.

For
n\geq 2
, the wreath product
M\wr\mathcal{I}_{n}
has presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup X_{G}}:{R_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup R_{G}\cup R}\rangle

via
\Psi\mathord{\restriction}_{(X_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup X_{G})^{*}}
, where
R
consists of (
9.60
), (
9.63
), and the following relations:

t_{i}t_{i+1}s_{i}=t_{i}t_{i+1}.

(9.71)

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
9.59
, we quickly obtain the presentation

\textup{{Mon}}\langle{X_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup X_{G}}:{R_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup R_{G}\cup R_{1}\cup R_{\Omega}}\rangle,

where:

•

R_{1}
consists of relations (
9.60
) and (
9.63
), and

•

R_{\Omega}=\big\{{(N({\bf a}),N({\bf b}))}:{({\bf a},{\bf b})\in\Omega}\big\}
for any generating set
\Omega
for the congruence
\theta
on
M_{0}^{n}\rtimes\mathcal{G}_{n}
.

This time, Lemmas
9.52
,
9.54
and
4.74
(i)
tell us that
\theta=\Omega^{\sharp}
for

\Omega=\big\{{(({\bf v}_{ij},\mathrm{id}_{\bf n}),({\bf v}_{ij},\tau_{ij}))}:{1\leq i<j\leq n}\big\},

where
{\bf v}_{ij}={\bf v}_{i}{\bf v}_{j}={\bf 1}_{{\bf n}\setminus\{i,j\}}\equiv\mathrm{id}_{{\bf n}\setminus\{i,j\}}=(t_{i}t_{j})\Psi
for each
1\leq i<j\leq n
, and
\tau_{ij}
is the transposition
(i,j)
. Noting that
\tau_{ij}=((s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})s_{i}(s_{i+1}\cdots s_{j-1}))\phi_{G}
, the resulting set
R_{\Omega}
then consists of the relations

t_{i}t_{j}=t_{i}t_{j}\cdot(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})s_{i}(s_{i+1}\cdots s_{j-1})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for each $1\leq i<j\leq n$,}

(9.72)

and we note that (
9.71
) contains only those with
j=i+1
. But it is easy to see that (
9.63
) and (
9.71
) together imply (
9.72
). Indeed, writing
\sim
for the congruence generated by the claimed set of relations
R_{M_{0}^{n}}\cup R_{G}\cup R
, it is easy to see that

t_{i}(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})\sim(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})t_{i}\and t_{j}(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})\sim(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})t_{i+1},

(9.73)

and then

\displaystyle t_{i}t_{j}(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})s_{i}(s_{i+1}\cdots s_{j-1})

\displaystyle\sim(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})t_{i}t_{i+1}s_{i}(s_{i+1}\cdots s_{j-1})

by (
9.73
)

\displaystyle\sim(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})t_{i}t_{i+1}(s_{i+1}\cdots s_{j-1})

by (
9.71
)

\displaystyle\sim t_{i}t_{j}(s_{j-1}\cdots s_{i+1})(s_{i+1}\cdots s_{j-1})

by (
9.73
)

\displaystyle\sim t_{i}t_{j}

\displaystyle\text{by $R_{G}$.}\qed

Remark 9.74
.

Relations (
9.71
) are not included in the presentation from Theorem
9.59
, even though these relations obviously hold in the monoid
M\wr\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}_{n}
. We leave it as an exercise to show that (
9.71
) follows from the relations listed in Theorem
9.59
.
